- **After an interrupt the host exits with code 3**, after sending `handler.failed` with kind `interrupted`. goja leaves its promise job queue stuck when an interrupt lands inside a promise job, so every later `await` in that VM would hang; a fresh host is the only safe state. The supervisor restarts it (2–12 ms, the T241 spike). Exit codes: 0 shutdown or end of stdin, 1 the script could not be read, bundled, compiled or run, 2 a bad command line or hello, 3 interrupted.
- **The `$` API** (`internal/modapi`, the mods design §5). One schema lists the API's events, with their payload types (`internal/modbus`'s), and `$`'s methods, each with its params, result and the grant the broker checks. The host builds `$` from it after hello (`modapi.Bind` on `modhost.Options.Bind`): `$.on`, `$.log`, `$.tool.handle(id, handler)` (answers the manifest's agent tool `id`; a second call replaces the first) and `$.codehost.handle(method, handler)` (answers code host method `method`, one of `pr`, `prState`, `prHead`, `failedLog`, `merge`, `doctor`; anything else throws) are the host's own; every other method is a request named by its path (`$.ui.cell.set(column, target, spans)` sends `ui.cell.set {column, target, spans}`, the positional arguments named by the schema) and returns a promise of the broker's answer. Two are answered in the host and return their value: `$.settings()` (hello's settings, a fresh copy, `{}` for none) and `$.api.has(name)` (whether this tm has that method or event). A missing required argument, or a string argument that isn't a string, throws a `TypeError` in the mod; grants and everything else are the broker's to check. The TypeScript declarations mod authors import (`import type { TM } from "./types/tm"`, which also declares `$` and the timers globally) are generated from the same schema into `internal/modapi/tm.d.ts`: a test fails when they differ, and `go test ./internal/modapi -update` rewrites them. Not in v1's `$`: `ui.popup.open`, `ui.section.set` and `command.register` (Phase 2; panel sections came as the view slots `details.section` and `coordinator.section` of `$.ui.view.set`, commands as `[[provides.commands]]`).
- **Views** (`internal/server/modviews.go`, `internal/modview`; the mods design §6). `$.ui.view.set(view, widget, options?)` (the `views` grant) publishes the widget tree of a view the manifest declares, checked and cleaned by `widget.Decode` (control and format characters stripped, a glyph wider than one cell, an emoji or a CJK character, replaced by `?` rather than the tree refused; at most 1 MB of JSON, 256 widgets, stacks 4 deep, 5,000 rows, 4 KiB in one string but 64 KiB and 2,000 lines in a text widget's `text`, so a long markdown file fits in a few widgets); a list's or table's row may be a `head` (mod API 1.2: its spans drawn as a section rule across the width, after a blank line unless first; the cursor skips it, a filter hides it, and it has no cells, actions or `more`) and may carry `more`, at most 64 lines of spans drawn under it while the cursor is on it (a task's steps), without asking the mod; a table whose columns have no titles draws no header, a tree with time spans (`ago`, `until`, mod API 1.7) is laid out again each second it shows, for one project the mod runs for (`options.project`, as `view.opened` names it) or for all, a `details.section`'s also for one task or thread of that project (`options.task` `T12` or `options.thread` `t-0003`, one of them, as `view.opened` names it; for any other slot, or without the project, refused), with a tab's `count`, a card's or a section's `height` (its lines: a card's 8 when it asks none, at most 20; a `sidebar.section`'s, a `coordinator.section`'s and a `details.section`'s 4, at most 12, a right-pane section of a mod whose `api` is below 1.7 that asks none getting 12; a `sidebar.project.section`'s 2, at most 4) and, since mod API 1.7, a section's `badge` (spans drawn at the right end of its header, at most 4 of them and 12 cells; refused for a view that isn't a section, and a sidebar section's `count` is refused: its number goes in the badge); `null` clears it. A section whose tree is `null` (for its row, and for all projects) draws nothing, not even its header, so a mod shows a section only where it has something to say, and a view a mod's settings leave out stays off the screen. A view keeps at most 64 trees and a mod 4 MB of them, so a section's mod clears a row's tree on `view.closed`. Consoles poll the trees (`mod.views`) and say which views they show: a view shown by a first console sends the mod `view.opened {view, slot, project, task, thread, width, height}` (a section shown for another row is another view on show: the details panel moving from T12 to t-0003 closes the one and opens the other), and when no console shows it any more (or the consoles showing it stopped polling for 15 s) `view.closed`. A project tab opened to show a task picked elsewhere (the Tasks tab from NEEDS YOU) names it in `view.opened`'s `task` (mod API 1.4), for as long as that popup shows it; the console puts the cursor on the first row that binds an action on that task once the tree has one (the human's first key or click there gives up), so the mod need only list it. A human's declared key or click in a view (`mod.view.input`, refused for an agent caller) is sent to that mod alone as `ui.key` / `ui.click` (`{view, widget, row, key, target: {project}}`), and where the cursor of the view's focused list is as `ui.cursor` (`{view, widget, row, target: {project}}`, `row` `""` for an empty list): once the view shows on a console with its tree, then each time a key, a click, the filter or a publish puts it on another row or list (a console sends one at a time per view, the moves made meanwhile coming down to the latest), so a mod can follow the selection, a viewer beside its list. `$.ui.view.open(view)` (never a section's, which shows with its panel: `bad_params`) works only within 5 s of a key or a click (a cursor move doesn't count), once, and asks that input's console to show the view; otherwise it is refused (`denied`), so a timer or an event can't pop a view in front of the user. A host that (re)starts loses its trees, and gets `view.opened` again for each of its views on show; a failed host's views show its failure, not its trees. Mod API 1.7 adds two view slots, `sidebar.section` (a section of the band above or below the projects sidebar's tree, for the current project) and `sidebar.project.section` (a section under each active project's threads, for that project), at most 2 of them a mod, and two keys to a view in `[[provides.views]]`: `opens`, on a section of any of the four section slots, names its companion view, one of the same mod's views whose slot is `project.tab`, `dashboard.card` or `full` (checked at load); `enter` or a click on the section's header sends the mod `ui.click {view, target: {project}}` (the section's project: the current one for the band and the right pane), which is the human's input, so its handler may `$.ui.view.open` the companion. That click is the only input a section's mod gets. `description` (any view, at most 80 characters) is shown on the sidebar's details line while the cursor is on the section's header. The consent names the panels a mod draws and where (`Panels it draws: a project-popup tab "Review queue"`, `a details-panel section "Last report" (on the dashboard and beside a thread's pane)`, `a coordinator-panel section "Reports" (beside the coordinator's pane)`, `a sidebar section "Ticker" (above or below your projects, on every screen)`, `a section under each project "ci status" (in the sidebar)`).
- **Supervisor** (`internal/modsup`, the mods design §7 and §8). The server gives it every installed, valid, compatible and enabled code mod (§5.3), looking at `config.toml` and `~/.terminatr/mods` again every 3 s, so enabling, disabling, regranting, limiting or updating a mod takes effect without a server restart; a changed mod's host is stopped and started again, which also clears a failure. Each host is `tm mod host --budget 5s <bundle>` from the server's own (pinned) binary, with an empty environment (only `SystemRoot` on Windows), its working directory the mod's data folder `state/mods/<id>/`, and its stdout the protocol. Before each start a user mod's `main` is bundled with `modhost.Build` into `cache/mods/<id>/` (cached while its files are unchanged); a bundled mod gives its precompiled script, written there once and run without esbuild. After hello the server subscribes the mod to the event bus (`Server.SubscribeMod`, the hello's events and later `subscribe`s as the queue's filter) and delivers its events one at a time (`modbus.Deliver`); a host that ends stops delivery, and the next one subscribes afresh and gets `mod.ready`, never a replay.
  - **Lazy start.** `cache/mods/<id>/host.json` records the bundle the last host ran and the events it handled. While the bundle is the same, the next host (a server start, an enable, a change) starts on the first of those events, which is then delivered to it; the mod is `idle` until then. A host that also handles agent tools records them there too, and an idle one starts on the first call of one (a call waits for a starting host; a mod in backoff or failed answers at once); a mod that handles only tools idles until then. A mod never run, one whose bundle changed, one that handles nothing but `mod.ready` and no tool (it runs on timers), and any code mod that declares columns (`provides.columns`), built-in or the user's, start at once: such a mod draws on the sidebar, and the cells shown from its last run (§5.3 **Cells**) would otherwise stay stale until something changed, since a lazy host gets no `mod.ready` before its first event.
  - **States**: `idle`, `starting` (until the hello answer), `running`, `backoff`, `failed`. A host that exits unasked or with 1 or 2, or is killed, has crashed: it is restarted after 1 s, 5 s, then 30 s (back to 1 s once a host ran 5 minutes), and a third crash within 5 minutes leaves the mod `failed` with the reason, its areas blank, until it is changed or restarted (`Supervisor.Restart`). A host that exits 3 (interrupted, §3.7 above) is restarted at once and isn't a crash; three interrupted handlers in a row (no handler of another event completing between them) fail the mod.
  - **Limits.** The CPU budget is the host's (5 s a slice). Every 2 s the supervisor reads the hosts' resident set from the OS (`/proc/<pid>/statm`, `ps` on macOS, the working set on Windows) and kills a host past 256 MiB, the only memory limit since goja has no heap cap; every 30 s each host must answer `stats` within 10 s or is killed. Both count as crashes. A host gets 15 s to answer hello and 2 s to exit after `shutdown` when it is stopped (a disable, the server stopping), then is killed.
  - **OS sandbox** (`internal/plat/sandbox`, the mods design §7). Each host starts in the OS sandbox: it may run the tm binary and read its bundle (and the system libraries and time zone a program needs to start), and nothing else; it opens no other file, writes none, makes no network connection and starts no program, since the broker does its I/O over the pipes. macOS: a seatbelt profile (`deny default`) through `/usr/bin/sandbox-exec`, which then execs the host in place. Linux: landlock (every file right the kernel's ABI knows handled, TCP bind and connect from ABI 4, signals and abstract sockets scoped from ABI 6), applied to the server thread that starts the host, which the host inherits, with `no_new_privs`; once the host has read its bundle it adds a seccomp filter on all its threads that refuses opening files, sockets, `execve` and kernel surfaces (`ptrace`, `mount`, `bpf`…) with `EPERM` (amd64 and arm64). Windows: a restricted token (only `SeChangeNotifyPrivilege`, Administrators deny-only, access checked also against the RESTRICTED, Everyone, Users and logon SIDs, so the user's own files are refused) at low integrity (no writes), the bundle given an ACE for RESTRICTED, in a Job Object that allows one process, kills it with the server and denies the desktop and clipboard; network isn't refused there by the OS. Where the sandbox can't apply (a kernel without landlock and no seccomp filter for its architecture, a server already in a seatbelt sandbox) the host runs without it: said once in its log, in `mod.hosts`'s `sandbox` (`none: <why>`, else the mode, as `seatbelt`) and as a `tm doctor` warning; the language and process sandboxes still apply.
  - **Log.** `logs/mods/<id>.log`: the host's stderr (`stderr: …`), its `$.log` lines (`info: …`), its failed handlers with their stack, and the supervisor's own lines (`supervisor: started: pid 812 in 19ms …`, `… restarting in 5s`, `failed: …`, `stopped`), rotated at 1 MiB to `<id>.log.1`. What the host gives the log is one line each, at most 8 KiB cut on a character's edge, with a newline, an escape or any other control or format character written out (`\n`, `\x1b`, `‮`, a backslash doubled; `textsafe.Escape`), so a mod's text can't pass for a line of tm's or act on the terminal that shows the log; a stack is a line per frame, indented. Starts, restarts and failures also go to the server log.
  - **Safe mode.** A server started with `TERMINATR_NO_MODS=1` (what `tm --no-mods` sets, in its own environment and so in a server it starts, launchd's included) gives the supervisor no user mod, only bundled ones, so a mod can never lock someone out of their own dashboard. The mods stay installed and enabled; `mod.hosts` says `no_mods`, the Mods tab says so on its first line, `tm doctor` warns, and `mod.restart` is refused. A user mod's views (tabs, cards, full views) say `<name> is off in safe mode (tm --no-mods)` instead of waiting for it to draw. The console follows: with `TERMINATR_NO_MODS` set it loads no user mod's data, so it uses the built-in theme, icon set, layout and keymap (a keymap mod can make keys unreachable) and offers no mod's in the pickers; the footer says so once (`safe mode (tm --no-mods): built-in theme, icons, layout and keymap…`), and the console puts its own sidebar (the built-in layout's, with `ui.json`'s top level) on the view and writes nothing under a layout in `ui.json`, and General settings has a read-only **Safe mode** row. Settings in `config.toml` stay; they apply again outside safe mode. `tm --no-mods` finding a server already running with mods says `tm --no-mods server restart`, in the console's message line as it starts (and on stderr).
- **Scripts and bundles.** A mod's `main` is TypeScript or JavaScript. esbuild bundles it with its imports into one ES2017 IIFE (ES2017 keeps async functions, which goja runs, and lowers async generators, which it lacks). Imports are relative paths inside the mod's folder (after symlinks) and only `.ts`, `.mts`, `.js`, `.mjs` and `.json` files; a bare (`node_modules`) or outside import fails the bundle, and type-only imports are dropped. `modhost.Build` bundles at install, update and `tm mod dev` into a cache folder: the bundle is named by the sha256 of its content, and `bundle.json` beside it records esbuild's version and the sha256 of every file read, so an unchanged mod isn't bundled again and a bundle whose bytes don't match its name is rebuilt. The host runs a `.js` file as a bundle and bundles a `.ts` or `.mts` file itself (development and tests).

---

## 4. Dashboard

`tm` with no arguments opens the dashboard. It is a client and holds no state: it is one of the two screens of the console's view (§3.3, Views), whose selection, current project and sidebar it shows. Every console running `tm` joins view `main`, so when one opens a session, goes back to the dashboard or moves the sidebar, the others follow; `tm --own` opens a console with a view of its own.

**The coordinator owns all communication.** The user talks only to coordinators, and threads talk only to their coordinator. The dashboard shows threads, tasks and the inbox so the user can see where things stand, but it has no keys that act on them: acknowledging a report, sending a thread its next prompt and marking a task done are the coordinator's `tm` commands (§10), which it runs when the user asks. The keys that reach the coordinator from the task list, `D`, `A` and `x` (**Delegate**, **Accept and send back**, below), change no task either: they only ask the coordinator. A thread's pane takes keys like any other, for the edge cases (reading along, answering a prompt); typing into it tells its coordinator (§4).

```
 PROJECTS                    2 │ tm dashboard                                  ● server ok · 6 sessions · v0.15.0
 ■ terminatr              3 ⚑ │ NEEDS YOU 1 ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
 └─ coordinator              ○ │  T8 Remove the prefix caption         ◆ review    ▰▰▰▰▰  3/3  t-0001
    ├─ T3 Bootstrap re…  60% ● │ terminatr ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    ├─ T4 libghostty s…  30% ● │  coordinator                          ○ idle      2 inbox
    └─ T5 Claude spike       ▲ │  T3 Bootstrap repo + spec             ● working   60% 3/5 ▸ Write SPEC §8  4m  t-0002
 ■ foodperfect             0   │  T4 libghostty spike                  ● working   30% 2/7 ▸ Build the lib  1m  t-0003
 └─ coordinator              ▲ │  T5 Claude spike                      ▲ blocked   permission  0m  t-0004
                               │  T6 Docs pass                         ✓ done      report new  PR #12  t-0005
                               │  tasks: 1 needs you (t lists them) · 3 in motion · 4 on deck
                               │ OTHER SESSIONS 1 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
                               │
                               │────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
                               │ ≡ menu · enter attach · a project · t tasks · i inbox · , settings · ? help · prefix+q quit
```

**Version**. The help (`?`) opens with the version of the running server, faint (`terminatr v0.15.0`), and of this tm when it differs (`terminatr v0.15.0 (tm v0.16.0)`), and the `≡` menu has it as its title; the dashboard's header says only `● server ok · 6 sessions`. `session.list` carries `version` and `build` (an older server sends none and the sidebar shows nothing; the slim strip and a sidebar too short for the tree plus its footer show none either). The sidebar's footer (below its key bar) holds one faint hint, and only one: when this tm's build is not the server's (`brew upgrade` without a restart) it reads `tm v0.16.0 is installed, the server runs v0.15.0: run tm server restart in a terminal` (a restart ends running turns, so no key does it), short as `run tm server restart` when the sidebar is too narrow for it; when this console's tm is the older one (started before an upgrade the server has restarted on since), restarting the server fixes nothing, so it reads `this console runs tm v0.24.0, the server v0.25.0: quit it and run tm again` (short `old console: quit, run tm again`), in the warning colour, and the dashboard's footer message (or an attach's status bar) says the same once; else, when the server's daily check (§10.1) found a newer release, `tm v0.16.0 is out: brew upgrade terminatr` (`tm update` for a direct install), short as `v0.16.0 is out: brew upgrade terminatr`. The dashboard's own footer carries no version or hint. The settings popup (`,`) opens with a faint About line: `About: tm v0.16.0 · server v0.15.0 · update check on` (`server unknown` while the server hasn't answered, `(another build)` after the server's version when its build is not this tm's). `[ui] update_check = false` turns the check, and so the second hint, off.

- **Rows.** There are three sections, in the order of the layout's `dashboard.sections` (below; the order here is the default). NEEDS YOU comes first: the current project's. Then the current project's own section (headed by its slug): its coordinator, its threads, its other sessions and its task counts; the sidebar's tree lists every project, so the list has no section of all projects. A click on a project in the tree shows its section. A click on the sidebar's `PROJECTS` header, or `enter` on it, shows every project's NEEDS YOU together instead (**All projects**, below). OTHER SESSIONS, by default last, lists the sessions outside the projects (shells, the user's own agents); when there are none but the header counts some, it says how many are in the projects. The header names the app (`tm dashboard`), never a project, so a project called `terminatr` isn't named twice.
- **NEEDS YOU** lists only what waits on the user, of the current project only (`internal/tui/rows.go`, `buildRows`): its coordinator when `blocked` (a question or a permission dialog) and its tasks in the board's Needs you group (`review` or `blocked`); another project's never show there, so the project's rows carry no project label (the section is the project's, as the one under it is). The blocked sessions of the user's own outside the projects (an agent started with `tm session start`, which has no coordinator) belong to no project, so they show under every project's, last, by their session id. The project's rows, in order: its blocked coordinator, then its held messages and paused links (§7.9: `terminatr  1 message waits  ▲ blocked  enter opens Messages`, `enter` opening the project popup's Messages tab), then its coordinator's open questions (§7.5 **Questions**: `terminatr  2 questions waiting  ▲ blocked  enter answers them`; `enter` has the coordinator open them, `questions.open`, the footer saying so, and the sidebar's project row hints as for a task that needs you), then its tasks, one row each (`terminatr  T8 Remove the prefix…  ◆ review  3/3  t-0001`: the task, its status, its steps and its thread; the section says why it is there, so its rows carry no mark and share the other sections' columns). A task row is there to be seen: `enter` (or a double-click) opens the project popup on its Tasks tab with that task selected, and the details panel shows the task's notes and steps; nothing on the dashboard changes it, which stays the coordinator's (`tm task`, when the user asks); the task list's `A` and `x` ask the coordinator to accept it or send it back (§4, **Accept and send back**). Everything about threads (a blocked thread, an unacknowledged report, a `Waiting for you` self-report) goes to the coordinator's inbox; the coordinator asks the user when it needs them.
- **All projects.** A click on the sidebar's `PROJECTS` header (off its `⚑ N`), or `enter` or `→` with the sidebar's cursor on it, shows every project's NEEDS YOU in one list: the view's current project is then `*` (`allProjects`, which no slug can be), so every console of the view shows it, and the header's title is ` tm dashboard · all projects`. Each row is labelled with its project in the first column (`terminatr  T8 Remove the prefix…  ◆ review  3/3  t-0001`, `foodperfect  coordinator  ▲ blocked  question`), the projects in the sidebar's order, each project's rows as above; then the shared projects of paired servers (§16), from the mirror's last watch of each (`peer.list`, §16.10; the remote dashboard's own NEEDS YOU, below), labelled `mini › acme-api`: its open questions and what its ProjectWatch says waits on its user (`T3 Rate limits  ◆ review  in review  t-0009`), where `enter` shows that project's remote dashboard; then the user's own blocked sessions, as in a project's. With nothing anywhere it says `nothing waits on you in any project`. It has no project section and no mod cards; OTHER SESSIONS stays. `enter` on a row does what it does in a project's NEEDS YOU, and the keys about a project (`a`, `t`, `i`) act on the selected row's project, with none on a session outside the projects. The `⚑ N` badge of a hidden sidebar counts what this view lists of this server's, and a click on it opens this view. While it shows, the header is drawn as the row you are on, and `tab` into the sidebar puts the cursor there.
- **Task counts.** The project's section ends with its task counts; when some need the user, the count says where to find them: `1 needs you (t lists them)`, as the project popup's overview does with `(Tasks tab)`.
- **Per-row data.** Each row shows:
  - the state from the server's arbitration (§8.4);
  - the derived percent, done/total, and the current todo or step after `▸` (§7.3); the self-reported activity appears only when there are no todos or steps;
  - the time since the last change, and the linked task id.

  Selecting a thread row shows its full todo list, its task's steps and its latest report's state (`report 2, new · for the coordinator`, or `read`), to read: in the details panel beside the list, or under the row when the window is too narrow for the panel. A report's `## Next` lines are the coordinator's and never show in the TUI. Threads are ordered as in §7.4. A thread that called `tm done` shows `✓ done` (unless it is working or blocked); a row with an unacknowledged report says `report new`, and a blocked row its reason, first after the state, so neither is cut off; then the progress and the PR number from the report, and the thread's own id last, which a narrow row cuts first.
- **Details panel.** A panel shows everything about the selected row: a thread's state, task, progress, PR, report and the lines above; a task's notes and steps; a session's directory, command and progress; a project's coordinator, counts and inbox. Where it shows is its place (the layout's `details.place`, §5.3, or the Settings row): `right` (the default), right of the list behind a `│` divider when the dashboard (the window less the sidebar) is at least 120 columns wide, the list taking 65% of the width by default; `bottom`, under the list across the dashboard behind a `─` rule, when the body (between the header and the footer) has at least 16 rows, taking 40% of them by default and leaving the list at least 8; `off`, no panel. Without the room for its place, or off, a selected thread's details show under its row instead. `<` and `>` make the list smaller and larger (its width beside the panel, its height above it), dragging the divider with the mouse does the same, and `|` hides or shows the panel at its place (at the right when the layout says `off`); when it has no room the footer says why. The wheel over the panel scrolls it, as `↑` `↓` do while it has the keyboard (`tab`). The place and the size are each console's own, kept in `ui.json` (§5.1): under the layout's id when the layout sets that part, else at its top level.
- **Look.** Colours are the terminal's 16 ANSI colours, so they follow the user's theme; `NO_COLOR` turns them off. Every state also has its own glyph (● working, ▲ blocked, ○ idle, ◌ starting, ▷ running, ◆ in review, ✓ done; ⚑ in the sidebar: something in the project needs you), one meaning per glyph, so colour is never the only signal; the help's **Symbols** list names each one. Section headings are bold and UPPERCASE with a faint rule to the edge, uncoloured but NEEDS YOU (yellow); columns are two cells apart. `docs/STYLE.md` is the style guide every screen follows. A row shows a five-cell progress bar when it still fits. The list's columns adapt to its width: the title column takes a share of the room (20 to 40 cells), rows of the project's own section have no project column, and the state column is the glyph and word only. With the details panel the list takes 65% of the dashboard by default.
- **Help** (`?`) lists every key, grouped (the dashboard, the mouse, a session's `prefix+<key>` commands, the sidebar, the project popup, the settings, and a **Mod commands** group when mods add some), as wide as the window, each line wrapped so nothing is cut off; the arrows scroll it, `esc` closes it. Its first line names the configured prefix (`The prefix key is ctrl+b: …`), the one place besides the settings and the project popup that shows it, and it ends with **Symbols**: every glyph tm draws and its one meaning. The project popup's Keys tab is the same list: both draw from one table (`internal/tui/keymap.go`, the dashboard's part from the actions table), so they can't drift. The Mod commands group lists the enabled mods' commands that run for the project shown (none in safe mode, none of a mod limited to other projects): each with its title and the mod's name, its effective keys (the dashboard key and `prefix+<key>`; the ≡ menu when it has none or the core holds the key: core wins) and `tm mod run`; keys bound only inside a mod's own view stay in that view's hint.
- **Popups.** Help, the project popup, a shown task, prompts and the settings open as bordered boxes over the dimmed dashboard; `esc` closes the topmost, and no other key does: not `q`, not the key that opened it (a click outside closes it too). A prefix command over a popup is that command (the key table, below), never a key for the popup. Every popup and dialog has its title in its top border and its own keys in an action row inside its frame, the last row, each hint a button; the footer under it lists none of them and still shows messages, each until the next key. The whole scene under a popup dims, the sidebar too; a popup opened from another (a task from the Tasks tab, a setting's list, a question) draws over it, the one under it dimmed, and `esc` goes back to it. Popups have two sizes (docs/STYLE.md S7): dialogs (a confirmation, a question, one input, the prefix key) 64 columns by 12 rows, views (help, the project popup, the task view, the inbox, the settings, lists) four fifths of the window's width by four fifths of its height, uncapped, their content scrolling inside (`more ↓`). Both are centred on the whole window, header and sidebar included, shrinking to fit with a one-cell margin on a small one; a context menu stays where it was clicked. A question over a session (remote control) is the same dialog. Which popup is open is each console's own (overlays are per client, §3.3): opening one never opens it on the other consoles of the view.
- **Project popup** (`a` on the dashboard, the prefix then `a` in a session; `t`, the prefix then `t`, opens it on the Tasks tab and `i`, the prefix then `i`, on the Inbox tab: there is no separate task board or inbox): the selected (else the current) project in eight tabs; `←` `→` or `1`–`8` switch (not `tab`, which is the prefix command's next area), `esc` closes. Its size and place come from the window alone (a view, above), so switching tabs never moves or resizes it; the tab bar stays at the top and only the tab's content scrolls under it, keeping the selection in view (the arrows past the first or last item, `pgup` `pgdown` and the wheel scroll it too).
  1. **overview** (the tab names are lowercase): the slug, the goal, the repositories (`+` adds one from a typed path, `x` removes the selected one after a `y`; the coordinator can only remove one, with `tm project repo remove`), the machines (this one in v0.1), the coordinator's agent and state (or the agent a new one runs), the threads' agents, and the task counts.
  2. **inbox** (`i` opens the popup here): what waits for the coordinator, read-only, with the line `The coordinator handles these (tm inbox done).` under it; the rows select with the arrows, a click or the wheel.
  3. **tasks** (`t` opens the popup here): drawn by the built-in `tasks-tab` mod (§5.3, **Built-in mods**; Mod views): the project's tasks under section rules NEEDS YOU, IN MOTION, ON DECK, BACKLOG and DONE, each a row with its id, title, status, `n/n` steps and thread (`asked the coordinator` first while an item asks about it). The steps themselves show only under the task the cursor is on, unless it is done (the selected task's full list is also in the info panel and the shown task), so a long board stays short. BACKLOG (the open tasks) is listed after ON DECK and before DONE. DONE is folded: its rule and one line, `… N done (b shows them)`, until `b` lists them, newest first (by `updated`, then id), and `b` again folds them (`b done` / `b hide done` under the list). The cursor, the filter (`/`), the scroll and `j` `k` are the console's. Each row binds the actions its status allows (**Action binding**, §5.3), and the footer lists them in the core's words: `enter show`, and `A accept · x send back` in review, `x send back` when done, `c coordinator · D delegate` when blocked, `D delegate` when open or ready, none when started; the asks only while nothing already asks the coordinator about the task. `D`, `A` or `x` on a row that doesn't offer it says why from the live board (`not available: T2 is started: a thread already works on it`). tm checks the live board at the press, asks and runs them (**Delegate**, **Accept and send back**, below); `enter` (or a click on its hint) shows the task over the popup (its notes and steps, what it is blocked on, how to check it), with the same `D` `A` `x` `c` keys, and `esc` comes back to the tab. The keys aren't `d` and `a`, which are prefix commands (back to the dashboard, the project popup). `+ new task` is bound in the whole tab, whatever row the cursor is on: when mods that run for the project have task templates (§5.3; the built-in `default-templates` has `bug`, `spike` and `chore`) a menu offers `no template` and each one (`bug · Bug: …`, `<mod>:<name>` when two mods have the name), then a prompt asks the title; the task is the human's, added as `tm task add "<title>" [--template <name>]` adds it (`added T12 to <slug>` in the footer). `enter` on a task row of the dashboard's NEEDS YOU (or `t` with a task, a notification's task) opens the popup here with the cursor on that task as soon as the tab lists it; a done one has the done tasks listed. The tab follows the board at once: the mod lists the tasks again on `tasks.changed` (a title edited, a step checked, an ask made or handled). With the mod off (the Mods tab, `[mods.tasks-tab] enabled = false`) the tab says so and how to turn it on: `The tasks-tab mod draws this tab, and it is off.` and `Turn it on in the Mods tab (, then 4), or with tm mod enable tasks-tab.` (off for this project only: `--project <slug>`); on but not drawn yet, that it waits for the mod; a failed host shows its failure and log.
  4. **settings**: the project's workflow settings (§11.2; what its threads may do is the Access tab's) as plain labels, each with a line on what it does, changed in place with `enter` or `space`: Start threads (ask first / automatically), Parallel threads (a number, with how many work now: `10 · 3 working now`, and with a cap across projects how many work in all of them: `· 7 of 20 across projects`), Auto-close finished threads (off / when its pull request merges / N days after it finishes), Complete tasks (by you / when merged), Pull request follow-up, Remote control (with the running coordinator's state when `prefix+r` changed it since, or when it is off and tm turns it on once the coordinator is idle), Merge method (merge commit / squash / rebase, cycling), Keep my checkout current; then the project's own Active, Paused, Archive and Delete rows (§5.1; the last two ask first, and Active asks before it stops running sessions, as the sidebar's `space` does). A setting the project doesn't set itself follows *All projects* (§11.2) and says so after its value, faint (`on · all projects`); changing it here makes it the project's own, and `x` on one the project sets itself drops it, so it follows All projects again (`demo follows all projects in parallel threads: 10`); for auto-close that is the mode and the days together. The footer offers `x follow all projects` while such a setting is selected, and `+ - number` only on a number. `enter` steps a number through common values (1, 2, 3, 5, 10, 15, 20) and `+` / `-` change it by one; on auto-close, `enter` cycles the three choices and `+` / `-` change the days. The selected setting scrolls into view whole, with its line and note. A change is saved at once and shows on every console's next poll. Last, **Linked projects** (§7.9): its links in a line (`beta ⇄ · web (half-linked)`, or `none`); `enter` lists every other project with this side's entry, the other side's and the link's state (`linked both ways`, `linked, send only`, `off`, and `half-linked: …` for entries a hand edit left unmatched): `enter` steps the link through off, both, send and receive, one act that writes both sides with no question (send here is receive there, off removes both; on a half-linked pair it makes it whole), and `a` turns this side's ask first on or off. Each change is journaled in both projects (`human settings.links demo web both (web both)`). Under them, a group per paired server (`mini · paired server, linked · its user sets its side of each link`; `connecting`, `offline`, `not paired`) lists the projects it shares, from the server's mirror (`peer.list`, §16.10), and any this side still has an entry for (`mini/acme-api`, §16.11): `enter` steps the same way but writes this side only, a new entry asking first (as `tm project link`), `a` turns its ask off or on, and the row says `mini decides its side`; its other side is what the messages tell (`link.TheirSide`): `linked back both ways` (a message came from there and one of ours was taken), `takes ours`, `sends here`, `refuses ours` (its last answer was `not-linked`), or `their side unknown` before any crossed. Such a change is journaled in this project only (`human settings.links demo mini/acme-api both ask`). `r` on such a row asks that server's user to link back (a link request, §16.11), after a `y`: with this side's direction, or with none sets it both ways first, asking first; `asked already (R-4F2A-18)` while one waits. The other-side column then says the request's state (`asked, not sent yet`, `asked, no answer yet`, `accepted: both ways`, `declined`, `request expired`, `request not delivered`) until a message tells something newer (`refuses ours` after an acceptance); a project with an incoming request is listed with `asks to link (Messages tab: A or x)`. Then **Shared with paired servers** (§16.9): `off` until the user turns it on, which asks first (`Share demo with every paired server? …`); `enter` on `on` turns it off at once.
  5. **access** (`settings_access.go`, T574 §4.1, §5.2): everything that decides what the project's threads may do, rows as on the Settings tab (`· all projects` while it follows them, `x` follows them again), under sections: **Prompts and escapes**: Escapes (`sandbox_escapes`: `ask you` (the guard puts each escape to the user, never the coordinator or auto mode, §8.6), or `never`; `enter` flips it, for threads started or restarted from then on), Yolo mode (turning it on asks first), Coordinator approves; the sandbox's rules (below); **Guard**: Guard, Guard mode, Guard rules, Guard allowances, Guard refusals (§8.6); **Secrets**: Secret environment (§8.6); **Built in**: tm's rules, read-only, whose `enter` opens the effective access view with tm's own rules only (`Built in (tm) · demo`). `p` adds a preset's rules (below); `e` opens the effective access (§5.2, **One resolver**); the footer says `enter change · [x follow all projects ·] p add a preset · e effective access · ← → tabs · esc close`. The sandbox's rows show for the platform the server is on (the server's `session.list` says its `os`; a server that doesn't say is taken to be this console's). At the top, faint: `Prefer rules inside the sandbox (p adds a preset's): the thread's commands stay in it, opened only as far as the tool needs. Excluded commands (Outside the sandbox) run outside it: a last resort.` and the plain sentence that an excluded command an allow rule also covers runs outside the sandbox without asking, so that if it can run code the thread may run any code as you without asking; then `Shown: what applies on macOS, where this server's threads run.` (with `Hosts, local ports, guidance and the presets don't apply there` on Linux and Windows). Under **Inside the sandbox**, where they apply (`config.SandboxSettings`, macOS): **Network hosts** (a list), **Local ports** (on or off: `enter` opens them after asking first with what local binding opens: a thread can connect to every service on a local port, databases, Docker over TCP, Chrome's debug port, IDE extensions, and can take a port another program expects; Seatbelt doesn't limit listening to localhost; a service that runs commands is a way out of the sandbox; or closes them, for the project making the value its own, which is how a project drops all projects' on; `x` follows all projects again, asking first when that opens them; all projects' off is no setting at all), and **Guidance for threads** (a list of the texts tm ships, `config.Guides`). Under Inside the sandbox, also **Write folders** and (macOS) **Sockets** (lists); under **Outside the sandbox**, **Excluded commands** and **Approved commands**, and under **Permission rules**, **Allow rules** (§11.2 `sandbox`). Each excluded command says `asks first` or `runs without asking`; `w` switches the table's own one, asking first before letting it run without asking. A list row's value counts what threads get, with the project's own, the dropped and the refused (`2 (1 demo · 1 of all projects dropped)`); `enter` lists one list as the threads get it (T574 §4.2): all projects' entries first (`all projects`), the project's own (its slug), the dropped ones marked `─` (`all projects · dropped`) and a drop that matches none of all projects' rules (`dropped · matches none of all projects'`); `a` adds one to the table (checked as the file is; an entry all projects give already, or one the project drops, is refused saying so; asking first for every excluded command, every host, and any entry with a warning); `x` is the one key: on a project's own entry it removes it (`x remove`), on an all-projects entry it drops it for the project (`x drop for <project>`), on a dropped entry, a stray drop too, it takes the drop back (`x take it back`); the footer says which for the selected entry, and on the all-projects tab `x` removes. An entry has no line of warnings under it: the list's header says what it opens. An entry that fails its check is marked `✗` with the reason: threads start without it, and only it. Adding one that an allow rule covers, a host that serves many owners, or an entry with a warning says so in the asking-first confirmation. **Presets** (`settings_presets.go`, T574 §3.3, §4.3): `p` lists `config.Presets` with what each is for and its platforms; `enter` on one opens `Add <preset> for demo`: the rules the threads don't have yet, each `[x] host registry.npmjs.org`, then `Already there: …`; `space` leaves one out, `A` adds to all projects instead (and back), `enter add N rules` adds them in one write as plain rules, and nothing ties them to the preset afterwards; no remark on what a rule risks. What doesn't apply on the server's platform isn't shown: `config.toml` keeps it, and a launch skips it with a line in the server log. The `,` popup's Access tab is the same for all projects, each row naming the projects that set or drop their own (`demo sets its own`), its Built in row only saying a project's tab lists them. Human-only (`EditSandbox`), journaled `settings.sandbox` (`hosts + registry.npmjs.org`, `drop + docker compose *`, `preset browser-downloads: host …`).
  6. **keys**: the help's list.
  7. **memory**: what every thread is told besides its task; the coordinator keeps these files, and the human changes the notes here. Drawn by the built-in `memory-tab` mod (§5.3, **Built-in mods**; Mod views): a list of the memory files (CONTEXT.md, MEMORY.md, then the notes by title, each with its path and size and a note's `touches`, a link in `memory/` left out), then a `t` key for the note template (§7.6, **Memory notes**); a note's row, and the viewer of one, bind the core actions `memory.edit` (`e`: the console hands the terminal to `tm memory edit`, which opens the note in the human's editor, and the tab comes back when it exits) and `memory.rm` (`d`: tm asks `Delete memory/x.md of demo? …`, then deletes it as `tm memory rm` does), and the list (and the template) binds `memory.new` (`n`: `tm memory new` on the terminal asks the note's name, then opens the template in the editor); the mod hears `action.done` and reads the files again; `enter` or a click reads one, a note's front matter as one faint line (touches, standing, source) instead of its TOML, its headings in the head role, a link as its text, a glyph wider than one cell as `?`; there `n` and `p` go to the next and previous file and `enter` back to the list; the core keeps the cursor, filter and scroll, and a failed mod shows `mod failed` and its log. It reads the files again each time the tab is shown. The tab has no other drawing: with the mod off (the Mods tab, `[mods.memory-tab] enabled = false`) it says so and how to turn it on, as the Tasks tab does (`The memory-tab mod draws this tab, and it is off.`, `Turn it on in the Mods tab (, then 4), or with tm mod enable memory-tab.`).
  8. **library**: every file any of the project's threads attached to a report (`tm report --attach`, §7.2), the resolved and archived threads' too (an archived thread's files are read from its tarball, §7.6), newest first, grouped under a line per thread: `T142 <title> · t-0122 · 2026-10-08` (and `· archived`), then one row per file with its name and size, the newest group first and each group's files newest first. A file shows by name and size, never a path (the rule for the UI). `enter` (or a double-click) reads the selected file in a read-only, scrollable view over the popup (`↑` `↓` `pgup` `pgdown` and the wheel scroll, `esc` goes back): text and markdown as they are, JSON indented, anything else (a binary, an image) by name and size only; it reads at most 1 MB and says so. `d` asks `Delete <name> (<size>) of thread t-0122?` and `D` `Delete all N library files of thread t-0122?`; `y` deletes (from the thread's `library/` folder or, for an archived thread, by rewriting its tarball without the file) and journals each file as `library.rm <thread> <name>`; `n` or `esc` keeps them. The tab reloads with the tasks. `tm library list` is the same list (§10).
  9. **messages** (`internal/tui/links.go`, §7.9): one row per link of the project (`beta  ⇄ linked both ways · 1 unread · 1 waits for you · last M-3 2m`), the tab's label counting its held messages and paused links (`9 messages 1`); `enter` (or a double-click) opens the link's conversation over the popup: every message both ways, oldest first, each under a faint line `→ M-3 · Oct 14 15:12 · demo · re M-1 · sent` (`←` for the other side's, `(you)` after a message the human wrote, `read` once the other coordinator handled it, `waits for your OK` in the warning colour for a held one) with its text below, this project's indented further. `A` sends the selected held message (`link.approve`, the human's OK), `x` drops it after a `y`, `R` resumes a link the breaker paused, `m` writes a message as the human for this project (a prompt; sent at once, never held); each runs as the human, journaled. A paired server's link request (§16.11) adds its row (`mini/acme  · not linked, asks to link (R-4F2A-18)`) and counts in the label; on that row `A` opens a menu of this side's entry, the request's mirror first, each with and without ask first (`receive only, ask first`, `receive only`, `both ways, ask first`, …), and accepts with the one picked; `x` declines after a `y`. The text shows to the human only; coordinators read it framed (`tm link read`).
- **Mod views** (`internal/tui/modviews.go`; mods design §6). A code mod with the `views` grant draws the panels its manifest declares (`[provides].views`): a `project.tab` is a tab of the project popup after the core tabs (`0 Review queue 2`: its number, title and the count the mod sets, the tenth numbered `0`; `←` `→` and `0` reach it, and a tab past the tenth has no number; a tab bar too wide for the popup drops the space around each label), for the projects the mod runs for; a `details.section` is a section of the dashboard's details panel, for the row it shows (a project's own row, a task or a thread, of a project the mod runs for: its tree for that project, task or thread, a project's row also the one for all projects), and of a thread's info panel beside its pane, from the same tree as the thread's row; a `coordinator.section` one of the coordinator's info panel beside its pane, for its project; a `sidebar.section` and a `sidebar.project.section` are the sidebar's (**Sidebar**, above). Sections are headed, folded, placed and fitted as **Mod sections** (below) says; a section the mod hasn't drawn yet, or drew `null` for, adds nothing, not even its header. A section's body only shows: it takes no keys and draws no cursor row, and only its header takes keys and clicks, so a mod's interactive panels are its tabs, cards and full views. The attached console polls `mod.views` with its `session.list`, in one call for the info panel's sections and the sidebar's (sidebar sections only while `session.list`'s `mods.sections` names some and the full sidebar has the room): at once when what it shows changes, then every second while some show, and once more when none does; a `dashboard.card` is a card below the dashboard's sections, its header the view's title and the mod's name, its lines what the mod asks for (`height`, default 8) up to 20 and half the list; `z` folds it to its header and back (each console's own), and `enter` opens it as a popup. Each is a widget tree the mod publishes, drawn by tm (`widget.View` in the console's theme and icons): the cursor, the filter (`/`, `enter` keeps it, `esc` drops it), `tab` between lists, the scroll and the wheel are the console's, with no round trip to the mod; only the keys the mod declares in a keys widget, and a click on a row or a key hint, go to it (`mod.view.input`, as `ui.key` / `ui.click` with the list and the row under its cursor), and the cursor's moves after the fact, as `ui.cursor` (§3.7 **Views**): the console tells the mod where the cursor is when the view shows and each time it lands on another row or list, one send per view on its way at a time, and tells it again when the view shows anew after it was hidden. A mod's panels carry no mark of their own: a mod draws in tm's look, as tm's own panels do. Installing and enabling it is the human's explicit choice, after the consent screen names every panel it draws, and what protects the user is what a panel can do, not how it looks: a view only reads, and changing anything goes through core actions with the core's own check and confirmation, so a mod can't act by itself; the Mods tab lists every mod with what it provides, and `tm --no-mods` starts without the user's mods. While the mod hasn't drawn it, a tab, a card or a full view says it waits (`Waiting for <mod> to draw <title>…`); a section, in the sidebar or the right pane, instead shows nothing until its mod's first tree (**Mod sections**, below); when the mod's host failed or waits to restart it says `▲ mod failed: <error>`, names its log and that `r` in the Mods tab restarts it, and shows no stale tree; the rest of the popup and the dashboard are untouched. The console polls the views with `mod.views` every second, and at once when what it shows changes, saying which it shows (the open mod tab, the unfolded cards and the details panel's sections, by its row's project, task or thread, while the list shows, an open card, the sidebar's band, and its per-project sections, one for each project whose rows are on screen, folded ones included); when the dashboard ends it says none, but for the sidebar's sections when it ends for a session, whose sidebar shows them on (the dashboard and the attached console run in one process, one console to the server, and each starts from the other's last list), so a mod hears no `view.closed` and `view.opened` on every switch. In a session, enter on a band section's header that opens a view shows the view the mod opens over the session, as a command's. A mod's request to open a view (`$.ui.view.open`) is acted on once, by the console whose key or click the mod's handler answers: its tab in that project's popup, its card unfolded and selected (the dashboard then lists the section's project, as the click's), or its full view. A `full` view takes the dashboard's body (over a popup it was opened from, too) and is opened only by the human: from the ≡ menu (`<title> · <id>`, one item per full view) or by the mod's `$.ui.view.open` in its handler for a key or a click in another of its views; nothing opens it by itself. It keeps the header (`tm dashboard · <title>`, the server's state), the sidebar with its needs-you marks and the footer with its keys and messages, and its first line is NEEDS YOU's count, as the dashboard heads that section (`NEEDS YOU 0`, faint, when nothing waits), so it never hides what waits for the user; under it the tree, drawn and handled as in a card's popup. `esc` returns to what was under it. It shows the tree for the project the dashboard lists (or the one the mod's open request names).
- **Mod sections** (`internal/tui/sidesections.go`, `panelsections.go`; mod API 1.7). The four section slots share one look: a header (`▾ TICKER ──── 1 ☒`: `▾` unfolded or `▸` folded, the view's title, a faint rule, the mod's `badge` at its right end) over the mod's lines; core's own headings have no fold mark and never fold. A section, in the sidebar or the right pane, shows from its mod's first tree for its row: until then it takes no row, not even its header, as with a `null` tree, so nothing waits on screen. **Folds** are the view's: every console of it follows, `ui.json` keeps them with the tree for new views (§5.1), and sections start unfolded. A sidebar section's id is `mod.view` (`mod.view@slug` under a project), a right-pane section's `panel:mod.view`, one id for every project and row, so folding the ticker folds it beside every coordinator (`view.sections`, §3.3). A folded section is still on show, so its badge stays live. **Companion view**: on a section whose view names one (`opens`), `enter` or a click sends its mod a `ui.click` for the section's project, and the mod's handler may open that view (§3.7 **Views**); without one they fold or unfold it. **The right pane**: a `coordinator.section` goes in the coordinator's info panel, a `details.section` in the dashboard's details panel and in a thread's info panel. Each panel has a head nothing goes above: the coordinator's title, counts, context and its red `▲ PR host failing · inbox` line; a details panel's title and state line. Under it come core's blocks, which keep their order and never fold: the coordinator's `needs` (NEEDS YOU), `inbox`, `threads` and `deck` (ON DECK); a details or thread panel's `core`, the rest of it as one block. The layout's `[coordinator] sections` and `[details] sections` (§5.3) put each section after the block listed before it, or just above the first block listed after it; without them, or for a section a list doesn't name, sections go at the bottom in mod order (built-in mods first), where the panels' sections always were. A panel under 12 rows draws them all at the bottom. Sections take at most half the panel's rows on screen, their headers alone a quarter and those above or between core's blocks a quarter; where they don't fit they give way, those above core's blocks first (the lowest of them first), then those between them, bodies to their headers and then headers, the selected one last (`+N` on the last shown, `…` on one cut for room; the bottom ones give way only for the shares), so that every line core marks as must-see and that would be on the first screen without them is on it with them, in order: the head, the NEEDS YOU heading and its items with the questions, and on a thread's panel its `needs you` line, its QUESTION OPEN block and a red PR line. The panels scroll as before: the budget bounds what sections take of one screen. **The panels' keys**, while the info panel (attach) or the details panel (the dashboard) has the keyboard, from one table (`panelActions`) the help and a keymap see:

  | Id | Key | What it does |
  |---|---|---|
  | `panel.up` `panel.down` | `↑` `↓` (`k` `j`) | scroll the panel |
  | `panel.page-up` `panel.page-down` | `pgup` `pgdown` | a page up / down |
  | `panel.first` `panel.last` | `home` `end` | the top / the end of the panel |
  | `panel.section-next` `panel.section-prev` | `]` `[` | select the next / previous mod section's header, round the ends, scroll it into view and draw it in the selection colour; with no section shown, on the dashboard, the next / previous project's coordinator as on the list |
  | `panel.unfold` `panel.fold` | `→` `←` | on a selected header: unfold / fold the section |
  | `panel.enter` | `enter` | on a selected header: open its companion view, else fold or unfold it; with none selected, as before (a thread's task; on the dashboard, the list's `enter`) |
  | `panel.save-clear` | `c` | beside a coordinator: save and clear it (`tm clear`, §7.5 **Auto-clear**); the details panel leaves it to the list |
  | `panel.back` | `esc` (fixed) | drop the selected header, then give the keyboard back |

  So while the details panel has the keyboard and shows sections, the plain `]` `[` select its sections rather than switch projects; the prefix's `]` `[` still switch, and so do the plain keys from the list. A header scrolled off drops the selection. While the panel has the keyboard and shows sections, the status bar (attach) or the footer names the keys (`] [ sections`, and on a selected header `· ← fold · enter open`). With the mouse, a click on a header's fold mark folds or unfolds it, and a click elsewhere on a header or on a section's line gives the panel the keyboard, selects the header and does what `enter` does. `z` `Z` stay the sidebar's and never fold the right pane. **Safe mode**: user mods' sections don't show at all, in the sidebar or the right pane; built-in mods' do. **A failed mod**'s section is its header (folded, its badge a red `▲ failed`) and the red `▲ mod failed: …` line with its log and the restart hint, never its stale tree.
- **Delegate** (`D` on a task in the project popup's Tasks tab or a shown task, on the dashboard or over a session with the prefix): on an `open`, `ready` or `blocked` task it asks `Delegate T12 to the coordinator?`; `y` adds a `delegate` inbox item for the project's coordinator (subject `T12`, `the user asks to delegate T12`) and a journal line (`human task.delegate.ask T12`), and the footer says `asked the coordinator to delegate T12`. Until the coordinator marks the item done, the task's row and its shown view say `waiting on the coordinator` (`waiting on coordinator` in the task view's narrower rows; right after the status, and the title gives way so it is never cut), and `D` again only says so; so do `A` and `x` (one ask per task at a time). On a `started`, `review` or `done` task, `D` does nothing but say why in the footer. Confirming is the user's go-ahead (§9, `start_threads = "propose"`): the coordinator runs `tm task delegate T12 --approved-by-user`, proposing instead only at the parallel threads cap or when the task needs something from the user first. The task itself changes only when the coordinator delegates it. Refused when `tm` runs inside an agent.
- **Accept and send back** (`A` and `x` on a task in the project popup's Tasks tab, on a row or a shown task): on a `review` task, `A` asks `Accept T12? The coordinator marks T12 <title> done.`; `y` adds an `accept` inbox item (`the user accepts T12`, journal `human task.accept.ask T12`) and the footer says `told the coordinator you accept T12; it marks it done`. It is the user's acceptance, the only way besides telling the coordinator in chat: the coordinator runs `tm task status T12 done --approved-by-user`. `x` asks `Send T12 back. What should change?` for a one-line note (at most 200 characters; `esc` or an empty note cancels, `T12 not sent back`); `enter` adds a `send-back` item carrying it (`the user sends T12 back: <note>`, journal `human task.sendback.ask T12 <note>`). The coordinator forwards the note to the task's thread and moves the task back to `started`; when that thread is resolved it proposes a new thread with the note instead. `x` works on a `done` task too: the coordinator reopens it the same way, which is how the user takes back a completion made by the project's complete-tasks setting (§11.2). Rows wait on the coordinator as with `D`. Otherwise both keys only say why in the footer (`A` needs `review`, `x` `review` or `done`). Refused when `tm` runs inside an agent.
- **Adopt** (`T` on a session row outside the projects, in OTHER SESSIONS or NEEDS YOU, or `adopt as a thread` in the `≡` menu and the row's right-click menu): makes an agent the user started by hand a thread of the current project (§9, **Adopt**). The session must run an agent (one started with `tm session start --agent`, or one found running in a shell's foreground, §8.1); on a plain shell, a project's session or another row, `T` only says why in the footer. It asks `Adopt s-12 (claude in ~/src/app on fix-login) as a thread of terminatr? The coordinator links it to a task and briefs it.`; `y` records the user's go-ahead in the server (`session.adopt.ask`, on the human socket: §9, **Adopt**) and adds an `adopt` inbox item for that project's coordinator (subject `s-12`, `the user asks to adopt session s-12 (claude in /Users/me/src/app on fix-login) as a thread`) and a journal line (`human thread.adopt.ask s-12`), and the footer says `asked the coordinator to adopt s-12`. A second `T` while the item is open offers to take the go-ahead back (`Withdraw your go-ahead to adopt s-12 as a thread of terminatr? The coordinator can't adopt it then.`; `y` drops it in the server, `session.adopt.ask {withdraw}`, marks the item handled and journals `human thread.adopt.withdraw s-12`). To adopt into another project, switch to it first (`p`). Confirming is the user's go-ahead, and the only one an agent's adopt takes: the coordinator runs `tm thread adopt s-12`, with `--task` when the user named one or one plainly fits, and asks the user otherwise. Refused when `tm` runs inside an agent.
- **A task, shown** (`enter` in the Tasks tab): its title, status, thread and steps, then what the user needs to act on it, then its notes and steps. A `blocked` task says `Blocked on: <its latest blocked note>` (the note `tm task status T12 blocked --note` wrote), and `c` opens the project's coordinator, attaching to it (started if none runs; over a session, the view shows it), so the user can answer. A `review` task says whether its change shipped: from its thread's pull request (the ticker's, else the report's `PR:` line), `PR #61 open, not merged yet`, `PR #61 closed without merging`, or `PR #61 merged` (the ticker saw it merge, or the host's merge subject is on the default branch as last fetched: GitHub's `Merge pull request #61 from …`, Azure DevOps' `Merged PR 61: …`). Then `How to check`: the thread report's `## Check` lines (§7.2) and the task's notes that start `Check:` (after a status note's `review (date): `); without either it says the report doesn't say. Nothing here fetches.
- **Settings** (`,`): the settings of every project, changed in place the same way: the prefix key (`enter`, then press the new `ctrl+<key>`; inside tmux, which takes Ctrl+B, pick another), the details panel's place (right, bottom or off; `(no room: under the row)` when the window has none for it) and the list's size beside or above it (this console's, `ui.json`), the sidebar's mode: full, the slim strip or hidden (the view's), the theme (*follow terminal*, the built-in one on the terminal's own colours, Claude panes included, or an enabled theme mod's, §5.3; `[ui] theme`), the icons (below, or an enabled icons mod's set), the layout (the sidebar's column order: the built-in one or an enabled layout mod's, §5.3; `[ui] layout`, applied to the view so every console of it follows), the context hint (the percent of a coordinator's context window from which a save and clear of it is offered, §7.5 **Auto-clear**, `[ui] context_hint`, off or 30–80 in steps), Needs you first (the sidebar lists the projects where something needs you first, within the active and within the inactive ones, the pinned projects still on top; `[ui] needs_first`, off when unset), Models (below), and Agent mod, Agent mod band and Agent mod pane (§8.6 **Mods**: Agent mod loads terminatr's mod in Claude panes, early access, needing Claude Code 2.1.289 or newer, applying to sessions launched after the change, off until turned on; Agent mod band is the band, status entry and toast in the pane while the feed still runs, on unless turned off, and says `needs Agent mod on` after its value while Agent mod is off; Agent mod pane is whether a coordinator opens its /tm pane by itself, off unless turned on (tm's info panel beside the coordinator shows the same), also saying `needs Agent mod on`). **Models** (tm ships no model list: the installed agents say which models the user can use, §8.2 **Models**) shows each installed agent's count of models, or that they are unknown (`claude 12, codex unknown`; `no agent installed`); `enter` opens the Models page, one block per installed agent: its line (`claude  12 models · asked 2.1.296 today (Claude Max, firstParty) · default the agent's own`, or `unknown: logged out of codex`) and its models as the agent described them (its display name and description, a tag such as `no auto mode` from what it reports, `older` for the versions behind its aliases), then the ones the user hid (`-`) and the ones the user's account refused (`!`, with the agent's message). `*` (or space) makes a model the default a thread runs when the coordinator picks none, and again makes the agent run its own default; `h` hides a listed model from the coordinator, or shows it again; `a` adds a model the agent takes but doesn't list (a Bedrock inference profile, a custom provider's), one word, only while the agent's models are known; `enter` renames one the user added and `d` removes it (on a listed model it says `h` hides it); `u` forgets a refusal; `r` goes back to what the agent lists (all the user's keys removed); `R` asks the agent again. Each change is saved at once (`[agents.<name>]` `hide`, `add`, `default_model`, §5.1, §8.2); a save replaces the older `models` list, keeping its names as added ones. A name the agent already offers, or not one word, is refused under the list. The popup has six tabs, `1 general` (those), `2 all projects`, `3 access` (all projects' prompts and escapes, sandbox rules and presets, guard and secret environment, as the project popup's Access tab), `4 mods`, `5 peers` and `6 logs` (below), switched as the project popup's are (`←` `→`, `1`–`6`, a click on the tab bar). All projects holds the project workflow settings (§11.2) that every project follows unless it sets its own, as the project popup's Settings tab shows them, saying `for all projects` in the footer messages; under each, the projects that set their own value say so (`demo and web set their own`). Turning yolo mode on (its Access tab) asks first, naming every project it reaches. A project's own settings are in its popup.
- **Mods** (the `,` popup's fourth tab, `internal/tui/modstab.go`; mods design §9): every installed user mod (§5.3) on a line, then the built-in ones: its mark (`●` on, `○` off, `▲` failed or unusable, `◆` on but wanting more than it is granted), name, what it provides (`theme · icons`, `code · column`, `built in` last for a mod that ships inside tm), version, and what it runs with in the consent's words (`safe`, `reads your projects · talks to api.linear.app`, or for a data mod that changes what agents run or read `no permissions · replaces how tm starts claude · adds task templates`), `off`, `wants more: runs glab`, or why it can't run (`incompatible: … run tm update`), `· only <projects>` when limited; An enabled code mod is marked by its host's state (`mod.hosts`): `●` running, with its pid and memory (RSS) under the list; `◌` starting; `●` idle, `· waits for its first event`; `▲` in yellow `restarting in 5s: <its last error>` in backoff; `▲` `failed: <its last error>` when failed; a data mod is marked as above, and an enabled one whose data file doesn't load (`mod.CheckData`: a theme with a bad colour, an icon set with a two-cell glyph, a keymap naming an unknown action, a layout with an unknown column, a template that doesn't parse, a rules file, template or agent manifest holding a character that doesn't show, such as a zero-width or tag character) `▲` `doesn't load its <kind>: …`, each problem under the list. The selected mod's description and authors are under the list, for a built-in one that tm's defaults are drawn from it and its folder can be copied to start one's own (and, for one tm can't do without, what replaces it), and for a failed one that its cells and panels stay blank, that `r` restarts it and `l` shows its log. With no mod of the user's installed it says, under the built-in ones, that `i` installs one and `e` lists the examples. In safe mode its first line says no user mod runs. `enter` turns an on mod off; on an off one, or one that wants more, it opens the mod's consent, drawn by tm: what it is and provides (the panels it draws, a code host, its commands), what it changes in what agents run or read without a permission (a role's policy it replaces, rules it adds, each agent manifest, named as replacing how tm starts a built-in agent such as claude in every project, task templates; a mod with any of these is not called safe), each permission it asks for in plain words in its colour (reading tm's own data or telling the coordinator one colour, files and network another, running programs red; new ones marked `new`; for agent tools, that Claude gets them only while the Agent mod is on), the defaults it suggests, every rules addendum, agent manifest, task template, agent tool and guard rule in full, then `Enable <name>?` (or `Grant <name> what it wants more?`, where `o` turns it off instead); `y` turns it on with every permission it asks for, as `tm mod enable`. `p` lists those permissions, granted or not: `enter` takes one back at once or grants one after `y` (a mod runs with what is granted of what it asks for). `d` shows the defaults a mod suggests for all projects (§5.3 **Suggested defaults**), each beside what they follow now, and `y` there applies them to an on mod, as `tm mod defaults --apply`; the consent lists them too. `s` is the mod's own settings from its manifest (`[mods.<id>.settings]`): a yes/no turns over, a choice steps, a number changes with `+` `-` within its range, `enter` on a text setting opens a line with its value (pasting works; typed control characters become spaces), `enter` saves it and `esc` leaves it, `x` puts one back to its default. A text value is one line of at most 500 characters (`mod.MaxTextSetting`); the write refuses a longer one or one with a control character. `l` shows the end of its log (`~/.terminatr/logs/mods/<id>.log`, the last 64 KB). `r` restarts an enabled code mod's host now (`mod.restart`; a failed one gets a fresh count); a data mod or one that is off has none. `u` updates it from its recorded source as `tm mod update` does, asking first, with what changed, when the update asks for more or changes text an agent obeys. `x` removes it after `y` (its data kept 30 days). A built-in mod is never removed or updated on its own (it updates with tm); `default-templates`, `memory-tab`, `pr-ci-column`, `tasks-tab` and `ticker` can be turned off, and the others can't (`enter` says what replaces it: a pick in General settings, or an enabled keymap or policy mod). `i` installs a mod as `tm mod install` does (`internal/tui/modinstall.go`): it asks for a folder (`~/` is the home folder) or a git URL, the subfolder forms included (`<git-url>//<subfolder>[@<ref>]`, a GitHub `/tree/<ref>/<folder>` link; pasting works), then fetches and checks it in the background with the same checks, in a dialog that says what and for how long (`Fetching and checking it… 4s`; `esc` drops it, and what comes is thrown away), and refuses what `tm mod install` refuses, with its reason, before any consent: a bad URL (plain `http://` and `git://` too), a subfolder that leads out of the repository, more than 2000 files or 64 MB, a mod API this tm doesn't have, an id already installed (`tm mod update <id>`) or a built-in mod's (`enter` there takes the line back to change it). Then it draws the consent above, for the fetched copy, with `From <source>. It installs off` and `Install <name>?`; `y` installs it disabled, with nothing granted, as `tm mod install` does, selects it and asks `Turn it on now?`, and `y` there turns it on as `enter` would (every permission it asks for, which the consent just showed for this copy). Installing and turning on stay two answers, as `tm mod install` then `tm mod enable` are, so an install alone never runs a mod's code. `e` lists terminatr's example mods (§13 of the mods design): the link to the repository's `examples/mods` folder and, for each, the surface it shows, what it asks for and its one-line install of its folder at this tm's release tag (`tm mod install https://github.com/theclifmeister/terminatr/tree/v0.38.0/examples/mods/nord`, `main` for a build that isn't a release; **Subfolder sources**, §5.3; for `pr-ci-column`, `memory-tab` and `tasks-tab`, whose ids are built-in mods' and so refused, a `git clone` and a `cp -R` under another id first); `↑` `↓` select one and `i` (or `enter`) installs it from that line's URL, as `i` in the tab does (a built-in one says it is built in already), and `tm mod examples [--json]` prints the list (an agent may run it). Every change is the human's and saved at once to `config.toml`; tm refuses it inside an agent. When a mod's host fails (the supervisor's state), the footer says so once, `mod Linear sync failed · , then 4 for details`; the coordinator is never told. A project's popup has a **Mods** row on its Settings tab once some mod is limited to some projects: the limited mods that run for it (or `none limited to it`); `enter` opens this tab.
- **Peers** (the `,` popup's fifth tab, `internal/tui/peerstab.go`, §16.8), laid out as the other settings tabs are, in two sections the cursor moves over as one list (`↑` `↓`, `k`; `j` pairs here, so only `↓` moves down). **SHARED PROJECTS** comes first and shows at once, with nothing paired too: every active project on this server (an archived one isn't listed) with a box, `[x] acme-api` or `[ ] blog`, for its **Shared with paired servers** setting (§16.9), the same setting as the project's own Settings tab shows, one value in the settings file. `space` (or `enter`) on it, or a click on the selected one, toggles it: sharing asks first (`Share acme-api with every paired server? …`; `n` says `not shared`), unsharing goes at once. With no active project it says `No active project on this server to share.` **PAIRED SERVERS** follows, with this server's id in its head (`PAIRED SERVERS · this server 4F2A-91C3`), then per server as `tm peer list` has them its link line: its glyph in the link's colour, its alias, the link (`◉ mini  linked · 38 ms`, `◌ connecting`, `○ offline 4m · relay`; from the mirror while the dashboard polls it, so it follows the link), its `tm` version once known, how many projects it shares with this server, or the link's last error while it is down; a long line is cut with `…`. Under it two setting rows the cursor stops on. **panes** (§16.12) shows `off · view · write` side by side with the current one in brackets (`panes  [off] · view · write`), and under it what that lets the server do (`it can't open this server's panes`, `it can watch the coordinators and threads of the projects you share, not type`, `it can watch and type into …`). `←` `→` on it, or a click on a word, change it, as `tm peer panes` does: a level that lets the server do more asks first (`Let mini type into the coordinator and thread panes of the projects this server shares? …`; `n` says `panes unchanged for mini`), a lower one applies at once, and both reach panes already open (§16.12); then `peer.reload`, and the tab reads the list again. Users see `off`, `view` and `write`; the wire and `peers.json` keep `type` for write. **relay** shows the relay its link goes through (its host for `wss://`; a pinned relay as its host and `(pinned)`) and, once a link went through it, the relay's tm (`relay.example.net · v0.27.0`), with `· update available (u)` when the latest release this server knows of (its update check's, else its own version) is newer. `u` on either of a server's rows asks, then has its relay update itself, as `tm peer relay-update` does (`internal/tui/peerrelay.go`, §16.2), the footer saying what came of it. `e` on either of a server's rows, or `enter` or a click on its selected relay row, asks for its relay, starting from its own, and sets it as `tm peer relay` does (`relay for mini set; set it on mini too`, and the private-address warning when it is one). Elsewhere in the tab `←` `→` switch tabs, as on the others. `x` on a server's row removes it after asking (`Remove mini? Its link closes at once and its projects leave the sidebar; …`; `n` or `esc` says `mini kept`), as `tm peer remove` does: unpinned, then `peer.reload`, and the tab reads the list again. `n` pairs with another server and `j` joins one's pairing, as `tm peer pair` and `tm peer join` do (`internal/tui/peerpair.go`, the same exchange, §16.8): `n` asks for the relay (the one of the server paired last to start with), then shows the code once it works (`Pairing code (works once, for 10 minutes; 9:41 left):`, the code, how to pass it on) and waits; `j` asks for the code. Both then show the other server's name, id and tm, and `Check: 418 207`; `y they match` pins it, `n they don't` doesn't, and `esc` cancels at any stage (`not paired`). A pinned server's panes row is selected and the footer says `paired with mini; its link comes up in a moment`; the alias is the other server's host name, as `tm peer pair` without `--as`. A failure stays in the popup in words (the relay can't be reached, nobody is pairing with the code, the digits weren't confirmed, the other side said no) with `enter try again`, which asks again from what was typed. The code shows in the popup only, never in the footer, an error or a log. With no server paired the section says to press `n` or `j` (or use `tm peer` in a terminal), and ends with where a relay comes from: `No relay yet? On a Linux server with a domain: sudo tm relay install --domain NAME; without one, on an address both servers reach: sudo tm relay install --ip (see the README).` The pairing popup notes a relay on a private address, and that a pinned relay's code needs this release or later on the other server. The action row: `space toggle · ← → change · n pair · j join · e relay · u update relay · x remove · esc close`. Listing, pairing, sharing, removing, the switch, the relay and its update are the human's (`human-only` when tm runs inside an agent).
- **Logs** (the `,` popup's sixth tab, `internal/tui/logstab.go`): the logs on the server's host, read through the server (`log.list`, `log.read`, §11.1), so a console of another host's server shows that server's.
