Until that mark exists a project whose table has no list keeps `PROJECT.md`'s (absolute paths only), as before; once it does `PROJECT.md`'s `repos` is never read again, so one a coordinator writes there later approves nothing. A project whose list can't move (a `PROJECT.md` or `config.toml` that doesn't parse, a table in a form the line editor can't change) is logged and still gets the mark, so it fails closed: it has no repos until the user adds them, rather than `PROJECT.md`'s, which its coordinator could write meanwhile. Only a mark that can't be written leaves the move to the next start. What the projects' lists held at the move is trusted as the older `tm` trusted it.
- **Rename** (`tm project rename <slug> <new-slug>`) changes a project's slug, so its only name. Refused, before anything moves: by an agent (`human-only`); an unknown project or an invalid new slug; a new slug taken by a project folder, a `worktrees/<new-slug>/` folder or a `[projects.<new-slug>]` table (`project-exists`); settings written as dotted keys or an inline table, which the line editor can't rename (`settings-form`); and, by the server, while a thread of the project runs (`sessions-running`, naming them; `tm thread stop` them, and `tm thread restart` brings each back afterwards). The same slug is refused (`unchanged`). When a server runs, the CLI asks it (`project.rename`): it stops the project's coordinator, and between two ticker sweeps renames `projects/<slug>/` and then `worktrees/<slug>/` (the second failing undoes the first, and the coordinator comes back as it was), and files the ticker's memos (`state/ticker.json`: nudged items, completed tasks, threads' PR states) and the views' current project and selected rows under the new slug; then it starts the coordinator again, fresh, under the new slug. Without a server the CLI does the same itself. Once the folders have moved the rename stands, and what didn't go through after that is printed as notes: `[projects.<slug>]` and any table under it renamed in `config.toml`, header lines only, so comments and order stay (the repo list goes with it), and the project's key line in other projects' `links` tables (§7.9); other projects' `messages/<slug>/` folders renamed; each moved worktree reconnected with its repo (`git worktree repair`, run in it); the `worktree` path of each thread record under the old folder rewritten (an adopted thread's folder elsewhere stays); agents' state kept by folder carried over for the project folder and each moved worktree (`agent.Mover`: for Claude, its conversations under `~/.claude/projects/<folder>` for both the given and the real path, so `tm thread restart` resumes, and `~/.claude.json`'s settings for the folder, copied, so its trust holds); `AGENTS.md` regenerated; a `project.rename` journal line (`human project.rename terminatr termilator → terminatr`). The journal, inbox, tasks, memory and reports move with the folder unchanged. Branches keep their names: existing threads stay on `tm/<old-slug>/…`, and only new threads get `tm/<new-slug>/…`; `tm doctor` looks a branch's thread up by the records' `branch` too, so a renamed project's branches aren't taken for leftovers.
- **Front matter** is TOML between `+++` lines, as in herdr-projects.
- **Code host.** Each repo's pull requests are asked of its code host (`internal/codehost`), picked per repo from `git remote get-url origin`: `dev.azure.com`, `*.visualstudio.com`, `ssh.dev.azure.com` and `vs-ssh.visualstudio.com` remotes (`https://[user@]dev.azure.com/{org}/[{project}/]_git/{repo}`, `https://{org}.visualstudio.com/[DefaultCollection/][{project}/]_git/{repo}`, `git@ssh.dev.azure.com:v3/{org}/{project}/{repo}`, `{org}@vs-ssh.visualstudio.com:v3/{org}/{project}/{repo}`; a project-less URL's project is the repo, names are percent-decoded) are Azure DevOps; `github.com` and anything else is GitHub. `PROJECT.md` may override it for all the project's repos: `code_host = "github"` or `"azure"`, and with `"azure"`, `azure_url` (the organization or collection URL, e.g. `https://tfs.example.com/tfs/Coll`) for an Azure DevOps Server whose host isn't recognized; the project and repo then come from the remote's `…/{project}/_git/{repo}` path. The host CLI is `gh` for GitHub and `az` for Azure DevOps; a project may mix both. Azure DevOps is asked through its REST API with GETs only: `az rest --method get --resource 499b84ac-1321-427f-aa17-267ca6975798 --url <REST URL built from the target> --headers Accept=application/json`, the Microsoft Entra token of `az login`, not through the `azure-devops` extension, whose SDK sends `X-VSS-ForceMsaPassThrough` and so signs a work login whose email is also a personal Microsoft account in as that account (`TF400813`, seen live). Credentials (az's token, the PAT) go only to https URLs without userinfo on `dev.azure.com`, `{org}.visualstudio.com`, for that repo's own `azure_url` alone, the host of its `origin` remote (https, ssh or vs-ssh form) when the repo is one the user registered in `config.toml` and no adopted or checkout thread works in it (its agent could edit the remote), the host of the az CLI's default organization (`az devops configure`) or a host in `config.toml`'s `[azure] hosts`, an override for any other (`codehost.TrustedAzureURL`, checked in every call that attaches one); an `azure_url` on any other host, or on a host only `PROJECT.md` names, is dropped and a vs-ssh remote's organization must be a plain name. It is the same token the extension would get, so Conditional Access holds alike. When Azure DevOps refuses az's token and the organization's tenant (its anonymous `X-VSS-ResourceTenant`) isn't the default subscription's, tm retries once with `--subscription` of one in that tenant and keeps it for the organization; when az is missing, not logged in or refused and `AZURE_DEVOPS_EXT_PAT` is set, it does the same GET itself with the PAT (Basic, never on a command line). Auth is the user's own login (`gh auth login`, `az login` with Entra; the PAT also works but is not asked for), never a token terminatr stores. Without the CLI or a login (or the PAT) the host cannot be asked and degrades as in the table below; `tm doctor` checks each host in use (OPERATIONS.md). With no Azure repo in use, `tm doctor` still shows `az` and `az login` when `az` is installed (in the server's context), marked "no project uses Azure DevOps" and ok either way; the access and git origin lines stay per Azure repo. The ticker picks a repo's host once per repo sync, so a changed origin is seen at the next sync. `code_host` may also name a kind a **code host mod** serves (§5.3: lowercase letters, digits and dashes, not `github` or `azure`, e.g. `"gitlab"`; a value that isn't such a kind, `"GitLab"`, picks by origin as before): the project's repos are then asked of the one enabled code mod that provides that kind, runs for the project and holds the `commands` grant (`codehost.Mod`, reached through the server's supervisor, so only the server's ticker and its forwarded `tm thread` commands reach it). The mod gets the origin's server and project path (`{kind, host, path}`, `git@gitlab.com:acme/shop.git` → `gitlab.com`, `acme/shop`), never the repo's folder, and tm keeps only the fixed words of its answers (`PR`'s; a URL only when it is one of this repo's PR URLs with the same number). Without such a mod (none enabled for the project, no `commands` grant, two of them, or no server) the host is `Unserved`: every lookup fails with a reason in fixed words and the fix (the `gh-failing` item names the kind, `gitlab`, and says what to do), and nothing is merged. What is destructive stays tm's, whoever supplies the host (the mods design §11): merge commits are found by git alone (GitLab's `See merge request <path>!<n>` in a merge commit's message), the prompts' hints are tm's fixed text (`glab mr view <n>`, `--comments`), a mod's word that a PR merged never deletes a branch at `tm thread resolve` unless git finds the branch's commits on a remote (`kept branch … (PR merged, but 1 unpushed commit on …)`), a merged PR's head reported by a mod never counts as pushed when auto-close checks a worktree, and `tm thread merge` makes its checks itself (§9) and asks the mod only to merge the checked head by the project's merge method; a mod that doesn't merge by that method (the example GitLab mod has no per-merge rebase) answers `{refused: "method", allowed: [...]}`, which `tm thread merge` reports as `merge-method-not-allowed`. `tm doctor` shows, per kind in use, the mod that serves it and the mod's own checks (`codehost.doctor`: its CLI and login).
  Without the host's CLI or login (`tm doctor` says which), per host:

  | Missing | Still works | Waits or is empty |
  |---|---|---|
  | `gh` or its login (GitHub) | threads, reports, tasks, the guard | PR polling, follow-up prompts, failed-run excerpts, `pr-merged` auto-close and `complete_tasks = "merged"`; the project gets a `gh-failing` item after 3 failed polls |
  | `az` or `az login`, and no `AZURE_DEVOPS_EXT_PAT` (Azure DevOps) | the same | the same, for that project's Azure repos; repos on the other host are unaffected |
  | build-validation policies (Azure) | PR state, review, conflicts, merge detection | no check results and no failed-run excerpt; a PR with policies reports them |
  | `git` credentials for the remote | local work | fetches and pushes (`tm doctor` runs `git ls-remote origin HEAD`) |

  PR lines in reports and in the dashboard take these forms: `https://github.com/<owner>/<repo>/pull/<n>`, `https://dev.azure.com/<org>/<project>/_git/<repo>/pullrequest/<n>` (or `https://<org>.visualstudio.com/…`), and a GitLab merge request's `https://<server>[:port]/<group>[/<subgroup>…]/<project>/-/merge_requests/<n>` (no `.` or `..` segment). Threads push their branch and report the PR's title and body; the coordinator opens the PR (`gh pr create`, `az repos pr create` or an `az rest` POST) and tm finds it by the thread's branch; completing PRs is the coordinator's and the guard refuses a thread's attempt (§8.6). terminatr's own releases stay on GitHub Actions (OPERATIONS.md, Releasing) whatever host a project uses.
- **Worktrees live outside the project folder.** Claude Code loads `CLAUDE.md` from parent directories, so a worktree under the project folder would inherit the coordinator's role file.

### 5.2 Access model

**Only the coordinator has the full context and writes project state.** It edits `CONTEXT.md`, `MEMORY.md`, `memory/` and the body and goal of `PROJECT.md` directly, and changes `TASKS.md` through `tm task`. It hands tasks to threads.

**Threads get a scoped brief and read-only access to the project.** They can read every project file by absolute path, but they write nothing there. They report back **only through `tm`** (§7.2–7.3), and the coordinator decides what goes into tasks and memory. Through `tm` a thread sees only its own project's data, tasks, threads, reports, inbox, library and memory (§11.1); that limit is `tm`'s, and its sandbox may still read other projects' folders on disk.

| | Coordinator | Thread |
|---|---|---|
| cwd | `~/.terminatr/projects/<slug>/` | its worktree (`~/.terminatr/worktrees/<slug>/<id>-…`) |
| Reads | everything in the project; thread worktrees (`Read` grant on `~/.terminatr/worktrees/<slug>/`, so it can review code without prompts) | its worktree; the project folder, read-only, by absolute path |
| Writes | `CONTEXT.md`, `MEMORY.md`, `memory/`, `PROJECT.md` (body and goal) directly; all `tm task` and `tm thread` operations | its worktree only (and what its commits need in the main repo's git dir, never its hooks or config: §8.6, Access) |
| Reports through | — | `tm status`, `tm report`, `tm done`, `tm task steps` on its own task (§11.1) |
| Server socket | allowed | allowed (in the sandbox's socket allow list) |
| Human socket (§3.2) | not allowed (never in a sandbox's allow list) | not allowed |

**Enforcement uses the agent's own permission settings plus its sandbox, not symlinks.** The core decides the policy per role and passes it to the agent as `LaunchSpec.Access` (a `Read` list and a `NoWrite` list of absolute directories) together with the socket path. The agent's manifest turns that policy into the harness's settings (§8.2). For Claude Code (§8.6) that means:

- a `Read(//<home>/.terminatr/projects/<slug>/**)` allow rule, so reads are silent;
- an `Edit(…)` deny rule on the same directory, which covers Write, Edit and NotebookEdit, so the file tools can't write there in any permission mode, yolo included (verified by the Claude Code spike);
- for threads, the Bash sandbox enabled. It blocks writes outside the worktree at the OS level and checks real paths. By default the agent may ask to run a command outside it (Claude's `dangerouslyDisableSandbox`), which the human or auto mode approves; such a command runs with the user's full rights and can reach the human socket (§3.2). `sandbox_escapes = false` (§11.2) closes that: Claude threads then start with `allowUnsandboxedCommands: false`, and what can't run in the sandbox doesn't run; the thread names it in its report instead. The coordinator's Bash isn't sandboxed, so the setting doesn't apply to it;
- for threads, the project's **sandbox lists** (`[projects.<slug>.sandbox]`, `[defaults.sandbox]`, §11.2; the project's own list when it sets one, else all projects' less those its `drop` names, `config.MergeSandbox`, `config.ReplaceKeys`): `excluded_commands` go into Claude's `sandbox.excludedCommands` (Bash rule syntax; such a command runs outside the sandbox with the user's full rights, the human socket included, even with `sandbox_escapes = false`), each asking first (the guard's `askFirst`: it answers a call with one of its commands "ask", rule `excluded`, the guard off too, and the session marks that prompt the user's as an escape's) or with `ask = false` running without asking (tm adds its `Bash(<command>)` to `permissions.allow`); `write` folders join the thread's `Access.Write` (Claude's `Edit(/<path>/**)` allow, Codex's filesystem write) and the guard's writable list, the launch refusing one that holds or is in tm's folder, the project folder or a repository's git dir of the project, or whose links lead where no write folder may be; and `allow` after tm's own `permissions.allow` rules (Claude merges both lists with the user's own settings, and its deny rules, tm's among them, win over any allow). `sockets` (macOS) go after tm's socket in Claude's `sandbox.network.allowUnixSockets` and a Codex thread's `network.unix_sockets`, by their path and where their links lead; the launch refuses one in or holding tm's folder, its run folder or the sockets' folder (the human socket), one whose links lead where no socket rule may be, and a folder (`socketRefused`). The server reads them from `config.toml` at every launch of a thread, start and resume, never from `session.start`'s parameters, which a coordinator writes; the manifest agent drops them for any other role (`LaunchSpec.Sandbox`). Codex has no per-session equivalent of excluded commands and allow rules (its exec-policy rules are files under `CODEX_HOME`, which tm doesn't write): its launch logs that its excluded commands reach it only as approved commands (below), its allow rules not at all;
- for threads, the project's **approved commands** (`approved_commands`, T306; `server/approved.go`): `tm run -- <words>` calls `command.run` (a stream on `tm.sock`, `proto.CommandRunParams`): the server places the caller by the pid walk (a thread's running session with a worktree; never the human, whatever the socket), reads the thread's approved commands from `config.toml` at that moment (`approvedFor`: the project's list, all projects' added, drops and refusals as the other lists; for a Codex thread also each excluded command that fits the form, `config.ApprovedOf`, asking first as it does: T574 §4.6 case 3), and matches the argv word for word (`config.ApprovedMatch`: a last `*` takes any further arguments). A command checks as words without quotes, `$`, shell operators or patterns, never `tm`, never a program alone with `*` (`make *`, `go *`), and never one `codeWarning` calls open (`sh -c *`, `npm run *`); its warning says a last `*` passes the program's options too. The guard then judges `shellLine(argv)` as a Bash call of the thread (`guard.Rules.JudgeCommand`, without its escape and excluded-command questions): any refusal or "ask" refuses the run. A Codex thread's excluded command that asks first puts a question on its session (`proto.Question.Escape`: `session.answer` takes it from the human only) and runs on `Run it`. The server answers `CommandRunResult`, journals `run.start`, and runs argv[0] (found on the run's `PATH`, or a path from the worktree) with no shell (on macOS through `sandbox-exec` with `runProfile`: `(allow default)`, then `network*` and `file-read*`/`file-write*` denied in tm's run folder, the sockets' folder, its keys and guard log, and `config.toml` denied, so the run and what it detaches can't reach the human socket; a sandbox-exec that can't apply fails the run; Linux and Windows run it unconfined), in the worktree, with the server's environment as a session's (`sessionEnv`, tm's bin first) plus the thread's `TERMINATR_*` variables and its temp folder as `TMPDIR`, `TERM=dumb`, no stdin, in a process group of its own; its output comes as `run.output` lines (bytes, by stream) and a last `run.exit` with its code. While it runs its pid is in `Server.runs`, so the pid walk places what it starts in the thread. The client hanging up, the thread's session ending or the server stopping kills the group (`run.end … killed`); a thread runs at most `maxRuns` (4) at once; each output line waits at most `runWriteWait` for the client; a Windows `.cmd` or `.bat` program takes no argument with cmd.exe's metacharacters (`batchArgs`); the question of one that asks first shows the whole line, and one longer than `maxAskLine` (1000) is refused; `asks.answer` refuses a `Question.Escape` answer that isn't the human's under its lock, so no question can take its place between the check and the answer; once it exits, the rest of its group is killed and its output closed after `runWaitDelay`. Each thread's brief lists its approved commands (`approvedGuide`), with how to run them. A detached process that leaves the group escapes that kill, and one that re-parents leaves the pid walk, which then calls it the human (on macOS the profile still keeps it off the human socket; on Linux and Windows it isn't kept off). The run gets the server's whole environment (its tokens, `SSH_AUTH_SOCK`), as an excluded command does; it has no time limit. A socket rule in the threads' temp folders is refused at the check, and at launch one in a write folder too (`threadWritable`);
- for threads, the project's **hosts, local ports and guidance** (`hosts`, `local_ports`, `guides` in the same tables, §11.2; tm 0.37's preset switches `browser_testing`, `browser_downloads`, `local_dev_servers` read as the rules they unfold into), mapped by the server at launch on the platforms `config.SandboxSettings` gives (from the T561 findings; macOS only, as the browser downloads preset's hosts were: narrower elsewhere, never wider): local ports to local binding (`LaunchSpec.Sandbox.LocalBinding`: Claude's `sandbox.network.allowLocalBinding`, Codex's `permissions.tm.network.allow_local_binding`), hosts to `LaunchSpec.Sandbox.Domains` (Claude's `sandbox.network.allowedDomains`; a Codex thread's `permissions.tm.network.domains`, through its proxy), and each guidance text named (`config.Guides`) after the brief (read from a brief of at most 1 MiB), written as `brief.md` in the session's runtime dir at every launch (both agents read `BriefPath`). `browser-testing`: launch Playwright's headless shell with `--single-process`, since neither agent's Seatbelt profile has a `mach-register` rule, which multi-process Chromium needs, and no setting adds one; such a browser holds one context at a time, so the guidance's ready `playwright.sandbox.config.ts` sets `reuseContext` (one context per worker) and exports a per-test browser fixture; no local binding is needed (Playwright's pipe transport). `browser-downloads`: npm's cache and the browsers path are the thread's own (`~/.npm` and `~/Library/Caches/ms-playwright` aren't writable), with a `curl` fallback for Playwright's installer, whose proxy agent Claude's sandbox proxy resets near the end of a large download. The texts are worded from the thread's other rules: which of `config.BrowserDomains` (`registry.npmjs.org`, `cdn.playwright.dev`, `playwright.download.prss.microsoft.com`, `storage.googleapis.com`, where the CDN redirects Chrome for Testing builds) its hosts open and which not, and whether local ports are open; `browser-downloads` without `browser-testing` gets the downloads part alone. A rule that doesn't apply on the server's platform (`runtime.GOOS`) stays in `config.toml`, is left out of the launch, and the server logs `sandbox hosts skipped: doesn't apply on Linux`. **An entry that fails its check** (one written by hand) is refused on its own (T574 P10): the rest still hold; the server logs one line per refused entry and launch (`sandbox excluded "make a$X" refused, the thread starts without it: …`), `session.start` returns it in `warnings` (`tm thread start`/`restart` print it to stderr), and the Access tab marks the entry `✗` with the reason until it is fixed or removed. A `config.toml` that doesn't load leaves all of the project's sandbox rules out (fails closed), said the same way. The excluded-command check refuses every `$` that starts an expansion (`$X`, `${`, `$1`, `$@`, `$*`, `$$`, `$?`, `$#`, `$!`, `$-`), not only the first.
- the server's socket in `sandbox.network.allowUnixSockets`. Without it, sandboxed `tm` calls fail with `EPERM`. It is the only socket listed: the human socket (§3.2) is not, so a sandboxed thread, and anything it starts, detached or not, can't connect to it (`TestNoHumanSocket` checks every embedded manifest).

**A thread's own temp folder** (T587). Every Claude session of the user keeps its scratchpad and task output (`tasks/*.output`, its subagents' transcripts) in one shared `/tmp/claude-<uid>`, so a thread's sandboxed Bash could read the human's and the coordinator's sessions there, and write and rename their folders. So each thread gets a folder of its own, `/tmp/tm-<uid>/<10 hex>` (`home.ThreadTempDir`: a hash of the tm home, the project and the thread id; real path, `/private/tmp` on macOS; `<TEMP>\tm\…` on Windows), made private (0700, owned by the user, no link; its parent too) at each launch and kept across restarts like the worktree, but made anew when it holds a link (a direct child, or one of its `claude-<uid>` folder's: the thread writes there); `tm thread resolve` removes it, only when it and its parent are such private folders (attachments are copied to the library before). Short on purpose: Claude 2.1.302 keeps its sandbox's `TMPDIR` in `$CLAUDE_CODE_TMPDIR/claude-<uid>` only while that is at most 44 bytes (AF_UNIX socket paths, the manifest's `[guard] temp_max`), else falls back to the shared folder. The launch sets it as the agent's temp variables (`LaunchSpec.TempDir`, `[launch.env]`: Claude's `CLAUDE_CODE_TMPDIR` and `TMPDIR`, Codex's `TMPDIR`; an empty value sets nothing). Three tm rules in the resolver show it: `temp` (the folder), `no-read` on `/tmp/tm-<uid>` (the other threads'; its own open within) and `no-read` on each agent's shared temp folder (`agent.SharedTemps`: the `{uid}` entries of `[guard] temp_scratch` agents, Claude's `/tmp/claude-<uid>`), skipped where the thread's folder would be too long for its agent (a long uid; the rule says `NOT DENIED` and why, and the launch warns) or where the shared folder holds the thread's own folders (a tm home under it). The guard's floor keeps the thread's file tools out of the other threads' folders (§8.6, Guard). Claude gets the `no-read` folders as `sandbox.filesystem.denyRead`, its own folder as `allowRead`, and Read and Edit deny rules for those that don't hold its own (`Access.Closed`); its file tools ask before reading other threads' folders, as anywhere outside the worktree. Codex gets them as `none` in its profile, its own `write`. Checked live (Claude 2.1.302, Codex 0.162 seatbelt): Bash's `TMPDIR` is the thread's, read and write there; other threads' folders, their parent and `/tmp/claude-<uid>` refused for reading, listing and writing; under Codex, `none` on `/tmp/tm-<uid>` beats the workspace profile's `/tmp`: renaming the root, its own folder or a sibling, removing a sibling, and a file or link in the root are refused, `/tmp` itself still writable. A thread's forwarded file arguments take its agent's temp folder in its own folder only (`argfile.go`), never the shared one; a thread launched before keeps the old roots until it restarts.

**One resolver, and what a thread launched with** (T574 phase 1). The server works out a session's access as one list of rules (`server/access.go`, `proto.AccessRule`): each with where it acts (`inside` the sandbox, `outside` it, the `brief`, `prompts`, the `guard`, `env`, or the `agent`'s own files), its kind and value, its source (`tm`, `all projects`, the project's slug, `tm default`, or an agent file), the platforms and agents it applies to, and why the launch leaves it out (`skipped`: another platform; `dropped`: all projects' rule the project drops; `refused`: an entry that fails its check). tm's file rules come from `accessFor` (the git-dir grant also as one `git-dir` rule naming the repo whose git dir it opens, shown only; both from the thread record's repo, §9 **Git in a worktree**), the sandbox rules from `config.ThreadSandbox`, for a Codex thread a tm `host` rule per host of its recorded repo's git remotes (note `git remote host`, agents `codex`; `server/remotehosts.go`: the fetch and push URLs in `<common git dir>/config`, read with `git config --file`, so no include and no worktree config, which a thread writes; https, ssh and scp forms, host names only, never a local path, `file://`, an address or a one-word name), so its limited network still pushes (coordinator decision, 2026-10-18; Claude threads' sandbox asks per host instead), yolo and escapes from the session's record, the guard's settings (read live, never marked for a restart) and the secret variables' names and sources from `config.toml`. The launch takes `LaunchSpec.Access`, `.Sandbox`, `.Yolo` and `.NoSandboxEscapes` from these rules (`launchAccess`, `launchSandbox`, `launchFlags`); `TestAccessLaunchEqual` checks, over a matrix of configs, platforms, roles and folders, that they equal what `config.toml` says (`config.ThreadSandbox`, worked out apart) down to each agent's rendered files. Each thread launch (start, restart, resume) keeps its rules in `threads/<id>/access.json`, written by the server only (the thread can read it, not write it). `access.show` (the human's; a thread's for its own thread only; the project's coordinator's for its own project's thread-to-come and any of its threads, never another project's, T603; only the human is shown the agent's own files below) gives a project's thread-to-come or a thread's launch record, each rule `config.toml` added or removed since marked `added` or `removed` (a restart applies it), plus the rules of the agent's own settings files (`agent.OwnRuler`: Claude's `~/.claude/settings.json` and the worktree's `.claude/settings.json` and `settings.local.json`), shown only, their excluded commands with how the guard treats them (escapes, §8.6). `access.explain` (the human's, or the project's coordinator's for its own project only, T603; a thread's is refused) says whether a project's thread runs a command inside or outside the sandbox (an excluded command that takes it, `config.ExcludedMatch`), whether it asks first (an allow rule covering it, `config.SandboxCovered`; yolo), and the guard's verdict by the agent's shell tool. `tm access show [<project>] [--thread <id>] [--json]`, `tm access explain [<project>] [--thread <id>] [--json] -- <command>`, `tm access preset list|show <name> [--json]` and `tm access preset add <name> [<project> | --all]` (the human's, as are `add`, `rm`, `drop`, `undrop` and `set`: the coordinator and threads are refused; `access` being a checked command, §11.1: the server's view of the caller narrows the environment's, and `add`, which writes `config.toml` from the caller's process, refuses when no server answers; adds the preset's rules the threads don't have yet, printing each rule added or there already, journaled `settings.sandbox`), and the TUI's effective access view (`e` on the dashboard's list, a thread's row or the project; `e` on the project popup's Access tab, and its Built in row for tm's rules alone) read them.

**Why not symlinks.** The symlink spike (`docs/research/symlinks.md`) showed:

- Claude Code resolves every link and checks the **real path**, so a link gives no access that an absolute path doesn't. It only adds a second path that also needs a rule.
- The Write and Edit tools refuse to write to a file that is itself a symlink.
- `git worktree remove` (without `--force`) and `git clean -fdx` **silently delete ignored files** in a worktree, which would include any report kept there.

So **nothing terminatr-owned lives in a worktree**. There is no `.terminatr/` folder, no `info/exclude` entry, no symlink, no mirror and no copy-home step. A worktree is an ordinary checkout. Removing it by any means loses nothing, because reports and status are already in the project folder.

**Live context.** Threads read `PROJECT.md`, `CONTEXT.md`, `MEMORY.md`, `memory/` and `TASKS.md` by absolute path whenever they need them. They see edits the coordinator makes after they started; this fixes herdr-projects' snapshot problem without copying.

**The coordinator.** Its cwd is the project folder, so it reads everything directly, and writes there but for what tm alone writes (`Project.Owned`): `threads/`, `inbox/`, `tasks/`, `journal/`, `messages/`, `uploads/`, `JOURNAL.md`, `TASKS.md`, `questions.json`, the role files and tm's lock files are in its `NoWrite` lists. tm itself, which writes them outside any sandbox, doesn't trust them either: it never appends to or creates a file through a link there (`fsx.OpenNoLink`), and a `thread.toml` whose paths aren't absolute or whose branch or base reads as an option is refused; a missing worktree is made again only inside `worktrees/<slug>/`, and never for an adopted thread. `AGENTS.md` (with `CLAUDE.md` as a symlink to it) makes it the coordinator. That symlink sits in the coordinator's own folder and is read, never written, so the spike's write refusal doesn't apply. How it learns the rules is in §7.8.

### 5.3 User mods

User mods extend terminatr. This section covers what exists so far: `internal/mod`, `tm mod`, the hosts code mods run in under the supervisor (§3.7), and the broker. A mod is a folder `~/.terminatr/mods/<id>/` with a `mod.toml` manifest: `id` (lowercase letters, digits and single dashes, at most 40, the folder's name; `enabled`, `band` and `pane` are reserved, being the older `[mods]` keys), `name`, `version` (semver), `api` (the mod API it was written for, `MAJOR.MINOR`), an optional `min_tm`, `description`, `authors`, `homepage`; `[provides]` (`main`, a `.ts` or `.js` entry point that makes it a code mod; `columns`, at most 12 cells wide as the sidebar draws them (`view.MaxModColumnWidth`), `views`, `commands` (below, **Commands**) and `tools` (agent tools, §8.6: at most 16, each `{id, description, roles, input}` with an `id` of lowercase letters, digits and underscores, starting with a letter, so that `<mod id>_<id>` is at most 48 characters; a description of at most 2048 bytes; `roles` thread and/or coordinator, both when unset; `input` a JSON schema of an object, `properties` each with a `type` of string, integer, number, boolean, array or object and any other JSON schema keys, and `required`), all of which need `main`, and `tools` also the `tools` permission (asked with `permissions.tools = true`, which offers nothing without a tool); `code_host`, the code host kind the mod serves (mod API 1.1; lowercase letters, digits and dashes, not `github` or `azure`), which needs `main` and `permissions.commands` (the host's CLI it runs, `glab`), and whose consent says that tm asks it about the pull requests of projects whose `code_host` names it and has it merge one `tm thread merge` checked; the data files `theme`, `icons`, `keymap`, `layout`; `rules`: addenda by role, `coordinator` or `thread`, and policies, `coordinator_policy` or `thread_policy`; the folders `templates` and `agents`; `guard`, guard rules, below), `[permissions]` (`project_read`, `views`, `notify`, `inbox`, `tools`, `net` hosts, `commands` program names, `files` read and write roots (each absolute or `~/…`, without `..`, and never the home folder, a folder that holds it or a disk's root, under any spelling: `/`, `~/`, `~/.`; the consent says so plainly of a root that is or holds the home folder, and the broker opens nothing under one), `actions` the bindable actions that need a grant of their own (mod API 1.3: `session.clear`, `question.answer`; any other id, one listed twice, or a list without `project_read` is invalid), whose consent line names each in tm's words and says tm asks before each one runs; only a code mod asks for any, and there is none for credentials) `[settings]` (each `{type = int|bool|string|choice, default, min, max, options, label}`; a text default, an option and a label are one line of at most 500 characters without control or format characters, as a value the human sets; the settings page shows the default cleaned and quoted) and `[defaults]` (values it suggests for all projects' settings, below). Every path it names is inside its folder, links included. Keys tm doesn't know are ignored, so a newer manifest of the same API major still loads, with two exceptions that make it invalid, since a mod may tighten and never loosen (mods design §7): a key named as one of `config.toml`'s safety settings (`yolo`, `guard`, `guard_off`, `guard_mode`, `guard_allow`, `merge`, `merge_method`, `coordinator_merges`, `complete_tasks`, `start_threads`, `models`, `parallel_threads`, `global_parallel_threads`, `coordinator_approves`, `branch_name`; `mod.SafetyKeys`; a mod can't set a thread's secret environment either, as it suggests only `mod.DefaultKeys`) anywhere in it but a tool's input schema (`[provides.tools.input]`, the agent's), `[[provides.guard]]` itself aside, and a key a guard rule doesn't have.

**Guard rules** (mods design §7, Tighten only; `[[provides.guard]]`, at most 32) add refusals to the guard's (§8.6, **Guard**), and need no permission, so a data mod may carry them: each `{id, commands, writes, reads, roles, message}`, with an `id` of lowercase letters, digits and dashes that is no core rule's (`force-push`, … `credentials`), at least one of `commands` (each the words of a command refused: a program's name, then words its arguments include in that order, others between them allowed; a word ending in `*` matches any word it starts: `"kubectl delete namespace*"`), `writes` (files and folders no write or patch tool writes in) and `reads` (those no read or glob tool reads, a glob by the part before its first wildcard), each list at most 32 and each path absolute or from `~/`, without `..`; `roles` thread and/or coordinator (both when unset); and a `message` of at most 400 bytes, the sentence the agent reads (why, and what to do instead). A rule has nothing that lets a call through or turns a core rule off. The rules of the enabled mods that run for a session's project and role reach its guard as `mod:<mod>.<id>`; the consent screen shows each in full.

**Suggested defaults** (mods design §7, Tighten only; `[defaults]`, mod API 1.1) are values a mod suggests for all projects' non-safety settings, keyed as `config.toml`'s `[defaults]` (`auto_close = "days"`): only `auto_close`, `auto_close_days`, `pr_followup`, `pr_poll_seconds`, `auto_clear`, `fast_forward_checkout` and the four `archive_*_days` (`mod.DefaultKeys`), never a safety setting (which makes the manifest invalid, as above), which agent or model runs, or remote control. Each value is checked as the settings popup's write would be (`config.CheckSetting`: its type and range). They need no permission and change nothing by themselves: the consent lists them (`Defaults it suggests for all projects (…)`), and the human applies them to an enabled mod with `d` in the Mods tab, then `y`, or `tm mod defaults <id> --apply`. Applying writes each to `[defaults]` as the human's change (journaled `settings.<key> … (all projects, suggested by mod:<id>)` in each project that follows all projects), except one `[defaults]` already sets, which stays the human's, and one already at the value; a project's own setting still wins over `[defaults]`.

**API check.** This tm speaks mod API 1.9 (`mod.APIMajor`, `mod.APIMinor`; 1.9 added the bindable actions `memory.edit`, `memory.new` and `memory.rm` and a target's `path`, below, **Action binding**; 1.8 added `thread.changes`, the broker call and the event, a thread's git changes before its PR exists, below and **Events for user mods**; 1.7 added the view slots `sidebar.section` and `sidebar.project.section`, a section's `opens`, `badge` and per-slot `height`, a view's `description`, time spans in widgets (`ago`, `until`), the `ticker.changed` event and the layout keys `[sidebar] sections` and `project_sections`, `[coordinator] sections` and `[details] sections`, below and §4 **Mod sections**; 1.6 added a theme's pane palette, below; 1.5 added layout.toml v2, below; 1.4 added the `tasks.changed` event, the `task.new` action, `actions` on a keys widget (bound in the whole view) and `task` in a project tab's `view.opened`; 1.3 added the bindable actions `session.clear` and `question.answer`, a target's `question` and `option`, `[permissions] actions`, the `session.question` event and an action's `consent` in `$.actions.list`; 1.2 added `steps`, `updated` and `asked` to `tasks.list`'s tasks, `head` and `more` to a list's or table's rows, and no header for a table whose columns have no titles; 1.1 added `[provides] code_host` and `$.codehost.handle`, `[[provides.commands]]` with the `command.run` event, `[[provides.guard]]` and `[defaults]`). It loads a mod whose major matches and whose minor is at most its own, as `proto.Check` does for the protocol; otherwise the mod is listed as incompatible with the reason (`review-age needs mod API 1.5; this tm has 1.4: run tm update`). A `min_tm` newer than this tm does the same; a source build passes it.

**`[mods.<id>]` in config.toml** holds `enabled` (off when unset: a mod is installed disabled), `grants` (the permissions the human accepted; the mod runs with granted ∩ requested, and those asked but not granted are its "wants more"), an optional `projects` list (only these projects; unset, every project) and `[mods.<id>.settings]` (checked against the manifest; a bad value keeps the default and `tm doctor` says so). Enabling, granting, limiting and setting a mod are human calls: an agent caller is refused `human-only`. They edit the file line by line, as every settings write does (§11.2).

**Data mods** (mods design §2 tiers 1-3; `internal/mod/data.go`) are files tm reads, with no host. Each file is read from inside the mod's folder again when used (a link out of it is refused) and is at most 64 KiB. One theme, one icon set and one layout are in use at a time, picked in General settings from the built-in one and those of the enabled mods (`[ui] theme`, `[ui] icons`, `[ui] layout`, the mod's id); enabling a second theme mod doesn't switch to it. A **theme** (`theme.toml`) has `[light]` and `[dark]` tables of roles (`head`, `sel`, `sel_focus`, `faint`, `accent`, `title`, `bad`, `warn`, `good`, `info`, `plain`), each `{fg, bg, bold, faint, reverse}` with a colour as an ANSI name, a 0-255 index or `#rrggbb`, a second entry being the truecolor choice and the first its ANSI fallback; a role left out is the built-in one (`default-theme`'s), except `sel_focus` (the sidebar's keyboard row while the sidebar has the focus; the built-in one is cyan reverse), which falls back to the theme's own `sel`; a key it doesn't know is refused. **Since mod API 1.6** a scheme's table may have a `pane` table, the pane palette (`[dark.pane]`, `[light.pane]`): `fg` and `bg`, the panes' default foreground and background, and `palette`, all 16 ANSI colours (black, red, green, yellow, blue, magenta, cyan, white, then the bright ones), each `#rrggbb` only; any of them may be left out, and what is left out stays the terminal's own. The console draws the panes' symbolic colours with it on a truecolor terminal, and paints its own areas (sidebar, panels, header, footer, status bar, popups, the gaps between them) in its `bg` and, where a role sets none, its `fg`, so the window matches the panes (§3.3, Rendering); the built-in theme (Settings' *follow terminal*) has none, so without one, in safe mode, or without truecolor the panes are as before. Claude panes started (or resumed) under one follow the theme's colours: a generated Claude theme over Claude's `dark-ansi` or `light-ansi` one, for the palette's scheme, with the roles' and the palette's colours in truecolor (§8.6); a running Claude keeps its theme until then. A theme mod without a pane palette leaves Claude's own theme alone; the built-in theme gives Claude its ANSI theme, so Claude follows the terminal's colours as the rest does (§8.6). A pane table in a mod whose `api` is below 1.6 doesn't load (`CheckData`, and `tm mod install` refuses it: `theme: [dark.pane] needs api = "1.6" in mod.toml`); a bad colour or a `palette` without 16 entries is refused as a role's bad colour is. The nord example sets one for each scheme. Each console resolves it for its terminal's background, light or dark (OSC 11): the dashboard asks it when a mod's theme is in use, an attach as it starts and again on each mode 2031 update, so the dashboard and a session agree; the 2031 scheme itself goes only to the panes' programs, since a terminal's scheme (the system's appearance) can differ from its background. An **icon set** (`icons.toml`) is `parseIcons`' format (§4 **Icons**). A **keymap** (`keys.toml`) binds action ids to keys, `task.delegate = ["D"]` (§4 **Action ids**); every enabled keymap applies, in the order the mods were enabled (their `[mods.<id>]` tables' order): an action or a key an earlier mod binds stays the earlier mod's (`key conflict`), and what `ApplyKeymap` refuses keeps its default. A **layout** (`layout.toml`; `internal/mod/layout.go`) states only what it changes: every key is optional, an absent one keeping the built-in value (or the one the user set in the console). v1 (any mod API 1.x) is `sidebar = [...]`, the order of the sidebar's columns: built-in ids (`count`, `ci`, `state`, `remote`, `paused`, `inactive`, `needs`) and mods' (`review-age.age`), each once, at most 32; the safety columns it leaves out still show, at the end. **v2, since mod API 1.5** (the layout v2 design §2), makes `sidebar` a table (the type of `sidebar` says which form a file is) and adds tables: `[sidebar]` `columns` (as v1), `side` (`left` | `right`), `width` (cells 14–200, or a share of the window `"10%"`–`"60%"`; default 36) and `mode` (`full` | `slim` | `hidden`), and since mod API 1.7 `sections` and `project_sections` (below); `[details]` (the dashboard's details panel) `place` (`right` | `bottom` | `off`) and `size` (the panel's: cells 24–200 at the right, rows 6–100 at the bottom, or a share `"20%"`–`"70%"`; default `"35%"` right, `"40%"` bottom; refused with `place = "off"`), and since 1.7 `sections` (below); `[coordinator]` (the coordinator's info panel, mod API 1.7) `sections` (below); `[header]` `style` (`shown` | `hidden`; the header is one row, so `compact` is refused); `[footer]` `style` (`shown` | `compact` | `hidden`); `[status_bar]` `style` (`shown` | `compact`; never hidden); `[rows]` `density` (`normal` | `compact`); `[dashboard]` `sections`, the list's sections in order (`needs`, `project`, `other`, `cards`). **Mods' sections in the layout** (mod API 1.7; §4 **Mod sections**) are lists of `"<mod>.<view>"` ids, each once, at most 32; an id no enabled mod has is skipped, and a layout can't hide a section (the Mods tab turns its mod off). `[sidebar] sections` orders the band, with the reserved `"tree"` (at most once) marking where the tree sits: sections before it go above the tree, those after it below, and without it all are below; `[sidebar] project_sections` orders the sections under each project, always below its threads (`"tree"` is refused there). `[coordinator] sections` and `[details] sections` place the right pane's sections among core's blocks, named by reserved ids: the coordinator's `needs`, `inbox`, `threads` and `deck`, and the details panel's (and a thread's info panel's) `core`. A section goes after the block listed before it, or with none before it just above the first block listed after it; with no block in the list, all go at the bottom in the list's order. The blocks a list names must be in core's order, each once (`coordinator.sections: "threads" before "needs": core's blocks keep their order (needs, inbox, threads, deck)`); they place sections, they never reorder or hide core's blocks, and one left out still shows in its place. In every list, a section it doesn't name goes last (below the tree, at the bottom of the panel), after those it names, in mod order: the order the Mods tab lists, built-in mods first, which is also the order with no layout. So `[coordinator] sections = ["needs", "ticker.ticker"]` puts the ticker just under NEEDS YOU, and `[sidebar] sections = ["ticker.band", "tree"]` puts the ticker's band view above the tree (with its `place` setting `sidebar` or `both`). `mod.ParseLayout` refuses, joining every error with its key, an unknown key or table, a wrong type, a value it doesn't know (naming those it takes), a size out of range, and unknown or repeated columns and sections; it doesn't refuse what is only a fallback, which the console fixes up as it loads: `needs` and `project` left out of `sections` come back (`needs` first, `project` after it), while leaving out `other` or `cards` hides them; a hidden sidebar is 0 columns with a `⚑ N` badge for what needs you, and `b` brings it back; a narrow window still cuts the sidebar to leave the panes 60 columns, then shows the slim strip, and a details panel with no room shows under the selected row. A layout with any v2 table in a mod whose `api` is below 1.5 doesn't load (`CheckData`, and `tm mod install` refuses it: `layout: [sidebar] side needs api = "1.5" in mod.toml`), so an older tm refuses such a mod by its API, not by a parse error. The built-in `default-layout` sets only the columns and documents every other key, commented out with its default: the template to copy. The console that picks a layout, and every console as it starts, puts its columns on its view (`view.columns`), so every console of that view agrees; picked, the whole layout applies (its sidebar too, the view's), while a console starting on a view keeps the view's sidebar where the layout doesn't set it. Each part of the console's layout comes from, highest first: the console's own change under the layout in use (`ui.json` `layouts.<id>`, written when the user changes a part that layout sets: a drag, `{` `}`, `b`, `<` `>`, `|`), the layout, the console's own value (`ui.json`'s top level, written when the user changes a part the layout doesn't set), the built-in one; so a layout's width under one layout leaves the user's own for the others, and going back to `default` brings it back. `ui.json` keeps changes under at most 16 layouts, dropping the one changed longest ago. The status bar's style is geometry, so it is the view's too (`view.status`, the view's `status_compact`), sent once when a console picks a layout or starts; the header, the footer and the density are each console's own, drawn by its dashboard (§4). (So far the console applies the columns, the sidebar's side, width (a share kept as a share in the view) and mode, the details panel's place and size, the header, the footer, the status bar and the density; the sections too: the list's sections come in the order of `dashboard.sections`, the mods' dashboard cards being the `cards` section; leaving out `other` hides the sessions outside the projects (a blocked one still shows in NEEDS YOU), leaving out `cards` hides the cards, and the Mods tab says so on a mod that has one.) **Rules** (`[provides] rules`): `coordinator` and `thread` are addenda, appended to `tm skill <role>` by mod id; `coordinator_policy` and `thread_policy` replace that role's policy part, the first enabled such mod winning (§7.7). **Task templates** (`templates/<name>.md`): a first line `# <title with {title}>`, the notes, and the list under `## Steps`; `tm task add "…" --template <name>` (or `<mod>:<name>` when two mods have the name) lays one on the task, `--notes` replacing its notes and `--step` adding after its steps. **Agent manifests** (`agents/<name>.toml`) load as if in `~/.terminatr/agents`, the first enabled mod winning a name and the user's own file winning over a mod's. The look, the keys, the layout and the agents follow a mod being enabled; rules and templates follow its projects too. A data file that doesn't load leaves the built-in one: the console says so once as it starts (`mods: …` in the dashboard's footer, on stderr for `tm attach`), and the picker says so when picked.

**Registry.** `mod.Load` lists every folder in `~/.terminatr/mods` with its state: invalid (no or a broken manifest, an id that isn't its folder's, or a `[mods.<id>]` table with a key of the wrong type), incompatible, disabled or enabled; then the built-in mods. One broken mod never stops the others or tm. `tm doctor` warns about invalid and incompatible mods, a data file that doesn't load (`mod.CheckData`, with the console's own checks of themes, icon sets and keymaps), an enabled mod waiting for permissions, refused settings, `[mods.<id>]` tables that name no installed mod, and an installed folder named as a built-in mod (it isn't loaded); from a running server (`mod.hosts`), code mods whose host failed or is backing off, and safe mode.

**Built-in mods** (`internal/mod/bundled.go`, mods design §11). tm's defaults ship as data mods embedded in the binary (`internal/mod/bundled/<id>/`, read from it, never from disk), listed after the installed mods with `built in`: `default-theme` (the built-in theme, every role in both schemes), `unicode-icons`, `nerd-icons` and `ascii-icons` (the three built-in icon sets, each whole, with `base` set to itself so a copy keeps its glyph rules), `default-keymap` (the keys of every action that can be rebound), `default-layout` (the sidebar's column order, every v2 key documented), `default-policy` (the coordinator's and the threads' policy parts, §7.7) and `default-templates` (task templates `bug`, `spike` and `chore`). tm draws its defaults from these files: the console parses the theme and icon sets as it starts, applies the keymap under the keymap mods' (a key a mod binds goes to the mod's action as before; the shipped keymap is the tables' own keys, checked by a test, so applying it changes nothing), and `default` in the Theme and Layout pickers and `unicode`, `nerd` and `ascii` in the Icons picker are these mods (their ids name them too; the pickers don't offer them twice). Each can be copied as a folder, given another id and installed to start one's own. They are enabled unless their `[mods.<id>]` table sets `enabled = false`, come after the user's mods (an enabled keymap or policy mod of the user's goes first; a user mod's template of a name a built-in one has is the one `--template <name>` picks), have no permissions (but the code mods), take the version of the tm they ship in, and update with it: `tm mod remove` and `update` refuse them, as `tm mod install` refuses a mod with one's id. Only `default-templates`, `memory-tab`, `pr-ci-column`, `tasks-tab` and `ticker` can be turned off; the others are tm's defaults and `tm mod disable` says what replaces them. The four code mods are the only drawing of what they draw: turned off, the Tasks and Memory tabs say the mod is off and how to turn it on, the PR/CI column takes no room, and the coordinator's info panel has no TICKER section (core's PR-host warnings stay). A built-in mod's settings are an installed one's: `[mods.<id>.settings]` over its manifest's defaults, set in the Mods tab (`s`); a change restarts its host with them.

  One built-in mod is code: `pr-ci-column`, the sidebar's PR/CI column (mods design §11, proof 1 of the mod API; §13 example 7). Its source is `examples/mods/pr-ci-column` (`main.ts`); `go generate ./internal/mod` precompiles it with esbuild into `internal/mod/bundled/pr-ci-column/main.js` and a copy of its manifest naming that bundle, and a test fails while they are stale. It runs with what it asks for (`project_read`) without a consent screen, as part of tm, in a host the supervisor starts with the server (it never waits for its first event), running the embedded bundle without esbuild. It draws each thread's PR checks (`pr_checks` in `mod.ready`'s snapshot, `checks` in `pr.changed`) as `ci.pass` in `good`, `ci.fail` in `bad` or `ci.run` in `faint` in its column `ci` on thread rows, clears a thread's cell when its PR has no checks or the thread is resolved, looks the project up again (`project.get`) after its queue dropped events, and keeps the targets it drew in its store so a new host clears the cells of threads gone meanwhile. It is the column's only drawing: turned off, the column takes no room (§4 **Sidebar**). Measured on an M-series Mac (`TestMeasurePRCI` with `TERMINATR_MEASURE_BIN`, `BenchmarkSidebarCI`): its host answers hello about 48 ms after it starts and publishes its first cell about 50 ms after (10 projects of 8 threads: all 60 cells by 53 ms), a `pr.changed` becomes a cell in under 1 ms, the host idles at about 23 MiB RSS, and a sidebar frame of 90 rows draws in about 0.65 ms, as it did with the Go cell the mod replaced (building the rows, once a poll, takes about 8 µs more).

  Another is `memory-tab`, the project popup's Memory tab (proof 2 of the mod API, §4 **memory**; §13 example 8), whose source is `examples/mods/memory-tab` (mod API 1.9: its rows bind the memory note actions), precompiled the same way. It runs with what it asks for (`project_read`, `views`) without a consent screen; since it draws no column its host is lazy: after its first run it waits for the tab's `view.opened`. The Memory tab has no drawing of its own: turned off (`enabled = false`), the tab says the mod is off and how to turn it on.
  The third is `tasks-tab`, the project popup's Tasks tab (§4 **tasks**; mods design §12, Phase 2), whose source is `examples/mods/tasks-tab` (mod API 1.4), precompiled the same way. It runs with `project_read` and `views` like `memory-tab`, its host lazy; it reads the board (`tasks.list`) when the tab shows and again on `tasks.changed`, binds the core's task actions on its rows and `task.new` in the whole view, and handles no key but `b`. Opened to show a done task (`view.opened`'s `task`), it lists the done tasks. The Tasks tab has no drawing of its own: turned off, the tab says the mod is off and how to turn it on.
  The fourth is `ticker` (§4 **Info panel**, **Mod sections**), whose source is `examples/mods/ticker` (mod API 1.7), precompiled the same way, with `project_read` and `views`. It draws when the ticker last polled a project's PRs and synced its repos, and whether its PR host answers, as key/value rows of time spans that the console moves on by itself, with a red `PR host ▲` badge while PR polls fail. Its view `ticker` is a `coordinator.section`, `band` a `sidebar.section`; its `place` setting picks where it shows: `right` (the default, beside the coordinator's pane), `sidebar` (the sidebar's band) or `both`. A view the setting leaves out gets a `null` tree, which draws nothing. It publishes on `view.opened` and `ticker.changed` and never polls. Its host starts with the server, as every mod with a sidebar section's does. Turned off or failed, it draws nothing; core's warnings of a failing PR host, the red line in the coordinator's panel and the red `▲` after the project's name in the sidebar, stay. The /tm pane's Ticker rows are core's.

**`tm mod`** (mods design §8). `install <path | git-url[//subfolder][@tag] | github-tree-url>` takes a folder or a git URL (`https://` or `file://`; plain `http://` and `git://` are refused; **https and public URLs only**: every SSH form (`ssh://`, `user@host:path`) and a URL with a user, password or token in it (`https://user:TOKEN@host/repo`) is refused with `mods install from public URLs only`, a URL git or curl could read differently than it looks (an empty or slash-led host such as `https:///user:TOKEN@host/x`, a backslash, whitespace or a control character, or a query or fragment such as `?token=x`) is refused too, and git runs with no prompt and none of the user's settings: no system or global git config (so no `insteadOf`, `http.extraHeader`, cookie or client certificate), no credential helper, an empty `HOME` (so no `~/.netrc`), and none of tm's own `GIT_CONFIG_PARAMETERS`, `GIT_SSH*`, `SSH_AUTH_SOCK` or `GIT_SSL_*` variables, with only https and file allowed, so a private repository fails instead of using the user's logins; a record from before that holds an SSH source is shown as it is, one with userinfo with `***@` for it (Mods tab, `tm mod show`, `list --json`), and reading it rewrites `.tm-source.json` with the userinfo redacted, so the secret no longer sits on disk, and its `update` is refused until the mod is removed and installed again from https) at an optional tag or branch, the mod at its root or in a subfolder (**Subfolder sources**, below); there is no catalogue, and tm never installs a mod a project's repo carries. It copies the mod into a staging folder beside the installed ones (a git URL shallow-cloned first; `.git` folders left out; a link that is absolute or leads out of the mod (followed name by name as the OS resolves it, through other links, so `d -> ..` and `e -> d/../x` lead out; a loop, or a missing name before a `..`, refuses it too), anything but files, folders and links, or more than 2000 files or 64 MB refuse it), checks the manifest and the API as the registry does, shows the consent screen and, after a `y` on a terminal (`--yes` without one), moves it into `~/.terminatr/mods/<id>/` disabled, with `.tm-source.json` recording the source (`git`, `ref`, `subdir`), the commit, the subfolder's git tree and the files' sha256. A `[mods.<id>]` table left from an earlier install is removed first, so nothing is enabled or granted without consent. The consent screen names the mod, what it provides, each permission it asks for in plain words (`reads your projects`, `draws its own panels`, `shows notifications`, `tells the coordinator`, `offers tools to agents`, `reads …`/`writes …`, `talks to …`, `runs …`; none is `safe`), and every rules addendum, agent manifest, agent tool (its description and each input with its schema, which agents read) and guard rule (what it refuses and its message) in full between `----- begin <file> -----` and `----- end <file> -----` (a tool's "file" is its name); the mod's text is shown with control characters replaced. `enable <id> [--project <slug>]` asks the same and then writes `enabled = true` and `grants` = every permission the manifest asks for; with `--project` the mod also runs for that project (added to `projects`, or the list begun when it was off; nothing to add when it already runs for all). Enabling an enabled mod that wants nothing more for another project doesn't ask. `disable <id> [--project <slug>]` turns it off, or takes the project off its list (the last one off turns it off; refused for a mod that runs for every project); its grants stay. `update [<id>]` fetches each recorded source again (a version tag moves to the newest version tag; a folder or branch is fetched as it is), does nothing when the files' hash is unchanged, and otherwise asks again only when the new version asks for a permission it didn't, more of one (another host, command or root), starts changing what agents run or read (a policy, rules, an agent manifest, task templates), or brings new or changed rules addenda, agent manifests, task templates, agent tools or guard rules, which it shows; on a `y` an enabled mod is granted those permissions. `list [--outdated] [--json]` shows each mod, the built-in ones last (`built in · safe`; `builtin` in JSON), with its mark (`●` on, `○` off, `▲` invalid or incompatible, `◆` wants more), id, name, version, kinds and what it runs with or why not, `· only <projects>` when limited; `--outdated` only those whose source moved on (a folder whose files changed, a newer version tag, a branch at another commit; for a subfolder source, only when the subfolder's git tree there differs from the one installed) with what an update would bring. `remove <id> [--purge]` asks first, deletes the mod's folder (a link is only unlinked), its `[mods.<id>]` table and its `cache/mods/<id>/` (its bundle), and keeps `state/mods/<id>/` 30 days (marked `.removed`; `tm mod install` and `remove` sweep older ones; a reinstall takes it back) unless `--purge`, which also deletes its log. `validate [<dir>…] [--json]` checks folders as `install` would, and that their data files load (`mod.CheckData`), the installed mods when none is named (not the built-in ones), and exits 1 if any is invalid. All but `list`, `validate`, `new`, `test` and `logs` (below) are human calls: an agent is refused `human-only`, its identity checked with the server as for `tm project`. Changes take effect for what reads the registry next; the supervisor that starts and stops hosts live comes with the host. `run <id> <command> [--project <slug>]` runs one of a mod's commands (**Commands**, below), the human's only. `defaults <id> [--apply] [--yes]` shows the defaults a mod suggests (**Suggested defaults**, above), each beside what all projects follow now (`yours in [defaults], stays` for one the file sets, `already` for one at the value); `--apply` applies them to an enabled mod after a `y` (`--yes` without a terminal), the human's only.

**Subfolder sources** (`internal/mod/source.go`). A mod can live in a folder of a repository rather than at its root, in one syntax for any host: `<git-url>//<subfolder>[@<ref>]`, the first `//` after the URL's `://` (or scp's `:`; for `file:///abs`, after the path's leading `/`) starting the subfolder, and an `@` after it naming the tag or branch (`https://example.com/mods.git//themes/nord@v1.0.0`, `file:///srv/mods//nord`). A GitHub folder's link as the browser shows it, `https://github.com/<owner>/<repo>/tree/<ref>/<subfolder>`, reads as `https://github.com/<owner>/<repo>//<subfolder>@<ref>` (a ref with a `/` in it needs the generic form; a `/blob/` link is refused as a file, and an `@` after a tree URL as a second ref), and `tm mod list` shows a GitHub subfolder source at a ref as its tree URL again. The subfolder must be a relative slash path of names made of letters, digits and `. _ + -`: a leading `/`, an empty, `.` or `..` name, a `.git` name (any case), a name starting with `-`, and `\`, `:`, `%` or spaces are refused, when parsed and again when fetched (a `.tm-source.json` is checked again). tm clones shallow and sparse (`--depth 1 --filter=blob:none --sparse`, then `sparse-checkout set --cone` on the subfolder), finds the subfolder in the commit's tree (`git ls-tree`; missing, a file or a link there are refused), checks that each name on the way in the checkout is a real folder, not a link, and copies only the subfolder in, with the checks of a whole repository: a link that is absolute or leads out of the subfolder, anything but files, folders and links, or more than 2000 files or 64 MB refuse it, and `.git` is never copied. An update fetches the same subfolder, a version tag moving to the repository's newest version tag; `list --outdated` names a newer tag, or a branch's new commit, only when the subfolder's tree there (fetched shallow, without file contents) differs from the installed one. terminatr's example mods install this way (`tm mod examples`, the Mods tab's `e`): their lines pin the running tm's release tag, so an example is as that release made it and speaks its mod API (main's may already need the next release), and `tm mod update` moves it on; a build that isn't a release (`dev`, snapshots) pins `main`.

**Mod dev tools** (mods design §8, Dev workflow). `tm mod new <id> [--kind column|hook|theme|icons|keymap|rules] [--dir <dir>]` scaffolds a mod in `./<id>` (or `--dir`, which must be empty; a `hook` unless `--kind`): `mod.toml`, a `README.md`, and for a code mod (`column`, `hook`) `main.ts`, `types/tm.d.ts` (`modapi.TS()`, the API of the tm that made it), a strict `tsconfig.json` and a fixture in `tests/`; for a data mod its file (`theme.toml`, `icons.toml`, `keymap.toml`, `rules/coordinator.md`). Every scaffold validates, and a code one passes its own fixture. `tm mod dev <dir> [--yes]` validates the folder, links `~/.terminatr/mods/<id>` to it (refused when that id is installed from elsewhere or linked to another folder), enables it after the consent question unless it is already on with all it asks for, has the server start its host now (`mod.restart`, starting a server if none runs), prints its log as it grows, and restarts the host each time a file in the folder is saved (`.git`, `node_modules` and hidden folders aside; an invalid save is shown and not loaded) until `^C`; it then disables the mod if it enabled it, leaving the link, which `tm mod remove` unlinks without touching the folder. A data mod is only linked and enabled. `tm mod test [<dir>…] [--json]` (the current folder when none is named) replays each code mod's fixtures, `tests/*.json`, against it in a fresh host run in-process, with a fake broker in place of the server's, and exits 1 if one fails, or if a code mod has none; a data mod is only validated. A fixture is `{name, now, grants, settings, store, files, answers, events, expect}`: `now` (optional, RFC 3339, e.g. `"2026-10-13T12:00:00Z"`; another format fails the fixture) pins the clock: it is the time stamped on every event (`meta.time`), the `now` of a `clock.minute` step whose `data` has none (the minute it falls in) and what the script's `Date` reads, so a test of "done today" doesn't depend on the day it runs; without it the clock is real. `grants` default to all the mod asks for (each must be one it asks for), `settings` overlay the manifest's defaults, `store` and `files` seed the broker's `$.store` and `$.fs`; `answers` are `[{method, params, result | error}]`, the first whose `params` match a call answering it; `events` are `[{name, project, data, wait_ms}]`, sent one at a time, after `mod.ready` (added when the list doesn't start with it); an event the mod's grants wouldn't get, or a name the API lacks, fails the fixture. A step named `tool.call` is an agent's call of one of the mod's tools instead, its `data` `{tool, input, project, role, thread, session}` as the server sends it (a tool the manifest doesn't offer, or a mod not granted `tools`, fails the fixture); a handler's throw is its answer `error: <message>`, and counts among `failures`. `expect` is `{calls, no_calls, logs, failures, store, tools}`: `calls` must be made in that order among others, `no_calls` never, each of `logs` must be part of a `$.log` line, `failures` handlers may throw or reject (0 unless set), `store` must be part of the store at the end (`null` for a key that must be gone), and each of `tools` must be part of the answer to the `tool.call` step of its turn. Params, results and the store match as a subset: an object holds the expected keys, an array has as many matching items, anything else is equal. Unanswered, the broker serves `store.*` and `fs.*` from its own, lists nothing (`projects.list`, `tasks.list`, `actions.list`, `memory.list`), answers `thread.get` and `report.get` null and `actions.available` false, fails `project.get`, `memory.read`, `http.fetch` and `process.run` (a `process.run` the broker would refuse, by `CheckRun` and the argument paths by name in a made-up home, is refused even when answered), and takes the ui, `annotate` and `inbox.raise` calls, all recorded; a method the mod isn't granted is refused as by the broker. A failed fixture prints its problems and the calls the mod made. `tm mod logs <id> [--follow|-f] [--lines|-n N]` prints the last N lines (50) of `logs/mods/<id>.log` and, with `-f`, what is added, across rotations, until `^C`.

**Broker** (`internal/modbroker`, the mods design §4 and §7). A code mod's host does nothing outside its VM itself: each `$` call is a request the server's broker answers (`Server.CallMod`; the supervisor hands each host request to `Server.ModRequest`, which answers it on the host's connection from a goroutine of its own, at most 8 of a mod's calls at once, so a slow call never holds up its events). The broker takes the mod as the registry has it now (read anew at most once a second, so a grant the human takes back applies within it), refuses a mod that isn't enabled, and refuses a method whose grant the mod doesn't run with (granted ∩ requested). Refusals answer `-32010`, a missing project, thread, file or key `-32011`, a failure `-32012`, bad params `-32602`, an unknown method `-32601`. The methods so far, by their wire names; params are an object keyed by the `$` method's parameter names (mod API v1's schema, which the `$` bindings send), and an options object may also come flat:

- no grant: `settings`; `store.get|set|del {key, value}` (any JSON, the whole store at most 1 MiB, in `state/mods/<id>/store.json`); `fs.read {path}` and `fs.write {path, text, append}` in the mod's own folder `state/mods/<id>/` (a relative path is there), at most 8 MiB.
- `project_read`: `projects.list` (`[{slug}]`), `project.get {slug}` (its `project.watch`), `tasks.list {slug}` (`[{id, title, status, steps_done, steps_total, thread, steps, updated, asked}]`, in board order; mod API 1.2 added `steps` (`[{text, done}]`), `updated` (the date it last changed) and `asked` (`delegate`, `accept` or `send-back` while such an inbox item asks the coordinator about it)), `thread.get {project, thread}` (the thread as the watch shows it, `null` when it isn't an open thread) `report.get {project, thread}` (`report.stored`'s shape, `null` when there is none), `thread.changes {project, thread, options: {mode, path, patch}}` (mod API 1.8, or the options flat; `internal/changes`): what the thread's worktree changed, read by tm with git itself, read-only, with fixed arguments (`status --porcelain=v1 -z`, `diff --numstat -z -M` and `--name-status`, `rev-list --count`, `merge-base`, `for-each-ref`; with the worktree's git dirs taken from the repo, never from the worktree (§9, **Git in a worktree**), `GIT_OPTIONAL_LOCKS=0`, no external diff or textconv, no shell, no argument from the mod but the checked path), never a checkout, commit or fetch, so a mod needs no `commands` grant for it. → `{state, mode, branch, base, head, ahead, unpushed, pushed, files: [{path, old, status, add, del, binary, bucket}], totals: {files, add, del}, buckets: {uncommitted, unpushed, pushed}, truncated, patch, patch_truncated}`: `state` `none` (the thread has no repo, its worktree is gone, or it isn't a checkout of the repo by the repo's own records), `clean` or `dirty`; `mode` `base` (the default: tracked and untracked files against the merge base of HEAD and the thread's base) or `uncommitted` (against HEAD); the base is the record's (`origin/main`, or `--base`'s), else origin's default branch, else the repository's checked-out branch (an adopted thread, a repo without origin); `ahead` counts HEAD's commits since it, `unpushed` those `refs/remotes/origin/<branch>` lacks (all of them when it doesn't exist: never `@{u}`, which a tm branch has as `origin/main`), `pushed` whether it exists; a file's `status` is `A`, `M`, `D`, `R` (with `old`), `T` or `?` (untracked, its lines counted by tm reading it: a regular file, up to 8 MiB, binary with a NUL), its `bucket` the newest layer it changed in (`uncommitted` when the status lists it, else `unpushed` when HEAD differs from `origin/<branch>` in it, else `pushed`), and `buckets` split the totals by it. `path` (relative, with slashes) narrows `files` to that file, the totals staying the thread's, and with `patch` adds its unified diff (`diff --no-color -U3`, `--no-index` against nothing for an untracked file) up to 256 KiB; it must stay inside the worktree with its links resolved and is refused in a `.git` folder, a `.env` file, a credential store or `~/.terminatr` outside the worktrees (**Paths and credentials**). Paths are relative to the worktree, and no absolute path is answered; paths and the patch are data, cleaned of control and format characters as widget text is. Bounded as `process.run`: 30 s for the whole call, 1 MiB per git output (`truncated`), at most 2,000 files listed (the totals count all read). Audited as `broker: thread.changes <project> <thread> [mode] [path]`. `memory.list {slug}` (`[{path, title, bytes}]`: `CONTEXT.md`, `MEMORY.md`, then `memory/<name>.md` by title, regular files only, never a link) and `memory.read {slug, path}` (the text of a file `memory.list` names, 1 MB at most; any other path is not found), for the projects the mod runs for only.
- `inbox`: `inbox.raise {project, item: {subject, summary}}`: an item of kind `mod` whose subject starts with `mod:<id> `, both made one line without control characters (80 and 1,000 characters); a mod with 20 unhandled items in a project raises no more until the coordinator handles them. A nudge names it only as `from a mod`. Journaled `mod:<id> inbox.raise <item>`.
- no grant, the mod's own areas only: `ui.cell.set {column, target, spans}` and `ui.status.set {spans}` (**Cells**, below), and `ui.toast {text, level}`: a short message in every dashboard's message line, once, the mod's name in front (`Standup: demo: 3 started`). The text is one line (control characters dropped, at most 200 runes; empty is refused), the level `info` (the default), `warn` or `error` (drawn as an error). The server keeps a toast 10 s, the last 8 over all mods, and sends them with `session.list`'s `mods` (`toasts` `{seq, mod, name, text, level}`); a console shows the ones whose `seq` it hasn't shown, and a dashboard opened later doesn't repeat them.
- `notify`: `ui.notify {notification: {title, text, level, actions: [{id, target: {project, task} | {session, question, option}}]}}` (or the fields flat): an OS notification, see **Notifications** below. Audited; at most 6 a minute per mod, and a mod keeps 5 in the tray.
- `project_read`, annotations: `annotate {target: {project, task | thread}, key, spans}` (`internal/modstore`): badges by a task or thread, kept in the mod's own folder (`state/mods/<id>/annotations.json`), never in TASKS.md. The target must exist and the mod must run for its project; a key is 1 to 32 of `[a-z0-9._-]`; at most 6 spans of 32 characters each (one line, control characters stripped, an unknown role plain), 8 keys per target and 2,000 targets per mod; null or no spans removes. Journaled `mod:<id> annotate <task:T12|thread:id> <key>` when something changed. The task popup, the dashboard details panel and the thread details show each enabled mod's badges (with `project_read`, running for the project) under the title, a line per mod; the consoles read the file and clean it again, as the mod may write it itself.
- `files`: `fs.read` under the manifest's `files.read` and `files.write` roots, `fs.write` under `files.write`.
- `net`: `http.fetch {url, options: {method, headers, body, timeout_ms}}` → `{status, url, headers, body}`: `https://` on port 443 only, no user or password in the URL, a host name (never an address) the manifest lists (`*.example.com` is any name under it), redirects only to such URLs (at most 5), the environment's proxy not used, and the connection only to a public address: the broker looks the name up and connects to the first address that isn't loopback, private, link-local (cloud metadata), shared, multicast or unspecified, so what was checked is what is reached. Bodies up to 4 MiB out, 8 MiB in; 30 s, at most 60.
- `commands`: `process.run {argv, options: {cwd, stdin, timeout_ms}}` → `{code, stdout, stderr, truncated, timed_out}`: `argv[0]` is one of the manifest's program names exactly, looked up in the server's `PATH`; no shell; the environment is only `PATH`, `HOME`, `USER`, the locale, the temp and XDG folders and Windows' system variables, plus `TERM=dumb` and `NO_COLOR=1`, so no token reaches it; `gh` or `glab` runs on its own login, so with the human's access, and takes none of the commands that would show or change that login, its settings or aliases, or add extensions (`auth`, but `auth status` without `-t`/`--show-token`, `config`, `alias`, `extension`); the working folder is the mod's own or one its files grant may read; no argument may name a file or folder the mod couldn't read (`Paths.CheckArgs`: an absolute or `~` path, a relative one leading out of the working folder or through a link, also as an option's value, `--x=`, `-x<path>`, a `key=` value, an `@file`, a `file://` URL or an absolute or `~` path after a colon, `host:/x`), checked as `fs.read` checks a path; `git` takes none of the options that name another folder, repository or program or change a setting (before its command only `--no-pager`, `-p`/`-P`, the pathspec and `--no-*` switches, `--version` and `--help`; nowhere `--git-dir`, `--work-tree`, `--exec-path`, `--config-env`, `--upload-pack`, `--receive-pack`, `--exec` or an abbreviation of one), `git config` only reads, git runs no program it is given (`rebase -x`/`--exec`, `bisect run`, `submodule foreach`, `difftool -x`/`--extcmd`, `grep -O`, `clone -u`, `help -w`; `filter-branch`, `send-email`, `credential` and its helpers, `daemon`, `instaweb` and the server-side commands are refused whole), git sets no config of the repository it makes, nor makes one the mod could write the config of (`clone -c`/`--config`, `--template`, `--separate-git-dir`, `--bare`, `--mirror`; `init --template`, `--separate-git-dir`, `--bare`), nor changes the human's (`maintenance register`, `unregister`, `start`, `stop`), every git runs with `-c core.fsmonitor=false -c core.hooksPath=/dev/null -c core.pager=cat -c protocol.ext.allow=never -c safe.bareRepository=explicit` first, so no repository's config names a program for it and a bare repository the mod wrote isn't one (`modbroker.RunArgv`), and git looks for its repository no higher than the mod's folder or files root the working folder is in (`GIT_CEILING_DIRECTORIES`) (`modbroker.CheckRun`, T385, T408). A commands grant of any program but git and a short list of vetted read-only ones (`jq`, `grep`, `cat`, `ls`, `shellcheck` and the like: `mod.narrowTools`, none with an option that runs a program or writes a file) is a grant to run anything, as the human, and the consent says so, `runs make: anything it wants, as you`; `gh` and `glab` show as `runs glab with your GitLab login: anything it wants, as you`, since the broker refuses only their login, settings, alias and extension commands.
