A commands grant is trust in that program: the broker checks the arguments as written, not what the program makes of them; 30 s, at most 120, then the program and what it started are killed; 1 MiB of each output kept.

**Cells** (`internal/modstore`, `internal/server/modcells.go`; the mods design §4 and §6). Events are pushed, cells are published: a mod sets a value and the consoles draw the latest one, so a mod is never called while a frame is drawn and a slow mod shows a stale cell, never a slow sidebar. `ui.cell.set {column, target, spans}` sets the mod's cell in one of its own manifest columns for a target, one of `{project}`, `{project, task}`, `{project, thread}` or `{session}`; a project's target needs the mod to run for that project, a session's for the session's project. `null` or `[]` spans clear it. `ui.status.set {spans}` sets the mod's one status-bar segment. Spans are `widget.Span`s, `{text, role, icon}` (role one of `accent`, `good`, `warn`, `bad`, `info`, `faint`, `head`; icon a name of the icon set), or a time span (mod API 1.7): `ago` or `until`, an RFC 3339 time drawn in place of `text` with the console's own clock, `ago` as `40s ago`, `until` as `1m20s` and then `due` (`age.Words`, as the ticker's ages), so it moves without a new publish; a span has at most one of `text`, `ago` and `until`, and a time that doesn't parse is refused. They are allowed wherever spans are, in cells, status segments and every widget kind, at most 16, 256 bytes of text in all, control and format characters stripped, a wide glyph (emoji, CJK) drawn as `?` (`widget.CheckSpans`). A mod keeps at most 2,000 cells. The server keeps the latest cell per mod, column and target and sends them with the rows consoles already ask for (protocol 13, added fields only): `session.list`'s `mods` (the enabled code mods' `columns` `{id: "<mod>.<column>", title, width, where}`, the cells of projects, tasks and threads, the `status` segments with the mod's name) and each session's own `cells`, and a project's `project.watch` (`cells`, also what `$.project.get` answers). Slots: `sidebar.project`, `sidebar.coordinator`, `sidebar.thread` (§4, Projects sidebar), `dashboard.task` (NEEDS YOU's task rows) and `dashboard.thread` (the project's thread rows), where a mod's columns follow the state column, as wide as declared, the rest of the row giving way; a thread row's cell is the thread's, else its task's, else its session's. The status bar (§4) shows the segments after everything else, each cut to 24 cells, when there is room; they give way first. The store is written to `cache/mods/cells.json` (a second after a change, and when the server stops) and read back when it starts, checked again, so the last cells show after a restart before the hosts publish anew. A mod turned off or removed loses its cells, and so does a column its manifest no longer declares; while its host has failed its cells and segment don't show.

**Commands** (`internal/mod/commands.go`, `internal/server/modcommands.go`, `internal/tui/modcommands.go`; mods design §2 tier 4, §8, §9). A code mod declares what the human can run in `[[provides.commands]]`: at most 16, each `{id, title, key, menu}` with an `id` of lowercase letters, digits and dashes, named `<mod>:<id>` in messages; a `title` of at most 48 characters; an optional `key` on the dashboard's list (`g`, `G`, `ctrl+g`, `f5`; never esc, ctrl+c, d, q, r, tab, shift+tab, enter, 1-9 or a `prefix+` key, and two of the mod's commands never share one); and `menu = false` to leave it out of the ≡ menu. They need no permission; the consent lists them (`Commands you can run: Relabel (g)`). The human runs one four ways: its key on the dashboard (not over a popup), prefix then its key anywhere (in a session, where the plain key still goes to the program, on the dashboard and over a popup), its entry in the ≡ menu (the dashboard's after the core's entries and the mods' full views, `Relabel · lab` with its key; a session's after the prefix commands, with `prefix+g`), or `tm mod run <id> <command> [--project <slug>]`; each sends `mod.command {mod, command, project, selection, console}` (the human's: an agent is refused; refused too for a mod that isn't an enabled code mod, has failed, doesn't run for the project, or is a user mod in safe mode), and the mod gets `command.run {command, project, selection}`, which starts a host that waits for its first event. From the dashboard the project is the one shown and the selection the selected row (`{project, task, thread, session}`); from a session (`internal/tui/attachcommands.go`) the session's project, the session selected (its thread and task too); a mod limited to other projects isn't offered there. A console's command lets the mod open one of its views on that console, as a key in its view does (`$.ui.view.open`); `tm mod run` doesn't. `mod.command` answers `{after}`, the open requests' sequence then, and a session watches `mod.views` for a few seconds for an open of that mod after it: a full view or a project tab shows over the session (as a prefix command's popup does; esc goes back to it), a dashboard card on the dashboard. Prefix then a command's key over a popup on a session closes it and the session runs the command. The core's keys come first: a key the dashboard or the sidebar has (bound by the keymap), a prefix command's (`a i t , ? n ] [ { } b | ;`, even bound elsewhere), `N`, the prefix and the locked keys stay the core's, so prefix then a command's key never means a core command, and a key two mods bind goes to the one enabled first (`mod.Registry.Commands`). A command that lost its key keeps its menu entry and `tm mod run`, and the Mods tab shows `key conflict: g is lab:relabel's` (or `… is the core's list.down`) for its mod. The help (?) and the project popup's Keys tab list the commands under **Mod commands**, with their keys and `tm mod run` lines.

**Notifications** (`internal/modnotify`, `internal/tui/modnotice.go`; mods design §5 and §7, notify). The server keeps what mods sent in a tray (30 minutes, 20 in all) and lists it in `session.list` (`mod_notices`), only for mods still enabled with the `notify` grant, so a grant taken back empties it at once. A mod's words are data: title (60) and text (240) become one line without control characters, so no escape sequence reaches a terminal. Consoles draw each notification as an OS notification (OSC 777, OSC 9 in iTerm2) titled `terminatr · <id>`, never by the mod's own title, once over all consoles (`notify.claim`, the first caller wins; the dashboard and an attached session both claim), and `N` on the dashboard lists them with their buttons. A button is a core action id on a task of a project the mod runs for, at most 3, from `task.show`, `task.delegate`, `task.accept`, `task.send-back`, `task.coordinator`, or `question.answer` on `{session, question, option}` (mod API 1.3) of a session in such a project, refused unless the mod may bind it (**Action binding**) and the session's open menu asks that question, unanswered and single-select, with that option (the list shows `answer "Blue"`); no session action is a button; its label is the core's (`Accept`, `Send back`, `Answer`…), never the mod's. Pressing it (keys 1-3) runs the core's own key for the action on the task as the board has it now, so the core's precondition check and confirmation (`Accept T12?`) come first and name the task from live state; a press within 300 ms of the list or selection changing is ignored. The notification goes from the tray when pressed or dismissed (`d`, `notify.dismiss`); both calls are the human's (an agent is refused `human-only`). The mod is told nothing and acts on nothing.

**Paths and credentials.** There is no permission for credentials. Whatever its roots, a mod never reaches: the guard's credential stores (`agent.Secrets`: `~/.ssh`, `~/.aws`, `~/.config/gh`, … and each agent's own), the other private places (`modbroker.Keychains`, on macOS, Linux and Windows): keychains and password managers (`~/Library/Keychains`, `/Library/Keychains`, `~/.local/share/keyrings`, `~/.password-store`, 1Password, Bitwarden, KeePassXC and the like, Windows' `Credentials` and `Protect`), browser profiles (Chrome, Chromium, Firefox, Safari, Edge, Brave, Arc, Vivaldi), shell and REPL histories (`~/.bash_history`, `~/.zsh_history`, fish's, PowerShell's `PSReadLine`), package-manager credentials (`~/.npmrc`, `~/.yarnrc.yml`, `~/.m2/settings.xml`, `~/.gradle/gradle.properties`, `~/.pypirc`, the cargo, gem, terraform, NuGet and Composer files, `~/.docker/config.json`, `~/.kube/config`), cloud CLIs' folders (`~/.aws`, `~/.azure`, `~/.config/gcloud`, and `doctl`, `hcloud`, `heroku` and the like), and other tools' token files (`~/.config/glab-cli`, `~/.config/hub`, `~/.vault-token`, `~/.pgpass`); each group has a test (`TestKeychainGroups`, `TestPathsCheckPrivate`), a `.env`, `.env.*` or `.envrc` file, anything in a `.git` folder, and `~/.terminatr` outside its own folder. A path is checked by name first (`~/` is the home folder, `..` is cleaned), so nothing outside the roots is looked at on disk, then again with its links resolved; a link whose target is missing is refused, since writing through it would create the target. On Windows, network paths, data streams, names ending in a dot or space and 8.3 short names are refused. Names compare without case where the file system does. The check and the open are two steps; a mod can't make links, so only another process could move a file between them. The path and URL checks have fuzz tests (`FuzzPathsCheck`, `FuzzCheckURL`).

**Audit.** What a call changes in a project is journaled under the mod's caller, `mod:<id>` (`caller.Mod`, §11.1). Every call outside tm (`fs.write` outside the mod's own folder, `http.fetch`, `process.run`), every `thread.changes` and every refusal is a line in the mod's log, `broker: …`.

**Action binding** (`internal/modaction`, the mods design §11). Mods render, the core acts: a mod never runs an action, and there is no `$.actions.run`. A row of a mod's list or table may name core actions, `actions: [{id, target}]`; a keys widget may too (mod API 1.4), for actions no row stands for, bound in the whole view whatever row the cursor is on (a row's own action on the same key comes first); a notification button names one the same way. The bindable actions of mod API v1, with the target each takes: `task.show`, `task.delegate`, `task.accept`, `task.send-back` on `{project, task: "T12"}`; `task.new` (mod API 1.4: the core's new-task menu and title prompt; it asks nothing more, the human types the title), `task.coordinator` and `project.activate` on `{project}` (a task field ignored); `session.attach` and `session.clear` (key `C`, asks) on `{session}`; `question.answer` (key `a`, asks) on `{session, question, option}`, the text of a question the session's open question menu asks and the label of one of its options (mod API 1.3); `memory.edit` (key `e`) and `memory.rm` (key `d`, asks) on `{project, path: "memory/<name>.md"}`, a note of the project's memory folder, and `memory.new` (key `n`) on `{project}` (mod API 1.9: the console runs `tm memory edit` or `new` on its terminal, the human's editor in it, and comes back when it exits; `memory.rm` removes the note as `tm memory rm` does, §7.6). Settings, merges, the guard, repos and library deletes are not bindable. A published tree that names any other id, the same id twice in a row, a target field other than `project`, `task`, `thread`, `session`, `question`, `option` and `path` (each at most 256 bytes, a question 1,024, no control characters), or a target of the wrong shape is refused. Each action's label, key, whether it asks and its precondition are the core's: `$.actions.list` (no grant) returns `[{id, label, key, on, confirm, precondition, consent}]`, the key being the default (a console shows its keymap's). A binding is in a mod's reach only with `project_read` and a target in a project the mod runs for (a session's own project; a session outside the projects is out of reach). `session.clear` and `question.answer` act on an agent's conversation, so they also need a grant of their own (`consent` set): the mod lists them under `[permissions] actions` and the human grants `actions` (the consent: `offers you keys and buttons that clear an agent session's conversation (session.clear); …; tm asks you before each one runs`); without it a view that binds one is refused at `ui.view.set`, a button at `ui.notify`, `$.actions.available` and a press at `mod.action.check`. `$.actions.available {id, target}` answers whether the precondition holds now, to grey a row out; out of reach it is refused (a session not running and one out of reach both answer false), so it can't be used to read a board or list sessions. In a view the key is the console's: the key of a bound action runs the action the row under the cursor binds and never reaches the mod as `ui.key`; the keys of actions that change state (D, A, x, space, C, a, d, or what the keymap binds them to) are never the mod's on any row, and a keys widget's hint for one is not drawn; a navigation's key (enter) stays the mod's on a row that binds none. Such a key on a row that doesn't bind its action, but binds one on a task, says why it isn't available on that task from the live board (`not available: T2 is started: a thread already works on it`), and runs nothing. The hint for the cursor's row is drawn in the core's words (`D delegate · enter show`). A press counts only when the row's bindings and text, which row the cursor is on and its place, had been unchanged for 300 ms; a publish that changes them restarts the wait (the human's own moves don't, nor the console putting the cursor on a task picked elsewhere); an action of the whole view waits on its own bindings only. A view's first tree counts as still from the start, so the first press after a view opens doesn't wait. An early press says `the row just changed` and does nothing. On a press the console first asks the server whether the binding is in the mod's reach (`mod.action.check {mod, action, target}`, human-only, on the human socket only; out of reach, it says `not available` and the mod hears nothing; in reach, it answers a one-time press token, `{token}`), then loads the project's board and checks the precondition with the same function `$.actions.available` uses (and the Tasks tab's keys), refusing a stale or lying row with `not available: <why>`; then it runs the core's own code path, as the human, with the core-drawn confirmation for every action that asks (delegate, accept, activate, and deactivate even with nothing running; send back asks for the note), naming the target from the live board, never the mod's text. `session.clear` asks `Clear demo t-0003's conversation? …`; `y` calls `session.clear {id}` (human-only, journaled `session.clear s-4`), which pastes the agent's clear prompt (`[inject] clear`, `/clear` for Claude) as a plain prompt once the agent is idle, dropped if the session ended meanwhile, and is refused for an agent without one. `question.answer` checks the live menu (the question asked, unanswered, single-select; the option one of its labels exactly; a question where several may be picked is answered in the session), asks `Answer demo t-0003's question "Which port?" with "8080"? It goes to the agent as your answer.`, and `y` calls `session.answer {id, index, answer, question}` with the question's text, which the server checks again as it answers (refused once the menu asks another, the question is answered, or the option isn't one of its labels), journaled `session.answer s-4 Which port? → 8080`. A mod never answers or clears anything itself: only the human's stable press and `y` in tm's confirmation do. Asking the coordinator checks the task again as it writes the item, and a second ask on a task that waits on the coordinator drops nothing. Once an action ran (`ok` true, or false when it failed or was refused), the console calls `mod.action.done {mod, action, target, ok, token}`, human-only, with the token its check gave for this press; the server refuses a done without one, with a token used before, older than 30 minutes, given to another console (peer process) or for another binding, so a done follows a check of that very press and one check makes at most one done. Checking the binding's reach again, it queues `action.done {action, target, ok}` for that mod alone. A press the human answers `n` to sends nothing.

---

## 6. Tasks

Tasks follow tsk's model: a small, fixed set of statuses, short ids, flat steps, idempotent commands with clear exit codes, and plain or `--json` output. The human, the coordinator and threads all use the same commands. tsk's 50k lines of TUI polish are not copied, and neither is its global JSON store.

### 6.1 Model

| Field | Notes |
|---|---|
| `id` | `T<n>`, per project, assigned when created, never reused (the counter is in the `TASKS.md` front matter) |
| `title` | One line |
| `status` | `open` (captured) · `ready` (on deck) · `started` (in motion) · `blocked` (waiting on something) · `review` (finished, waiting for the human) · `done` |
| `notes` | Free markdown, optional. It becomes the core of a thread's task text when the task is delegated |
| `steps` | A flat, ordered checklist: `- [ ]` / `- [x]`. Steps are numbered 1… by position. Ticking a step never changes the status |
| `thread` | The thread running the task, e.g. `t-0003` (optional; set by delegation) |
| `owner` | Optional: `me` or an agent name, so the coordinator knows who is meant to do it |
| `created`, `updated` | Dates |
| archived | Archived tasks move to `tasks/ARCHIVE.md`, which keeps `TASKS.md` short enough for every agent's context |

Board groups, derived only from status:
- **NEEDS YOU** = `blocked` + `review`
- **IN MOTION** = `started`
- **ON DECK** = `ready`
- **BACKLOG** = `open` (captured, for later; wording only, the status stays `open`). It is not on deck: the counts (`N on deck`) and the lists of tasks on deck leave it out, and the task lists show it after ON DECK, before DONE
- **DONE** = `done` (until archived)

### 6.2 `TASKS.md` format

`tm` rewrites the whole file in a canonical order: groups, then id. It stays readable as plain markdown:

```markdown
+++
next_id = 13
+++
# Tasks

## Needs you

### T7 Pick a licence for the repo
status: review · owner: me · thread: t-0002 · updated: 2026-10-04

MIT or Apache-2.0? See t-0002's report.

## In motion

### T12 Fix login redirect after OAuth
status: started · owner: claude · thread: t-0005 · updated: 2026-10-04

Users land on /home instead of the page they came from.

- [x] Reproduce with a test
- [ ] Fix the redirect
- [ ] Open a PR

## On deck

### T9 Add tm doctor
status: ready · updated: 2026-10-03

## Done

### T3 Bootstrap repo and write the spec
status: done · thread: t-0002 · updated: 2026-10-04
```

The parser keys on `### T<n> <title>` headings and on the `status:` line straight after them. The rest is the notes, except a trailing run of checklist items, which are the steps.

### 6.3 CLI

The project defaults to `$TERMINATR_PROJECT`, or to the project whose folder or thread worktree contains the cwd. `--project <slug>` overrides it.

```sh
tm task add "Fix login redirect after OAuth" --notes "Users land on /home…" --step "Reproduce" --step "Fix"
tm task add "…" [--notes-file f.md] [--status ready] [--owner me]   # status: open (default) or any other; owner: me or an agent
tm task add "crash on start" --template bug   # a mod's task template (§5.3): "Bug: crash on start", its notes, its steps then --step's
tm task add --json < plan.json            # bulk: [{"title":…,"notes":…,"steps":[…],"status":"ready","owner":…}]
tm task list                              # grouped board, plain text
tm task list --needs-you --json           # machine-readable
tm task list --status started,blocked
tm task list --archived                   # the archive (tasks/ARCHIVE.md) instead of the board
tm task show T12 [--json]
tm task help                              # every task command
tm task status T12 started                # also: open ready blocked review done
tm task status T12 done --approved-by-user  # the coordinator, once the user accepted the work (§6.4)
tm task status T12 blocked --note "waiting for API key"
tm task edit T12 [--title "…"] [--notes "…" | --notes-file f.md] [--owner O]
tm task steps T12 add "Open a PR"
tm task steps T12 check 2                 # idempotent; also uncheck, rename N "…", remove N
tm task archive T12 | tm task unarchive T12
tm task delegate T12 [--agent claude] [--model M] [--repo PATH] [--base B] [--approved-by-user] [--over-cap]
                                          # = tm thread start --task T12 (§6.5, §9)
```

- **Output.** Plain text by default, one task per line in `list`:

  ```
  T12  started  Fix login redirect after OAuth   t-0005 working 40% "Testing"  2/3
  ```

  `--json` prints stable objects: `{"id","title","status","notes","steps":[{"n","text","done"}],"thread","owner","created","updated"}`, plus `"thread_state"` when the task has a thread and the server is reachable.
- **Exit codes** (tsk's contract):

  | Code | Meaning |
  |---|---|
  | 0 | done, or already true (idempotent) |
  | 1 | refused: some items failed, valid ones were saved; stable error codes such as `unknown-task`, `human-only`, `invalid-status`, `empty-title` |
  | 2 | usage error; nothing saved |
  | 3 | I/O or server error; check with `list` and retry what is missing |

- **Idempotency.** `add` with `--json` reports `created`/`existing` per title, matching on exact title among open tasks. `status`, `edit`, `check`/`uncheck`, `archive` and `unarchive` are idempotent.
- **Change from tsk:** tsk's step `toggle` is not idempotent, so `tm` uses `check`/`uncheck` instead.

### 6.4 Who may change tasks

| Caller | May |
|---|---|
| Human | everything, including setting `done` |
| Coordinator | everything: add, edit, set `open`/`ready`/`started`/`blocked`/`review`, steps, archive, delegate; `done` only with `--approved-by-user` |
| Thread | read every task; on **its own task** only: `tm task steps add` (to write down its plan, §6.5) and `check`/`uncheck`; nothing else |

- **Only the human accepts work.** This is tsk's rule, because "done" is the human's acceptance of the work and the one signal the coordinator must never fake. Since the user talks only to the coordinator (§4), the coordinator relays it.
  - **Standing acceptance** (`complete_tasks`, §11.2). The user may accept in advance, per project: with `merged`, the ticker (caller `ticker`, the human's rights) marks a task in `review` done once its thread's PR merged (§7.5). (An old `released` setting reads as `user`, and `tm doctor` warns until the user picks again.) Tasks without a merged thread PR, and tasks owned by `me`, are never completed this way. The user can take it back: `x` on a done task sends it back, and the coordinator reopens it. The default, `user`, leaves every completion to the user's word.
  - The server enforces it by caller (§11.1). An agent call to `tm task status T12 done` exits 1 with `human-only` and says how to relay the user's decision.
  - Once the user says the work is accepted ("mark T12 done"), the coordinator runs `tm task status T12 done --approved-by-user`. It is journaled as `coordinator task.status T12 done (approved by the user)`, as `tm thread start --approved-by-user` is. Threads can't set `done` at all.
- **Threads don't change task status or notes.** Adding and ticking steps on their own task is the one exception to "only the coordinator writes project state". It keeps a thread's plan on disk, and it is journaled.
- **Threads change nothing else.** A thread call to any other task command exits 1 with `coordinator-only`. The coordinator, which is the only agent writer of project state (§5.2), moves the task after reading the thread's report.
- A human may make any change, either from a shell outside terminatr or from a `shell` session inside it.

### 6.5 Tasks and threads

- **Delegation.** `tm task delegate T12` starts a thread with the task's title, notes and steps as its task text. It sets `thread: t-…`, and moves the task to `started` if it was `open` or `ready`. Both changes are made on the coordinator's call, so they are coordinator writes. A task has at most one live thread; delegating again refuses while that thread is unresolved. Like `tm thread start`, it refuses at the project's parallel threads cap, or at the cap across projects (`over-global-cap`), unless `--over-cap`, which the coordinator adds only on the user's word (§9).
- **Status flowing back.** Nothing a thread does changes the task's status. `tm done` and new reports become inbox items (§7.5), and the coordinator then sets `review`, `blocked` or `ready` itself. The dashboard and `tm task list` show the thread's live state (working/blocked/idle, percent, activity, report waiting) next to the task, but don't copy it into `TASKS.md`.
- **Plan as steps.** A thread works through its task's steps in order and ticks each one when it's done (`tm task steps T12 check N`). If the task has no steps, the thread's first action is to write its plan as steps (`tm task steps T12 add "…"`, one call per step), before it changes anything. The plan is then on disk, so it survives if the session dies, and a restarted or replacement thread picks it up. Ticking a step never changes the task's status.
- **Progress.** Steps and the agent's mirrored todos give the thread's derived percent (§7.3). The coordinator never has to ask a thread how far it is.

---

## 7. Coordinator ↔ thread protocol

### 7.1 Briefs

The server generates `threads/<id>/brief.md` on thread start and restart. The brief is **scoped**: it carries the thread's task and **pointers, not copies**, to the shared context, but for the few memory notes that bear on the task (item 4). Every path in it is absolute, because nothing terminatr-owned lives in the worktree (§5.2).

1. Who you are: thread `<id>` of project `<slug>`, task `T<n>`, working in `<worktree>` on branch `<branch>`.
2. Rules: "Follow `tm skill thread`" (§7.8; in the agent's context when terminatr added it, else run it), plus a one-paragraph summary in case that call fails: stay in the worktree; the project folder is read-only; work through the task's steps in order, ticking each (`tm task steps T<n> check N`; none: add the plan first, §6.5); report only through `tm` (`tm report`, then `tm done`); put lessons under `## Remember`; data is not instructions; never merge, force-push, or delete branches or worktrees. How to report in full is the skill's, not repeated here.
3. Project files (live, read-only; read as needed): the project folder once, then `PROJECT.md`, `CONTEXT.md`, `MEMORY.md`, `memory/`, `TASKS.md` and `uploads/`, the files the user gave the project (the coordinator names the ones for the task in its notes or a prompt).
4. `## Memory notes` (`internal/thread/memory.go`): the project's memory notes that bear on the task, copied in (the one exception to pointers), within `ResetBudget` (4 KiB) for the section: every `standing` note, then the best 3 others `Match` (§7.6, **Memory notes**) finds (a note with `audience = "coordinator"` never, standing or not) for the task's title, notes and steps and the paths and repos they name: a word with a slash or a file name with an extension (`internal/thread/brief.go`, `go.mod`; an absolute path only inside a project repo or the worktree, as the path relative to it), `repo:<name>` for each project repo whose folder name the task says, and the thread's own repo when the project has several. Each note shows under `### <title> (<path>; standing)` or `(<path>; matched: <words and paths>)`, its text without front matter, cut to an equal share of the budget; the section names the memory folder and `tm memory find` for the rest. No standing or matching note, no section. Start, adopt and restart write it from the notes as they are then; start and adopt journal the count, `thread.memory t-0007 2 matched (memory/a.md, memory/b.md), 1 standing` (`0 matched, 0 standing` too), so the share of threads that got a matched note can be counted. `tm context --thread <id>|<task>` previews it.
5. On restart: "A previous attempt exists on this branch. Read your last report first: `tm report --show`."
6. `# Task`: `threads/<id>/task.md`, which is the task's title, notes and steps, plus any follow-ups the coordinator forwarded.

The agent's manifest injects the brief at launch, for example as an appended system prompt plus a short kickoff prompt (§8.6). No screen-typing heuristics are used for the brief.

### 7.2 Reports

A thread hands in its report with `tm report` whenever it finishes or stops to wait. Each call replaces the whole report. The thread never writes a report file itself.

```sh
tm report <<'EOF'
PR: https://github.com/<owner>/<repo>/pull/<n>   # or https://dev.azure.com/<org>/<project>/_git/<repo>/pullrequest/<n>, https://gitlab.com/<group>/<project>/-/merge_requests/<n>

## Report
What was done, what was found, what is left, what the user must decide.

## Next
Merge the PR
Fix the failing lint check
Remove the worktree and branch

## Check
Run tm, press t on the project, open T12: the row says why.

## Remember
- Short durable lessons the coordinator may move into memory.
EOF
tm report --file /tmp/report.md --attach build/screenshot.png   # report from a file; attach files for the user
tm report --show                                                # print the current report (after a restart, say)
```

The format is herdr-projects': an optional `PR:` first line (a GitHub PR URL, an Azure DevOps one, `dev.azure.com/…/_git/<repo>/pullrequest/<n>` or the `visualstudio.com` form, or a GitLab merge request's, `…/<project>/-/merge_requests/<n>`), `## Report`, a required `## Next`, an optional `## Check` and an optional `## Remember`.

- `## Check` says in a few lines how the user can check the work (what to run, where to look). The task list shows it on the task while it waits in `review` (§4, **A task, shown**). Before storing a valid report, `tm report` runs the memory check on the thread's diff (§7.6, **Memory notes**) and adds a `Memory check: <what it flags>` line for each conflict to the end of `## Check` (a `## Check` it adds when the report has none, before `## Remember`), leaving out a line the report already holds, and prints them after `stored report <n>`; it never refuses a report for them, and a report the lines would take over 64 KB is stored without them.

- `## Next` holds one imperative action per line, at most 100 characters each. The coordinator can send a line back to the thread as its next prompt (`tm thread prompt <id> --next N`), for example when the user picks one.
- **Validation is synchronous.** The server checks the format before storing anything. A malformed report exits 1 with the reason (`missing ## Next`, `line 7 over 100 chars`, `bad PR line`), so the thread fixes it right away instead of the coordinator finding out later.
- **Storage.** The server stores the report as `threads/<id>/REPORT.md`, moves the previous one to `threads/<id>/reports/<n>.md`, and copies `--attach` files into `threads/<id>/library/` (a file whose name isn't one line of plain text is refused, `invalid-attachment`). It reads attachments with its own permissions, so the thread needs no write access to the project folder; for that reason an agent's attachments, like every file an agent's forwarded command names (`--file`, `--notes-file`), open only in the thread's worktree, a coordinator's project folder and its threads' worktrees, or the agent's own temp folder, where its sandbox has it write (its manifest's `[guard] temp`: Claude's `$CLAUDE_CODE_TMPDIR/claude-<uid>`, else `/tmp/claude-<uid>`; Codex's inherited `$TMPDIR`, else `/tmp`; a user manifest's own), less its subfolders the agent's sandbox keeps from it (`[guard] temp_private`: Claude's `bash-edit-diff`) where Claude's temp folders are each per-user `[guard] temp` entry that resolves and are shared by all the user's Claude sessions (`[guard] temp_scratch`), so an agent's file there must be a regular file loose in the temp folder itself (Claude sets a thread's TMPDIR to it, so what it writes lands there; one level, owned by the user, one hard link, no symlink; with its own temp folder, §5.2, that temp folder is the thread's alone, in it, and the shared one no root) or in its own scratch folder (`<temp>/<its worktree, or its project folder for a coordinator, with every non-alphanumeric character a -; a Windows path starts C-->`, its Claude Code scratchpad), never in another working folder's (an encoded folder of the temp folder), compared exactly (a case variant is another folder), through no symlink at all (checked on the open file: its path as the kernel has it), the temp folder and the scratch folder themselves real folders of the user, not links the agent planted (`/tmp` above them is resolved, they are not), and none for an adopted thread or one in a main checkout, whose scratch folder the user's own sessions share (the refusal says so; the agent is the thread's record's when the session list doesn't name it, T586), not every temp folder, which holds files its sandbox keeps from it; an agent whose session, agent or temp folder the server can't tell gets no temp folder; in the server a caller with no session is told by its thread's record, else gets no temp folder; a mod's call, and a command that runs in the agent's own process (no server, so its sandbox opens the file), get `os.TempDir()`, never through a `..`, link or symlink out of them (`outside-roots` otherwise, T310; its message lists the roots the agent may name); a thread's worktree is no root when it is the home folder or holds it, and the file must be a regular one (opened non-blocking, so a FIFO can't hold the server) with a single hard link, outside every credential store (`agent.Secrets`; `invalid-file` otherwise), so the server can't be made to copy a file the agent's sandbox keeps from it. It then adds an inbox item (§7.5). The attachments are for the user: `tm thread show` lists their names (`attached:`, `attachments` in `--json`), the coordinator points the user at them by name, and the info panel (§4) lists their names, never a path. The project popup's Library tab (§4) lists, reads and deletes every thread's attachments, and `tm library list` / `tm library rm` (§10) do the same from the CLI.
- **Memory stays with the coordinator.** `## Remember` lines are suggestions. Only the coordinator moves them into `MEMORY.md`. A report that has them raises a `remember` item (§7.5) until the coordinator files or dismisses them. Validation takes each `## Remember` line without its list bullet (`- `, `* `), as for `## Next`.

### 7.3 Progress, status and done

The coordinator learns a thread's exact progress without asking it. Three sources feed it, and the first two need no effort from the agent:

1. **Task steps.** These are the durable plan, kept in `TASKS.md` (§6.5).
2. **Mirrored todos.** This is the agent's own live todo list: Claude Code's `TaskCreate`/`TaskUpdate`, Codex's plan updates, or whatever a manifest maps (§8.2). Hooks capture every change, either the whole list or a one-item diff, and the server keeps the list. Where the agent keeps its own copy on disk, the server re-reads it to heal the mirror. The agent does nothing extra.
3. **Self-report.** `tm status --percent 40 --activity "Testing" [--needs-you "question"] | --unknown`. This is the fallback when neither of the first two exists. A self-report expires after 5 minutes.

**Derived percent.** Let *S* = the number of task steps and *s* = checked steps. Let *T* = the number of todo items and *c* = completed todos.

| The thread has | Percent | Source label |
|---|---|---|
| steps and todos | (*s* + *c*/*T*) / *S* | `steps+todos` |
| steps only | *s* / *S* | `steps` |
| todos only | *c* / *T* | `todos` |
| neither | the self-reported percent | `self` |

- **Steps set the scale; todos fill in the current step.** Agents often keep one todo list for the whole job rather than per step, so this can overstate progress, but by at most one step's worth. It never jumps back when the agent starts a new todo list.
- The result is rounded down to a multiple of 5 and capped at 95 % until `tm done`.
- **done/total counts the list that sets the scale:** steps when the task has any, else todos. With 2 of 4 steps checked and 1 of 3 todos done, every view shows `55% 2/4`; step and todo counts are never added together.
- **Every view shows the same value.** The dashboard row, the details panel, the attach status bar and `tm` all show what `STATUS.md` holds, derived by this one rule. A session that is not a thread (a coordinator, an agent in a shell) has only its todos and uses the todos row; with no `tm done`, it reaches 100 % once every todo is done.
- An in-progress todo counts as not done.
- **The current item** is the first `in_progress` todo; if there is none, it's the first unchecked step.
- **The self-report's other fields still count when derived progress exists.** `--needs-you` (or the activity `Waiting for you`) is the explicit "blocked on the human" signal, which harness hooks can't express for a question asked in plain text. The activity text is shown only when there are no todos or steps.

**`threads/<id>/STATUS.md`** is written by the server only. It is rewritten on every todo change, step change or `tm status`:

```markdown
+++
percent = 45
percent_source = "steps+todos"
current = "Fix the redirect"
steps_done = 1
steps_total = 3
todos_done = 2
todos_total = 4
activity = "Testing"            # last self-report, if any
needs_you = ""
updated = 2026-10-04T12:00:00Z
+++
## Todos
- [x] Write a failing test
- [x] Find where the redirect URL is dropped
- [~] Fix the redirect           (in progress)
- [ ] Run the full suite
```

Todo updates don't create inbox items (there would be too many). They show up in `tm context` and on the dashboard.

**Done.** `tm done ["summary"]` refuses unless a valid report has been stored since the thread's last prompt, because the report is what the coordinator reviews. It sets the percent to 100 and raises no inbox item: the report's own item says the thread handed in. `tm report` marks the thread's older unhandled `report` items done, so each thread has one `report` item, the latest. The task's status is left to the coordinator (§6.5).

### 7.4 Thread state

The dashboard and `tm context` show one merged line per thread. It combines:

- the agent state (§8.4): working, blocked, idle or exited;
- progress (§7.3): the derived percent with its source, done/total, and the current todo or step;
- the report status: none, new (unacknowledged) or acknowledged;
- the PR state: what the ticker last saw (§7.5), in fixed words (`#12 open, checks pass, approved`; `2 checks failed`, `checks pending`, `changes requested`, `review required`; `#12 merged`, `#12 closed`), else the report's PR URL, else nothing.

For example:

```
T12 (t-0005) Fix login redirect   working  45% steps+todos  1/3 steps · 2/4 todos  ▸ Fix the redirect   report: new  PR: #12 open, 1 check failed
```

`tm thread list --json` gives the ticker's fields as `pr_state` (number, url, state, checks, failed, review) beside the report's `pr`.

Threads are grouped as herdr-projects does: Waiting on you → Ready for review → Working → Idle → Resolved.

### 7.5 Inbox and ticker

- The ticker is part of the server. It is an event loop plus a 15 s sweep. It turns changes into `inbox/<ts>-<kind>-<subject>.md` items. Each item has TOML front matter (`id`, `kind`, `subject`, `created`, `summary`, `needs_user`) and no body text taken from untrusted sources. Changes that produce items:
  - a thread becomes blocked, or goes idle with an unacknowledged report
  - a thread stores a report (`tm report`) or calls `tm done`
  - a PR's state changes (the host CLI, `gh pr view --json` or an `az rest` GET of the PR, every `pr_poll_seconds`, 2 minutes by default, fixed fields only)
  - a process exits
  - a server restart
  - the user types into a thread's pane (§4)
  - the user asks, from the task list or the dashboard, to delegate, accept, send back or adopt
  - a task completed by the project's complete-tasks setting
  - auto-close holds a thread back for its unpushed work
  - a new report has `## Remember` lines not yet filed
- Rows: the dashboard's Inbox tab and the /tm pane list a project's items as rows, one per kind per subject: the latest item with `×N` when N are unhandled, so a thread's repeated `idle` or `blocked` items read as one. A row leads with the kind, in words (`PR opened`, `checks failed`, `resolved`; the TUI's `kindWord` and the mod's `kindWord` agree) and in its own color (red for what went wrong, green for what finished, yellow for the rest), then the task's ref, what happened, and last the task's title, which is what a narrow row cuts. The parts come from the summary (`project.Parts`, sent in `tm watch --project --json` as `task`, `what`, `title`, `count`); a summary without a thread label is all `what`. `tm inbox list` still lists every item.
- Kinds: `report`, `thread-resolved` and `needs-you` come from the `tm` commands, `takeover` from the attach client (the first input into a thread's pane during an attach, §4; from a paired server's console, raised by this server, §16.12), `delegate`, `accept` and `send-back` from the task list (`D`, the user's go-ahead to delegate the task named in its subject; `A`, the user's acceptance of it; `x`, the user sending it back, the note in its summary; §4), `adopt` from the dashboard's `T` (the user asks to adopt the session in its subject, §4), `memory` from `tm memory new`, `edit` and `rm` and the Memory tab's keys when the human made the change (subject the note, `memory/x.md`; summary `the user added memory/x.md: read it`, `the user edited memory/x.md: read it again before you change it` or `the user removed memory/x.md: don't write its content back`; one per note and change while unhandled; §7.6, **Memory notes**); the ticker adds `blocked` (`needs_user` unless it is a permission prompt the coordinator may approve; on a question its summary says to ask the user and relay the answer with `tm thread answer`), `idle` (once per report, and not while that report's own item is unhandled), `exited`, `server-restart`, and `pr-opened`, `pr-checks-failed`, `pr-review` (approved or changes requested), `pr-merged`, `pr-closed`, `pr-conflict` (main moved and a thread's open PR now conflicts with it; one that is only behind raises nothing), `task-done` (a task completed by `complete_tasks`), `close-held` (auto-close kept a thread open for its uncommitted or unpushed work, once per reason, below), `gh-failing` (`needs_user`: `gh` failed on 3 PR polls of the project in a row), `slot-free` (a thread slot freed for a coordinator a cap refused, §9), `prompt-waiting` (a prompt for a thread has waited 10 minutes in its session's queue, once per prompt, while a turn that never ends holds it: its summary gives the wait, why (`its agent is working`, or the hold) and `tm thread prompt <id> --now "…"`, which steers a prompt in and drops the queued copy), and `remember` (a new report has `## Remember` lines: one item per report that has them, its summary in fixed words with their count, `T12 Fix it (t-0003)'s report 2 has 2 lessons under ## Remember, not yet filed: put what lasts in memory as your own summary, or dismiss them (tm thread show t-0003), then tm inbox done <id>`; the lessons themselves stay in the report; a newer report's item moves the thread's older one still unhandled to done, since a thread hands in its whole report again; the coordinator files them or dismisses them and marks the item done, so lessons no longer wait unnoticed on a report the coordinator read for its Next); `tm link` adds `link-message` (a linked project's coordinator, or the human for it, wrote: subject the other project, `message` the message's id, a fixed-word summary `demo wrote M-17 (1.1 KB, re M-15, 1 file); read it with tm link read M-17`), `link-held` (`needs_user`: one of this coordinator's messages waits for the user's OK), `link-paused` (`needs_user`, in both projects: the breaker paused a link), §7.9, and `link-undelivered` (a message to a paired server's project was refused there or not taken in 72 hours, the reason in fixed words, §16.11). An item's optional `message` field names the message a link item is about.
- A summary names its thread by its task and title, the thread id in brackets, e.g. `T10 Make needs-you tasks easy to find (t-0003) opened PR #53`, so the coordinator needs no lookup (`thread.Label`: the task's title from `TASKS.md`; a thread without a task by its id and own title, `t-0003 (title)`; one printable line of at most 60 runes).
- What the ticker already reported (per-thread state, PR fields, the default-branch head each open PR was checked against, nudged item ids, each repo's last sync, its last remote control try) is kept in `state/ticker.json`, so a server restart repeats nothing. `TERMINATR_TICK_SWEEP`, `TERMINATR_TICK_PR`, `TERMINATR_TICK_NUDGE`, `TERMINATR_TICK_REMOTE` and `TERMINATR_TICK_REMOTE_GRACE` shorten the intervals for tests.
- **PR polling.** For each unresolved thread with a repo, through the repo's host (below is GitHub's call; Azure DevOps asks the REST API through `az rest`: the PR by number or the repo's PRs by `searchCriteria.sourceRefName`, then for an open PR its policy evaluations (`artifactId` of the project's id and the PR, api-version `7.1-preview.1`) and its statuses at once, and maps the same fields: `completed` is merged, `abandoned` is closed, and a merge is told from the PR's state, so squash and merge commits both work, with or without build-validation policies; the host's merge subject on the default branch, `Merge pull request #N from …` or `Merged PR N: …`, and a default-branch head that already contains a PR's head ("Main moved" below) are only hints, the first for a PR the host can't answer for, the second to ask the PR at once), `gh pr view <report PR URL, else the branch> --json number,url,state,reviewDecision,statusCheckRollup,mergedAt,headRefOid,mergeCommit,baseRefName,mergeable,mergeStateStatus` in the repo, every `pr_poll_seconds` (§11.2; 2 minutes by default, 30 seconds at least), until the PR merged. Only those fields are kept, each checked against a strict pattern. A failed host call (no PR yet, no network) is retried at the next poll. The timers are on screen: `state/ticker.json` keeps when each thread's PR and, per project, any PR and the repos were last checked, and `project.watch` (§3.3) carries them as times, never ages, so a state only changes when the ticker acts: `ticker` (`pr_checked`, `synced`, `pr_poll_seconds`, `gh_failing`) and, per thread, `pr_checked`. The coordinator's info panel, `tm watch --project` and the /tm pane show one line, `ticker · PRs checked 40s ago, next 1m20s · synced 1m ago · PR host ok` (`PR host failing`, in red, while the last PR poll failed; `PR host none` when no repo has a PR host), and a thread's row adds `· checked 30s ago` to its PR (`PR #156 · checks running · checked 30s ago`); readers work out the ages on their own clocks. `tm thread list` and `tm context` read them from `state/ticker.json` (§7.4).
- **No PR host.** A repo with no origin, a local one (a path, `file://`), or one on a well-known host that is neither GitHub nor Azure DevOps (gitlab.com, bitbucket.org, codeberg.org, git.sr.ht, gitea.com, gitee.com) has nothing to poll, unless the project's `code_host` says otherwise (§5.1; for GitLab, a code host mod, §5.3): `codehost.Pick` gives it `NoHost`, whose PR lookup is "nothing to look up" (no CLI call, no poll recorded, never a failed one), `tm doctor` adds no `gh` line for it, and a project whose repos all have none shows `PR host  none` in the ticker section (`no_pr_host` in `project.watch.ticker`, never "failing"). Any other host is taken for GitHub (it may be GitHub Enterprise). The host is picked again after each sync of the repo, so a remote added later is polled from the next poll; a count and `gh-failing` item kept from before are cleared (moved to done) once a project has repos and none has a PR host.
- **PR host failing** (inbox kind `gh-failing`, kept for stored items). A poll (a sweep of a project in which a host CLI was asked anything) counts as failed when every such call failed; "no pull requests found" is a working host, and a CLI that isn't installed counts for nothing (`tm doctor` warns about that). After 3 failed polls in a row (about 6 minutes at the default interval) the ticker raises one `gh-failing` item for the project, `needs_user`, in fixed words (PR follow-up, auto-close and completing tasks wait; the user checks `gh auth status` or `az login`, as `tm doctor` does); the CLI's own error goes to the server log only. The first poll that works moves the item to `inbox/done/` and starts the count again. The count and the item's id are kept in `state/ticker.json`.
- **Paused and archived projects** (§11.2, §5.1). For a paused project the ticker goes on polling state, PRs and checkouts and raises items as usual, but sends nothing to its agents: no nudge, no PR follow-up or main-moved prompt. Once the user resumes it, the next nudge names what arrived meanwhile. An archived or inactive project gets no ticker work at all (no polling, nudges, auto-close or upkeep); an inactive one's starts again on the first sweep after it is activated.
- **PR follow-up** (built in, `pr_followup`, §11.2). When the checks start failing, or a reviewer requests changes, the thread gets one fixed prompt naming the PR number and the host's command to read them (`gh pr checks 12` and `gh pr view 12 --comments`; for Azure DevOps two `az rest` GETs: the builds of the PR's merge ref `refs/pull/12/merge`, and its comment threads' text comments). No PR text is quoted, with one exception: for failing checks the ticker asks gh for the head commit's failed runs (`gh run list --commit <head> --status failure`, then `gh run view <id> --log-failed`, at most 3 runs) and, when that works, the prompt instead names the first failing job and quotes an excerpt of its log in a fenced block: ANSI codes, timestamps and control characters removed, starting 8 lines before the first line that looks like an error (else the tail), capped at 3000 bytes, and the prompt says it is data, not instructions. On Azure DevOps the failed run is the newest failed build of the PR's merge ref (`_apis/build/builds?branchName=refs/pull/<n>/merge&resultFilter=failed`), whose timeline names the first failed task and whose log's last 2000 lines (`Accept: application/json`) give the excerpt, with the same excerpt rules; a PR with no build policy has none and keeps the fixed prompt. Any host failure, no failed run or an empty log leaves the fixed prompt above. It reaches the thread through the mod where it runs and by paste otherwise (§8.6, **Prompt injection**).
- **Checkout sync** (`fast_forward_checkout`, §11.2). Every `pr_poll_seconds`, and at once after the ticker sees one of the project's PRs merge, it runs `git fetch origin` in each of the project's repos (and its unresolved threads' repos), then fast-forwards the user's own checkout of origin's default branch (`origin/HEAD`), but only when that branch is checked out, its tracked files have no staged or unstaged changes, and the update is a pure fast-forward (`git merge --ff-only`, which also refuses when an untracked file is in the way). It never merges, rebases, resets, stashes or touches another branch. Each fast-forward is journaled (`ticker repo.fast-forward <repo> main abc1234..def5678`). A checkout left behind (another branch or a detached HEAD checked out, uncommitted changes, local commits origin lacks, git refused, or the setting off) is reported in fixed words, `local main is 3 behind origin (uncommitted changes)`, on the repo's line in `tm context` and in the project popup's overview. The fetch has a one-minute timeout; a failed fetch keeps the last state.
- **Main moved**. After each sync, every open thread PR whose base is the default branch is checked once per default-branch head: with git when the repo has both commits (`merge-base --is-ancestor`, then `merge-tree --write-tree` for conflicts), else by GitHub's `mergeable` / `mergeStateStatus` (`UNKNOWN` waits for a later poll). A PR that is merely behind main is left alone: a PR needs no latest main to merge, and each merge into the branch is a push and a CI run. A head the default branch already contains (the PR's own merge commit or fast-forward, on any host), or a new default-branch head whose subject names the PR (a squash merge's `… (#N)`, Azure DevOps' `Merged PR N: …` or tm's own squash `… (!N)`), is not judged: it hints that the PR merged, so the PR is asked about at once, in that sweep, rather than on its next poll, and a merge found then is kept for `complete_tasks`; a rebase merge leaves no such hint and waits for the poll, which reads the merge from the PR's state and the host's merge commit id. A PR judged conflicting is asked about again with gh right before the prompt: one that is merged or closed by then, or that gh can't answer for yet, gets none (the latter is retried on the next sweep). A PR that conflicts gets one fixed prompt, when `pr_followup` is on and the thread's session runs: `[tm] main moved to abc1234 (#61 merged), and your PR #59 conflicts with it. Merge origin/main into your branch (no rebase, no force-push), fix the conflicts, rerun the tests, push, and hand in your report again with tm report once CI is green.` The PR number comes from the head commit's subject, by the repo's host (GitHub's `Merge pull request #N` or `… (#N)`, Azure DevOps' `Merged PR N: …`, merge or squash), digits only, else from a thread PR that sweep found merged, and is left out when there is neither. A conflict also raises a `pr-conflict` item for the coordinator, which says whether the thread was prompted. Resolved threads are never prompted.
- **PR prompts go stale**. Every prompt the ticker sends about a PR (failed checks, requested changes, conflict) can wait in the session's queue behind a busy agent. Right before it is delivered the PR is asked about again with gh: one that is merged or closed gets nothing, and the drop is journaled (`ticker prompt.dropped t-0055 t-0055: #122 is merged`). A PR gh can't answer for counts as open.
- **Auto-close** (`auto_close`, `auto_close_days`, §11.2, §9). Once a thread is due and its agent is idle, exited or stopped, the ticker runs `tm thread resolve` as caller `ticker`, once; resolve's own rules apply (never forced, the branch deleted only when its PR merged or the default branch has its commits). Before that it checks the worktree: with uncommitted changes or unpushed commits the thread stays open, and a `close-held` item (once per reason) tells the coordinator; it closes on a later sweep once the work is committed and pushed.
- **Completing tasks** (`complete_tasks`, §11.2, §6.4). After each checkout sync, when the setting is `merged`, every task in `review` that names a thread, isn't owned by `me`, and whose thread's PR merged is completed. The ticker keeps each thread PR's merge commit in `state/ticker.json` past the thread's auto-close; for one merged before that, or one the host can't answer for, it reads the PR from the thread's report and finds the host's merge subject on the default branch (`Merge pull request #N from …` on GitHub, `Merged PR N: …` on Azure DevOps). A squash or rebase merge (or a custom message) leaves no such commit: for a resolved thread the ticker then asks the host about the report's PR, once it is merged from the thread's branch takes the host's merge commit id, and keeps it. The task is set `done` with a note (`done (2026-10-05): merged (PR #65), by the project's setting`), journaled as `ticker task.done T12 merged (PR #65)`, and a `task-done` item tells the coordinator. Each task is completed once per merge commit: one the user sent back stays open until a new PR of it ships.
- **Keeping remote control on** (`coordinator_remote_control`, §11.2). While the setting is on, every sweep looks at the project's running coordinator; when its remote control reads off (after a start, a resume, a server restart, or when it dropped), the ticker turns it on the same way `tm project remote on` does (`session.remote`: the manifest's in-session text, `/remote-control <slug>` for Claude; else a resume with the flag). Only when the coordinator is idle with no prompt queued, once it has read off for a minute in the same process (an agent that starts or resumes with it reads off until it has connected), and at most once every 10 minutes per coordinator. Each try is journaled (`ticker remote.on <slug> s-4 prompted`). The user's own off (`prefix+r`, `tm project remote off`) holds: the server keeps it on the session's record (`remote_held` in `sessions.json` and `session.list`), also across a resume or a server restart, and the ticker leaves that coordinator alone until it is started anew; an on lifts it. With the setting off the ticker does nothing. *Signal:* the session's remote control as `session.list` reports it: the agent's own word when the manifest names a `status_field` (Claude's session file, `bridgeSessionId`), else tm's record of the last start or toggle. Limits: an agent without a `status_field` can't show a drop, so only a coordinator that tm itself has off is turned on; a disconnect made inside the agent (Claude's own `/remote-control` menu) reads as a drop and is turned back on; a try the agent ignores is retried after 10 minutes.
- **Auto-clear** (`auto_clear`, §11.2; off by default). While the setting is on, every sweep looks at the project's running coordinator and clears its conversation once its context (`session.list` `context` / `context_window`, which the mod reports per turn from the session's last request, never the turn's summed usage) reaches `[ui] context_hint` (40% by default; 0 turns auto-clear off too). Only when nothing waits on it: the coordinator is idle, with no prompt queued and no question menu open; its inbox is empty; no thread session has a question menu open or is blocked; and no unresolved thread's STATUS.md has a needs-you question. Every condition must hold, sweep after sweep, for 2 minutes in the same process (so a reply to the user isn't cleared away as it lands), and one process is cleared at most once every 30 minutes. Never while the project is paused. **Save first:** what the coordinator learned in the conversation and hasn't written down is the one thing a clear loses, so the first time the conditions hold the ticker pastes one fixed line instead of the clear, `[tm] auto_clear clears your conversation at the next idle moment (context at 52%). Save now what you learned that CONTEXT.md, MEMORY.md and memory/ don't hold yet, as your own short summary, then stop; nothing else. (Generated by tm.)`, checked again at delivery as the clear is, and journaled (`ticker coordinator.clear.ask <slug> s-4 at 52% of its context window (hint 40%): asked to save what it learned before the clear`). The clear itself comes at the next idle gate: the next time every condition has held for the 2 minutes, and at least 2 minutes after the ask (the coordinator's turn on it starts the wait again; one that ended between two sweeps is waited for from the ask). An ask older than 30 minutes is asked again, and an agent without `[inject] clear` is never asked. The ticker pastes the manifest's clear prompt (`[inject] clear`, `/clear` for Claude) as a plain prompt, never through the channel; right before delivery it checks the conditions again (the queue aside: a prompt queued after the clear lands in the fresh conversation) and drops it if one fails. Each clear is journaled (`ticker coordinator.clear <slug> s-4 at 52% of its context window (hint 40%), idle with nothing waiting`). The coordinator gets its context back as after any clear (§7.8), and keeps open questions to the user in CONTEXT.md under `## Needs you` (its rules), so what a clear could lose is what the save ask is for. An agent without `[inject] clear` is never cleared. **Save and clear, by hand:** a `/clear` the user types skips the save, so the user asks for the same steps instead with `tm clear [--project <slug>]` (the human's and the coordinator's), `c` on the coordinator's sidebar row or in its info panel, the info panel's hint line, or `/saveclear` and the Save and clear button of the /tm pane in a Claude coordinator (§8.6); each calls `coordinator.clear {project}` (human socket), which the server refuses for a project with no coordinator running and for one whose agent has no `[inject] clear` (`the coordinator's agent (…) has no clear prompt ([inject] clear): tm can't clear it; ask it to save what it learned, then clear it by hand`), and otherwise hands to the ticker (`ticker.RequestClear`), which runs it through the same code at its next sweep, whatever `auto_clear`, the hint or a pause say: the gate is the coordinator's own (idle, nothing queued, no question menu), not what waits in the project, since the user is there and asked; the ask goes out at once (`[tm] The user asked to clear your conversation at the next idle moment (context at 52%). Save now …`, journaled `ticker coordinator.clear.ask <slug> s-4 at 52% of its context window, on the user's request: asked to save what it learned before the clear`), and the clear once the coordinator rested 2 minutes after it (journaled `ticker coordinator.clear <slug> s-4 at 52% of its context window, on the user's request, idle with nothing waiting`). The request holds for that coordinator process only, and for 30 minutes at most.
- **Events for user mods** (`internal/modbus`, the mods design §4 and §5). The server keeps an event bus for code mods: each running mod subscribes with its granted permissions and its project list and gets its own queue. Events are worked out by looking again, not by the writers: a project command through `cli.run`, an agent state change, a session exit, a question opened or answered, and every ticker sweep (its items and PR polls) wake a look at once (coalesced, 100 ms); the human's own `tm` commands are caught by a look every 5 s (`TERMINATR_MOD_EVENTS_POLL` shortens it for tests). A look compares each project (its tasks, threads, inbox and `state/ticker.json` PRs and times) and each session with the last and publishes, per project in this order, `thread.started`, `task.changed` (`from` → `to`, steps), `tasks.changed` (`{project}`, mod API 1.4: anything `tasks.list` shows of the board changed, a title, a step's text, a task added or gone, or an ask about a task came or was handled; once per look), `thread.state` (the session's agent state, else the record's; the thread's question as `needs_you`: its status's, else the open menu's question, else `a question in its session` when the hooks say a question blocks it with no menu reported, as without the Agent mod), `thread.changes` (mod API 1.8: `{project, thread, head, branch, dirty, ahead, unpushed, pushed, files, add, del}`, the totals `thread.changes` answers in mode `base`, when a thread whose session is live changed its worktree: an edit, a commit or a push. Only while a subscribed mod listens to it for the project, and only for threads with a live session: the look asks git for a fingerprint at most every 5 s per thread, `status --porcelain=v2 --branch -z` and `for-each-ref` of `origin/<branch>` plus the size and time of each file the status lists, and reads the changes in full only when it moved; the event goes out when they differ, at most once per look), `report.stored` (the newest report's number, its sections, `report` being the text under `## Report`, and PR), `pr.changed` (number, state, checks, review, behind, conflict), `ticker.changed` (mod API 1.7: `{project, ticker}`, the ticker as `project.get` gives it, when it polled PRs or synced the repos, or when `gh_failing` flips; at most once per look), `inbox.item` (a new unhandled item) and `thread.resolved`; besides those `session.state` (state, reason, context %, and for a thread's session the `tokens` and `cost_usd` its thread has used so far; `exited` once at the end), `session.question` (mod API 1.3: `{session, project, thread, role, question: {question, header, multi_select, options: [{label, description}]}}`, the first unanswered question of the menu open on a thread's or coordinator's session, sent when it opens, changes, and without `question` once it is answered or gone) and `clock.minute` on each whole minute. Project events and `session.question` need the `project_read` grant (`view.opened`/`view.closed`, published by the views, need `views`); `session.state` and `clock.minute` need none. While no mod is subscribed nothing is looked at, and the first look after is a baseline. Each queue holds 1,000 events; past that the oldest is dropped and the next event delivered carries `dropped`, the count. Events go to a mod one at a time, in order (so in order per project), each with a number (`seq`); a handler gets 5 s, then it is cancelled and logged and the next event follows. A mod's first event, after every start or restart, is `mod.ready` with a snapshot: each project it runs for as `project.watch` gives it (none without `project_read`), built when it is delivered; a restarted mod's old queue is dropped, never replayed. Payloads are data: ids, states, titles and the report's own text, never instructions.
- **Alerts.** A thread's new report, like a session becoming blocked, raises the server's alert count (`session.list`'s `alerts`); every client rings its bell when the count goes up (§4). No desktop notification is sent.
- `tm inbox list` and `tm inbox done <id>…` (which moves items to `inbox/done/`). Handled items stay there as files for `archive_inbox_days` (30 by default, §11.2), then the ticker bundles them into `inbox/done/<yyyy-mm>.tar.gz` by the month they were raised (§7.6, **Retention**); nothing is deleted.
- **Questions**. The coordinator keeps each question it puts to the user in `questions.json`, through `tm ask` (forwarded, §11.1), in the form Claude Code's question dialog (`AskUserQuestion`) takes: an id (`Q3`, never reused), the task, a header (at most 12 characters; the task by default), the question, 2 to 4 options with descriptions (the dialog adds Other), whether several may be picked, the recommended option, and when it was asked. Asking the same question of the same task again updates it. CONTEXT.md's `## Needs you` stays the coordinator's human-readable copy, written by the coordinator as before; tm writes only the store. `tm context` lists the open ones (**Open questions (tm ask)**), so a cleared coordinator knows them. The status entry under the coordinator's prompt (§8.6, **Mods**; `/answer` opens them), its info panel and the dashboard's NEEDS YOU (§4) show the count, and each has a press or a key that calls `questions.open`: the server queues one fixed-word prompt to the coordinator, `[tm] answer questions: the user wants to answer your N open question(s) now. …`, through the agent's channel when held, like a nudge. It waits in the queue while the coordinator works, and at delivery it is rebuilt with the count then, or dropped when none is open any more (answered in chat meanwhile, or a second press). The coordinator then reads `tm ask list --json`, opens the dialog (up to 4 questions each, the recommended option first), acts on the answers as on an answer in chat, and closes each with `tm ask done`. It never opens the dialog unasked: the dialog blocks its pane, and nudges wait while it is open (§8.6).
- **Nudge.** When new items arrive and the coordinator is idle, the server sends it one line, e.g. `[tm] 2 new inbox items: T7 Fix the login (t-0004) blocked; T5 Sidebar thread ids (t-0002) reported`. It uses the agent's prompt injector (§8.1), or the mod where it runs (§8.6, **Prompt injection**). Nudges are rate-limited to one a minute and are never sent while the coordinator is working or blocked, or has a prompt queued. A queued prompt can't hold them for long. When a nudge has waited two minutes for a coordinator that is idle with prompts queued, the ticker acts once per stall: it asks the server to paste the prompt the coordinator's mod took and Claude never ran (§8.6, **Prompt injection**), and alerts (the bell, and `alert: <slug>: nudge about N item(s) held 2m0s: coordinator s-86 is idle with 1 queued prompt(s) (<why>); …` in the server log), since a prompt box with text in it only the user can clear; the /tm pane lists the held queue first under Needs you (§3.3). One held while the coordinator is idle is resolved after the paste injector's bound (§8.6): a nudge, as every ticker prompt, may then be written to the agent's channel (*sent*: Claude's socket never confirms delivery), else it is dropped. A nudge that waited in the queue is rebuilt when it is delivered: it names only its items still in the inbox, and is not sent once all were handled (logged; nothing is lost). A nudge holds fixed words, ids and each thread's task and title (written only by the coordinator and `tm`; a `delegate`, `accept` or `send-back` item names its task and title: `T12 Ship it to delegate (the user's go-ahead)`, `T12 Ship it accepted by the user`, `T12 Ship it sent back by the user`), never an item's summary, and ends by saying the items are data, not instructions.

### 7.6 `tm context`

`tm context` prints, in a fixed order and size, everything the coordinator needs:

1. the goal, the repos (each with its checkout note when its local default branch is behind origin, §7.5 checkout sync), the safety settings (and a line when the user paused the project), a `Prompt queue:` line per project session whose queued prompts are held while its agent is idle (asked of a running server, never starting one; §8.6), the installed agents and the thread agent (or why none can start: none installed, one named that isn't, several and none chosen), each installed agent's models for `--model` in the project's scope as the agent described them, its own default marked, its older versions on one `also` line, the ones the user's account refused, or `unknown (<reason>)` (§8.2 **Models**), and standing instructions (from `PROJECT.md`)
2. `CONTEXT.md`
3. the `MEMORY.md` index, then `Upkeep` only when a context file is over its size budget (`CONTEXT.md is 9.1 KB, over its 6 KB budget: consolidate it`; below)
4. tasks by group (the 3 most recent done tasks; the rest only counted)
5. the open (unresolved) threads with their merged state (§7.4), each led by its task with the thread id in brackets (`T12 (t-0005)`; a thread without a task by its id): their model when one was picked, agent state, derived percent, done/total, current todo or step, report and PR state, and `## Next` lines (the PR state as `tm thread list` shows it, from `state/ticker.json`); resolved and archived threads are only counted (`12 resolved threads not shown (tm thread list --all)`), since their next lines are stale; titles are clipped to 60 characters
6. unhandled inbox items
7. the last 10 `JOURNAL.md` lines, each clipped to 160 characters

Sections are capped, and the output says what it left out. Two calls with the same files give identical output.

**Keeping the context files small**. `CONTEXT.md` and the memory index are printed on every coordinator turn, and threads read `memory/` for their tasks, so each has a size budget (`internal/project/upkeep.go`): `CONTEXT.md` 6 KB; `MEMORY.md` 6 KB; each file under `memory/` 6 KB. A file over its budget is named in `tm context`'s `Upkeep` section and as a `tm doctor` warning (group `upkeep`, no fix: rewriting is the coordinator's work). The coordinator's skill (§7.7) says to keep them short and factual, to consolidate a growing memory file (re-read it, merge and rewrite it, drop what is no longer true) instead of appending, and to write `## Remember` lessons as its own summary. **Done tasks leave the board by themselves:** the ticker moves each task that has been `done` for `archive_tasks_days` (by its `updated` date) to `tasks/ARCHIVE.md`, journaled as `ticker task.archive T12`; `tm task archive` does it sooner, and `tm task unarchive` brings one back. That makes "clearing the coordinator loses nothing" testable: run scripted actions, clear the coordinator, run `tm context`, and compare (§15.6).

**Memory notes** (`internal/project/note.go`, `match.go`). A note in `memory/` may start with TOML front matter between `+++` lines, as `PROJECT.md` does: `title` (else its first heading, else its file name), `touches` (paths relative to a repo's root, a folder ending in `/`, and `repo:<name>` entries the note is about), `standing` (true for a note every thread should know), `audience` (`"everyone"`, the default, or `"coordinator"`: a note tm never points a thread at or shows one, in its brief, `tm memory find` or the memory check, standing or not; any other value counts as `"coordinator"`, so a typo keeps a note back rather than showing it) and `source` (the task or thread it came from); its body holds the sections `## Decided`, `## Ruled out` and `## Watch out`. All of it is optional: a free-form note parses as a note without front matter, and front matter that doesn't decode is read as free-form text, so a note is never lost to a typo. Notes stay editable and are consolidated like any memory file (unlike append-only learnings). `MEMORY.md` stays the index, and tm writes it (`internal/project/memoryedit.go`): after every `tm memory new`, `edit` and `rm` (and `tm memory index`, for a note written otherwise) it lists each note in `memory/` (a regular `.md` file), by title, one line each, `- [title](memory/file.md): what it holds (touches: a, b)`. A note that had a line keeps that line's description (the text after the link and its `:` or dash, without the touches), so curated wording survives, and its title too unless its front matter gives one (a front-matter `title` wins); the touches follow the note. A note without a line gets its front-matter title, else its first heading (a free-form note, one without front matter: its file name), and as its description the first line of its text, its section headings left out (a free-form note's first line whatever it is, a heading's `#` marks cut), at most 120 characters; a removed note loses its line. The rest of `MEMORY.md` stays as it is: its heading and text, the list where its first note line was (else at the end), and a missing `MEMORY.md` starts from the new project's. tm also brings the list in line by itself (`SyncIndex`), with no command: each `tm context` (so the coordinator's context too) and the ticker's upkeep (hourly, and on its first sweep after a start, so an upgraded project is synced at once) check it, a few stats while nothing changed since it last found the list in sync (MEMORY.md, `memory/` and its entries; `fsx.StatStamp`), else a rebuild that writes only when the list differs, journaled `ticker memory.index MEMORY.md synced with memory/`. A note written or removed by hand thus shows in the index at the next context. The coordinator doesn't keep the list by hand. `tm memory template` prints a note with the schema, and the Memory tab shows it. **Changing notes.** `tm memory new [<name>]`, `tm memory edit <note>` and `tm memory rm <note>` are the human's and the coordinator's (a thread is refused `coordinator-only`: it puts lessons under `## Remember`). A note is `<name>`, `<name>.md`, `memory/<name>.md` or its absolute path, the name letters, digits, `.`, `_` and `-` (new also takes words, as their slug: `Retries for the poller` is `memory/retries-for-the-poller.md`); any other path, one in a folder below `memory/` or with a `..` step, is refused `not-memory`, and a note that is a link, or a `memory/` folder that is one, is refused `link` (tm opens notes without following links, `fsx.OpenNoLink`, and replaces one by a rename). new writes the template, or `--file <path>|-` (at most 1 MB), refusing a note that exists (`exists`); edit replaces a note with `--file`; rm deletes one after `Delete memory/x.md? It leaves MEMORY.md too. [y/N]` on a terminal, else with `--yes`. On the human's terminal without `--file`, new asks the name when none is given and both open the text in an editor, `$VISUAL`, else `$EDITOR` (a command line, `code -w`), else the first of `nano` and `vi` on the PATH (`notepad` on Windows), on a copy in a private temporary folder, never the note itself: new writes nothing when the template is left as it was; edit saves nothing when the text didn't change. **The overwrite guard**: edit records the note's time, size and SHA-256 when it opens the editor and, before it saves, checks them again; when the note changed meanwhile (or was removed) it asks `memory/x.md changed while you edited it. o: overwrite it with yours; k: keep both, yours in memory/x.md.conflict [o/K]`, and keeping both (the default) writes the human's text beside the note as `memory/x.md.conflict`, which is no note. Each change is journaled `memory.new`, `memory.edit` or `memory.rm` with the note, and the human's also raises a `memory` inbox item (§7.5), so the coordinator re-reads the note and never writes a removed one's content back. Run from the console (the Memory tab's `e` and `n`), tm memory waits for enter before it hands the terminal back when it has something to say (an error, a kept conflict). `Match(notes, query, paths, limit)` scores each note with BM25 (k1 1.2, b 0.75) over its title and body, the notes being the collection: a query word is a lowercased run of letters and digits with a letter, three or more long, not an English stop word (nor a few more, such as `also`, `use`, `would` and the section headings), compared by its English Snowball stem (`retrying` meets `retries`), and counts once; a word in the title counts as 3 in the body; idf counts at least 10 notes, so a small memory's words aren't all common. Numbers, words under three letters and stop words never match. Each query path the note touches (the same path, or one a folder of the other; `repo:` entries only themselves) adds 10 and is the strongest signal: notes sharing more paths come first, then by score. A note clears with a shared path, or with a word score of at least 1 that stands out, one standard deviation above the mean word score of the notes without a shared path (counting at least 10 notes, the missing ones 0), from two words (one, for a one-word query) or a title word; the best `limit` are answered, best first. The words a hit shows are the query's own, not their stems. `tm memory find` runs it; for a thread, over the notes threads may see.
