Text typed into the terminal and never sent stays there while the user drives the agent from another device (remote control prompts don't touch the box), so a held prompt used to wait forever, and a coordinator's nudges with it (§7.5). Now, once held for 10 minutes (`TERMINATR_PROMPT_HOLD` for tests), the head prompt is resolved: the ticker's own fixed-word prompts (nudges, PR follow-ups, main conflicts) are written to the agent's channel when it has one (Claude's socket; its "another Claude session" framing is fine for them), and anything else (the human's or the coordinator's words, a slash command) or a prompt whose channel fails is dropped. A prompt written to the channel is *sent*, never *delivered*: Claude's socket doesn't answer the sender, and may still hold the message for approval or drop it (2.1.291). Either way the user's text stays in the box, the server logs it, a drop rings the bell, and the session's project journals it: `ticker prompt.sent s-4 held 10m0s: prompt box not empty`, `ticker prompt.dropped …`, `ticker prompt.waiting …` (the channel needs a token no hook has reported yet, below). `session.list` reports `queued_since`, `queue_held` (`prompt box not empty`, `dialog on screen`, `the mod took it, the agent hasn't run it`, `waiting for the agent's messaging token`) and `queue_held_since`; a hold of a minute or more shows in `tm session list` (`1 queued (held 3m0s: prompt box not empty)`), the TUI's details, `tm doctor` and `tm context`, and `tm agent explain` shows the queue.
  - **Priority prompts** (`tm thread prompt --now`, `--interrupt`; `session.prompt` `mode` `now` or `interrupt`, `session.PromptNow`). The queue's routes (the mod, the paste injector, Codex's `codex queue`) run a prompt only once the turn ends, so a thread that never goes idle got its coordinator's prompts late. Both agents take a prompt typed mid-turn at the next tool boundary, in the running turn (checked live on Claude 2.1.302 and Codex 0.162.0):
    - **`--now`**, while the agent works, its box is empty and no dialog is on screen: the prompt is pasted at once, skipping the queue and the mod (`steered`). Claude queues it (`Press up to edit queued messages`) and hands it to the model after the next tool result; Codex adds it to the turn at once and the model reads it at its next step. A queued prompt with the same text that the mod hasn't taken is dropped, so a prompt sent again with `--now` doesn't run twice. Idle, blocked, a draft in the box or a dialog: it goes the usual way (`queued` or `channel`), and the output says why. Claude's `ctrl+x ctrl+s` would hand the prompt over at once by moving a running tool to the background; tm doesn't use it, so a running command is never moved aside.
    - **`--interrupt`**, while the agent works: the turn is stopped for the prompt with the agent's interrupt key, the way its manifest says (`[inject] interrupt`, `interrupt_paste`, §8.2). Claude (`before`): the prompt is pasted, then Esc 1 s later; Claude stops the turn and sends what it queued at once as a turn of its own (a bare Esc first, then waiting for idle, would race that: the status file reads idle for about a second in between). Codex (`after`): Esc; the prompt heads the queue and is pasted once Codex is idle (about 3 s) with an empty box (a steer pasted before the Esc stays in the history unanswered). An agent without an interrupt key gets the prompt steered, and the output says so. The coordinator uses it only on the user's word.
    - The coordinator hears of a prompt stuck in the queue: `prompt-waiting` (§7.5).
  - **uds-messaging socket** (Go, `internal/agent/claude`; docs/research/claude.md §4a): one NDJSON line `{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":"…"}}` to `messagingSocketPath` from the status file. It queues correctly both idle and mid-turn, and avoids all three paste hazards, but it is **only for the server's own fixed-word prompts once held** (above), never the injector for every prompt.
    - **Framing:** 2.1.289 delivers a socket message to the model as "Another Claude session sent a message: … not typed by your user", with caveats against treating it as the user's approval; 2.1.291 queues it as a peer message (`isMeta`) with slash commands skipped. That is wrong for prompts from the human or the coordinator, so `claude.toml` keeps `paste`, and the Claude adapter reads `inject.prompt = "channel"` as paste too.
    - **Auth (2.1.291):** the inbox may require a first line `{"type":"auth","token":…}` and then drops unauthenticated lines silently. Claude gives its children a valid token in `CLAUDE_CODE_MESSAGING_TOKEN`; `claude.toml` names it in `inject.token_env`, so `tm hook` passes it to the server with each event (`hook.event` param `token`, outside the payload). The session keeps the latest in memory only, never logs or stores it, and the channel writes the auth line and the message in one write. Claude makes the token up at start (random, and it unsets an inherited one), so tm can't set it at launch: hooks are its only way to the server.
    - **No token, no send (T493):** before any hook has reported the token (a lost `SessionStart` and no turn since), the channel writes nothing (`agent.ErrNoToken`), since the line could be dropped unseen. The held prompt is neither sent nor dropped: it goes back to the head of the queue, held `waiting for the agent's messaging token`, journaled `ticker prompt.waiting s-4 held 10m0s: prompt box not empty` with the bell, once. The agent's next hook brings the token and the prompt is sent; a box that empties first gets it pasted as usual.
    - **Sent, not delivered:** the socket never answers on the sending connection (receipts go only to a Claude-style inbox, which tm doesn't run), and may still hold a message for approval or drop it. A nil write is journaled `prompt.sent` and logged "delivery not confirmed".
    - It is used only with a socket named in a trusted status file (any version, §8.8). A failed connect or write drops the held prompt with the error.
  - **Liveness** (the optional `agent.Prober`): every 5 s the session checks the agent's pid (`kill(pid, 0)`), then the agent's probe. Claude's is a connect-only dial of the messaging socket (250 ms, nothing written; Claude closes it): no socket file (ENOENT) or a refused connection (ECONNREFUSED) means gone, any other error is unknown. A gone pid makes the status file stale (a crashed agent leaves its last state behind), so state falls back to hooks and the screen; a gone probe keeps held prompts off the channel. `tm agent explain` shows `liveness`.
- **Re-injection after `/clear` and compaction:** the `SessionStart` response carries `hookSpecificOutput.additionalContext`, fetched fresh from the server each time (verified for `startup`, `clear` and `compact`; §7.8). With the mod it is a one-line pointer, and the context itself arrives as a context block (**Mods**, The role's context).
- **Workspace trust.** Trust gates every hook, ours included. Claude records an accepted dialog in `~/.claude.json` (`$CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR/.claude.json` when set) as `projects["<dir>"].hasTrustDialogAccepted`, and a directory below a trusted one is trusted too. **A thread never waits on it**: before launching a thread whose cwd is a worktree tm created (`~/.terminatr/worktrees/<slug>/<dir>`, nothing above or beside it), the server asks the agent to trust that directory (the optional `agent.Truster`; Go, `internal/agent/claude`). Claude's adapter adds the entry for the worktree and its real path, keeping every other key and entry (on Windows the keys are forward-slash paths, `C:/Users/a/p`, as Claude writes them); it writes atomically (temp file and rename, the file's mode kept), takes a `.lock` directory next to the file for up to 2 s (best effort), and leaves a file that isn't valid JSON alone. A failure is logged and the launch goes on. Coordinators and sessions started by hand get nothing: elsewhere the dialog shows as blocked / trust, and the human answers it. The bypass-permissions warning (yolo) is never pre-accepted. Keys sent within about 0.5 s of the dialog painting are dropped.
- **Mods**. Claude's mods API (function hooks in a TypeScript module, early access) lets a plugin draw in Claude's own UI and keep state there. The per-session plugin `--plugin-dir` loads is also terminatr's mod when `LaunchSpec.Mods` is set: `hooks/hooks.json` names `./register.ts` under `modules`, beside the command hooks, and `plugin.json` names the type contract `./types/index.d.ts`. The Go agent writes the module (`hooks/register.ts` and the files it imports: `feed.ts`, `view.ts`, `band.tsx`, `turn.ts`, `ask.ts`, `deliver.ts`, `guard.ts`, `guarded.ts`, `tools.ts`, `pane.tsx`, `dashboard.ts`) and the contract from files embedded in the binary (`internal/agent/claude/mod/`, `agent.Modder`). With the mod the session's state comes from the mod (below; §8.4 rule 0), and only the command hooks it doesn't replace stay. It also answers question menus (below), guards tool calls (**Guard** below) and gives a thread typed tools for its reporting (below).
  - **Session state from the mod**. The mod follows the turn from its own events and reports each transition to the server; the server prefers it (§8.4 rule 0).
    - **Events → states** (main loop; a subagent's events don't end its spawner's turn): `turn.start` → `working`; `turn.complete` → `idle` (`aborted` → `idle/interrupted`, `error` → `idle/error`; a dialog doesn't outlive its turn); an `AskUserQuestion` `tool.call` → `blocked/question` while it runs, then back; `classic.PermissionRequest` that no hook beneath decided → `blocked/permission` (`question` for `AskUserQuestion`), ended by `classic.PostToolUse`, `PostToolUseFailure` or `PermissionDenied` for the same tool and loop; `/compact` (`session.compact`, not `precompute`) → `working` while it runs; `session.end` → `exited`, or `idle` for `clear`. `prompt.submit` is not a state: a slash command such as `/remote-control` submits a prompt but runs no turn, and a prompt queued into a running turn changes nothing. The turn lives in `$.state` (`terminatr.turn`), so a reload of the module carries on.
    - **The channel.** The server opens a Unix socket per session with the mod, `<run dir>/s/<id>/mod.sock` (mode 0600 in the 0700 runtime dir; its path is the session's identity, `TERMINATR_MOD_SOCKET` in the session's environment), that speaks HTTP (the guard's `GET /v1/rules`, `POST /v1/resolve` and `POST /v1/denied` are under **Guard** below): `POST /v1/state` with `{"state","reason","event"}`, answered `204`, `400` for anything that isn't `idle`, `working`, `blocked` or `exited` with short words, `410` once the session is gone. HTTP because it is what a mod can speak to a socket (`$.http.fetch` with `socketPath`), not tm.sock's NDJSON with its hello; and not a long-lived `tm` child fed on stdin, since `$.process.spawn` writes a child's stdin once and closes it. The other direction shares the socket (**Prompt injection** above): nothing can push into a mod, so it pulls. `GET /v1/prompts?wait=<s>` (default 20, at most 25) is a long poll answered `200` with the head of the prompt queue, `{"id","kind":"prompt"|"command","text","command","args"}`, as soon as the mod delivers it, `204` when the wait ends without one, `410` once the session is gone; `POST /v1/prompts/<id>/ack` with `{"result":"taken"|"submitted"|"refused","error"}`, answered `204`, `404` when `<id>` isn't the queue's head (any more), `400` for another result. The heartbeat, not the poll, says the mod is there; the server counts the polls only to paste a head nobody polls for (**Prompt injection** above). `POST /v1/log` with `{"text"}` puts one line, cut to 300 characters, in the server's log as `session <id>: mod: <text>` (`204`; `400` without text), for what nothing else would show: the mod's prompt loop failing or started again.
    - **The prompt loop**. The mod's loop that polls and hands prompts on runs for the session's life: a `/clear` fires `session.end` but no `session.start` and may start `$.state` afresh, so the loop claims its owner id (`terminatr.deliverer`) back when it finds it wiped, and stops only when the server answers `410` or a newer load's loop took the id over. A loop that throws posts why to `/v1/log` and runs again after 5 s; one that made no round for 60 s, unless it is handing a prompt on (a slash command may wait a whole turn), is started again by the heartbeat, which logs it the same way. A socket that can't be opened launches the session without the mod (logged).
    - **Only transitions are sent**, one report in flight at a time with only the latest waiting behind it, never awaited by a hook (a wedged server holds nothing up; the exit report waits 0.5 s at most), and a heartbeat every 10 s (`"event":"beat"`) carries the state held, so a report lost to a server restart is healed by the next beat.
    - **Usage**. On each `turn.complete` that carries `usage` (a subagent's turns too: they are spend), the mod sends `POST /v1/usage` over the same socket with numbers and the model's id only: `{"turn","model","input","output","cache_read","cache_creation","cost_usd"}`, the four token counts of the turn and the rise of `$.session.usage().cost.usd` (Claude's own ledger, so prices are Claude's) since the last turn, kept in `$.state` (`terminatr.usage`). The server answers `204`, `400` for a negative or absurd number or a long word, `410` once the session is gone, and adds it to `[usage]` of the thread's `thread.toml` when the session is the thread's current one (a shell or coordinator session keeps none). Totals show in `tm thread show` (also `--json`, as `usage`), the dashboard's and info panel's thread, task and project panels, `tm task show` (summed over the task's threads) and `tm project list`; resolved threads still count. No text from a turn is ever sent or stored. A session without the mod, or an agent that reports none, shows no usage, unless its manifest reads usage from its JSONL file (`[jsonl_tail.usage]`, §8.2), which feeds the same totals and context use. **Context on /clear**: `session.end` with reason `clear` starts no `session.start`, so the mod sends `POST /v1/context/clear` (no body, `204`) and the server forgets the session's context use at once; the panes show none until the next turn reports, and a crossing of the hint after it rings anew.
    - **Context reminder**. The same report carries `context` and `context_window`: `$.session.usage().context`'s `tokens` and `window`, the input side of the session's last API response against its model's window, as Claude's status line counts them (a subagent's turns send 0, since their context is their own). Never `turn.complete`'s `usage`: that sums every request of the turn (Claude Code 2.1.292's API types), so a turn of ten tool calls would read about ten times its context. The server keeps the latest per session in memory (`SessionInfo.context`, `context_window`; the window is the agent's, else 200k, or 1M for a model id marked `[1m]` or `-1m` or once the context passes 200k) and shows it as a percent: on the coordinator's sidebar row, in its count/% column (`42%`, faint below the threshold, yellow from it, red from 80%), and as the first line of the /tm pane (`context 84k / 200k · 42%`, coloured the same, with `past the context hint: /saveclear saves what it learned, then clears` and a Save and clear button from the threshold; `project.watch` carries it as `context`). The threshold is `[ui] context_hint` in `config.toml` (a percent, 40 when unset, 0 for never; Settings, *Context hint*, steps off, 30–80). When a coordinator's context rises from below the threshold to it the server counts an alert, so every console rings its bell, and the pane toasts once; neither repeats until the context fell below the threshold (a `/clear`) and reached it again. The toast says `Context 42% full: /saveclear saves what the coordinator learned, then clears it`. Nothing is sent to the coordinator's prompt until the user asks: `/saveclear` (registered for a coordinator, band or not, `$.command.register`) and the button run `$TERMINATR_BIN clear` themselves (`tm clear`, §7.5 **Auto-clear**), with no model turn, and say what came of it (`Asked: the coordinator saves what it learned, then its conversation is cleared once it is idle`, or `Not cleared: <why>`). They call tm rather than prompting the model to save and run it, since a clear can't be typed from inside a turn and tm's own save ask can't be skipped. A `/clear` typed by hand still works and skips the save. A Codex coordinator has no `/saveclear`: Codex 0.162 has no custom prompts or commands a session can add, so there the user runs `tm clear` or presses `c` in tm.
    - **Fewer `tm hook` processes.** With the mod, `hooks.json` keeps only what the mod doesn't report: `SessionStart` (the context response, the transcript path, the todo reset), `PostToolUse` matched to `TaskCreate|TaskUpdate|TodoWrite` (todo mirroring), `SubagentStart`/`SubagentStop` (background counters) and `SessionEnd`. That drops `UserPromptSubmit`, `PreToolUse`, the `PostToolUse` of every other tool, `PostToolUseFailure`, `PermissionRequest`, `Notification`, `Stop`, `StopFailure` and `PreCompact`: from 13 events to 5, and from two spawns per tool call to none outside the todo tools. The todo snapshot re-read that `Stop` triggered runs on the mod's `turn.complete`. Without the mod (setting off, an older Claude) all 13 stay: the events the manifest names (`.HookEvents`), none other. `PermissionDenied`, `TaskCreated` and `TaskCompleted` are not registered: no entry maps them (`PermissionDenied` comes from auto mode's classifier, before any dialog, and the turn's next event moves the state; the task events repeat the `TaskCreate`/`TaskUpdate` `PostToolUse`), so each would be a `tm hook` process for nothing.
  - **A thread's tools**. In a session whose `TERMINATR_ROLE` is `thread`, with the mod's socket, the mod registers four tools at `session.start` (`$.tool.register`), for what a thread otherwise runs through the shell (§11.1): `mcp__terminatr__report` (`{pr?, report, next[], check[]?, remember[]?, attach[]?}`, the report's sections, `tm report`), `mcp__terminatr__status` (`{needs_you?, percent?, activity?}`, `tm status`), `mcp__terminatr__steps` (`{check[]?, uncheck[]?, add[]?}` on the thread's own task, `tm task steps`) and `mcp__terminatr__done` (`{summary?}`, `tm done`). Each has a JSON schema the model fills; the mod passes it on as `POST /v1/tools/<name>` on the session's mod socket, and the server checks it (unknown fields, ranges, one-line items, no `## ` line inside `report`), writes it out as the command it stands for (the report as the Markdown `tm report` takes, flags in their `--flag=value` form, a step's text and a summary after `--`) and runs it in the server as `cli.run` would, as the session's record's thread, in its cwd: the socket names the session, so no pid walk and no permission rule. `steps` runs one command per step, check, uncheck then add, and stops at the first refusal. Answers: `200` `{"text"}` (what the commands printed), `400` `{"error"}` for an input that isn't one, `422` `{"error"}` when the command refused (with what ran before it), `403` for a session that isn't a thread's, `404` for no such tool, `410` once the session is gone. The mod returns the text as the tool's result, and any error as a deny, which the model reads as the tool's error, naming the shell command when the server didn't answer. A `tool.check` hook lets the tools run without a permission prompt unless a rule beneath denies them, and `tool.describe` keeps them in the prompt's tool list, not behind ToolSearch. The thread skill (`tm skill thread`) says to prefer them when listed. A mods-off session, an adopted session (its variable still says `shell`), a coordinator and any other agent keep the shell commands, which stay as they are.
  - **Tools from user mods** (the mods design §1 and §2, tier 5). An enabled code mod that holds the `tools` grant (§5.3: `offers tools to agents`) may offer agents tools (`[[provides.tools]]`), answered by its code (`$.tool.handle`, §3.7). The grant is checked on every list and every call against the registry as it is now (read anew within a second), so when the human revokes it the mod's tools leave `GET /v1/tools` and `tool.list`, and `POST /v1/tools/<name>` and `tool.run` refuse them; a session's agent that registered them earlier gets the refusal as the tool's error. At `session.start` a thread or coordinator session with the mod's socket lists its tools with `GET /v1/tools` (`{tools: [{name, description, inputSchema}]}`: a thread's own four, then each enabled mod's tools for the session's role, of the mods that run for its project, by name; none while the server runs no mod hosts, and only bundled mods' in safe mode) and registers the mods' ones, named `<mod>_<tool>` (`mcp__terminatr__lint_lint_report`; a mod id has no underscore, so the first one splits the name, and the thread's own tools have none). Each description starts `[mod: <id>]`. A call goes to the server as `POST /v1/tools/<mod>_<tool>`, where the input is checked against the tool's schema (an object, only its properties, each of its JSON type, the required ones there; `400` otherwise), and then to the mod's host as `tool.call` with the session's project, role, thread and id, through the supervisor, which starts an idle host first; the whole call has 60 s. Answers: `200` `{"text"}` (what the handler returned), `422` `{"error"}` with the message of what it threw, `404` for a tool the session isn't offered (its mod not enabled for the project, without the `tools` grant, or not for the role), `502` when the host has no such handler, `503` when the mod failed, is restarting or ran out of time. Each call is a line in the mod's log, with the session and how it ended. Like the thread's tools they run without a permission prompt unless a rule beneath denies them (the human granted `tools` and saw each tool's description in full when enabling the mod); the engine places them, behind ToolSearch by default. A mod enabled later reaches sessions started after it. The mod never runs inside the agent and sees only the input the agent gave and the session's ids: mods reach agents only through these tools and the coordinator's inbox.
  - **`tm mcp`**. The same tools as an MCP server on stdio (newline-delimited JSON-RPC; `initialize`, `ping`, `tools/list`, `tools/call`), for an agent that speaks MCP but has no mod, such as Codex. `tools/list` is `tool.list` (§3.3): a thread's `report`, `status`, `steps` and `done` (the agent prefixes its server's name), with the schemas of the mod's, and the user mods' tools for the session, a coordinator's included; with no server it lists the thread's four. It does no work of its own: each call is `tool.run` (§3.3) on the control socket, where the server finds the thread from the peer pid (the agent started this process, so it is inside the session's process tree; no `TERMINATR_*` variable is needed, and an agent that filters the environment of its MCP servers is fine), checks the input and runs it exactly as the mod socket's `POST /v1/tools/<name>` does. A refusal comes back as a tool result with `isError`. Outside a thread it answers `refused`; with no server, the tool result says `tm server:` and why. Codex (0.160, live check): it starts the server outside its sandbox (a `read-only` session still reached `tm.sock`), and asks to approve every MCP call, which `approval never` turns into a failure, so its launch arguments are `-c mcp_servers.terminatr.command=<tm> -c 'mcp_servers.terminatr.args=["mcp"]' -c mcp_servers.terminatr.default_tools_approval_mode=approve` (the Codex manifest's). The band and /tm pane have no equivalent there; the info panel covers them.
  - **What it does:** on `session.start` it runs `$TERMINATR_BIN watch --session $TERMINATR_SESSION --json` (§10) for the session's life and keeps the latest `Watch` line in `$.state` (`terminatr.watch`). Outside a terminatr session (no `TERMINATR_BIN`/`TERMINATR_SESSION`) it starts nothing.
  - **Band and status entry**, in thread and coordinator panes alike, from that line; `[agent_mod] band = false` (the server sets `TERMINATR_BAND=off`) turns all three below off, the feed still runs. The TUI's info panel stays as it is.
    - **The band** (`ui.render` on `AbovePrompt`): `T50 · steps 1/3 · #12 open, 2 checks failed, behind main · 2 need you`, then `now: <current item>`. The task id is bold, a PR to act on (failed checks, conflicts, changes requested, behind) red, the needs-you and inbox counts yellow. From 100 body columns (`bodyColumns`) it is one row; below that, or while the thread waits on the user (`needs you: <question>`, a row of its own), it is a column. A coordinator has no task and no band: what waits shows in the status entry (`3 need you · 1 in inbox`). It is **quiet** (the plugin passes to the engine) with no task and nothing waiting, while a survey holds the band, and once the session has exited. Claude draws its own `[-]` beside it to collapse it (ctrl+x ctrl+a). Claude raises the band on the terminal and desktop surfaces only; the tree validates on all four.
    - **Questions waiting**. A coordinator with open questions (`questions` in its line, §7.5 **Questions**) gets them in the status entry, after the counts: `2 need you · 1 in inbox · 2 questions · /answer`, and the entry alone shows when they are all that waits. Claude's status entry is text only and cannot be pressed, so the mod registers a `/answer` command for a coordinator (`$.command.register`, with the band on) that runs `$TERMINATR_BIN ask open` (`questions.open`), and a toast says what came of it (`Asked: your questions open in a dialog once the coordinator is free`, or `Not asked: <why>`). It never blocks: the prompt box stays the user's, and an answer typed in chat works as before; the count goes once the coordinator closes the last question (`tm ask done`). A coordinator has no band.
    - **The status entry** (`$.ui.status`), the same in short under the prompt, beside the user's own statusLine: `T50 steps 1/3 · #12 open, checks pending · 2 need you`; cleared when quiet. It is what Remote Control and mobile show.
    - **A toast when a CI run finishes:** the PR's checks went from `pending` to pass or failed between two lines (`T50 #12: checks passed`, `T50 #12: 2 checks failed`), once per run, shown 15 s rather than Claude's default 4 s (a real Claude draws it, but 4 s passes unseen when the user reads elsewhere).
  - **The /tm pane** (`hooks/pane.tsx`, `hooks/dashboard.ts`), in coordinator sessions only (`TERMINATR_ROLE=coordinator`): the project's dashboard beside the coordinator, where the user lives; the TUI's dashboard stays as the fallback (mods off, an older Claude, other agents). **When to use it**: in tm the coordinator's info panel (§4, **Info panel**) shows the same and is the default, so /tm stays shut unless asked for; open it with `/tm` where tm's panel isn't drawn, in Claude desktop or through Remote Control on the phone, or for its buttons (Accept, Send back, Merge, Delegate) and its report pane.
    - **Feed:** on `session.start` it runs `$TERMINATR_BIN watch --project $TERMINATR_PROJECT --json` for the session's life (started again 5 s after it ends, as on a server restart) and keeps the latest `ProjectWatch` (§3.3, **Watch**) in `$.state` (`terminatr.project`).
    - **Opening it:** `/tm`, registered with `$.command.register` and answered by a `command.run` hook (no model turn), opens it at any width, inline above the prompt on the main screen, or closes it. With `[agent_mod] pane = true` (the settings popup's **Mods pane**; off when unset, and the server then sets `TERMINATR_PANE=off`) it also opens by itself at start, but only where it docks as a sidebar: once a drawing says the layout is fullscreen (`viewport.isFullscreen`, which the band's `AbovePrompt` hook passes on; within 10 s, else never), and Claude seats a pane opened unasked only from 144 columns, holding it until the terminal is that wide. The mobile app reports no fullscreen, so on a phone it opens with `/tm`.
    - **Layout** (sized to `bodyColumns`): `demo · 5 need you · 1 in inbox · 4 threads`; **Needs you**, in the feed's order, each a row `T63 in review Dashboard pane` (in the TUI's words, docs/STYLE.md: `prompts held`, `in review`, `asks you`, `checks failed`, `blocked`) with its buttons beside it (on a row of their own under 60 columns), then the thread's question (yellow) or its PR (red when it needs acting on); **Inbox** (dim summaries); **Threads**, one compact row each (`t-0059 T64 working · steps 3/5 · now: mod tools · #121 open, checks pending`, dim when stopped or done), pressed to open its report; **On deck**, the first five ready tasks with Delegate. The same tree validates on the terminal, desktop, vscode and mobile surfaces, so Remote Control and the phone show it.
    - **Buttons never act themselves.** Each sends the coordinator a fixed line as the user's own words (`$.prompt.submit` with `asUser`, queued until it is idle): Accept → `accept T63`; Send back → `send T63 back: <note>` (the note from a field in the pane, one line of at most 200 characters; the phone draws no field, so there it puts `send T63 back: ` in the prompt box for the user to finish); Merge (a task in review whose PR is `mergeable`) → `merge PR #120 for T63`; Delegate (a blocked task with no thread, or one on deck) → `delegate T70`. The coordinator then does it as if the user had typed it; only a press can raise these, never the model. A task asked about (by the pane while its status holds, or by the TUI's inbox items) shows `asked the coordinator: accept` instead of its buttons. Report runs `tm thread show <id> --json` and opens the thread's latest report in a second pane, `tm-report` (titled `Report · t-0059`) (Markdown, focused, closed by Esc or Close), with no model turn.
  - **When it loads:** (a Go agent that is an `agent.Modder` and says `ModRequired()`, having no hooks to fall back on, loads its mod whatever `[agent_mod] enabled` says; the version check stays. The settings text names the optional Modders and their versions from the registry, and `agent.Doctor` lets an agent add `tm doctor` checks, as Claude's unsafe-plugin check.) Only with `[agent_mod] enabled = true` in `config.toml` (the settings popup's **Agent mod** row turns it on and off, and **Agent mod band** the `[agent_mod] band` below; both change the one line in place, are read when a session launches, so only sessions launched after the change see them, and the UI names neither the file nor the keys, §4; off by default while the API is early access, until two releases have run it green), and only for a Claude Code at least `ModsMinVersion` (**2.1.289**, the tested build). The server reads the version with the manifest's `version_args` from the `claude` on the sessions' `PATH`, once per binary (cached by path, size and modification time, so an upgrade is read again at the next launch), with a 5 s limit. An older or unreadable version, or the setting off, launches the session as before, and the server log says why when the setting is on. `--plugin-dir` loads a mod with no hot-reload question.
  - **Checked by:** `claude plugin validate` and `claude plugin test` on the plugin exactly as a session gets it, plus the mod's `tests/*.test.ts(x)` (never written into a session; the band's run on the terminal, desktop, vscode and mobile surfaces; `state.test.ts` holds the events → states map, transitions only, the heartbeat and a wedged server to it): `TERMINATR_CLAUDE_PLUGIN_CHECK=1 go test ./internal/agent/claude -run TestModPlugin`, which needs `claude` on `PATH` and no login. Both commands work offline. CI's `claude-mod` job runs it with a pinned Claude Code (`CLAUDE_CODE_VERSION`, bumped together with `ModsMinVersion`; no login, no secrets, `CLAUDE_CODE_DISABLE_NONESSENTIAL_TRAFFIC=1`) on every push to main and on pull requests that change `internal/agent/claude/`, `manifests/claude.toml` or `ci.yml`; otherwise the `changes` job skips it, and a job skipped by its `if` counts as passed for required checks.
  - **The role's context** (§7.8): a `prompt.context` hook adds a `terminatr` block to the conversation's context blocks, which Claude reads for a new conversation and again after `/clear` and compaction (the mod also invalidates them on a `SessionStart` from either). Its text is `GET /v1/context` on the mod's socket (`200` text, `204` for a session outside a project, `410` once gone), fetched fresh each time and bounded by 2 s; when that fails there is no block (the command hook would have asked the same server). The `SessionStart` command hook brings no copy of it in a session with the mod: the server answers it with one line saying the context is in the conversation's `terminatr` block (`modContextLine`; nothing for a session outside a project), so the model reads the context once instead of twice and Claude's 21 KB hook output file and 2 KB preview are gone. A session without the mod keeps the full copy. The mod's own `classic.SessionStart` hook only invalidates the blocks after `/clear` and compaction; a resume keeps the recorded block, so a resume under another build gets the full context from the command hook instead (§7.8, step 4); its tests (`tests/context.test.ts`) and the server's (`TestHookContextWithMod`, `TestHookContextResumed`, `TestModChannel`) cover the block, the line, the full `/v1/context` and the resume under another build (§7.8, step 4); that Claude re-reads the blocks after `/clear` is Claude's documented behaviour, seen by hand, not by a test.
  - **Question menus**: a `tool.call` hook on `AskUserQuestion` opens the menu (`next(e)`) and at the same time runs `$TERMINATR_BIN session ask $TERMINATR_SESSION` with the menu (§3.3, **Ask**). Whichever answers first answers the call: the user in the pane (the hook kills `tm session ask`, which takes the question off the server), or `tm thread answer` (the hook returns `{ result: { questions, answers } }` while `next(e)` is pending, which takes the menu down; the model reads the answers as the user's). When `tm session ask` exits without answers the menu stays the user's. Checked live on 2.1.291: one question, a multi-select, free text and two questions in one call. Outside a terminatr session it leaves the menu alone. A hook that fails is skipped, so the menu then works as without the mod.
  - **Guard**: the standing rules (the thread and coordinator skills, `PROJECT.md`) as checks on each tool call. A `tool.call` hook judges every call before it runs, in every permission mode and ahead of any dialog, against the rules the server sends; a call that breaks one is refused with the rule's sentence as the tool's error (`terminatr guard (merge): merging is the coordinator's job in this project (merge = "coordinator"). Leave the PR open with CI green and say so in your report.`; each sentence says what to do instead, a thread's ends on its report, the coordinator's on the user, and none repeats the call's text). A call no rule matches goes on to Claude's own rules, sandbox and mode, so the guard only adds refusals, and for a PowerShell command it can't read, a question (below). A `tool.check` hook gives `$.tool.check` queries the same answer.
    - **Rules:** `force-push` (`git push` with `-f`, `--force`, `--force-with-lease`, `--force-if-includes`, `--mirror` or a `+refspec`); `push-default` (a push whose target is `main`, `master` or a repo's default branch as origin last said, or `--all`); `worktree-only`, threads only (the agent's `write` and `patch` tools (§8.2, `[guard.tools]`: Claude's `Edit`, `Write`, `MultiEdit`, `NotebookEdit`, Codex's `apply_patch`) outside the worktree, the temporary folders and the session agent's `[guard] writable` folders (§8.2; Claude's `~/.claude/plans` and `~/.claude/projects`); Bash's writes are the sandbox's, but a shell command naming the main repo's git `hooks`, `info` or `config` (`gitConfig` in the rules, §5.2) other than to view it (`cat`, `ls`, `grep` and the like), or redirecting into it, is refused too: the sandbox refuses it anyway, the guard says why sooner); `delete-branch` (`git branch -d/-D`, `git push --delete` or `:branch`, `git worktree remove|prune`, `git update-ref -d`, `gh repo delete`, `gh api -X DELETE …/git/refs/heads/…`, `gh api graphql` with a `deleteRef` mutation; on Azure DevOps `az repos delete`, `az repos ref delete`, `--delete-source-branch` given true on `az repos pr create|update`, and a write method on a `_apis/git/…/refs` route or DELETE on a repository, by `az rest`, `az devops invoke` or `curl`/`wget`/`http`/`xh` to `dev.azure.com` or `*.visualstudio.com`; `rm -r` of the thread's worktree, tm's worktrees folder, a project's folder in it or a worktree in that); `merge`, threads only and only under `merge = "coordinator"` (`gh pr merge`, also with options such as `-R` before or between the words, `gh api …/pulls/<n>/merge` with or without a query, `gh api graphql` with a `mergePullRequest`, `enablePullRequestAutoMerge` or `enqueuePullRequest` mutation; on Azure DevOps `az repos pr update|create` with `--status completed`, `--auto-complete` or `--bypass-policy` given true or bare, and a write method on a `…/pullrequests/<id>` or `…/merges` route through the same clients as above (for `az rest`, an `X-HTTP-Method-Override` header's method counts as the method; for the HTTP clients, the method `-X`, `--request` or `--method` names, else the one their data makes it: `curl -d`, `--data…`, `--json` or `-F` a POST unless `-G`, `-T` a PUT, `wget --post-data` or `--post-file` a POST, an HTTPie or xh data item a POST), so completing a PR is refused with or without build policies and whatever its merge strategy: the guard reads the verb, never the commit; opening a PR, listing, showing, voting, commenting on threads and `--description` edits pass); `credentials` (the agent's `read` and `glob` tools (Claude's `Read`, `Grep`, `Glob`) and shell readers such as `cat`, `base64`, `cp` or a `<` redirect of `~/.ssh`, `~/.aws`, `~/.gnupg`, `~/.netrc`, `~/.git-credentials`, `~/.npmrc`, `~/.pypirc`, `~/.config/gh`, `~/.config/gcloud`, `~/.azure` (the az CLI's token cache and its azure-devops extension's PAT store live under it), `~/.azure-devops`, `~/.docker/config.json`, `~/.kube`, and every agent's `[guard] secrets` (§8.2: `~/.claude/.credentials.json`, `~/.codex/auth.json` and `$CODEX_HOME/auth.json`, …); `gh auth token`, `gh auth status --show-token`, `az account get-access-token`, `az devops login`, an Azure DevOps call by `curl` and the like carrying `-u`, `--user`, an `Authorization:` header or `$AZURE_DEVOPS_EXT_PAT`, `echo`, `printf` or a reader given `$AZURE_DEVOPS_EXT_PAT`, `printenv` of a name ending in `PAT`, `security find-*-password|dump-keychain|export`, `git credential fill`, `printenv` with no name or with a name that looks secret (`TOKEN`, `SECRET`, `KEY`, `PASSWORD`, `PASSWD`, `CREDENTIAL`, any case), a bare `env`, `export`, `declare` or `typeset` with no names, and `set` alone; `printenv HOME PATH` is fine). A coordinator is held to all but `worktree-only` and `merge`. Auth on Azure DevOps is the user's `az login` (Entra); agents get the same environment as any other Claude session (no env filtering), so the guard alone keeps `$AZURE_DEVOPS_EXT_PAT` from being read or echoed. The credentials refusal's sentence points the agent at the secret environment (below) for a token a tool needs.
    - **The floor:** some refusals hold whatever the human allowed or turned off (`guard_off`), in either mode, whenever the guard is on (only `guard = false` lifts them): reading tm's own files (the run folder with its sockets and the sessions' runtime folders, the server's keys `state/peer`, the guard's log `state/guard`; served as `own`, judged under `credentials` but whichever rules are on); a thread's file tools (read, glob, write, patch) in another thread's temp folder (`otherTemps`, `/tmp/tm-<uid>`, but in `ownTemp`, its own; rule `temp-folders`, T591: the sandbox keeps Bash out, and Claude's deny rules can't carve its own folder out); printing a secret variable tm gives the thread (`printenv NAME`, `echo $NAME`, `${NAME}`, PowerShell's `$env:NAME` and `Env:NAME`, any case) or the whole environment while it has one (`env`, `printenv`, `export`/`declare`/`set` alone, `Get-ChildItem Env:`, a reader of `/proc/<pid>/environ`); a force-push whose target is a protected branch, or `--all`/`--mirror`; every `merge` refusal (a thread merging under `merge = "coordinator"`: that setting, not an allowance or `guard_off`, changes it); and writing tm's settings (`config.toml`, any agent) or the project folder (a thread; the coordinator, whose folder it is, only the parts tm alone writes: `threads/`, `inbox/`, `tasks/`, `journal/`, `TASKS.md` and the rest of `Project.Owned`), rule `tm-files` (T574 §3.1): a file tool by where its write lands, a Bash command by the paths it redirects into (`>`, `>>`, `&>`, `>|`, and the file after a `tee >(…)`) or gives a command that writes (`rm`, `mv`, `tee`, `touch`, `mkdir`, `chmod`, `truncate` and the like, all their paths; `cp`, `install`, `ln`, `rsync`, their target, `-t DIR` and `--target-directory DIR` included; `sed -i`, `perl -pi`; `dd of=`; `git config --file F`). For a remover or mover (`rm`, `rmdir`, `mv`, `rsync`) and a recursive `cp -r`, `chmod -R` and the like, a folder that holds one of those paths counts too: `rm -rf ~/.terminatr` and `mv ~/.terminatr /tmp/t` are refused. So is an agent running the `tm` commands that write `config.toml` in the tm process itself and are the human's alone: the dashboard (`tm`, `tm --own`), `tm mod` but its agent subcommands (`list`, `show`, `validate`, `new`, `test`, `logs`, `examples`), and `tm project new|remote|activate|deactivate|pause|resume|archive|unarchive|delete|rename|link|open|repo add`, and `tm access add|rm|drop|undrop|set|preset add` (as `tm`, a path ending in `tm` or `$TERMINATR_BIN`). Their human-only check takes the caller from the environment narrowed by the server's process-tree walk (`caller.Narrower`); an agent with no sandbox (yolo) that unsets its session's variables and leaves its session's process tree (`setsid`, a double fork) would pass for the human. Relative paths follow a `cd DIR` or `pushd DIR` only where it is a plain command at the line's top level (split outside quotes, `( )`, `{ }`, `$( )` and backticks): joined by `&&` it moves them for the rest of its list (`cd ~/.terminatr/projects/demo && rm TASKS.md`); joined by `;` or `||`, or after a `||`, the old folder stays possible too (the `cd` may fail or not run), and every possible folder is judged; in `( )`, a pipeline or the background it moves nothing; anywhere else (`{ }`, `if`, a substitution, a line with a heredoc) it leaves them unknown. A part that runs shell code the line doesn't show in the shell itself (`eval`, `trap`, `source`, `.`, also through `command` or `builtin`) may move the folder at any point after it, so the rest of the line keeps "unknown" among its folders. A relative path given to a writer while the folder is unknown (that, or a `cd` to a variable, a substitution, `cd -`, `popd`) is refused, since it could be anywhere. For a remover, a wildcard in a path's last part counts where it could name a folder holding those paths (`rm -rf ~/.term*`, `~/.terminatr/proj*`). **Known limits**, which the sandbox and tm's `Edit(...)` deny rules keep covering wherever they apply (not a yolo thread's shell): a write the guard can't see in the words of the line: an interpreter (`python -c`, `node -e`, a script file), an editor, `curl -o`/`wget -O`, `tar -C`/`tar -x`, `find -delete`/`-exec`, `xargs` with a writer, a path held in a variable or made by a glob it can't place, a symlink a command made earlier pointing into those paths, and PowerShell commands (not judged for this floor yet). Escapes rest on the screen too: a process of the agent's that writes the terminal itself (`/dev/tty`) can repaint it, hiding a permission dialog from the screen rules or drawing one. The rules serve `floor` (`["force-push"]`, plus `"merge"` for a thread under `merge = "coordinator"`) and `noWrite` (those paths): both matchers refuse those cases with the rule off. A refusal on the floor carries `"floor": true`.
    - **Yolo** (T574 case 4): a yolo session's guard keeps its floor only: no rule, allowance, learn mode or mod rule is used, and nothing is asked.
    - **Escapes** (T574 case 6): a thread's Bash call with `dangerouslyDisableSandbox` (its manifest's `escape` field of the tool) asks to run outside the sandbox; while the thread's escapes are on and it isn't yolo, the guard (rules `askEscapes`) answers it with "ask" (rule `escape`), so a prompt comes even where an allow rule or auto mode would let it run unasked. This holds with `guard = false` too: the rules are then `askEscapes` alone, with no rule and no floor. The server marks the call, by its id (the hook's `tool_use_id`, or the mod's own id on `POST /v1/escape {"id","done"}`). Its end (`PostToolUse`, `PostToolUseFailure`, `PermissionDenied`, the mod's `done`) or the turn's only lets the mark go: it goes once no permission dialog has shown for 8 seconds after it (a dialog on screen holds it and starts that again), since hooks can come from any process in the agent's tree, even before the escape's dialog has drawn. A permission dialog that says it runs the command outside the sandbox (the manifest's `[guard] escape_rule`, Claude's `blocked-escape-dialog`: the guard's "asks to run outside the sandbox" or Claude's "Run outside of the sandbox") is an escape with no mark at all. While one is marked and the session is blocked on a permission prompt (by its state, or a permission dialog on screen), `session.list` says `escape` (parallel calls: another prompt ending doesn't end the mark), and a `tm thread prompt --now` isn't pasted (held: `a call asks to leave the sandbox`). The mod refuses an escape it can't report, rather than open its prompt unmarked. The ticker's item says only the user answers it; the coordinator's `tm thread approve` and `answer` are refused (`human-only`), whatever `coordinator_approves` says, and the server checks again as the keys go: `session.keys` with `relay = "coordinator"` (how those commands send them) is refused while the session is on an escape's prompt, and so is `session.answer` from anyone but the human (an agent, or `relay = "coordinator"` from tm's commands). A Codex thread's prompts (`-a on-request`) are all escalations (`[guard] prompts_escape`), so each is the user's; with escapes off it runs with `-a never` and asks none.
    - **The user's own excluded commands** (T574 case 7): a command Claude runs outside the sandbox through `sandbox.excludedCommands` carries no flag, so the server reads the thread's agent's own settings files live (`agent.OwnRuler`: `~/.claude/settings.json`, the worktree's `.claude/settings.json` and `settings.local.json`; their stat is in the rules' stamp, so a change, the thread's own writes to its worktree's included, reaches the rules at the next call rather than after the minute they are kept) and serves their excluded commands as `unsandboxed`, with tm's (`config` sandbox `excluded_commands`) as `tmExcluded` and `noEscapes` for escapes off; not under yolo, not for an agent without such files (Codex). A call of a shell tool with an `escape` field that one of `unsandboxed` takes, and that none of `tmExcluded` takes as Claude matches them (`config.ExcludedMatch`), is an escape: asked (rule `escape`, as above) or, with escapes off, refused. They are matched more widely than Claude does, so the guard asks rather than misses: any simple command of the line (as typed, and without its wrappers and assignments) or the whole line, `*` any text, a trailing ` *` (or the older `:*`) no arguments too, and a pattern without `*` also with arguments (`docker` takes `docker ps`). tm doesn't change the user's files; the effective access view lists those rules with how tm treats them ("tm treats it as an escape: asks you", "…: refused, escapes are off", "tm excludes it too", or not used under yolo).
    - **Allowances** (`guard_allow`, §11.2): the human's exceptions, each a rule and a command line (`command`) or a file (`path`, absolute or from `~/`). A `*` matches any run of characters (`git push -f origin tm/*`, `~/src/shared/*`); without one it is that exact command (trimmed) or file (where a write lands, case folded where the file system ignores case). An allowance with `exact = true` is that command or file whatever it holds, its `*` matching only a `*`: allowing a refusal writes one so, as the call refused is one call, not a pattern. A refusal under an allowance's rule of a call it matches is lifted and the call judged again without that rule, so another rule can still refuse it; a mod's rule is named `mod:<mod>.<rule>`. Never on the floor; a `credentials` allowance is exact (a `*` in one is ignored, and refused when written); `merge` can't be allowed at all. A project's allowances add to all projects' (`[defaults]`). Both matchers pass the shared vectors' allowance and floor cases.
    - **Learn mode** (`guard_mode = "learn"`): the call is still refused, nothing passes before the user says so; the sentence the model reads ends with `The user reviews refused calls: if they allow this one, you will be told and may retry it; until then don't work around it.`, and each refusal newly listed in the guard's log (below) files an inbox item of kind `guard` that needs the user. `enforce` (the default) lists refusals the same way, quietly.
    - **The guard's log** (`internal/guardlog`, `state/guard/refusals.json`, mode 0600): each refusal's project, thread, session, rule, tool, summary and its `subject`, the whole command line or file an allowance would name, one entry per project, rule and subject, counted and dated. It is the human's: mods can't reach tm's state folder (§5.3), the floor keeps agents from reading it, and nothing of the subject goes to the journal, the server log or an inbox item. The console reads it in its own process (the Access tab, **Guard refusals**: the latest first, the whole command on enter; `a` allows it for its project, `A` for all projects, `x` keeps refusing it, which stops listing it; one refused again after it was allowed, its allowance gone, is listed anew). An allowance is written to `guard_allow`, journaled as `guard.allow <thread> <rule> <tool>: <summary> (this project|all projects)` (`guard.keep` for keep refusing), and the thread's running session gets the prompt `[tm] The user allowed the <tool> call the guard refused (<rule>: <summary>), for <scope>: you may retry it.`; the rules reach every session within a second (below).
    - **Secret environment** (`[projects.<slug>.env]`, `[defaults.env]`, §11.2): variables a thread's session gets in its environment when it starts, each `NAME = { from = "gh-auth-token" }` (the output of `gh auth token`), `{ from = "keychain:<item>" }` (the login keychain's generic password of that service, macOS) or `{ from = "env:<NAME>" }` (the server's own environment); a project's name replaces all projects' of the same name. Threads only: a coordinator gets none. The Access tab's **Secret environment** (under Secrets, after the guard's rows, a project's and all projects'), lists them by name and source, a project's with all projects' marked (`· all projects`, `· all projects, replaced here`); `a` adds one (the name, checked as the file is, then a source picker: gh auth token, a keychain item or a variable of the server's environment, which asks for what it names), `x` removes the selected one from its table, and `t` tests it: `secret.test {project, name}` (human-only; `project` "" for all projects' table) has the server get that table's variable from its source with its own environment, as a thread's start does, and answer `{ok, error}`, the error the source and why (an exit status, never output), never the value. The console and the server's methods handle names and sources only; the writes go through the same line editor as the other settings (a name with the source, never a value), journaled `settings.env NAME from <source>` or `NAME removed`. The value is read at each start (10 s at most each) and goes into the session's environment only: never its arguments or runtime files, a prompt, a report, the server log or the journal; a source that fails is logged by the variable's name and why (an exit status, never output), and the thread starts without it. Codex filters its commands' environment by name (`KEY`, `SECRET`, `TOKEN`): with a secret environment it is launched with `shell_environment_policy.ignore_default_excludes=true` and `exclude` naming the session's other such variables, so only the secret ones pass; Claude passes its environment on as it is. The guard's floor keeps the agent from printing them, so the thread's tools (npm, a registry client) use them and the agent doesn't see them. `tm doctor` warns about a project repo whose `.npmrc` reads a registry credential from `${NAME}` (`//host/:_authToken=${NAME}`) its threads don't get.
    - **Bash** commands are split into simple commands on `;`, `&`, `|`, newlines, `( )` and `{ }`, and read shell-style: quotes, `#` comments, env assignments, the wrappers that run a command (`sudo`, `doas`, `env`, `timeout`, `nice`, `ionice`, `xargs`, `stdbuf`, `caffeinate`, `command`, `exec`, `nohup`, `time`, …, with their options) and the keywords before one (`if`, `then`, `do`, `!`, …), `git -C`/`-c`. A `$( )` or backticks are a word of their command and their inside is read as commands too (also inside double quotes and `${ }`); a word with text beside a substitution is judged by that text. `bash`, `sh`, `zsh` (and the like) `-c STRING`, `eval STRING` and `pwsh -c` are judged on the string. A heredoc's body is read as commands when the line has a shell that could run it (`bash <<EOF`, `cat <<EOF | sh`, `ssh host bash <<EOF`), otherwise only for what an unquoted one substitutes; `(( ))` and `$[ ]` are arithmetic, their `<<` a shift. That catches what agents type; what is written to hide (a script, an alias) is left to the sandbox and the permission rules.
    - **PowerShell**: Claude's shell tool on Windows without Git Bash is `PowerShell` (`[guard.tools]` `syntax = "powershell"`), and Windows has no Claude sandbox behind the guard. Its commands are split on `;`, `|`, `&&`, `||`, newlines, `( )`, `$( )`, `@( )`, `@{ }`, `{ }` (script blocks are read as commands) and a closing `&`, with PowerShell's quoting: `'…'` (`''` a quote), `"…"` (backtick escapes, `$( )` inside read as commands), here-strings `@'…'@` and `@"…"@`, backtick line continuations, `#` and `<# #>` comments, typographic quotes and dashes; assignments (`$x = git …`) are stripped, a command's name is its file name without `.exe` and case (`& "C:\…\git.exe"` is `git`). The same rules then hold: `git`, `gh`, `az`, `curl` as in Bash; `Remove-Item -Recurse` and its aliases (`rm`, `ri`, `del`, `rd`, …) of a worktree (`delete-branch`); `Get-Content`, `Copy-Item`, `Select-String` and the like (and their aliases) of a secret, the `Env:` drive listed (`Get-ChildItem env:`) or a secret-looking variable read from it, and `$env:AZURE_DEVOPS_EXT_PAT` printed (`credentials`); `Invoke-WebRequest`/`Invoke-RestMethod` (and Windows PowerShell's `curl`/`wget` aliases) to Azure DevOps by `-Method` and route, and with `-Headers`, `-Credential`, `-Token` or `-Authentication` as an authenticated call; a command line handed to `pwsh`/`powershell` (`-Command`, or the first argument), `cmd /c` or `bash -c` (`sh`, `zsh`, …) is judged too. Paths may be Windows paths (`C:\Users\me\.ssh`, `~\.ssh`, `$env:USERPROFILE\…`); both matchers read backslashes as slashes and keep the drive, for every tool. What the guard can't read is **asked** rather than let through: a command named by a variable or an expression (`& $x`, `& (…)`, `. $x`), a string evaluated or a program started with what it is given (`Invoke-Expression`/`iex`, `Invoke-Command`, `Start-Process`, `Start-Job`, `Add-Type`, `Set-Alias`/`New-Alias`, `pwsh -EncodedCommand`), and a .NET method that runs, reads or deletes (`::Start(`, `.Invoke(`, `::ReadAllText(`, `::GetEnvironmentVariable(`, …), and a command the guard judges by its arguments (`git`, `gh`, `az`, the HTTP clients, `Remove-Item`, the readers, `cmd`, `bash`, `pwsh`, a mod rule's command) given an expression as an argument (`git push (…)`, `$(…)`, `@(…)`; what it holds is read as commands too). An ask is rule `unknown`: the mod lets it through `tool.call` and in `tool.check` turns Claude's `allow` into `ask` with the reason (`terminatr guard: it can't tell what this PowerShell command runs (invoke-expression), so it asks first.`), so the human's dialog, or auto mode's classifier, decides; Claude's own `ask` or `deny` stands. A refusal anywhere in the line wins over an ask. Writes by PowerShell (`Set-Content`, `>`) are, as Bash's, not the guard's.
    - **Where the rules come from:** the human's `config.toml` (`guard`, `guard_off`, `guard_mode`, `guard_allow`, `merge` and the secret environment's names, §11.2), never `PROJECT.md`, which the coordinator edits. On the mod socket `GET /v1/rules` answers `{"on","role","rules":[ids],"home","cwd","writable","worktrees","protected","secrets","tools","caseFold","mods","allow","own","secretEnv","learn","floor","noWrite","askEscapes"}` for the session (`410` once it is gone; `caseFold` is true where the file system ignores case in names, macOS and Windows, `internal/plat/caps`, and both matchers then compare paths with case folded, so `rm -rf ~/.Terminatr/worktrees` is refused as the worktrees folder); the mod asks at its first call after a load and again once its rules are a second old, so the human's settings and a guard mod enabled, disabled, updated or removed tighten or loosen running sessions at once, without a restart (the server answers from the rules it keeps, below). Outside a project, in a shell session, with `guard = false`, without the mod socket or when the server doesn't answer within 2 s, the guard is off and the session has exactly the rules it has without the mod; a `config.toml` that doesn't load leaves the guard on with the defaults. Only a failure of the check itself refuses the call.
    - **Rules from mods** (mods design §7, Tighten only; §5.3 **Guard rules**): `mods` lists the guard rules of the user's enabled mods that run for the session's project and role, each `{"mod","id","commands":[[words]],"writes","reads","message"}` with its paths absolute (`~/` the home folder, and each also with its links resolved) and the message on one line. Both matchers judge them as the core's: a shell tool's simple commands, read as above (a command's name without its folder, PowerShell's with case folded, and also under `caseFold`), each against the mods' commands before the core's rules; a write or patch tool's files against `writes` after `worktree-only`; a read or glob tool's against `reads` after `credentials`. A refusal is the rule `mod:<mod>.<id>` with the mod's sentence (`terminatr guard (mod:infra.no-destroy): …`), its summary the pattern's words or `<tool> of <path>`. They are in force with every core rule in `guard_off`, and off with `guard = false`: the guard switch is the human's. A mod can't list `guard_off` ids, turn the guard off or touch `merge`: such a manifest is invalid (§5.3).
    - **Where a write lands:** the mod has no file system, so before judging a write or patch tool's call it asks the server where each file the call writes lands: `POST /v1/resolve` with `{"paths":[…]}` (each made absolute, not cleaned) answers `{"paths":[…]}` in the same order, each with the symlinks (and on Windows the junctions) of its longest existing prefix followed, as without the mod (`Rules.Resolve`, below); a relative path comes back as sent, more than 256 is `400`. The call is judged where it lands and named as it gave the path. A write the server can't place within 2 s, or answers otherwise, is refused (`terminatr guard: where this write lands could not be checked …`, not journaled), never judged as written. Only calls a rule judges ask: a write or patch tool, with `worktree-only` or a mod's `writes` in force. The shared vectors carry `links` for such cases, which both matchers' tests follow the same way.
    - **Logged:** the mod posts each refusal, `POST /v1/denied` with `{"rule","tool","summary","subject","floor"}` (`204`; `400` for an unknown rule, or a mod's rule the session doesn't have), the summary its own short account (`git push with force`, `Edit of <path>`), never the call's text; the subject (the whole call) goes to the guard's log only. The server journals it (`<thread> guard.deny <thread> <rule> <tool>: <summary>`, or the coordinator's session id) and the thread's info panel (§4) lists its last three refusals from the journal ("Guard refused"). A refusal is not an inbox item: only a session refused 3 times within 10 minutes files one item of kind `guard` for the coordinator, and its count then starts again.
    - **Without the mod** (mods off, an older Claude, or an agent with no mod such as Codex) the server judges instead: the agent's `PreToolUse` command hook reaches it through `tm hook`, the server matches the payload's tool and input (`[hook]` `tool_field` and `input_field`, by default `tool_name` and `tool_input`) against the session's rules with the Go port of the matcher (`internal/guard`), and the manifest's `respond` template answers a refusal through `.Guard` before any dialog: Claude's `{"hookSpecificOutput":{"hookEventName":"PreToolUse","permissionDecision":"deny","permissionDecisionReason":"<sentence>"}}`, the same shape Codex reads; a PowerShell ask (`.Guard.Ask`) answers `"permissionDecision":"ask"` with the guard's reason. An allowed call gets no output, so the agent's own rules decide. Codex's `apply_patch` (`tool_input.command` is the patch) is held to `worktree-only` by each `*** Add|Update|Delete File:` and `*** Move to:` path. A write or patch tool's path is judged where it lands: the server follows the symlinks (and on Windows the junctions) of its longest existing prefix first (`Rules.Resolve`), and names the path as the call gave it. The server keeps a session's rules (for the hooks and `GET /v1/rules`) until what they are made of changes, `config.toml`, the project's `PROJECT.md` or an installed mod's `mod.toml` (by size and time), and at most a minute (the protected branches git says), journals each refusal as `POST /v1/denied` does (same line, same inbox rule; an ask is not journaled), and gives a session with the mod no hook guard. `claude.toml` and `codex.toml` each keep `tool_input` for `PreToolUse` with a `[[hook.keep_when]]`, or `tm hook` trims it away and the server has nothing to judge (checked live with mods off: a PR merge and a `Write` outside the worktree refused, `ls` let through). The hook's deadline (500 ms) is the guard's: a server that doesn't answer in time lets the call through, as a mod without rules does. `guard.ts` and the port pass the same vectors (`internal/agent/claude/mod/tests/guard-vectors.ts`); change them together. The access policy (§5.2), the sandbox and the prompts are as before.
- **Question menus.** An `AskUserQuestion` menu lists the options by number, then `Type something.`, a text field once focused. `[answer]` names the `blocked-question` rule, that option and Enter as its submit, for `tm thread answer` (§11.2).
- **Found against 2.1.289:**
  - The prompt box line is `❯` followed by a **no-break space** (U+00A0), which RE2's `\s` doesn't match; the empty-box rule (`inject.empty_rule`, used by the paste injector) allows for it.
  - A fresh session file has **no `status`** until Claude's first state change; until then the tracker uses hooks.
  - The status file is written ~100 ms **after** the hook: a hook newer than the file stands in for it for up to 1 s.
  - Claude saves a conversation only after the first prompt, so `--resume` of an **unprompted** session fails ("No conversation found"). The server records whether a session was prompted and relaunches unprompted ones fresh.
- **Not verified yet:** Linux (bubblewrap sandbox); `async` hooks; `PermissionDenied`/`StopFailure`/MCP elicitation; auto-compaction; the status file after a Claude crash (treated as invalid when the pid is dead); Ctrl+U to clear the input box; `skipDangerousModePermissionPrompt`; the `deleted` task status.

#### Codex

`internal/agent/manifests/codex.toml` plus `internal/agent/codex`, verified against codex-cli 0.160.0 on macOS, live in a tm pane. Codex keeps no status file: state comes from hooks, the rollout and the screen.

- **Launch:** `codex [resume <id>] -c check_for_update_on_startup=false -c 'projects={"<repo root>"={trust_level="trusted"}}' -c developer_instructions="<brief>" -c mcp_servers.terminatr.… [--approve-for-me | -a on-request | -a never] [-c default_permissions="tm" -c features.network_proxy=true -c permissions={tm={…}}] [-m <model>] -c hooks={…} -c hooks.state={…} -- <kickoff>`.
  - `resume <id>` is a subcommand, so it goes first. The id can't be chosen ahead: it is learnt from the hooks' `session_id`, and the first hook fires at the first prompt (for a thread, the kickoff in argv). `/clear` gives a new id, which the hooks report.
  - Folder trust is keyed by the **main repo's root**, a linked worktree's too (`.RepoRoot`, else `.Cwd`), and only the inline-table form is honoured: a dotted `projects."<dir>".trust_level` is not. Nothing is written to `~/.codex`.
  - The brief is a value (`toml (file .BriefPath)`); Codex sends it again as developer instructions after `/clear` and `/compact`.
  - `tm mcp` is the MCP server `terminatr`. Codex starts it outside its sandbox with a cleared environment; `env_vars=["TERMINATR_HOME"]` passes on the one variable it needs for a non-default home, and `default_tools_approval_mode="approve"` keeps its calls from waiting on approvals.
  - A coordinator runs with `--approve-for-me` (Codex's auto-review); any other session (a thread, a plain session) with `-a on-request`, so an escalation asks and `tm thread approve` answers it: under 0.160's default policy for a session started this way an escalation is refused without a dialog. With escapes off (`sandbox_escapes = false`, `LaunchSpec.NoSandboxEscapes`) it is `-a never`: Codex can't ask to leave the `tm` profile, which still applies (T574 case 3; checked on 0.162, T577). Yolo is `--dangerously-bypass-approvals-and-sandbox`, with neither.
  - **A thread's sandbox** is permission profile `tm`, the access policy (§5.2) on top of Codex's workspace-write, rendered with `pathmodes .Access`:

    ```
    -c default_permissions="tm"
    -c 'permissions={tm={extends=":workspace",
         filesystem={"<project dir>"="read","<repo>/.git/objects"="write","<repo>/.git/refs"="write",
           "<repo>/.git/logs"="write","<repo>/.git/worktrees/<wt>"="write","<repo>/.git/packed-refs"="write",
           "<repo>/.git/packed-refs.lock"="write","<repo>/.git/hooks"="read","<repo>/.git/info"="read",
           "<repo>/.git/worktrees/<other>"="read","<home>/config.toml"="read","<repo>/.git/config"="read",
           "<repo>/.git/config.lock"="read"},
         network={enabled=true,mode="full",unix_sockets={"<tm.sock>"="allow"},
           domains={"<host>"="allow",…},allow_local_binding=true}}}'
    -c features.network_proxy=true
    ```

    Its network is limited (T574 case 5): tm's socket and its host rules (`LaunchSpec.Sandbox.Domains`: the browser downloads preset's hosts, and for a Codex thread its **git remote hosts**) as the proxy's `domains`, each once, host to `"allow"`, wildcards (`*.example.com`) as Codex takes them; `domains` is left out when it has none, which leaves the socket alone. Its mode is `"full"`, not the coordinator's `"limited"`: limited also lets only GET, HEAD and OPTIONS through the proxy, so a `git push` over https (a POST) gets a 403; full keeps the domains (another host, and loopback, still refused; none without domains) and lets every method through (measured on 0.162: `git ls-remote` and a POST reach `github.com` under full, the POST is a 403 under limited). ssh remotes go through the proxy's SOCKS port (`GIT_SSH_COMMAND` with `nc -X 5`, set by Codex), under the same domains. `allow_local_binding=true` only with the local-ports rule (local dev servers): under the proxy loopback connects and binds are refused without it. Without `features.network_proxy`, either mode is silently open (T577; checked for full too), so before a thread starts the Go agent runs the coordinator's probe on its flags without the local binding (`limited`, probe.go): tm's socket connects, a direct connection to a loopback listener is refused, and, since the sandbox refuses every direct connection anyway, the probe also asks the proxy the sandbox's variables name (`HTTPS_PROXY`, as Codex sets them) to CONNECT: to `tm-probe.invalid:443`, in no domains, which must be refused, and to `localhost:<the listener's port>`, which only the probe's copy of the profile lists (a listed name that doesn't resolve is refused like an unlisted one, so the listed one names this computer and needs no internet), which must be taken (`unix=ok tcp=refused proxy=ok`; measured on 0.162: a 403 `x-proxy-error: blocked-by-allowlist`, and a 200; a Codex without the proxy feature answers `proxy=none`, one that ignores `domains` `proxy=closed`). The coordinator's probe makes the same check; when that doesn't hold it starts the thread in the closed form (`closedNetwork`: no `features.network_proxy`, `network={enabled=false,unix_sockets=…}` whatever the network said, no mode, domains or binding) and the launch's warning names the Codex version. On Windows a thread keeps the open form it had before T583, unprobed (`openOS`, as on Linux below; coordinator decision, 2026-10-19: the closed form would break `git push` and everything networked that worked there), with the launch warning and effective-view note `network not limited on Windows: Codex's proxy needs its elevated sandbox`; a Windows coordinator keeps its closed form (`closedNetwork`), as before. **On Linux no form both reaches tm's socket and limits the network** (`openOS`): Codex 0.162's seccomp filter refuses every `AF_UNIX` socket in proxy-routed mode unless `dangerously_allow_all_unix_sockets`, which would open every socket, the human socket too (`network.unix_sockets` names sockets on macOS only; `linux-sandbox/src/landlock.rs`, `NetworkSeccompMode::ProxyRouted`, and its README: "In managed proxy mode … seccomp blocks new AF_UNIX/socketpair creation"), and with the network off (`Restricted`) it denies `connect` altogether; CI's `TestSmokeCodexSandbox` saw both. So, by the coordinator's decision (2026-10-19: never break tm for a Codex thread, never wider than before), a Linux thread keeps the form it had before T583 (`openNetwork`: no `features.network_proxy`, `network={enabled=true,unix_sockets=…}` with its local binding, no mode or domains), unprobed: tm's socket connects, the network is open as it was, and the file limits hold. The launch warns `network not limited on Linux: Codex's Linux sandbox can't allow tm's socket with its proxy on`, and the effective view shows a tm rule `network open` with that note, its host rules marked not used (on Windows the same, with its own note). A coordinator's probe still requires the socket, so on Linux a coordinator runs without its profile, as before. `tm doctor`'s `codex thread sandbox` runs the same checks in a scratch folder.

    - Read is `read`, Write and WriteFiles are `write`, NoWrite and NoWriteFiles are `read` and win over a Write of the same path. The git paths are §8.6's (Access): none of the main repo's git dir is `write` but what a worktree's commits and pushes need. `deny` would block reading too.
    - Codex carves git dirs out of every broader grant: a linked worktree can't take `index.lock` in its own git dir until that dir is granted `write` by name. `--add-dir` and `sandbox_workspace_write.writable_roots` don't reopen it.
    - `tm.sock` needs `network.enabled`; the proxy keeps everything else but the thread's domains closed (above).
    - Checked on macOS (seatbelt) in a tm-hosted Codex thread (an isolated tm server, gpt-6-luna): `git commit` in the worktree, `tm task steps` and `tm report` over `tm.sock`, the project folder and `config.toml` readable but not writable. That test home was under `/tmp`, which `:workspace` makes writable: a `read` path carves write out of a broader grant. Without the profile (`codex sandbox`), the commit and the connect fail with `Operation not permitted`.
    - Checked on Linux (bubblewrap, codex 0.160.0, Ubuntu 24.04 CI runner) by `TestSmokeCodexSandbox`, which runs `codex sandbox -P tm` with the profile as rendered (no login): the worktree, a commit from it, a push from it writable, and a branch deleted in it on macOS only (deleting a ref takes `packed-refs.lock`, which bubblewrap can't grant: it binds only paths that exist); the main repo's git `hooks`, `info` and `config` and another worktree's git dir refused (also run on macOS, codex on seatbelt); `/tmp` and `$TMPDIR` writable; a sibling folder and the home folder refused; the project folder readable, not writable; `tm server status` over a `tm.sock` outside the writable roots. bwrap needs an unprivileged user namespace: with Ubuntu 24.04's default `kernel.apparmor_restrict_unprivileged_userns=1` every sandboxed command fails with `bwrap: setting up uid map: Permission denied` (CI sets it to 0; `tm doctor` warns).
    - Needs Codex 0.128 or later: `extends=":workspace"` and `codex sandbox -P` are in 0.128, not 0.125 (0.126 and 0.127 were never published); `default_permissions` is in 0.113. Read from the binaries' strings, not run.
    - A yolo thread or coordinator has no sandbox to shape.
  - **A coordinator's sandbox** is profile `tm` too, rendered with `pathmodes .Access .Cwd`, plus Codex's network proxy, kept only when a probe at launch says it holds on the installed Codex:

    ```
    -c default_permissions="tm" -c features.network_proxy=true
    -c 'permissions={tm={extends=":workspace",
         filesystem={"<project dir>"="write","<home>/worktrees/<slug>"="read","<home>/config.toml"="read"},
         network={enabled=true,mode="limited",unix_sockets={"<tm.sock>"="allow"}}}}'
    ```

    - The cwd (the project folder, its Read grant) is written `write` by name, by its real path. Codex lets the cwd's own write win over a `read` of the very same path (checked), but a `read` of a path below it does carve write out, so the grant isn't left to that tie.
    - Its network is the socket only: `mode="limited"` with no domains, enforced by the network proxy, an experimental feature in 0.160. Without the feature `limited` is not enforced and the network is fully open. So `gh` and `az` fail in the sandbox, escalate, and go to auto-review as they did before the profile (workspace-write, network off); a merge stays reviewed.
    - `coordinator_merges` (`Access.Commands`) is not mapped for Codex (keep auto-review): the coordinator's PR merge escalates and Codex's auto-review decides, with the setting on or off, as before the profile. The PR host's domain stays closed: allowing it in `network.domains` would open all of `gh`/`az`, not just the merge, and an execpolicy `allow` rule (a project's `.codex/rules/*.rules` loads) still runs behind the proxy.
    - **A newer Codex** may rename, change or drop the experimental `network_proxy` feature or the profile syntax. Without `--strict-config` (a flag only, no config key or variable; tm never passes it) Codex ignores a `-c` key or `features.<name>` it doesn't know and starts, so a renamed key would leave the coordinator unsandboxed or, worse, with the network wide open; with it, or for a changed value, Codex refuses to start. (`--enable <unknown>` is always an error, so tm sets the feature with `-c`; a profile without `default_permissions` is one too, and tm passes both.) So the Go agent (`internal/agent/codex/probe.go`) probes before a coordinator starts: `codex sandbox -P tm -C <cwd> -c features.network_proxy=… -c permissions=… -- tm codex-sandbox-probe <tm.sock> <loopback>`, the launch's own flags, with `tm codex-sandbox-probe` (a hidden command `cmd/tm` runs before anything else) dialling both. It holds when tm's socket connects and a TCP connection to a loopback listener tm opens for it is refused: limited mode refuses every address but the proxy's, so that needs no network and tells "proxy ignored" (TCP connects) and "profile ignored" (the socket doesn't) apart; a Codex error or exit, a timeout (15 s) or other output count as not holding. When it doesn't hold, the launch drops the profile's flags (the coordinator runs as before them: `--approve-for-me`, workspace-write, network off, `tm` calls escalated to auto-review) and carries a warning (`Launch.Warnings`) the server logs: `codex <version>: the coordinator's sandbox profile doesn't hold (<why>); running the coordinator without it …`. Verdicts are cached per binary (path, size, mtime) and flags. `tm doctor` runs the same probe against a scratch folder and socket (`agent.SandboxProber`) and warns under "codex coordinator sandbox", naming the version and the reason. Checked live on 0.160 (holds) and with a wrapper that renames the feature key (falls back, logged, doctor warns); the fake Codex's `sandbox` plays both and a refused key in e2e.
    - **On Windows** the network can't be limited to the socket. Codex 0.162 limits it to its proxy only in its elevated sandbox (firewall rules for a dedicated offline account, installed with administrator approval): its unelevated sandbox (a restricted token, the one tm sets) refuses managed networking ("managed networking requires the elevated Windows sandbox backend"), and `codex sandbox` on Windows never starts the proxy at any level, so the probe above could never hold (measured on 0.162.1, Windows 11 arm64: loopback TCP connects under the limited profile; read in codex-rs `cli/src/debug_sandbox.rs`, `windows-sandbox-rs`). So on Windows the Go agent gives the coordinator the profile's Windows form (`closedNetwork`): no `features.network_proxy`, and `network={enabled=false,unix_sockets={"<tm.sock>"="allow"}}`. The filesystem grants are the same; tm's socket connects through the write grant on its folder (as a thread's, `socketAccess`), and the network is off the only way the restricted token turns it off: `HTTP(S)_PROXY`/`ALL_PROXY` pointing at `127.0.0.1:9` and git's proxies alike (`NO_PROXY` keeps loopback), so `gh`, `az` and git fail in the sandbox and escalate to auto-review as on other systems, but a program that ignores those variables still connects (checked: loopback TCP connects, the variables are set). That is no weaker than the fallback (workspace-write with the network off is the same variables), and `tm` calls run in the sandbox instead of escalating. The launch's probe on Windows holds when tm's socket connects (whatever TCP does) and Codex takes the flags; `tm doctor` also checks, in a scratch folder whose parent the profile makes read-only, that a file outside the workspace can't be written (not at launch: `:workspace` on Windows includes `%TEMP%`, and no other folder is safe to try), and says under "codex coordinator sandbox" that the network is off only through proxy settings. Codex's elevated mode (a user's `[windows] sandbox = "elevated"` stands) would block the network for real under the same profile (the offline account's firewall rules); it is not checked.
