    - Checked on macOS (seatbelt) in a tm-hosted Codex coordinator (an isolated tm server, gpt-6-luna): `tm task add` and `tm context` over `tm.sock`, appending to `CONTEXT.md` and writing `memory/`, reading a thread's worktree; writing that worktree and `config.toml` refused (`operation not permitted`), `gh api user` refused by the proxy in the sandbox and run once escalated (auto-review). Under plain `:workspace` (`codex sandbox`) the socket connect fails with `Operation not permitted`.
  - Models: asked of `codex app-server` (JSON-RPC over stdio): `initialize`, `initialized`, `account/read` (the login: `apiKey`, `chatgpt` with its `planType`, `amazonBedrock`, or none while `requiresOpenaiAuth`) and `model/list` (`id`, `displayName`, `description`, `hidden`, `isDefault`, `upgrade`): the list Codex keeps for the login (`~/.codex/models_cache.json`, which tm doesn't read) or, logged out, the one built into the binary; the same over a ChatGPT login, logged out or with an API key, so it is not filtered by plan (0.162.0 lists `gpt-6-astra` on ChatGPT Plus, which runs it). A model the login refuses is learnt from the session (`[[jsonl_tail.refused]]`, below). tm passes no `-m` unless a model is chosen or set, so Codex runs its own default.
  - Any `-c` makes Codex run embedded, without its shared app-server daemon (the "⚠ 1 warning" in the footer says so), which is what tm wants: hooks inherit the pane's environment.
- **Hooks for one session, trusted for it** (Go, `internal/agent/codex`). Every event the manifest's `[[hooks]]` name gets one command hook, `"$TERMINATR_BIN" hook --agent codex` (timeout 5 s; 3 s for `SessionEnd` and `Interrupt`, which Codex clamps), in `-c hooks={Event=[{hooks=[{type="command",command=…,timeout=N}]}],…}`. Codex runs a hook only once it is trusted, so `-c hooks.state={…}` marks each one: the key is `/<session-flags>/config.toml:<snake_event>:0:0`, the value `trusted_hash="sha256:<hex>"` of the group's canonical JSON, `{"event_name":"<snake_event>","hooks":[{"async":false,"command":…,"timeout":N,"type":"command"}]}` (keys sorted, no spaces, no HTML escaping; codex-rs `hook_hash`). The command text never changes (the shell expands the variable), so neither does the hash. The hooks go before the kickoff.
- **Hooks review.** While any of the **user's own** Codex hooks is untrusted, Codex opens "Hooks need review" before the prompt. tm answers "3. Continue without trusting" itself (the `hooks-review` rule's `keys`): the user's untrusted hooks don't run in tm sessions, tm's do. The update screen, kept away by the flag, is skipped the same way ("2. Skip").
- **Hook → state:** `SessionStart` idle, answered with `additionalContext` (fires for `startup` and `resume` at the first prompt, and for `clear` and `compact`); `UserPromptSubmit` working; `PreToolUse`, `PostToolUse` and `PreCompact` working edges; `PermissionRequest` blocked/permission; `Stop` idle; `Interrupt` idle/interrupted (Esc on a turn **or on an approval**, so a blocked state can't go stale); `SubagentStart`/`SubagentStop` the `bg` counter by `agent_id` (they carry the parent's `session_id`); `SessionEnd` exited unless `reason=clear`. A question menu (plan mode's `request_user_input`) fires no hook: screen only.
- **Rollout** (`transcript_path`, `~/.codex/sessions/YYYY/MM/DD/rollout-*.jsonl`): `event_msg` `task_started` working, `task_complete` idle, `turn_aborted` idle/interrupted. `[jsonl_tail.usage]` reads `token_count` (checked on real rollouts: `last_token_usage.input_tokens` includes `cached_input_tokens` and is the context; `total_token_usage.total_tokens` tells a repeat; one turn per `task_complete`). A subagent writes its own rollout, which isn't read.
- **Screen rules** (fixtures in `internal/detect/codex_test.go`): folder trust, hooks review and update (blocked, priority 50); the approval ("Would you like to …" + "Press enter to confirm or esc to cancel") and the question menu ("Question k/N" + "enter to submit": "answer", or "all" on the last of several), "Implement this plan?", and `/new`'s "Where should the new conversation run?" (blocked, 40); `• Working (` + "esc to interrupt" (working, 20); the composer `› ` (idle, 0). The composer stays on screen while a turn runs, its placeholder ("Ask Codex to do anything") is **dim**, so the empty-box rule skips dim cells.
- **Priority prompts** (§8.6): a prompt pasted while Codex works is added to the running turn at once (`UserPromptSubmit` fires mid-turn; the `steer` feature is always on in 0.162.0), so `--now` pastes; `[inject] interrupt = "\u001b"`, `interrupt_paste = "after"`: Esc stops the turn (`■ Conversation interrupted`, idle/interrupted within about 3 s, the composer empty, a running command left as a background terminal), then the prompt is pasted. The app-server's `turn/steer` and `turn/interrupt` need the active turn's id, which no hook reports.
- **Prompts:** through `codex queue --thread <id> --message <text>` (`inject.prompt = "channel"`, the Go agent's `Prompt`, 1 s timeout), with the thread id the hooks report. The running TUI shows the message as the user's own prompt: at once when idle, as its own turn after the current one when busy, a draft in the composer left alone; the same with and without the shared app-server daemon (0.160). Exit 0 means only that Codex's durable queue has it (a thread with no TUI runs it on resume). A slash command reaches the model as text, so the agent refuses it; after `/clear` (`/new`, `/resume`, `/fork`) the TUI keeps the old thread loaded and a message queued for it runs there out of sight, while the new thread's id comes only with its first prompt, so the agent refuses every prompt until the hooks report another id; and before the first hook there is no id. A switch typed in the pane by hand fires no hook, so the core follows the keys sent to the pane (the optional `agent.Typist`, `internal/session/typed.go`): while the agent is idle or working it rebuilds the prompt box's line from typed text, pastes, Backspace and Ctrl+U, and on Enter hands the line to the agent, marked inexact when other keys (arrows, Tab, Esc, Alt or control keys) edited it; keys sent in any other state (an answer to a dialog) leave the box unknown. A line submitted during a turn counts too: 0.160 refuses a switch then, but tm doesn't rely on any version doing so. The Codex agent counts a line whose first word starts with `/` as a switch when that word is one of the four, could be a command-popup pick of one (no arguments and its letters in order in one's name: `/cl`, `/rsm`; Enter runs the entry the popup highlights) or the line is inexact. A note that was wrong (a pick of `/compact`, a `/clear` Codex refused mid-turn) ends at the next `UserPromptSubmit` naming the same thread, so it costs one pasted prompt. What this relies on, and what happens without it: the hooks' `session_id` (`SessionStart`, `UserPromptSubmit`; the hooks engine is said to date from 0.114, verified on 0.160 only) gives the thread id, and with no hooks there is no id, so every prompt is pasted and `Typed` does nothing; `codex queue` (0.149.0, its alphas from 0.148.0-alpha.22) fails on an older Codex and the prompt is pasted, the error logged; the four switch commands' names only widen what is pasted, so a version lacking one loses nothing; the popup's fuzzy pick is unverified, so any `/` line whose letters fit counts. Every failure falls back to paste, which reaches the visible thread whatever the version. All of these are pasted (bracketed paste, then Enter as a separate write: text and CR in one write is taken as a paste and not submitted), as is any prompt whose `codex queue` fails. `[inject] clear` is `/clear`, which starts over in place; `/new` first asks where to run.
- **Guard**: `PreToolUse` answers the server guard's refusal as a deny, which Codex honours before any approval; `[[hook.keep_when]]` keeps `tool_input` for it (Bash's `command`, apply_patch's patch text in `command`). Checked live in a tm thread: a PR merge command refused (merge), apply_patch adding a file in the home folder refused (worktree-only); both journalled. The temp folders stay writable under worktree-only.
- **Not verified yet:** Linux (the thread and coordinator profiles, and the network proxy, under landlock/bubblewrap too); approvals and answers through `tm thread approve`/`answer`; auto-compaction.

### 8.7 Adding a new agent

1. Write `~/.terminatr/agents/<name>.toml`, starting from a copy of `claude.toml`. Fill in `[identify]`, `[launch]` (including `resume_args` and the §7.8 kickoff), and the files the harness loads per session (a plugin dir, an `-e` extension, a settings file). Render `.Access` into the harness's permission and sandbox settings, so a thread can read the project but not write it, and can reach the socket. If the harness has no way to enforce read-only, say so in a comment; `tm agent list` then marks it `unenforced`.
2. Find the agent's best **level** signal. Is there a file it keeps current with its state (`[status_file]`), or a log it appends to (`[jsonl_tail]`)? Set `[identify] last_tested`; rather than pinning `tested_versions`, make the manifest check what the file says (an unknown value distrusts it), §8.8. Then map hook or extension events in `[[hooks]]`. Check for cases where a hook never fires (cancel with Esc, interrupts), because those are exactly what goes stale. Add a `counter` for background work, `session_field`, and `[hook]` trimming.
3. If it has a todo or plan tool, add `[[todos]]` entries: `replace` for a whole-list tool, `upsert` for diffs, `reset` on its context reset. Add a `respond` template on the event that fires after a context clear, if the harness has one.
4. List where it keeps its login under `[guard] secrets`, and any folder of its own that its threads write to outside the worktree under `[guard] writable` (§8.2). Map its shell, file-writing, patch and file-reading tools to their kinds under `[guard.tools]`, with the input fields that hold the command, path or patch (pi and opencode: lowercase `bash`, `edit`, `write`, `read`, paths in `filePath`); a tool left out is not guarded. If its hook payload names the event, the tool and its input other than `hook_event_name`, `tool_name` and `tool_input`, set `[hook]` `event_field`, `tool_field` and `input_field`; a plugin shim written for it should rather speak the canonical wire format (§8.2).
5. Add 3–6 `[[rules]]` for what only the screen shows: pre-hook dialogs (trust), blockers, and the idle prompt (with `skip_dim` if it shows ghost text).
6. `tm agent check <file>`, then `tm agent reload`. Start it with `tm session start --agent <name>`, or `tm thread start --agent <name>`. Use `tm agent explain <session>` while driving it through idle → working → blocked → idle.
7. Only if steps 2–3 can't express the harness's signals (a live protocol, a structured prompt channel): add `internal/agent/<name>/`, which wraps `agent.FromManifest`, calls `agent.RegisterGo` from `init`, and ships the manifest under `internal/agent/manifests/`. Add a golden test like `internal/agent/agent_test.go`.
8. To make it built-in: move the manifest into `internal/agent/manifests/`, and add fixture tests for its rules (captured emulator text for each state).

Built in besides Claude: **Codex** (§8.6, **Codex**). Expected next: **pi** (an `-e` extension file as data; Go only for `sendUserMessage` injection).

### 8.8 Tested versions and drift

Claude Code and Codex ship often. A release can rename a flag or a config key, change a hook payload, change the text tm matches on screen, or drop an experimental feature. **tm is not tied to an agent version**:
- A newer agent is supported until something actually breaks.
- The version is information only: each built-in manifest's `[identify] last_tested`, shown by `tm doctor`, `tm agent list`, `tm agent explain` and the session log. It never gates anything and never raises an alarm by itself. The built-in manifests pin no `tested_versions` (§8.2).
- tm checks what the agent actually does and falls back where it can.
- It logs what it finds changed (**drift**, below), and a weekly CI canary tries the latest releases.

**Versions.**
- Last tested: Claude Code **2.1.295** and Codex **0.162.0** (built and checked live on 0.160–0.162).
- `min_version` is the oldest version tm's generated files work with. `tm doctor` warns below it, naming `min_version_why`. Claude: 2.1.139 (exec-form hooks). Codex: none set.

**What tm relies on.** "Fragile" means undocumented or experimental: the first things to check when a release notes changes.

| Surface | Claude Code | Codex | Stability | When it changes |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Launch flags | `--plugin-dir`, `--settings`, `--session-id`, `--resume`, `--append-system-prompt-file` (not in `--help`), `--dangerously-skip-permissions`, `--model`, `--remote-control`, `--` | `resume <id>`, `-c`, `-m`, `-a on-request`, `--approve-for-me`, `--dangerously-bypass-approvals-and-sandbox`, `--` | documented, except Claude's hidden `--append-system-prompt-file` and Codex's newer `--approve-for-me` | the agent refuses to start: drift *early exit*, with its error |
| Config | `--settings` JSON: `permissions.allow/deny`, `sandbox.enabled`, `sandbox.network.allowUnixSockets`, `theme` (`custom:terminatr`, else `dark-ansi`, `light-ansi`); `<config dir>/themes/terminatr.json` (`name`, `base`, `overrides` by token), which Claude reloads live | `-c` keys: `check_for_update_on_startup`, `projects={"<dir>"={trust_level="trusted"}}` (inline table only), `developer_instructions`, `mcp_servers.terminatr.{command,args,env_vars,default_tools_approval_mode}`, `default_permissions` and `permissions.tm` (`extends=":workspace"`, `filesystem`, `network.unix_sockets`, `network.mode="limited"`; for a thread also `network.domains`, `network.allow_local_binding`), `features.network_proxy` | Claude documented; Codex's permission profiles are **fragile** (new), `network_proxy` **experimental** | Codex ignores an unknown key without `--strict-config`, so a renamed key is silent: only the canary sees it for a thread. A value it can't read refuses the launch (early exit). A coordinator's profile is probed before each launch and dropped, logged and warned by `tm doctor` when it doesn't hold; a thread's network is probed too and closed when it doesn't (§8.6) |
| Hook registration | plugin `hooks/hooks.json`, exec-form command hooks (`command` + `args`, 2.1.139+) | `-c hooks={…}` plus `-c hooks.state={…}`, trusted by a SHA-256 of the canonical JSON under `/<session-flags>/config.toml` | Claude documented; Codex's trust hash is **fragile** (reproduced from codex-rs, undocumented) | no hooks: drift *no hooks*; state from the status file, rollout or transcript, and the screen |
| Hook events | `SessionStart`, `UserPromptSubmit`, `PreToolUse`, `PostToolUse`, `PostToolUseFailure`, `PreCompact`, `PermissionRequest`, `Notification` (`permission_prompt`, `idle_prompt`, `elicitation_dialog`, `agent_needs_input`), `Stop`, `StopFailure`, `SubagentStart`, `SubagentStop`, `SessionEnd` | `SessionStart`, `UserPromptSubmit`, `PreToolUse`, `PostToolUse`, `PreCompact`, `PermissionRequest`, `Stop`, `Interrupt`, `SubagentStart`, `SubagentStop`, `SessionEnd` | Claude documented; Codex's `Interrupt` **fragile** | an event tm didn't register: drift *hook event*; a renamed one stops arriving (canary) |
| Hook payload and answer | `session_id`, `transcript_path`, `cwd`, `hook_event_name`, `source`, `reason`, `notification_type`, `tool_name`, `tool_use_id`, `tool_input`, `tool_response`, `agent_id`, `agent_type`, `prompt`, `last_assistant_message`; answers `hookSpecificOutput.additionalContext` and `permissionDecision: deny` | the same names (Codex mirrors Claude's), plus `trigger` | documented (Claude) | no `session_id`: drift *hook session*. Other missing fields lose only what they carry |
| Prompt channel | `uds-messaging` socket from the status file, `CLAUDE_CODE_MESSAGING_TOKEN`; only for the server's own held prompts | `codex queue --thread=<id> --message=<text>` | **fragile** (undocumented; Codex's is new) | the prompt is pasted (Codex) or dropped and logged (Claude). A Codex command that fails: drift *channel* |
| State files | `~/.claude/sessions/<pid>.json` (`status`: idle, shell, busy, waiting; `waitingFor`, `sessionId`, `version`, `messagingSocketPath`, `bridgeSessionId`); transcript JSONL (`[Request interrupted by user`, `system`/`turn_duration`); `~/.claude/tasks/<sid>/*.json` | rollout `~/.codex/sessions/YYYY/MM/DD/rollout-*.jsonl` (`event_msg` `task_started`, `task_complete`, `turn_aborted`, `token_count` with `last_token_usage`, `cached_input_tokens`, `model_context_window`, `rate_limits.primary.used_percent`) | **fragile** (undocumented) | an unknown `status` distrusts the file: drift *status file*. A changed rollout or transcript only loses that source and usage |
| Trust | `~/.claude.json` `projects["<dir>"].hasTrustDialogAccepted` (written for a thread's worktree) | the `projects` `-c` flag | **fragile** (Claude's file is undocumented) | the trust dialog shows: blocked / trust on the screen, the human answers |
| Screen text (§8.6 rules) | `Yes, I trust this folder`; `Yes, I accept` + `Bypass Permissions`; `detailed transcript`; `Do you want to ` + `❯ 1. Yes` + `Esc to cancel`; `Enter to select` + `to navigate`; `Type something`; spinners; `✳` title; `❯` box; `Disconnect this session`, `Remote Control disconnected` | `Trust this folder?`, `Hooks need review`, `Update available`, `Press enter to confirm or esc to cancel`, `Question k/N` + `enter to submit`, `None of the above`, `Implement this plan?`, `Where should the new conversation run?`, `• Working (` + `esc to interrupt`, the `›` composer | **fragile** (UI text, changes often) | a rule that stops matching can't be told from a screen that never showed it, so this isn't detected at run time. The other sources still carry the state; the canary looks for the text in each release |
| Env vars | sets `CLAUDE_CODE_ENABLE_TODO_TOOLS` (needed from 2.1.295), unsets `CLAUDECODE`, `CLAUDE_PID`, `CLAUDE_CODE_SESSION_*`, …; reads `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` | unsets `CODEX_THREAD_ID`, `CODEX_SANDBOX`, `CODEX_SANDBOX_NETWORK_DISABLED`; reads `CODEX_HOME` | **fragile** (undocumented) | a renamed variable is inherited by a nested agent; the canary checks the names |
| Other | `claude plugin list --json` (doctor's plugin check); the Mods API (early access, from `ModsMinVersion` 2.1.289, off unless turned on, with the hooks as fallback); the models probe: `claude -p --safe-mode --no-session-persistence --input-format stream-json --output-format stream-json --verbose` and the SDK `initialize` control request (`models[]`: `value`, `resolvedModel`, `displayName`, `description`, `supportsAutoMode`; `account`); a refused model: a transcript line with `error: "model_not_found"` | the models probe: `codex app-server`, `account/read`, `model/list` | tm asks the agent (§8.2 **Models**); it reads neither agent's models cache | the probe fails or its answer changes shape: the models are `unknown: probe failed: …` (doctor warns, the log notes it), threads run the agent's own default; a missed refusal shows in the agent |

**Drift at run time.**
- A session notes each kind of drift once, in the server log, with the agent's version and the last tested one: `session s-4: agent drift: codex 0.170.0, last tested 0.162.0: hook event "TurnEnded" is none tm registered (docs/SPEC.md §8.8)`.
- `tm agent explain` shows the notes as `drift`, beside `version` and `last_tested`.
- The version comes from the agent's `version_args` (cached per binary, read after the launch, never on its path), or from the status file when it names one. A newer version is logged once per session as information.
- The kinds:
  - *hook event*: an event the manifest doesn't register.
  - *hook session*: an event without the manifest's `session_field`.
  - *no hooks*: the screen has shown the agent working for 2 minutes and no hook event came.
  - *early exit*: the agent exited within 20 s of its launch, with its last three lines on screen (an unknown flag, a config value it can't read).
  - *channel*: the prompt channel's command failed (an exit status, or not found), so the prompt was pasted. The adapter's own refusals (no thread id yet, a slash command) are not drift.
  - *status file*: a value the manifest doesn't know.
- Nothing is refused because of a version, and every source keeps its fallback.

**The weekly canary** (`weekly.yml`, job `agent-canary`; `scripts/agent-canary.sh`, which refuses to run outside CI):
- Runs every week, and by hand, whatever main did. It installs the latest `@openai/codex` and `@anthropic-ai/claude-code` on a Linux runner and runs non-live checks only: a home of its own, so no login, and a dead proxy, so no model is called.
- It checks the flags and subcommands in `--help`.
- It runs the launch tm renders (`tm agent check --render DIR FILE`, which writes a sample launch's files and prints its argv and env):
  - Codex: `codex exec --strict-config` loads the `-c` flags, then each key group alone, so a failure names the key. tm's hooks, trusted by their hash, fire offline in `exec` with the fields tm reads, and SessionStart's context reaches the rollout.
  - Claude: in print mode it loads tm's plugin and settings and fires the exec-form hooks before it finds no login. `claude plugin validate` and `claude plugin test` run on the mod (`TestModPlugin`).
- It looks for the screen and file text of the table above in the binaries. That is a hint: text assembled at run time can be missed.
- It runs the models probe (§8.2 **Models**) through `tm doctor --json`: logged out, each agent's `logged_out` rule must match its answer (`unknown: logged out of …`), and Claude with `CLAUDE_CODE_USE_BEDROCK=1` must still list its models.
- A failure opens an `agent-drift` issue, or comments on the open one.
- Running agent binaries is for CI only, never on a developer's machine, which holds the user's logins.

**When bumping `last_tested`:**
1. Read the releases' notes for hooks (events, fields, trust), flags, config keys and permission profiles, the status file and rollout, and the dialogs tm matches (the table above).
2. Run the canary by hand (Actions, weekly, *Run workflow*): it must be green.
3. Run `make test-claude` or `make test-codex` (real agents, logged in, by hand; their version test logs a version newer than `last_tested`).
4. Capture any changed screen as a fixture in `internal/detect`.
5. Then bump `last_tested`. The mod's pin (`ModsMinVersion`, CI's `CLAUDE_CODE_VERSION`) moves only with the mod.

**Older versions** (notes, not gates; probed offline on macOS):
- Claude Code:
  - Exec-form hooks need 2.1.139 (doctor warns below it).
  - npm ships a native binary from 2.1.120, `cli.js` before.
  - The mod needs 2.1.289.
  - 2.1.295 enables the task tools only with `CLAUDE_CODE_ENABLE_TODO_TOOLS`; older builds ignore the variable.
- Codex:
  - 0.160 passed every check above offline.
  - In 0.100–0.130 the binaries lack several screens tm matches ("Trust this folder", "Hooks need review", the composer's placeholder). 0.100 also lacks the hook `permissionDecision` answer.
  - Codex builds 0.130 and older were killed on start on the test Mac (macOS 27), so their flags and keys weren't run.
  - `codex queue` dates from 0.149.0 (older: pasted); the hooks engine is said to date from 0.114; the thread profile's `extends=":workspace"` needs 0.128.
  - No Codex minimum is set; anything below 0.160 is unverified.

---

## 9. Worktree lifecycle

A worktree is a plain git checkout. Terminatr puts nothing in it: no `.terminatr/` folder, no `info/exclude` entry, no symlinks (§5.2). Everything the thread produces for the project goes through `tm`.

**Git in a worktree.** tm runs git one way (`internal/gitx`): no prompt, no optional locks, the C locale, no repository location from tm's own environment, `protocol.ext.allow=never`, a timeout, capped output, and git's last line of stderr (cleaned) in an error. A worktree is its thread's to write, so tm never lets it say where its repository is: every git run in it (resolve's and auto-close's check for changes, `thread.changes` and its fingerprint, `tm doctor`'s leftover worktrees) takes the common git dir from the repo tm recorded (for `tm doctor`, a repo of a project that has the worktree), checks that the worktree's `.git` is a file naming that dir's `worktrees/<name>` and that this names the worktree back (the main checkout: `.git` is that dir), and hands git both (`GIT_DIR`, `GIT_COMMON_DIR`, `GIT_WORK_TREE`), with no file system monitor and no hooks (`-c core.fsmonitor=false -c core.hooksPath=/dev/null`). For a linked worktree `GIT_DIR` is a folder tm makes for the run in `~/.terminatr/state/git` (on which every thread gets a deny rule, §8.6) and removes after: the worktree's `HEAD`, a `commondir` naming the common dir, links to its shared index files (at most 8, each a regular file, as the `index` must be), and `GIT_INDEX_FILE` its `index`; nothing else in its own git dir is read, not its `config.worktree` (with `extensions.worktreeConfig` on) nor its `commondir` (git's ref store reads that one even with `GIT_COMMON_DIR` set). These runs only read. A worktree that fails the check isn't read: `thread.changes` says `none`, resolve and auto-close keep it and say why (`not a worktree of the repository`), `tm doctor` keeps it. `git worktree remove`, run in the repo, gets the common dir and the same settings for the `git status` it runs in the worktree; when the repo has `extensions.worktreeConfig` on, that status would read the worktree's `config.worktree`, so tm's own check stands for it and the remove gets `--force`. tm's check for changes (resolve, auto-close, `tm doctor`) is `git status --porcelain --ignore-submodules=none --untracked-files=all`, so a `.gitmodules` the worktree wrote can't hide a submodule's changes, and resolve removes a worktree only once its thread's session has left the server's list (it waits up to 10 s after stopping it, else changes nothing). A thread's sandbox grant and deny rules in its git dirs (§8.6, Access) and the launch's `.RepoRoot` and `.GitDir` take the git dirs the same way, once at launch, from the thread record's repo and worktree, which must be the session's cwd; a thread whose folder fails the check gets none of them. The guard's rule on git configuration takes the record's repo's git dir alone, never asking the worktree, and covers every worktree's git dir there, so a `.git` changed mid-session leaves it whole. A main checkout (an adopted thread's, `checkout`) has nothing outside its agent's reach to check its git dir against: it is the recorded folder's `.git`, which must be a folder, and git isn't asked; its configuration files there get the same deny rules. Runs that must ask the worktree what it is (`tm thread adopt`, `git worktree repair` after a rename) get the same settings.

- **Create.** `tm thread start` with a repo:
  1. `git fetch origin`;
  2. base = `origin/HEAD`'s target, unless `--base` is given;
  3. `git worktree add --no-checkout -b <branch> <dir> <base>`, with no hooks and no file system monitor, then the checkout (`read-tree --reset -u HEAD`) as a pinned run (**Git in a worktree**): git makes the new git dir in the common dir, which a running thread's sandbox may write, so neither the gits worktree add runs there nor the checkout read what is in it (a restore re-adds a worktree the same way). `<branch>` is the `branch_name` template (§11.2) filled in, `tm/<slug>/<id>-<title-slug>` by default, and `<dir>` = `~/.terminatr/worktrees/<slug>/<id>-<title-slug>`;
  4. launch the agent with cwd = the worktree and the thread's access policy (§5.2).

  Without a repo, the thread gets an empty directory at the same place. It is never inside the project folder: that folder is read-only for threads, and it holds the coordinator's `CLAUDE.md`.
- **Adopt.** `tm thread adopt <session> [--task T12] [--title "…"]` (the coordinator on the user's go-ahead, or the human) makes an agent that is already running a thread, instead of starting one:
  - **What can be adopted:** a live session of this server outside every project (role shell, §3.4), in which an agent runs: started with `tm session start --agent <name>` (or the dashboard's agent session), or found running in a shell's foreground (§8.1, Identify). Its directory can be a linked git worktree, a repo's main checkout, or a plain folder. Refused, with a code (exit 1): a session that doesn't exist or has exited (`unknown-session`), a coordinator's or another thread's (`in-project`), a shell with no agent in it (`no-agent`), a task that already has a live thread or is done (as `start`). An agent running outside `tm` (another terminal, another tmux) has no pane here and can't be adopted: quit it and resume it in a `tm` session (`tm session start` a shell in its directory, then `claude --resume`), then adopt that session.
  - **What is recorded:** a new `thread.toml` as `start` writes one, with the next id: the title (`--title`, else the task's title, else the folder's name), the task (`--task`; linked and moved to `started` as `start` does), the agent (the session's), `repo` (the main checkout of the repository the session's directory is in; empty outside git), `branch` (the branch checked out there; empty when detached), `worktree` (the top of that checkout, else the session's directory), `session` and `agent_session_id` (the session's), and `adopted = true`; `base` stays empty (tm didn't branch it). When the directory is the repository's main checkout rather than a linked worktree, `checkout = true` too. `task.md` and `brief.md` are written as for `start`, and the journal says `thread.adopt t-0007 T12 <title> (session s-12)`.
  - **The session** becomes the thread's (`session.adopt`, §3.3): the server sets its role to `thread`, with the project and thread id, in its record and `sessions.json`. From then on everything that goes by the session's record treats it as a started thread's: the caller check (§11.1, the peer pid; the session's `TERMINATR_ROLE` variable still says `shell`, and the narrower of the two wins), so `tm report`, `tm status` and `tm done` from it are the thread's; the context re-injected after a clear (§7.8); the todo list mirrored into `STATUS.md`; the sidebar, the dashboard and the info panel; the ticker's state, report, PR and auto-close (§7.5); and a server restart, which resumes it as a thread. tm then queues a prompt (sent when idle, as `tm thread prompt`): `You are now thread t-0007 of project terminatr. Run tm skill thread, then read your brief at … and do what it says. Your earlier work in this session is part of the task.` It counts as the thread's last prompt.
  - **What doesn't change until a restart:** the agent keeps the access policy and permission settings it was launched with (an agent session outside a project has no project grants or restrictions, §5.2), and has no brief as a system prompt. `tm thread restart <id>` relaunches it as a started thread is (the thread's access policy, the brief, its conversation resumed); adopt itself never restarts it, so the work it is doing isn't cut off.
  - **Not capped:** the agent is already working; like a restart, adopt is not refused at the parallel threads cap, though the thread counts towards it from then on.
  - **Only on the user's go-ahead**: the session is the user's own, usually unsandboxed and in any folder, so an agent (the coordinator) adopts only a session the user asked it to with `T` on the dashboard (§4), whatever `start_threads` says; `--approved-by-user`, the agent's own word, is accepted and ignored. `T` records the go-ahead in the server (`session.adopt.ask {id, project}`, human-only, so only on the human socket: the inbox item, which an agent could write too, only tells the coordinator), with what runs in the session as the user saw it (the agent, its folder and, once known, its conversation id), until the session is adopted or exits, the server restarts, the user withdraws it (`T` again, §4) or an hour has passed (`AdoptAskTTL`, T335: a go-ahead the user forgot doesn't wait for good); it holds only while the session still runs that (another agent started in the shell since, or the same in another folder, needs `T` again); `tm thread adopt` asks it first (`check`) and `session.adopt` checks it again (`asked`) and uses it up. Without it the refusal is `needs-approval`, before the session is looked up, so it is the same for a session that doesn't exist or belongs to a project. The journal adds `(asked by the user)`. The human's own `tm thread adopt` needs no go-ahead.
  - **Closing:** resolve and auto-close treat it as a started thread, with one exception: a `checkout = true` thread's directory is the repository's own checkout, which tm never removes, and whose branch it never deletes (`kept checkout /Users/me/src/app (adopted; tm removes only worktrees)`). An adopted thread's linked worktree is removed (never forced) and its branch deleted under the usual rules; an adopted thread outside git keeps its folder.
- **Restart.** `tm thread restart <id>` reuses the worktree and branch, regenerates the brief, and resumes the agent session if it can. The thread's last report stays in `threads/<id>/REPORT.md`.
- **A repo that moved**. When a thread's recorded `repo` folder is gone, restart and resolve look for a repo of the project (its repo list, §5.1) that has the thread's branch: the repo moved there, so the record's `repo` becomes it and the worktree is reconnected (`git worktree repair <worktree>`, run in the repo). When none has it, restart refuses with `no-repo`, and resolve runs no git: it reports `repo … is gone`, keeps the worktree if it is still there, and resolves the thread.
- **Merge.** `tm thread merge <id|T<n>>` (coordinator; a thread is refused `coordinator-only`) is the only way the coordinator merges a thread's PR, so the policy is code, not prose and the agent never runs `gh`/`az` itself (an auto-mode classifier blocks a merge command it can't match to an allow rule exactly, and then flags the follow-ups too). In `tm`'s own process, through `codehost.Host` (GitHub and Azure DevOps), it takes one look and gives one answer, no polling: the project's `merge` must be `coordinator` (`merge-not-allowed`); the thread's PR is found by the thread's branch (`no-pr`), and must come from that branch of the repo's own origin, not a fork (`codehost.PR.Branch`, `codehost.InRepo`), with the report's PR line, when there is one, naming that same PR (`not-thread-pr`); `pr-closed` for a closed one; an already merged one is a no-op, exit 0; its checks must be `pass` on the PR's current head, also when none are reported yet (`not-green`); the host must call it `MERGEABLE` (`conflicting`; `not-mergeable` while the host hasn't said or gave no head). Then `Host.Merge` merges by the project's `merge_method` (§11.2) and nothing else: GitHub `gh pr merge <n> --merge|--squash|--rebase --match-head-commit <head>` (a squash adds `--subject '<PR title> (#<n>)'`, the title read from the PR just before, a title that can't be read refusing the merge; the body is left to GitHub; a merge commit and a rebase keep the host's message), Azure DevOps a completion with `mergeStrategy` `noFastForward`, `squash` or `rebase` and the checked head as `lastMergeSourceCommit`, the source branch kept; a squash also sets `completionOptions.mergeCommitMessage` to `<PR title> (!<n>)`, which replaces Azure DevOps' whole default message (`Merged PR n: …` plus the description); tm reads such a commit's trailing ` (!n)` as it reads `Merged PR n:` (`Azure.MergeCommit`, `Azure.MergedPR`), so the ticker still sees the squash at once. A host that refuses the method (GitHub's `Merge commits are not allowed on this repository`, likewise for squash and rebase, or a branch rule requiring linear history; an Azure DevOps branch policy limiting merge types; a code host mod's `refused`) is `merge-method-not-allowed`: the refusal names the methods the repo allows where the host says (GitHub's repo settings, less the refused method; Azure DevOps doesn't say) and points at the Merge method setting. Any other refusal (the head moved, a required review is missing) is `merge-failed`. Journaled `thread.merge <id> PR #n merged at <head> (<method>)`; the success line names the method too (`t-0003 merged PR #7 (squash, head 0123456, checks green)`). `coordinator_merges` (§11.2) allows exactly `Bash(tm thread merge:*)`; the coordinator rules name it as the only merge, its own command. Not built: a ticker-side `merge = "auto"`.

- **Resolve.** `tm thread resolve <id>`:
  1. stop the session;
  2. `git worktree remove <dir>`, **never forced**; a dirty worktree is kept and reported;
  3. delete the local branch **only if nothing on it is lost**: its PR is merged (asked of the repo's code host: `gh`, or `az` on Azure DevOps, where a completed PR counts as merged however it was merged, squash too, and an abandoned one as closed), or every commit on it is already on the default branch (`origin/HEAD` as last fetched, else the repo's checked-out branch; for repos without a remote or merged by hand). An unmerged branch, a squash-merged one without a merged PR, or one checked out elsewhere is kept; the remote branch is never touched;
  4. do the same for the thread's other branches (a thread that opens a second PR from a second branch): the head branch of every PR its reports named (`PR:` lines of `REPORT.md` and `reports/*.md`, the head and state asked of `gh`), and every local `tm/<slug>/<id>-…` branch. Each is deleted with `git branch -d`, never `-D`, and only when it is merged into the default branch (`origin/HEAD` as last fetched, else the checked-out branch), checked out in no worktree, and has no commit that no remote-tracking branch has. `-d` is checked against that default branch, not the checkout's `HEAD`, which may lag origin: the branch's upstream is pointed at it for the delete, and put back if git refuses. An open PR's branch is kept; any other kept branch is listed with why (`kept branch tm/demo/t-0004-second (not merged into origin/main)`), and `tm doctor` offers it later once it is merged;
  5. write one inbox item that lists what was removed and what was kept, and one journal line per deleted branch (`coordinator branch.delete t-0004 tm/demo/t-0004-second (PR #12 merged, merged into origin/main)`).

  **Discard**. `tm thread resolve <id> --discard` (coordinator; the coordinator skill has it used only on the user's word in chat) is for a thread that made no PR and left files in its worktree, which plain resolve keeps. The server's `tm`, not git run by an agent, removes the worktree with `git worktree remove --force` (uncommitted changes go with it) and deletes the thread's own branch with `git branch -D`, journaled as `branch.delete <id> <branch> (discarded)`; the resolve item says what was removed. It refuses, with code `discard-refused` (exit 1) and nothing touched, when the branch has commits no remote-tracking branch has (in a repo without remotes, commits not merged into another branch), when the thread is adopted (its checkout is the user's), when it has no repo to remove from, or when git can't say. The thread's other branches (step 4) are handled as in plain resolve. To keep a worktree clean, threads put output that makes no PR (research, reviews, notes) in the thread's library: they write it outside the worktree and hand it in with `tm report --attach` (§8.2); the thread skill and the brief say so. Then the branch has no commits and plain resolve and auto-close remove both.

  There is no copy-home step, because reports and attachments already live in the project folder. That is why removing a worktree any other way (by hand, `git worktree prune`, `git clean -fdx`) loses nothing.

  **Auto-close.** The ticker resolves a finished thread by itself, under the same rules, as the project's `auto_close` says (§11.2): `merged`, once its PR merged; `days`, `auto_close_days` days after it finished, i.e. after the earlier of its `tm done` (when no prompt came after it) and its PR's merge; `off`, never. In every case its agent must be idle, exited or stopped, and its worktree must lose nothing: no uncommitted changes, and no commit that isn't on a remote-tracking branch or the merged PR's head commit (a repo without remotes has nowhere to push, and resolve keeps its branch unless the default branch has its commits). A thread with such work stays open and the coordinator gets a `close-held` item. A thread without a repo has only a folder, which resolve keeps unless it is empty.
- **Parallel threads cap.** At most `parallel_threads` threads of a project (§11.2, default 10) may work at once. A thread counts while it is open (not stopped or resolved), its session runs, its agent is not idle (working, blocked or not yet known; a just-started agent is working until it takes its kickoff, §8.4), and it hasn't called `tm done` since its last prompt. At the cap, `tm thread start` and `tm task delegate` refuse with code `over-cap` (exit 1) and say how many work; the coordinator proposes the thread instead and starts it once one finishes. `--over-cap` starts it anyway, once: the coordinator adds it only when the user says so in chat, and it is journaled as `(over the parallel threads cap, approved by the user)`; it also counts as the user's go-ahead under `start_threads = "propose"`. Restarting a thread is not capped.

  **Across projects**. `global_parallel_threads` (§11.2, `[defaults]` only; unset or `0` is no such cap) caps the threads working at once in all projects together, counted by the same rule. Each start checks the project's cap first, then this one: at it, `tm thread start` and `tm task delegate` refuse with code `over-global-cap` (exit 1) and say how many work in which projects (`2 of 2 threads across all projects are working (1 in demo, 1 in other)`); only projects with a running thread session are read. The coordinator handles it as `over-cap`, and `--over-cap` passes both caps, journaled the same way. `tm context`'s Safety line shows the cap after `parallel_threads`, with the count by project while the server runs (`global_parallel_threads=8 (5 working: demo 3, web 2)`). **Slot-free nudge.** Each refusal at either cap records the project's coordinator as waiting (`state/slots.json`: one waiter per project, which keeps its place in line; `thread.Refused`), with what it wanted to start (the `--task`, else the title). On every sweep the ticker counts the free slots and tells the waiters, oldest first, one per free slot: a waiter is due when its project is under its `parallel_threads` and, with `global_parallel_threads` set, all projects are under it. It gets one `slot-free` item (`a thread slot is free: 9 of 10 working in demo, 19 of 20 across projects; you were refused (over-global-cap) at 14:02 for T52 …`), and the slot is held for it for 10 minutes (it counts as working for the waiters behind it), so one free slot never tells two coordinators. The waiter goes when its coordinator's next start passes the caps, when the hold ends, after a day, or when its project is archived or inactive; a paused project's waiter waits without an item until it is resumed. Needs no link.
- **Leftovers.** `tm doctor` lists worktrees under `~/.terminatr/worktrees/<slug>/` with no open thread, plus `tm/<slug>/…` branches merged into the default branch whose thread is resolved or gone (a second PR's branch left by an older resolve, or one merged after it). It removes nothing without `--fix` and a TTY confirmation. `--fix` deletes such a branch as resolve does its other branches (`git branch -d`, after checking again that nothing on it is lost) and journals it in its project (`human branch.delete t-0004 tm/demo/t-0004-second (merged into origin/main, tm doctor --fix)`).

---

## 10. The `tm` CLI

These commands are used by the human, the coordinator and threads alike. Exit codes follow §6.3 everywhere. `--json` is available on every read command. This table lists every command and flag (`TestSPECListsEveryCommand` in `cmd/tm` checks the commands); each command's own usage (`tm task help`, `tm thread help`, the message of a wrong call) says the same in short. `tm help` lists the commands (built from the CLI's own table); an unknown command prints `tm: unknown command "…"` and that list, and exits 2.

| Command | Who | What |
|---|---|---|
| `tm [--own]` | human | a console of view `main` (§3.3, Views): the dashboard or the layout it shows, as every other console of `main`; `--own` gives the console a view of its own |
| `tm --no-mods [<command> …]` | human | safe mode (§3.7, Supervisor): sets `TERMINATR_NO_MODS=1` for this tm and so for a server it starts, which runs no user mods; with no command, the dashboard |
| `tm attach [<session>]` | human | one session (the newest when none is named) beside the projects sidebar, in a bare view of this console's own, which no other console follows; the prefix then `d` leaves; a click on the sidebar switches to the full UI on view `main` |
| `tm server run [--detached] \| start [--no-launchd] \| stop [--yes] [--force] \| restart [--yes] [--no-launchd] \| status [--json]` | human | §3.1: `run` is the server itself, in the foreground unless `--detached` (how auto-start launches it on Linux; `--launchd` is how launchd runs it on macOS); `start` and `restart` go through launchd's GUI domain on macOS (`--no-launchd`: from this session, without the keychain over SSH); `stop` asks while agents run (`--yes` skips; `--force` SIGKILLs a hung server); `status` prints pid, uptime, version, protocol, sessions and the last restart's resumed and lost sessions |
| `tm server service install\|uninstall [--print]` | human | the login service (§3.1); `--print` shows the file and installs nothing |
| `tm project new <slug> [--goal "…"] [--repo PATH]… [--json] \| list [--json] \| open <slug> [--agent A]` | human | create a project folder named by the lower-case slug (§5.1); `open` starts the coordinator (role coordinator, cwd the project folder, remote control per `coordinator_remote_control`) unless one runs, then attaches with the status bar, in a bare view of its own as `tm attach`; without a terminal it prints the session id |
| `tm project remote on\|off [<slug>]` | human | turn the running coordinator's remote control on or off (§11.2); refused when no coordinator runs or its agent has none |
| `tm project pause\|resume [<slug>]` | human | pause or resume the project (§11.2): while paused no nudges, no PR follow-up, and thread starts refused (`project-paused`); state polling goes on. Also the Settings tab's `Paused` row |
| `tm project activate\|deactivate <slug> [--yes]` | human | activate the project, resuming its dormant coordinator and threads, or deactivate it, stopping them and keeping them to resume (§5.1); `deactivate` asks first on a terminal while any of them runs, otherwise it needs `--yes`. Also the sidebar's `space` and the Settings tab's `Active` row |
| `tm project archive\|unarchive <slug>` | human | hide the project from the sidebar and the dashboard and stop the ticker's work for it, or bring it back (§5.1); archiving is refused while its agents run (`sessions-running`). Also the Settings tab's `Archive` row (asks first) |
| `tm project delete <slug> [--yes]` | human | move the project folder to `~/.terminatr/.trash/` (§5.1): on a terminal it asks for the slug, otherwise it needs `--yes`; refused while its agents run. Also the Settings tab's `Delete` row (asks first) |
| `tm project rename <slug> <new-slug> [--json]` | human | rename a project's slug, its only name: its folder, worktrees folder, `config.toml` table and thread records move with it, git is told where the worktrees went, and a running coordinator is restarted under the new slug (§5.1). Refused while a thread of it runs (`sessions-running`) or when the new slug is taken (`project-exists`). Branches keep their names |
| `tm project repo add\|remove PATH [--project <slug>]` | human; coordinator `remove` only | change the project's repo list (§5.1 **Repos**); `tm thread start` defaults to the first repo. Each repo is one a thread can get a worktree of, so an agent's `add` is refused (`human-only`, T315): the user adds one (`+` in the project popup's overview) |
| `tm context [--thread <id>\|<task>] [--project <slug>] [--json]` | coordinator; `--thread` all (a thread: its own project) | §7.6; `--thread` previews a brief's memory notes, §7.1 |
| `tm skill coordinator\|thread` | agents | print the standing rules, versioned with the binary (§7.8) |
| `tm mcp` | agents | a session's tools (a thread's report, status, steps, done, and user mods' tools) as an MCP server on stdio, for agents without the mod (§8.6) |
| `tm task add\|list\|show\|status\|edit\|steps\|archive\|unarchive\|delegate\|help …` | all | §6.3, which lists every flag; threads only read, and add or tick steps on their own task (§6.4); the coordinator sets `done` only with `--approved-by-user` |
| `tm thread start [--task T12] [--agent A] [--model M] [--repo PATH] [--base B] [--approved-by-user] [--over-cap] "title"` | coordinator | §9; runs the project's `thread_agent` unless `--agent` names another (§8, §11.2); refused if `start_threads = "propose"` and the human hasn't approved (§11; `--over-cap` is the user's word on the cap only, so under propose it goes with `--approved-by-user`), at the parallel threads cap or the cap across projects (`over-global-cap`) without `--over-cap` (§9), for an agent with a `--repo` that isn't one of the project's repos (`unlisted-repo`, T315), with an agent that isn't installed (`agent-not-installed`), none installed (`no-agent`), or several installed and none named by `--agent` or the settings (`agent-not-chosen`), all before anything is created, with `--model` while the agent's models are unknown (`models-unknown`: logged out, the probe failed, no lister), a model the agent doesn't offer (`unknown-model`, §8.2), one the user's account refused (`model-refused`) or the project's `models` setting leaves out (`model-not-allowed`, §11.2), or while the project is paused (`project-paused`, also for `tm thread restart` and `adopt`). The thread keeps its model across restarts; `tm thread list`/`show`, `tm context` and the info panel show it |
| `tm thread adopt <session> [--task T12] [--title "…"]` | coordinator, human | make a running agent session outside the projects a thread (§9, **Adopt**); the dashboard's `T` asks the coordinator to (§4), and the coordinator adopts only a session the user asked for so |
| `tm thread list [--all] \| show <id> \| read <id> [--lines N] \| help` | coordinator | state, report, screen text. `list` shows the open threads and counts the resolved ones; `--all` adds them and the archived ones (`--json --all` gives `{threads, archived}`); `show` reads an archived thread from `threads/archive/` (§7.6). Here and below, `<id>` is a thread id (`t-0003`) or a task id (`T12`), which names the task's open (unresolved) thread; a task with several open threads, or only resolved ones, is refused (`ambiguous-task`, `no-open-thread`) with their ids, which still reach each one |
| `tm thread prompt <id> "text" \| --next N [--now \| --interrupt]` | coordinator | queue a prompt (sent when idle; refused while blocked). `--now` steers it into the running turn, read at the agent's next tool call; `--interrupt` stops the turn for it, only on the user's word (§8.6, **Priority prompts**). The output says how it went in (`queued`, `channel`, `steered`, `interrupted`) and why a `--now` or `--interrupt` prompt went another way |
| `tm thread approve <id> [--choice N]` | coordinator | answer a permission prompt (§11.2) |
| `tm thread answer <id> [--choice N[,N…]] [--option LABEL]… [--text T] [--question K]` | coordinator | relay the user's answer to a question menu (§11.2): through the mod by number, label or the user's words, else by keys on the screen with one `--choice N [--text T]` |
| `tm thread ack <id> \| stop <id> \| restart <id> \| resolve <id> [--discard]` | coordinator | acknowledge a report, stop, restart, resolve; `--discard` also removes a dirty worktree and its branch unless the branch has unsaved commits (§9) |
| `tm thread merge <id\|T<n>>` | coordinator | merge the thread's PR by the project's merge method (merge commit, squash or rebase) when merge = coordinator, checks are green on its head and the host says mergeable; else a refusal code (§9) |
| `tm status --percent N --activity "…" [--needs-you "…"] \| --unknown [--activity "…"] \| --needs-you "…"` | threads | progress, §7.3 |
| `tm report [--file F] [--attach F]… \| --show` | threads | hand in the report (stdin or file), §7.2 |
| `tm run -- <command> [args…] \| --list` | threads | have tm run one of the project's approved commands outside the sandbox, in the worktree, output streamed, exit code passed on; `--list` lists them (§5.2, approved commands) |
| `tm done ["summary"]` | threads | §7.3. `tm status`, `tm report` and `tm done` also take `--project <slug> --thread <id>`, for a human acting for a thread outside its session (a thread's own call always means its own thread) |
| `tm inbox list [--json] \| done <id>…` (`--project <slug>`) | coordinator | §7.5; handling a `link-message` item marks its message read in both projects |
| `tm link send <project>\|<server>/<project> "text" \| --file F\|- [--re M-12] [--attach <thread>/<file>]… \| read M-12 [--file NAME] [--json] \| log <project> [--last N] [--json] \| list [--json] \| approve\|drop M-12 [--approved-by-user] \| resume <project> [--approved-by-user] \| accept R-4F2A-18 [both\|send\|receive] [--ask\|--no-ask] \| decline R-4F2A-18` (`--project <slug>`) | coordinator (its own project), human; threads refused (`coordinator-only`); accept and decline the human's (`human-only`) | messages to linked projects' coordinators, §7.9; accept and decline answer a paired server's link request, §16.11 |
| `tm project link <a> <b>\|<server>/<b> [both\|send\|receive\|off] [--ask\|--no-ask] [--request]` | human (`human-only` for an agent) | links two projects in one call (§7.9, §11.2): a's entry as given (both by default), b's the mirror, off removes both; `--ask` is a's ask switch. For a paired server's project only a's entry, asking unless `--no-ask`; `--request` also asks that server's user to link back (§16.11) |
| `tm clear [--project <slug>] [--json]` | coordinator, human; threads refused (`coordinator-only`) | save and clear the coordinator as auto-clear does (`coordinator.clear`), §7.5 **Auto-clear** |
| `tm ask add "question" [--task T12] [--header H] --option "Label[: description]"… [--multi] [--recommend LABEL] \| add --file F\|- \| list [--json] \| done Q3… \| open` (`--project <slug>`) | coordinator, human; threads refused (`coordinator-only`) | the coordinator's open questions, §7.5 **Questions** |
| `tm memory find [<words>…] [--path <path>]… [--limit N] [--json] \| check [--thread <id>] [--json] \| template` (`--project <slug>`) | all (a thread: its own project) | the memory notes matching words and paths, best first (§7.6, **Memory notes**); `check` the notes a thread's diff speaks against (a thread its own, others `--thread`); `template` prints a new note with the schema. It makes the `memory-search` example mod's tool a core command |
| `tm memory new [<name>] [--file <path>\|-] \| edit <note> [--file <path>\|-] \| rm <note> [--yes] \| index` (`--project <slug>`) | human, coordinator | change the notes in `memory/` (an editor on the human's terminal, with the overwrite guard), then write `MEMORY.md`'s list again; the human's change is a `memory` inbox item (§7.6, **Memory notes**) |
| `tm library list [--json] \| rm <thread> <file>\|--all` (`--project <slug>`) | list: human, coordinator; rm: human, and the coordinator only on the user's word in chat; threads refused (`coordinator-only`) | §7.2, §4 |
| `tm session list [--json]` | human (an agent: its own session, a coordinator also its project's, §11.1) | the sessions in the server, with their role, project, agent and state |
| `tm session start [--cwd D] [--cols N --rows N] [-- CMD…]` | human | a shell session (or `CMD`) outside the projects, sized to this terminal unless `--cols`/`--rows` |
| `tm session start --agent A [--cwd D] [--brief F] [--kickoff T] [--model M] [--yolo]` | human | an agent session of the user's own: `--brief` is given to the agent as its system prompt, `--kickoff` is its first prompt, `--yolo` skips its permission prompts (refused in an agent call). Internal, for tests and the server's own launches: `--role coordinator\|thread\|shell`, `--project <slug>` and `--thread <id>`, which make the session a project's; people use `tm project open` and `tm thread start` |
| `tm session read <id> [--scrollback] [--json] \| keys <id> [--enter] "…" \| prompt <id> "…" \| stop <id>` | human | the screen as text; `keys` types raw text (`--enter`, before or after it, presses Enter after it; text that starts with `-` follows a `--`); `prompt` queues a prompt, pasted once the agent is idle (`-` reads it from stdin) and prints `queued` or `sent`; `stop` ends the session |
| `tm session wait <id> [--state S[,S…]] [--timeout 30s]` | human, tests (an agent: its own session, a coordinator also its project's, §11.1) | wait until the session's agent state is one of the states (any change when none is named); exit 1 at the timeout |
| `tm session ask <id> < QUESTION.json` | mods | open a question menu on the session (§3.3, **Ask**) and wait: prints the answers as one JSON object by question text once every question has one (exit 0), or exits 1 when it closed unanswered; killing it takes the question down |
| `tm watch --project <slug> [--json]` | anyone, mods (an agent: its own project, §11.1) | a project's dashboard (§3.3, **Watch**: `project.watch`), then a line on each change, until the server goes away. `--json` prints each as one `ProjectWatch` object per line, for the coordinator's /tm pane (§8.6); plain lines are for people: `demo · 2 need you · 1 in inbox · 4 threads · 3 on deck` |
| `tm watch [--session <id>] [--json]` | anyone, mods (an agent: its own session, a coordinator also its project's, §11.1) | a session's state (§3.3, **Watch**), then a line on each change, until the session ends (exit 0); the session defaults to `$TERMINATR_SESSION`. `--json` prints each state as one `Watch` object per line (NDJSON) for a mod to read; plain lines are for people: `s-3 thread working · T12 started 2/4: Write tests · #12 open, checks pass · 1 needs you · 3 in inbox`. Exit 3 when the server goes away, or when it is older than `session.watch` (restart it) |
| `tm access show [<project>] [--thread <id>] [--json] \| explain [<project>] [--thread <id>] [--json] -- <command>` | human; the project's coordinator, for its own project only (T603); a thread `show` for its own access only | read-only (§5.2, **One resolver**): `show` lists a new thread's access as rules (where, kind and value, source; skipped ones marked), or with `--thread` that thread's launch record (`threads/<id>/access.json`), RESTART TO APPLY on each rule config.toml added or removed since, and the agent's own files' rules shown only; `explain` says inside or outside the sandbox, whether it asks first, and the guard's verdict |
| `tm access add <kind> <value> [<project> \| --all] [--without-asking] \| rm <kind> <value> [<project> \| --all] \| drop\|undrop <value> [<project>] \| set local-ports\|escapes\|yolo on\|off [<project> \| --all] [--yes]` | human | the sandbox lists (kinds `write`, `host`, `guide`, `socket`, `excluded`, `approved`, `allow`) and settings of a project's table, the one here when none is named, or all projects' with `--all` (§11.2 `sandbox`), checked as the Access tab checks them (`config.CheckNewSandboxEntry`; an entry all projects give or the project drops refused saying so); `--without-asking` makes an excluded command `ask = false`; `drop` takes only an entry of all projects'; `yolo on` asks first (`--yes` without a terminal). `access` is a checked command (§11.1): an agent gets `human-only` whatever its environment says, and a write with no server to tell is refused; the guard's floor refuses them, and `tm access preset add`, to an agent past its sandbox. Journaled in each project reached (`settings.sandbox hosts + …`, `settings.yolo yolo = true`) |
| `tm agent list [--json] \| check <file> \| reload \| explain <session> [--json]` | human | §8: the agents tm knows, a manifest checked without loading it, the manifests read again, and why a session is in its state (§8.4) |
| `tm hook --agent <name>` | harness hooks | §8.2 |
| `tm mod host [--budget D] <bundle.js \| main.ts>` | the server | one code mod's host, speaking the mod host protocol on stdin and stdout (§3.7); not for people |
| `tm doctor [--fix [--yes]] [--json]` | human | checks and changes nothing; `--fix` offers to remove leftovers and restart an old server after a `y` on a terminal (`--yes` instead, needed without one); `--json` for scripts; exit 1 only when a check fails. Checks: toolchain (with `gh auth status`: a warning when `gh` isn't logged in or can't read its token, since the ticker's PR polls need it, §7.5; the code-host lines (`gh`, `gh auth`, the `az` lines, `git origin`) are the running server's, from `server.codehost`, since over SSH on a Mac this shell has no login keychain while the server's sessions do: each carries `source` "server" in `--json` (a field added to every check, empty for the others) and `[server]` in the text; where this shell fails a check the server passes, a `local shell` note (ok, source "local") says so instead of a warning; with no server, or an older one, the checks run in this shell and are labelled `local`), install method and newer release, server (a server of an older protocol is a warning whose fix is `tm server restart`; on macOS, a server whose sessions can't reach the keychain is a warning whose fix is a restart from the Mac, §3.1; a session whose queued prompts are held while its agent is idle is a `prompt queue` warning, §8.6), sockets, manifests, hooks, enabled Claude plugins known to be unsafe in tm sessions (`claude plugin list --json`; a warning naming the reason and `claude plugin disable <id>`, no fix; the list is `unsafePlugins` in `internal/doctor/plugins.go`, today `worktrees@supermods`, whose "Remove N finished" removes clean worktrees with no commits ahead, which includes a fresh thread's), leftovers, settings tm no longer has (`complete_tasks = "released"`, read as `user`, in a project's table or `[defaults]`), and the installed agents and their models (§8.2 **Models**; doctor asks each installed agent again: a fail `agent` line when none is installed, naming the agents tm knows; per installed agent an ok `<agent> models` line, `12 models, asked 2.1.296 today (Claude Max, firstParty)`, with the models the user hid, added and the account refused, or a warning `unknown: <reason>` (logged out, with the login to run; probe failed; no lister); a fail for a `thread_agent` / `coordinator_agent` that isn't installed or tm doesn't know, naming who sets it; and warnings for several agents installed with none chosen for all projects, a `default_model` the agent doesn't offer now, the older `[agents.<name>] models` list, `[agents.<name>]` of an agent tm doesn't know, a manifest's ignored `[[models]]`, a `coordinator_model` its agent doesn't offer, and each model in a project's scope no installed agent offers, naming who allows it), and the paired servers (§16.8: nothing for a server never paired, else one `peers` line with each link's state, a warning for a refused link, an old protocol or an unreachable relay) |
| `tm mod list [--outdated] [--json] \| show <id> [--json] \| install <path\|git-url[//subfolder][@tag]\|github-tree-url> [--yes] \| enable <id> [--project <slug>] [--yes] \| disable <id> [--project <slug>] \| remove <id> [--purge] [--yes] \| update [<id>] [--yes] \| validate [<dir>…] [--json]` | human; list, show and validate: anyone | user mods, §5.3: install by path or git URL only, consent on a terminal (`--yes` without one) |
| `tm mod new <id> [--kind column\|hook\|theme\|icons\|keymap\|rules] [--dir <dir>] \| dev <dir> [--yes] \| test [<dir>…] [--json] \| logs <id> [--follow\|-f] [--lines\|-n N]` | dev: human; the rest: anyone | the mod author's tools, §5.3: scaffold, run from a folder with a restart on every save, replay fixtures with a fake broker, read a mod's log |
| `tm relay serve [--listen ADDR] (--domain NAME [--state DIR] [--email ADDR] \| --cert FILE --key FILE \| --plain) [--trusted-proxy CIDR]… [--max-conns N] [--max-per-ip N] [--bandwidth MiB/s] [--total-bandwidth MiB/s] [--log-ips] [--update-request FILE] [--auto-update on\|off]` | human (`human-only` for an agent) | the relay two linked servers meet through, on the user's own host (§16): `--listen` defaults to `:443`; TLS from Let's Encrypt for `--domain` (TLS-ALPN-01, cached in the state directory, renewed by itself), from the certificate files, reloaded when they change, or `--plain` for a TLS proxy in front, whose `X-Forwarded-For` counts only from a `--trusted-proxy`; runs until SIGINT or SIGTERM |
| `tm relay install (--domain NAME [--email ADDR] \| --ip [ADDR]) [--auto-update=on\|off] \| update \| uninstall` | human, as root on Linux with systemd (`human-only` for an agent) | the relay as the `tm-relay` systemd service: install (with a daily update timer unless `--auto-update=off`, and a path unit for linked servers' update requests), update to the latest release (sha256 checked) and restart only when newer, remove (§16.6) |
| `tm peer pair [--relay URL] [--as ALIAS] \| join CODE [--as ALIAS] \| list [--json] \| remove ALIAS \| rotate [--yes]` | human (`human-only` for an agent) | pairing this server with another through a relay by a one-time code and a six-digit check, and the paired servers (§16.8) |
| `tm update [--check [--json]] [--yes] [--restart]` | human | §10.1 |
| `tm version` (or `--version`), `tm selftest`, `tm help` | anyone | the version and build id; `selftest` writes a line through libghostty-vt to show it is linked; `help` (or `-h`, `--help`) lists the commands |

Every agent-facing command prints short, stable, plain text. It never prints untrusted text (report bodies, PR comments) except in a clearly delimited block.

### 10.1 Releases, install and update

- **Releases.** release-please (`.github/workflows/release-please.yml`) keeps a release pull request open on main with the next version and its `CHANGELOG.md` entry, from the Conventional Commit messages since the last release; merging it tags the merge commit, creates a draft release with that entry as its notes and the tag's push starts `.github/workflows/release.yml`: release-please and the tag act as a GitHub App of the repository, since a pull request or tag made with `GITHUB_TOKEN` starts no workflow. A `v*` tag pushed by hand runs it too. It runs on one macOS runner. goreleaser cross-compiles darwin and linux × amd64 and arm64 with `zig cc` (§12), except that Apple's clang and ld link darwin: chained fixups keep `__DATA_CONST` clean (zig's linker left ~2.4 MB dirty in every tm process), at minimum macOS 13.0, both checked by `scripts/release/check.sh`. A build hook (`scripts/release/sign.sh`) signs each darwin binary with the Developer ID (hardened runtime, secure timestamp, identifier `dev.terminatr.tm`) and notarises it with `notarytool`, before it is archived. The archives, `tm-relay-docker.tar.gz` (§16.6) and `checksums.txt` go to that draft release (goreleaser's `use_existing_draft`, `mode: keep-existing`, no changelog of its own), or to a new one for a hand-made tag; `scripts/release/check.sh --signed` checks what was uploaded, Gatekeeper's verdict included; then the release is published, `Formula/terminatr.rb` is rewritten from `checksums.txt` in a pull request that merges itself (opened as the same App, so CI runs on it), and a Linux job pushes the relay image (§16.6) to ghcr.io. Snapshots (`make release-snapshot`, CI) skip signing and say so.
- **Gatekeeper.** A bare Mach-O can't carry a stapled ticket; Apple records the notarisation against its cdhash. A quarantined copy (a browser download) is accepted after an online check; curl and Homebrew formulae set no quarantine flag, so nothing is checked. `spctl --type execute` rejects every bare binary; `spctl --assess --type open --context context:primary-signature` reports `source=Notarized Developer ID`.
- **Homebrew.** One formula for macOS and Linux (`brew tap theclifmeister/terminatr https://github.com/theclifmeister/terminatr`, `brew install terminatr`), with `on_macos`/`on_linux` × `on_arm`/`on_intel` archives. Not a cask: casks are macOS-only, and the formula installs the signed binary unchanged.
- **Install method.** `internal/update` tells three apart: a source build (`version.Channel` empty: `make`, `go build`), Homebrew (the resolved executable is under a `Cellar/` or `Caskroom/`), and a direct install (any other release binary).
- **`tm update`.** Looks up the latest release through the GitHub API, unauthenticated; when the API refuses (60 requests an hour per address) it reads the tag from the `releases/latest` redirect instead.
  - Source build: refuses (exit 1) and says `git pull && make`.
  - Homebrew: never touches Homebrew's files; prints `brew upgrade terminatr` and runs it after a `y` on a terminal or with `--yes`.
  - Direct: asks on a terminal (`--yes` skips; without a terminal it needs `--yes`), downloads the platform's archive and `checksums.txt`, checks the sha256, extracts `tm` next to the installed one, checks it on macOS with `codesign` (a valid Developer ID signature with the hardened runtime, of the same team as this build's `version.TeamID`), runs `version` on it, and renames it over the installed file.
  - Windows: the archive is a zip with `tm.exe`, `conpty.dll` and `OpenConsole.exe`, all three installed. A running exe or loaded dll can be renamed but not overwritten, so `fsx.SwapIn` moves each old file to `<name>.old-<utc>` and renames the new one into its name (the program last; a failure restores what was replaced, and the error names the processes that hold the file, from the Restart Manager, `proc.Holders`). `CleanOld` deletes the `.old-*` files that nothing runs, at the next update; `scripts/install.ps1` does the same. No folder is renamed (Windows 11 refuses to rename the folder of a running exe), so no copy of `tm` in `%TEMP%` is needed. `install.ps1` is also a release asset (`releases/latest/download/install.ps1`).
  - The server: it keeps running the old binary (pinned, §3.6). `tm update` says so and how many sessions it has, and that a restart ends running turns (agents resume, shells are lost). It restarts the server only with `--restart`, or after a `y` on a terminal, and then `tm server restart` still asks when agents are mid-turn. Otherwise it prints `tm server restart` for later. The restart runs the new binary's `tm server restart`, which stops a server of any protocol (§3.3), so it works even when the old server is older than the `tm` that runs the update.
  - **Newer-release hint**. The server asks for the latest release itself, at most once a day: `session.list` consults `<home>/state/update.json` (`internal/update.CachedLatest`: the tag and when a check was last tried, a failed try included, so an offline machine makes one attempt a day and keeps the last tag) from a goroutine, never in the poll's way, at most hourly. It runs only for a release build (`version.Channel` set), and not with `[ui] update_check = false` or `TERMINATR_UPDATE_URL=off`. A newer tag goes out as `latest` and `upgrade` (`brew upgrade terminatr` for Homebrew, else `tm update`) in `session.list`; the dashboard shows it as its footer hint (§4, **Version**).
  - `--check` only reports the install method, path, and latest release (`--json` for scripts). `tm doctor` shows the same as its `install` group; `TERMINATR_UPDATE_URL=off` turns the network check off (the e2e harness does).

---

## 11. Permission model

### 11.1 Human calls and agent calls

The server tells them apart by the caller's pid (§3.2). Every project command (`tm task`, `thread`, `report`, `status`, `done`, `inbox`, `context`, `library`, `ask`, `project`) and `tm mod` and `tm skill` ask it with `caller.who` when they run in the CLI: the server walks the peer pid's ancestors to a hosted session. The CLI combines that answer with the session environment (§3.4) and keeps the narrower of the two, so neither unsetting the variables nor leaving the session's process tree widens an agent's rights. Without a server, read-only commands still work and the environment alone decides:

- If the calling process descends from a hosted session's process **and** that session's role is `coordinator` or `thread`, the call is an **agent call**.
- Everything else is a **human call**: a shell outside terminatr, or a hosted `shell` session.

A user mod is a third kind of caller, `caller.Mod`, journaled as `mod:<id>`. It never comes from a process: only the server's broker makes it, for the mod's brokered calls (§5.3). It has fewer rights than any agent, and every human-only check refuses it as it refuses an agent (`IsAgent`).

A paired server is the narrowest caller, `caller.Peer`, journaled as `peer:<alias>`. Only a link's dispatcher makes it (§16.9), never the environment or a socket, and it reaches only the read-only `peer.*` methods, `peer.message`, which delivers a linked project's message (§16.11), and `peer.attach`, whose stream types into a shared project's coordinator or thread while this server's pane switch for that peer is `type` (§16.12); every human-only check refuses it too.

Agent calls of project commands (`tm task`, `thread`, `report`, `status`, `done`, `inbox`, `context`, `project`) run **inside the server** (`cli.run`): the CLI sees `TERMINATR_SESSION`, sends its arguments, cwd and (when the command reads it) stdin, and the server runs the command with the caller it derived from the peer pid's process tree. That also lets a sandboxed thread's `tm report` write the project folder it can't write itself (§5.2). Without a server, the command runs in the CLI with the caller from the environment.

This is stronger than herdr-projects' TTY check, because an agent's own shell has a TTY. It is still **soft**: an agent with a shell could, for example, start a detached process outside its tree. This document says so plainly, as herdr-projects' docs do.

The human-only methods of the socket protocol, the console's above all (mod views, mod commands, notifications, a session's clear, and the press tokens of `mod.action.check` and `mod.action.done`), have a second gate that doesn't depend on the process tree: they answer only on the human socket (§3.2), which no agent's sandbox allows. An OS sandbox is inherited and can't be shed, so a detached or re-parented process of a sandboxed agent stays off it. What it doesn't cover: an agent running without an OS sandbox (a Claude coordinator, whose Bash isn't sandboxed; a yolo session; a command the human or auto mode lets out of the sandbox, unless `sandbox_escapes = false` keeps a Claude thread's in it, §5.2) reaches the human socket like any process of the user's, and only the pid walk stands between it and a human call there. `session.answer` has the same gate for the human, and is also open to the coordinator of the session's own project (§3.3 **Ask**). The server socket's other ways to act as the human are closed the same way: `session.start` (a shell session's processes are the human's), `session.stop`, `session.keys`, `session.prompt`, `session.adopt`, `session.adopt.ask`, `session.remote`, `session.model`, `questions.open`, attach, the `view.*` methods, `server.stop` and `agent.reload` answer only on the human socket, and `cli.run` on `tm.sock` only to a caller the pid walk places in an agent's session, so a process past the pid walk can't run human-only commands as the human. The sandbox's socket allow list is checked on macOS; on Linux it is not yet verified. On Windows Codex's sandbox reaches a socket by file access and is granted the run dir (§8.6), so the human socket is no gate there; the session's job (`Session.Holds`), which a process can't leave, settles who calls instead.

The methods that name a session or a project are scoped to the caller (`mayAddress` in `internal/server/scope.go`, from each method's entry in `internal/server/methods.go`): the human on the human socket names any; every other caller names what the pid walk allows, which also gives the session it found the caller in (`Caller.Session`, a shell's too). `hook.event` and `session.ask` name only that session: `tm hook` and the agent's mod run inside it. `session.read`, `session.wait`, `session.watch` and `agent.explain` name it too, and a coordinator also its project's sessions. `session.list` answers such a caller with those same sessions only, and without what only a console shows: the mods' notices (`mod_notices`) and `mods` (columns, the cells of projects, tasks and threads, status segments, toasts), and says so (`scoped`): `tm doctor` then leaves session runtime dirs unchecked, since it can't tell another project's live session from a leftover. `project.watch` names the agent's own project. `view.digest`, a test's request that a console check its mirror, answers on either socket (an e2e run inside a thread can't reach the human socket) but never to a caller in an agent's session. A caller the walk finds in no session names none of them on `tm.sock`: the human's own `tm` uses the human socket. Refusals are `refused`. The walk stops at the server's own pid, so what the server runs (the in-server CLI, mods) stays the human's even when a `tm` inside an agent's session started the server and still lives. A request asks the walk once, for its gates and its handler.

A forwarded command runs as the human in the server, so each checks its caller itself: a coordinator may read another project but changes only its own (`other-project`: tasks, the inbox, the library, questions, `tm ask open`, `tm link send`; a message to another project goes only through a link, §7.9), and the live session state a read command shows (`tm thread list` and `show`, `tm context`) is the agent's own project's only. A thread reads only its own project through `tm`: every command that takes `--project` or resolves a project (from `TERMINATR_PROJECT` or the cwd), `tm task`, `thread`, `context`, `report`, `status`, `done`, `skill` and the rest, refuses any other with one answer, `other-project: a thread sees only its own project`, given before the slug is looked up, so a project that exists and one that doesn't answer alike (`ownProject` in `internal/cli/project.go`); a thread with no project names none, and `tm project list` shows it its own project only. The socket methods that name a project (`project.watch`) were already the caller's own (above); `tm inbox`, `tm library`, `tm ask`, `tm link` and `tm mod enable|disable|run` refuse a thread whatever project it names. The limit is `tm`'s, not the disk's: a thread's sandbox may still let it read other projects' folders under `~/.terminatr/projects/` (§5.2), so this keeps their data out of what `tm` hands a thread, not out of reach of a file read. A thread call is further limited to its own thread: `tm status`, `tm report`, `tm done`, read commands, and `tm task steps` on its own task. Everything else exits 1 with `coordinator-only`.

Human-only operations:
- `task status … done` (§6.4), which the coordinator relays with `--approved-by-user` once the user accepted the work, and the ticker applies under the user's `complete_tasks` setting;
