- changing safety settings, or adding a repository to a project (§5.1 **Repos**). These live in `~/.terminatr/config.toml` under `[projects.<slug>]` and `[defaults]` (all projects), not in `PROJECT.md`, so the coordinator editing `PROJECT.md` can't touch them. `tm` writes them only from the TUI's settings popups and the sidebar's `p` (pin), on the human's keypress, and from the human-only `tm project activate|deactivate|pause|resume|archive|unarchive` (which refuse agent calls, §11.1): no socket method changes them but the human-only `project.active` and `project.rename` (§3.3), the dashboard refuses (`human-only`) when it runs inside an agent's session, and coordinators keep their `Edit` deny rule on the file;
- activating, deactivating, pausing, resuming, archiving, unarchiving and deleting projects (`tm project activate|deactivate|pause|resume|archive|unarchive|delete`, the sidebar's `space` and the project popup);
- `tm server stop|restart`;
- `--fix` in `tm doctor`;
- approving proposed threads.

File access is enforced separately, by the agent's own permission rules and sandbox (§5.2). The two layers back each other up: an agent that gets around `tm`'s caller check still can't write the project folder, and one that gets around a file rule still can't change tasks without `tm`.

### 11.2 Settings and approvals

The human changes these in the project popup's Settings tab, or for every project at once in the `,` popup's All projects tab (§4, **All projects** below), where they have plain labels and a line each on what they do, or by editing `config.toml` (§5.1). Each change from the popup is journaled in the project's `JOURNAL.md` (`human settings.yolo <slug> true`). The table names the keys for the file; the UI never does.

| Setting | Values | Default | Meaning |
|---|---|---|---|
| `start_threads` | `propose` / `auto` | `propose` | `propose`: the coordinator lists proposals, and threads start only after the human's go-ahead (the human tells the coordinator, or confirms `D` on a task in the task list (§4), which then calls `tm thread start --approved-by-user`; it is journaled) |
| `yolo` | bool | `false` | no sandbox and no prompts (T574 case 4): launch with the manifest's `yolo_args` (Claude `--dangerously-skip-permissions`, Codex `--dangerously-bypass-approvals-and-sandbox`), and a Claude thread's launch settings say `sandbox.enabled = false` (tm's `--settings` file outranks the project's and the user's own, T577), so its sandbox lists aren't written and show as not used. tm's `Edit(...)` deny rules are still written, and the guard keeps its floor only (§8.6), which holds `config.toml` and the project folder. Allowed, per the user's decision. It can only be turned on by a human call with a TTY confirmation |
| `coordinator_approves` | bool | `true` | the coordinator may answer in-scope permission prompts of its own threads with `tm thread approve`; never one to run a command outside the sandbox (an escape, §8.6 Guard), which is always the user's |
| `parallel_threads` | 1–99 | `10` | the most threads that may work at once; `tm thread start` refuses beyond it unless `--over-cap`, which the coordinator uses only on the user's word (§9) |
| `global_parallel_threads` | 0–999 | `0` (no cap) | `[defaults]` only: the most threads that may work at once across all projects, counted as `parallel_threads` counts them; `tm thread start` refuses beyond it with `over-global-cap` unless `--over-cap` (§9). A project's table setting it is an error. The All projects tab's Threads across projects row (`off · 3 working now`; `enter` steps through off, 5, 10, 15, 20, 30, `+` / `-` by one); once set, a project's Parallel threads row adds the count against it (`10 · 3 working now · 7 of 20 across projects`) |
| `auto_close` | `off` / `merged` / `days` | `merged` | when the ticker resolves a finished thread whose agent rests: never, once its PR merged, or `auto_close_days` after it finished (`tm done` or its PR merged); never with uncommitted or unpushed work (§9, §7.5). The older `auto_resolve = true/false` reads as `merged` / `off` when `auto_close` is unset; saving auto-close in the popup removes that project's `auto_resolve` line in the same write (unless it is in a form the line editor doesn't change) |
| `auto_close_days` | 1–365 | `7` | the days of `auto_close = "days"` |
| `complete_tasks` | `user` / `merged` | `user` | when a task is done. `user` ("by you"): only on the user's word. `merged` ("when merged") is the user's standing acceptance: the ticker marks a task in `review` done once its thread's PR merged; journaled, and the coordinator gets a `task-done` item. Never for a task without a merged thread PR or owned by `me`; `x` on a done task sends it back (§6.4, §7.5). The removed `released` reads as `user`; `tm doctor` warns until the user picks again in Settings |
| `pr_poll_seconds` | 30–3600 | `120` | how often the ticker asks the PR host about each unresolved thread's PR and fetches the project's repos (§7.5); a value outside the range makes the settings file invalid (`tm doctor` reports it). Set in `[defaults]` or a project's table, no popup row yet |
| `pr_followup` | bool | `true` | the ticker prompts a thread when its PR's checks fail, a reviewer requests changes, or the default branch moves and leaves its open PR conflicting (§7.5; merely behind is not asked) |
| `thread_agent` | an agent's name | unset: the one installed agent | the agent new threads run (`tm thread start`, `tm task delegate`) unless `--agent` names another. Unset, it is the only installed agent (shown `codex (auto)`; nothing is written); with several installed and none named, the dashboard asks once when a project opens and saves the answer as all projects' `thread_agent` and `coordinator_agent` (§8.2 **Installed agents**), and until then `tm thread start` without `--agent` is refused (`agent-not-chosen`). The popup's Thread agent row steps through the installed agents, so it writes only an installed name; a name that isn't installed is flagged under the row and in `tm context`, and refused, never replaced by another (`agent-not-installed`; `unknown-agent` for a name tm doesn't know). An empty name or one with spaces is an error |
| `coordinator_agent` | an agent's name | unset: the one installed agent | the agent a new coordinator runs (a click on the project, `tm project open` without `--agent`); a running coordinator keeps its agent until it is started anew. Resolved, asked for and refused as `thread_agent` is. The popup's Coordinator agent row, as Thread agent's. In `[defaults]` it replaces the older top-level `default_agent` (§5.1); the project popup's info tab names the agent a new coordinator would run, and its model |
| `models` | list of model names | unset (every available model) | the project's scope: the models the coordinator may pick for a thread, a subset of the available ones (what the installed agents offer, §8.2 **Models**), new ones included while unset. An empty list, a name with spaces or a repeated name is an error. `tm context` lists only the models in scope and a line saying the user limits them; `tm thread start` / `tm task delegate` refuse another with `model-not-allowed` (a name no installed agent offers is `unknown-model`); without `--model` the agent's default runs (the user's `default_model` only while it is in scope). The popup's Thread models row opens a list to allow or leave out each available model (at least one stays; with all allowed, All projects drops the line, a project keeps the full list and `x` makes it follow All projects). An allowed name no agent offers any more is never dropped silently: the list shows it `stale: no agent lists it` (enter leaves it out), `tm context` says `stale: … is allowed but no installed agent offers it now`, `tm doctor` warns, naming who allows it, and `--model` with it is `unknown-model` |
| `coordinator_model` | a model name | unset (the agent's default) | the model the coordinator runs, from the project's scope and its coordinator agent's models. A change reaches the running coordinator: switched live with its agent's switch command once it is idle (Claude: `/model <name>`), the conversation kept; an agent without one (Codex) keeps its model until its next start, which the coordinator's status bar and the row say (§8.2 **Models**). Threads keep the model they were launched with. The popup's Coordinator model row steps through them, the agent's default first. One its agent doesn't offer now (unknown, hidden, refused, out of scope, another agent's) passes none, so the coordinator runs its agent's default; the row flags it and `tm doctor` warns |
| `archive_tasks_days`, `archive_threads_days`, `archive_inbox_days`, `archive_journal_days` | 1–365 | `30` each | retention (§7.6, **Retention**): the days a done task stays on the board, a resolved thread's folder stays unpacked, a handled inbox item stays a file of its own, and a line stays in `JOURNAL.md`, before the ticker moves them to their archives. The popups show one `Keep history` row (one age when all four agree, else the four); enter lists the four, each stepped with enter (7, 14, 30, 60, 90, 180, 365 days), changed by a day with `+` and `-`, and made to follow All projects with `x` |
| `coordinator_merges` | bool | `false` | the coordinator's launch settings carry a permission allow rule for `Bash(tm thread merge:*)` (§9; never the raw host merge commands, which an auto-mode classifier blocks unless the command matches the rule exactly), so an auto-mode classifier that doesn't see the project's standing merge rule stops refusing the coordinator's merges (§5.2). The user's opt-in, popup row `Coordinator may merge`. Only effective with `merge = "coordinator"`; a thread never gets the rule and the guard still refuses its merges. A Codex coordinator gets nothing from it: its merge escalates to Codex's auto-review either way, and the PR host's domain stays closed to its sandbox (§8.6, Codex). Applies from the coordinator's next launch |
| `merge_method` | `merge` / `squash` / `rebase` | `merge` | how `tm thread merge` merges a PR (§9): a merge commit, one squash commit (the PR's title and number as its subject), or the PR's commits rebased onto the base without a merge commit. A repo whose rules require linear history refuses `merge` (`merge-method-not-allowed`). Popup row `Merge method`; a mod can't set it |
| `fast_forward_checkout` | bool | `true` | the ticker fast-forwards the user's own checkout of each repo to origin's default branch when that branch is checked out, clean and only behind; otherwise `tm context` and the overview say how far behind it is (§7.5) |
| `branch_name` | a template | `tm/{project}/{id}-{title}` | the branch of each new thread: `{project}` (the slug), `{id}` (`t-0468`), `{task}` (`T497`, empty without a task) and `{title}` (the title slug, at most 40 characters). It must contain `{id}`, every other `{…}` is refused, the result must be a branch name tm takes (`gitx.ValidBranch`, the rule the code hosts' PR branches are read by too: at most 100 characters of `A-Z a-z 0-9 _ . / -`, not starting with `-` or `/`, and passing git's `check-ref-format --branch` rules, checked in Go so resolving the setting runs no program; checked with and without a task and a title, and for a 64-character project slug without a title), and an empty `{task}` or `{title}` takes the `-` or `/` after it, else before it, so no doubled, leading or trailing separator is left; otherwise it is refused with the reason, on a write (`CheckSetting`) and at load (so `tm thread start` refuses before the thread's record exists), and never changed. A thread's title slug is shortened further when the name would be over 100 characters, and the filled-in name is checked again (`thread.BranchName`). Existing threads keep their `branch`; nothing parses the `tm/` prefix to find a thread's branch (resolve and `tm doctor` use the record's `branch`; only the extra branches of §9 resolve step 4 and the doctor's leftovers are found by the default `tm/<slug>/<id>-…` name). A safety key: a mod's manifest can't name it (`SafetyKeys`). The Settings tab's Branch names row edits it, with a preview. |
| `sandbox_escapes` | bool | `true` | a thread's agent may ask to run a command outside its sandbox, which the human or auto mode approves (§5.2). `false`: Claude threads start with `allowUnsandboxedCommands: false`, from their next start or `tm thread restart`; a server restart's resume keeps what the thread started with. With it off, what the sandbox refuses can't run in a thread at all: listening on local ports, unix sockets other than `tm.sock` (Docker's among them), ptys, writes outside the worktree and `$TMPDIR`, and `gh` (its TLS check fails in the sandbox on macOS). Codex threads and the coordinator are unchanged. Set in `config.toml` only, no popup row yet; mods can't suggest it |
| `paused` | bool | `false` | the user parked the project: the ticker sends its agents nothing (no nudges, no PR follow-up or main-moved prompts) and `tm thread start` / `restart` / `adopt` refuse with `project-paused`, while state, PRs and checkouts are still polled and items still raised (§7.5); the sidebar marks it `∥`. Set with `tm project pause|resume` or the `Paused` row; journaled `project.pause` / `project.resume` |
| `active` | bool | `true` | only an active project's coordinator and threads run and are resumed at a server start; an inactive one's stay dormant to resume at its activation, it shows collapsed in the sidebar marked `⊘`, and the ticker leaves it alone (§5.1, §3.6). Set with `tm project activate|deactivate`, the sidebar's `space` or the `Active` row; journaled `project.activate` / `project.deactivate` |
| `archived` | bool | `false` | the project is hidden from the sidebar and the dashboard, and the ticker leaves it alone (§5.1). Set with `tm project archive|unarchive` or the `Archive` row; journaled |
| `pinned` | bool | `false` | the project sits at the top of the sidebar's active projects (§4); the order only. Set with `p` on its row in the sidebar, or `pin to the top` / `unpin` in its menu; journaled (`project.pin`, `project.unpin`). A project's own, never `[defaults]` |
| `share` | bool | `false` | the project is offered to the paired servers (§16.9): its state, never its files. Only while active and not archived. Set in its Settings tab (**Shared with paired servers**, asking first) or under SHARED PROJECTS in the settings' Peers tab (§4), the same value; journaled as `settings.share`. A project's own, never `[defaults]` (an error: sharing is opted into project by project) |
| `coordinator_remote_control` | bool | `false` | a new coordinator starts with the agent's remote control on, and the ticker turns a running one's back on when it reads off (§7.5) (`[remote_control] args`, §8.2), named after the project, so the agent's own apps (the Claude desktop and mobile apps) list it by project. An agent without remote control starts without it, and the toggle says it has none. Threads never get it: the user reaches them through the coordinator |
| `auto_clear` | bool | `false` | the ticker clears the coordinator's conversation once its context reaches `[ui] context_hint`, only while it is idle with nothing waiting on it: no prompt queued, no question open, an empty inbox, no thread with a question or blocked (§7.5). It first asks the coordinator to save what it learned, then clears at the next idle moment; each ask and clear is journaled. The project's state lives in files, so the coordinator reads it back with `tm context`. `tm clear` takes the same steps on the user's request, the setting on or off |
| `merge` | `coordinator` / `thread` | `coordinator` | who merges a thread's PR. Under `coordinator` the mod's guard refuses a thread's `gh pr merge` or its Azure DevOps equivalent (§8.6, **Guard**); under `thread` it leaves the call to the agent's own permission check. Not in the popups: set in the file |
| `guard` | bool | `true` | the mod's guard checks each tool call of the project's agents against the standing rules (§8.6, **Guard**); off, they have the permission rules they have without the mod. The **Guard** row; turning it off asks first |
| `guard_mode` | `enforce` / `learn` | `enforce` | how the guard treats a refusal (§8.6, **Learn mode**): `enforce` refuses and lists it in **Guard refusals**; `learn` refuses too, tells the agent the user reviews it, and puts each new one to the user in the inbox. The **Guard mode** row (refuse / refuse, then ask you) |
| `guard_allow` | list of `{ rule, command }` or `{ rule, path }`, each with an optional `exact` | `[]` | the human's allowances (§8.6, **Allowances**): a `*` matches anything but in an `exact` one, a `credentials` one is exact, `merge` and an unknown rule are errors, as is one with neither or both of command and path, a path neither absolute nor from `~/`, a control character (but a command's newlines and tabs) or one listed twice. A project's add to all projects'. Written from **Guard refusals** (allow) and **Guard allowances** (change, remove); journaled by count only |
| `links` | table: other project's slug = `"both"` / `"send"` / `"receive"`, or `{ allow = …, ask = true }` | none | `[projects.<slug>.links]` only (`defaults.links` is an error): the other projects whose coordinators this one may write to (`send`), hear from (`receive`) or both; a message goes only when both sides allow it. The Settings tab's Linked projects list and `tm project link <a> <b>` always write both entries at once (the other's the mirror); a one-sided entry comes only from a hand edit and shows as half-linked (`tm doctor` warns). `ask = true` holds each message this coordinator sends there for the user's OK; §7.9. A key `"<server>/<slug>"` names a project on a paired server: only this side is here, and its `ask` is on unless it says `ask = false`; §16.11 |
| `guard_off` | list of rule ids | `[]` | guard rules left out: `force-push`, `push-default`, `worktree-only`, `delete-branch`, `merge`, `credentials`; an unknown id is an error. The **Guard rules** row lists them to turn each on or off; the floor (§8.6) holds whatever is off: a force-push to a protected branch (or `--all`/`--mirror`) and a thread merging under `merge = "coordinator"` stay refused with `force-push` or `merge` off |
| `env` | table: `NAME = { from = … }` | none | `[projects.<slug>.env]` and `[defaults.env]`: the secret environment of the project's threads (§8.6, **Secret environment**); `from` is `"gh-auth-token"`, `"keychain:<item>"` or `"env:<NAME>"`. A name tm, the agent or the system sets (`PATH`, `HOME`, `TERMINATR_*`, `CLAUDE*`, `CODEX*`, `LD_*`, `DYLD_*`, …) is an error. Settings, **Secret environment**: add (name, then source), remove, test (ok or why not, never the value); journaled by name and source |
| `sandbox` | table: `hosts`, `guides`, `write`, `sockets`, `approved_commands`, `excluded_commands` (a command, or `{ command = …, ask = false }` for one that runs without asking), `allow`, `drop` (lists of strings), `local_ports` (bool) | none, off | `[projects.<slug>.sandbox]` and `[defaults.sandbox]`: the sandbox rules of the project's threads (§5.2, T574 §3). Every list adds down the layers: all projects' entries, then the project's own. A file of tm 0.37 (without the top-level `sandbox_lists = "add"` that the migration and `config.EditSandbox` write) whose project sets `excluded_commands` or `allow` is read with tm 0.37's meaning, that list replacing all projects' (`[]` too): `config.SandboxOf` gives it as the lists and drops that give the same, and `config.EditSandbox` refuses to write it (`config.ErrLegacy`) until it moves (**Migration**, below). A project's `drop` names entries of all projects' lists (by value, in any list) its threads don't take (`drop` in `[defaults.sandbox]` fails to load). `local_ports` is the project's when it sets it, else all projects', else off. macOS only (`config.SandboxSettings`; Claude and Codex): `hosts` (a host name in lower case, or `*.example.com`; never an address in any form (a last label starting with a digit), a URL, a port, one word such as `localhost`, `*.localdomain`, a domain whose names resolve to any address (`nip.io`, `sslip.io`, `xip.io`, `lvh.me`, `localtest.me`, `traefik.me`, `localhost.direct`, `vcap.me`, or under one), or a public suffix or a wildcard over one (`golang.org/x/net/publicsuffix`: `co.uk`, `*.co.uk`, `*.github.io`); `config.SandboxWarning` names a wildcard and a host serving many owners, `storage.googleapis.com` among them), `local_ports` (local binding) and `guides` (the names of `config.Guides`: `browser-testing`, `browser-downloads`). The lists, Claude only, every platform: `excluded_commands` are commands in Claude's Bash rule syntax (`npx playwright *`) that run outside the sandbox; `allow` are permission rules (`Bash(npx playwright *)`). Each entry is one line of plain text, at most 300 characters; written, none twice; tool names compare whatever their case; an entry that fails its check loads, and is refused on its own at launch: an excluded command that matches every command (`*`), leaves its program open (`*e*`, `*-*`, `python* x`), is a rule (`Bash(…)`, `bash(…)`) is a single character, or holds `;`, `&`, `|`, `<`, `>`, a backquote, `$(`, `${` or `$X` (a variable) (it would never match); an allow rule that isn't `Tool` or `Tool(what)`, leaves out the target of Bash, PowerShell, Edit, Write, MultiEdit, NotebookEdit, Read, Glob, Grep or WebFetch (`Read`, `WebFetch`), or lets every call of one through (`Bash(*)`, `Bash(:*)`, `Edit(//**)`, `Read(~/**)`, `WebFetch(domain:*)`), or a Bash or PowerShell rule whose program is open (`Bash(*e*)`). `config.SandboxWarning` names, without refusing: a program that runs others or code with that left open (`npx *`, `bash *`, `/bin/sh *`, `env sh *`, past wrappers such as `env`, `sudo`, `timeout` and their flags; `sh -c *`, `python3 -c *`, `node -e *`), a whole tool or MCP server (`WebSearch`, `mcp__x`; one MCP tool, `mcp__x__y`, has no target and doesn't warn), and every `Edit(…)` rule (unprompted writes, and the Bash sandbox opened for writing there), more plainly on a tree high up (`~/<folder>/**`, `//Users/<name>/**`, a wildcard there) or a path through a file that starts programs (`.zshrc`, `.bashrc`, `.profile`, `.config`, `.ssh`, `.git`, `.claude`, `.codex`, `.terminatr`, `LaunchAgents`, …). A new allow rule may not be for Bash, PowerShell, Edit, Write, MultiEdit or NotebookEdit (`config.CheckNewSandboxEntry`; one already in the file keeps working until the migration moves it); a write folder is a plain absolute or `~/` folder, no wildcard, `.` or `..`, at least two folders below home (or `/Users/<name>`, `/home/<name>`) or the root, none of its parts a file that starts programs, never in a folder whose files are loaded or run as code (`~/Library` but `~/Library/Caches/<x>`, toolchains' and package managers' folders under home, `/usr`, `/opt/homebrew`, `/etc`, `/Library`, `/System`, `/Applications`, `/nix`) nor holding or in a folder on `PATH` (as the process checking has it: the console adding it; the server launching, its own PATH plus the user's login shell's, resolved once at its start with `$SHELL -ilc` within 5 s, `config.SetMorePath`), names compared with case folded where the file system ignores case (`config.CheckWriteFolder`); an excluded command has no `(` or `)` (tm writes it into a `Bash(…)` rule). `config.SandboxCovered` pairs an excluded command with a Bash allow rule of an older file whose pattern takes it: the command then runs without asking, as `ask = false` does, which the Access tab says under both. **Presets** (`config.Presets`) are named groups of these rules that tm ships, no key of their own: `browser-testing` (`guides = ["browser-testing"]`), `browser-downloads` (the four `config.BrowserDomains` in `hosts`, and `guides = ["browser-downloads"]`), `local-dev-servers` (`local_ports = true`); adding one adds its missing rules as plain ones. tm 0.37's switches `browser_testing`, `browser_downloads`, `local_dev_servers` are still read, as the rules they unfold into (a project's `false` as drops of them, `local_ports = false` for local dev servers); `config.EditSandbox` writes the plain rules in their place, and never the switches. **Migration** (`config.Migrate`, `server/accessmigrate.go`, T574 §6): at its start, before any session resumes, the server moves a file of tm 0.37 once (`config.MigrateFile`, under the file's lock): a copy beside it (`config.toml.bak-<major.minor>`, created new, another name when taken); the switches as their rules; a project's `excluded_commands` or `allow` as its entries plus `drop` of each of all projects' it lacked, or has asking otherwise (an entry of another list a drop of the same text would take kept as the project's own); a `Bash(…)` allow rule covering an excluded command of its table's threads removed, the commands it covers `ask = false`; `Edit`, `Write`, `MultiEdit` and `NotebookEdit` allow rules on `//abs/**` or `~/x/**`, and `worktree-only` allowances on `<folder>/*`, as write folders where `config.CheckWriteFolder` takes the folder (`Migration.Widened`: the check counts them as the same rule, but a write folder reaches further, so the note names each, says that sandboxed commands and Codex threads write there too and that `tm access rm write <folder> <project>` (or `--all`) undoes it, and needs the user); the rest kept as it is and listed (`Migration.Kept`). Each project's own table is then fitted to what its threads got (`config.fit`), and the new file is loaded and compared with the old, read as tm 0.37 read it, for all projects and every project without yolo: the rules given (a covering Bash rule counted as its commands' no-ask, and refused when the new file has one the old didn't; a file rule or path allowance as its write folder), which excluded commands run without asking, local ports, and every other setting; any difference refuses the migration (`Migration.Differs`), and the file stays as it was. Each project gets a journal line (`ticker settings.migrate`) and an inbox item (`access-migration`, needing the user when something was kept for them) listing what moved for all projects and for it, what was kept, a yolo project's rules left uncompared, and the two meanings that change (yolo turns Claude's sandbox off too; escape prompts go only to the user); a refusal or a file that can't move is said once per reason. Whatever the migration does, each project with yolo on gets an inbox item once per tm home (`state/yolo-meaning-told`), needing the user: yolo now turns the sandbox off for Claude threads too. `tm doctor` (Settings) lists what still has an old form (`config.OldAccess`). Access tab (§4): entries added, removed, dropped (`d`) and taken back (`u`), local ports on or off, presets added (`p`); human-only (`EditSandbox`), journaled `settings.sandbox <what>` |

**All projects**. `[defaults]` in `config.toml` takes the same keys as a `[projects.<slug>]` table, with the same values and checks (an unknown key or a bad value is an error naming `defaults.<key>`). Each setting of a project resolves key by key: the project's own value when its table sets the key, else the `[defaults]` value, else the default in the table above. A new project has no table, so it starts from All projects; so does every setting a project leaves unset, and changing All projects changes each project that doesn't set that setting itself, from the next read (the ticker's next sweep, the next `tm thread start`, a new coordinator). `tm` never copies the defaults into a project's table: a project's table holds only what the human set for that project. `active`, `paused`, `archived`, `pinned` and `share` are each project's own state, never all projects': `[defaults]` setting any of them is an error, and the All projects tab has no Active, Paused, Archive or Delete rows. The other way round, `global_parallel_threads` is all projects' only: a `[projects.<slug>]` table setting it is an error, and a project's Settings tab has no row for it. The older `auto_resolve` reads in `[defaults]` as in a project's table, and a project's own `auto_resolve` (or `auto_close`) overrides the defaults' `auto_close`. Settings popups write the table they show: the `,` popup's All projects tab writes `[defaults]`, a project's Settings tab writes `[projects.<slug>]`; `x` on a project's setting removes its line (and, for auto-close, the project's `auto_resolve` line), and refuses with the usual message when the file sets it in another form. A change to All projects is journaled in each project that follows it (`human settings.yolo <slug> true (all projects)`). The defaults are as much the human's as the projects' tables: the same write rules, no CLI command or socket method, and the coordinators' `Edit` deny rule covers the file.

**Remote control, live.** `tm project remote on|off [<slug>]` (human only) and the prefix then `r` on a coordinator's pane (asks first, in a dialog) change the running coordinator, not `config.toml`: through the manifest's in-session text when it has one, else by resuming the agent with or without the flag (refused unless it is idle). The change lasts until the coordinator is started anew, also across a server restart, which resumes it with the state it had; the setting decides again for a new coordinator. While the setting is on, the ticker turns a running coordinator's remote control back on whenever it reads off (§7.5): after a start, a resume, a server restart or a drop; the user's own off holds against it until the coordinator is started anew, and the status bar says so (`remote control off for demo; it stays off until the coordinator is started anew, then the project's settings turn it on again`). The result's `held` is true then. When the manifest names a `status_field`, the agent's own word wins, so a toggle made inside the agent (Claude's `/remote-control`) shows too. The project popup's Settings tab shows it as a plain row, `Remote control  on/off` with what it does, changed with `enter`, and the running coordinator's state when that differs; the UI never names the key or the file. The protocol call is `session.remote {id, on}` → `{remote_control, how, held}` (`how`: `unchanged`, `prompted`, `restarted`); every `on: false` marks the coordinator's record held (`remote_held`), every `on: true` clears it.

Rules for `tm thread approve`. It acts only when:
- the thread's state is `blocked` with reason `permission`;
- **and** a screen rule currently matches a permission dialog.

It refuses on questions, trust screens and dialogs it can't classify. It sends a single "allow once" choice, never "always allow". The coordinator's skill limits approvals to in-task, in-worktree, non-destructive actions. Anything outward-facing goes to the human: pushes to shared branches, publishing, deleting outside the worktree, new network destinations, and anything touching credentials. Every approval is journaled with the dialog text.

Rules for `tm thread answer <id> [--choice N[,N…]] [--option LABEL]… [--text T] [--question K]`. It relays the user's answer to a question menu (Claude's `AskUserQuestion`) on a thread, so the user answers through the coordinator instead of attaching. The coordinator's skill limits it to relaying what the user said in chat: it never chooses an answer itself. It is the coordinator's (or the human's), for the project's own threads, and no setting gates it. Every answer is journaled with the question and the answer (`coordinator thread.answer t-0003 Which color? → Blue`).

**Through the mod**, when the thread's session has a question menu open (§3.3, **Ask**; `tm thread show` lists it with its numbers). Its state is not checked: the open menu is the proof. It answers question K (1–4; by default the first unanswered one) with the options picked by number (`--choice 1,3`; the number after the last option is the menu's own words and needs `--text`) or by label (`--option Blue`, repeatable, any case), joined with `, ` as the agent joins a multi-select, and `--text` last. Refused: an unknown number or label (`no-option`, naming the labels), more than one option on a single-select (`single-select`), an option and words on a single-select (`not-text-option`), the words' number without `--text` (`needs-text`), a menu closed meanwhile (`no-menu`). A menu with several questions takes one call per question; the output says how many are left, and the mod gets the answers with the last.

**By keys**, otherwise (mods off, an older agent): one menu at a time, `--choice N [--text T]` only (`--option`, several choices or `--question` above 1 are refused, `no-mod-question`). It acts only when:
- the thread's agent has an `[answer]` table (else `no-answer`: the user answers in the pane);
- the thread's state is `blocked` with reason `question` (else `not-question`);
- **and** the screen's settled match is the `[answer] rule`, or another `blocked` rule whose reason is `question` (Codex's "Implement this plan?" and `/new`'s dialog, where a number picks an option too), else `no-menu`.

It reads option N (1–9) and the question from the screen (`no-option` when there is no such option). The option named by `text_option` needs `--text` (`needs-text`) and no other takes it (`not-text-option`). It types N, and prints the option's label without the description column Codex draws after it; for the text option it then types the text (control characters become spaces) and `submit`, about 300 ms apart, because a TUI reads one burst of input as one key. With `text_focus` the text option is reached by that key N−1 times, then `text_key`, the text and `submit`. A menu that shows `Question k/N` (Codex plan mode, several questions) is answered one question per call, in order: the output says how many remain, and `--question` must name the question on screen. Codex raises no hook while a menu is open, so the state comes from the screen rule.

Never automated, in any mode: merging PRs, force-pushes, deleting branches with unmerged work, forced worktree removal, and marking tasks `done` (except by the user's own `complete_tasks` setting).

**Data is not instructions.** Report bodies, PR text, inbox summaries and file contents are shown to agents as data. Prompts injected by the server contain only fixed text and ids, except a thread's PR-checks follow-up, which also carries a bounded excerpt of the failing CI log (marked as data) and goes only to that thread.

---

## 12. Toolchain and build

- **Go:** 1.26 or later. `go.mod` says `go 1.26.0`, the floor set by go.mitchellh.com/libghostty.
- **Zig 0.16**, to build libghostty-vt (Ghostty's `build.zig.zon` declares 0.16.0 as its minimum).
  - `make` uses a `zig` 0.16.x from `PATH`, or else downloads the pinned Zig 0.16.0 into `.build/` and checks its sha256 (macOS and Linux, arm64 and x86_64). `ZIG=…` overrides both. Other Zig versions are skipped, because Zig's build API changes between minor releases. The prerequisites are therefore Go, pkg-config, git, curl and a C toolchain.
  - The Zig build **fetches packages over the network** (aro, uucode, highway, simdutf and others) into `~/.cache/zig`, about 110 MB. CI caches it.
- **pkg-config** is a hard dependency: the bindings link with `#cgo pkg-config: --static libghostty-vt-static`.
- **git.**
- **cgo is required.** `CGO_ENABLED=0` doesn't compile the bindings. A pure-Go build would need the import behind a build tag, which is not planned.
- **libghostty bindings:** go.mitchellh.com/libghostty, pinned by commit in `go.mod`.
  - The `Makefile` pins the Ghostty commit (`GHOSTTY_REV`) that the bindings were developed against. Bump both together.
  - Neither the Go API nor the snapshot format is stable, so `internal/emu` is the only importer, and attach requires identical builds (§3.3).
- **Portable CPU target.** The `Makefile` builds with `-Dcpu=baseline` (override with `GHOSTTY_CPU`). Zig's default, the host CPU, produced an AVX-512 build that crashed with `SIGILL` on another machine.
- **Build cache.** The `Makefile` names the library build in `CGO_CFLAGS`. Go's build cache ignores pkg-config output and would otherwise keep linking a previous library path.
- **Linking:** static.
  - macOS: `tm` depends only on libSystem and libresolv. The spike's binary was 6.7 MB stripped.
  - Linux: glibc is linked dynamically. For releases, pin the glibc floor in the Zig target (e.g. `x86_64-linux-gnu.2.28`) or try `*-linux-musl` for a fully static binary (untested).
- **Other dependencies:**
  - `creack/pty`, for PTYs;
  - `dop251/goja`, the mods' JavaScript VM, pinned by commit in `go.mod` (it has only pseudo-versions; upgrade it on purpose), and `evanw/esbuild`, to bundle TypeScript mods (§3.7). Both are pure Go; together they add about 10 MB to the binary;
  - `charmbracelet/ultraviolet`, to decode the outer terminal's input;
  - Bubble Tea v2 and Lip Gloss v2, for the dashboard only.
- **Releases:** cross-compiled with `zig cc` (darwin linked by Apple's ld), one libghostty-vt build per target; darwin binaries signed and notarised (§10.1).
- **Why not our own bindings:** the libghostty C API changes often. The mitchellh bindings track it, and cover the terminal, formatter, snapshot, render state, and key, mouse, focus and paste encoders, all of which we need. Writing our own would mean following every upstream change ourselves. If the bindings stall, `internal/emu` is the seam where direct cgo calls could replace them.

---

## 13. Non-goals for v0.1

- Splits, zoom, tabs, or more than one pane visible per client. No copy mode: native selection works while the app doesn't track the mouse, and Shift-drag otherwise. tm's own mouse UI (clicks, menus, dragged dividers, §4) is in; inside a pane the program gets the mouse whenever it tracks it. Shift+PgUp/PgDn local scrollback is in.
- Kitty graphics compositing, and any rendering of panes through Bubble Tea.
- Windows; SSH remote machines; a web UI.
- Keeping agent processes alive across a server restart (a live PTY handoff over `SCM_RIGHTS`). Agents are resumed instead (§3.6); the handoff is a later spike.
- Agents other than Claude Code. Codex and pi come later through §8.7; plain shell sessions are supported.
- Headless agent modes (`claude -p`, `codex exec`) for threads.
- Plugins other than agent manifests; routines and schedules (PR follow-up is built in); several coordinators per project.
- Threads writing project files directly, and anything terminatr-owned inside a worktree (§5.2).
- Importing `~/.herdr-projects` or `~/.tsk` data.
- herdr-projects' switcher filter, routines, SSH machines, autoproject and checkout thread kind. Its thread adopt is in (§9, **Adopt**), for agents running in a `tm` session; agents in other terminals are not.
- tsk's TUI polish: multi-select, undo, search, wide stage, notices, trash.
- A native installer package (`tm update`, the Homebrew tap and `scripts/install.sh` and `scripts/install.ps1` exist, §10.1).

---

## 14. Open points

All three spikes have reported: symlinks (`docs/research/symlinks.md`), Claude Code (`docs/research/claude.md`) and libghostty (`docs/research/libghostty.md`). Their code is in git history under `spikes/` at commit `41983d953b6d`.

**What they settled:**

| Question | Answer | Where |
|---|---|---|
| Attach design | Mirror emulator per client: snapshot, then an ordered output/resize stream; input re-encoded against the mirror's modes. Verified with 0 digest mismatches across detach, window close and reattach. Raw passthrough was replaced | 3.3 |
| Prefix key, `TERM` | Ctrl+B, tmux's default (legacy `0x02` and `CSI 98;5u`), then `d` detaches; hints read `prefix+<key>`; `xterm-256color` + `COLORTERM=truecolor` | 3.3 |
| Mode 2026, wide characters, Shift+Enter, bracketed paste | All correct through the mirror; client honours 2026 holds; re-anchor after graphemes | 3.3 |
| Resize | Never on attach (Claude's inline mode duplicates scrollback rows on resize) | 3.3 |
| Snapshot compatibility | None between builds, so attach requires an identical build, and the client re-execs the server's binary | 3.3, 3.6 |
| Performance | Claude-level output costs < 0.3 % server CPU; keystroke → echo about 1 ms; coalesce PTY reads for firehoses | 3.3 |
| Hooks merge with the user's | Yes, all additive | 8.6 |
| Context after `/clear`/compact | `SessionStart` with fresh `additionalContext` works; `--append-system-prompt-file` survives `/clear` | 7.8, 8.6 |
| Hook reliability | Not enough alone: three Esc cases fire no closing hook. Status file first, then hooks, transcript, screen | 8.4, 8.6 |
| Hook delivery | Stream socket, about 4 ms, bounded deadlines, sync, exit 0; no datagrams (2 KB cap on macOS), no http hooks | 8.2 |
| Screen strings | New rules for 2.1.289; herdr's don't match | 8.6 |
| `TERMINATR_*` in hooks | Yes | 8.6 |
| Todos | `TaskCreate`/`TaskUpdate` diffs → `upsert`/`reset` ops plus a snapshot dir | 7.3, 8.2, 8.6 |
| Read-only project folder | `Read` allow + `Edit` deny + sandbox hold interactively and under yolo, on macOS | 5.2, 8.6 |
| Kickoff argument | Must follow `--` | 8.6 |
| Inherited env | Strip Claude's session variables | 3.4 |
| Socket paths | Short run dir; bind before spawn | 3.2 |
| Prompt injection | Paste works with preconditions and is the injector; the `uds-messaging` socket is used only to send the server's own held prompts (sent, never confirmed delivered) | 8.6 |
| Release binaries | `zig cc` builds with a glibc 2.28 floor (checked on Debian 10); darwin binaries signed with a Developer ID and notarised in the release workflow (`.goreleaser.yaml`, docs/OPERATIONS.md) | 10.1, 12 |

**Still open** (none of these blocks v0.1's core):

| # | Point | Affects | Plan |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Real outer terminals other than libghostty (Ghostty.app, iTerm2, Terminal.app): grapheme widths, terminals without the kitty keyboard protocol | 3.3 | Test on each; fallbacks for legacy keyboards |
| 2 | Linux: Claude in a pane, the bubblewrap sandbox with the §5.2 policy | 5.2, 8.6 | Check on a Linux box with a Claude login |
| 3 | Claude edge cases: auto-compaction, `async` hooks, `PermissionDenied`/`StopFailure`/MCP elicitation, the status file after a Claude crash, Ctrl+U, `skipDangerousModePermissionPrompt`, the `deleted` task status | 8.6 | Fixtures; the dead-pid rule covers the crash case |
| 4 | The undocumented status file and `uds-messaging` socket can change in any Claude release | 8.6, 8.8 | content checks and fallbacks (in place), drift logged; the weekly agent canary |
| 5 | Live server upgrade (PTY handoff over `SCM_RIGHTS` + snapshots) | 3.6 | Later spike; v0.1 resumes agents instead |
| 6 | Codex and pi under the same harness | 8.7 | After v0.1 |

---

## 15. Testing

Terminatr has a test strategy from the start, not a test phase at the end. Four principles:
- **Every feature's "Try it" becomes an automated scenario.**
- **Agents are faked by default.** The real `claude` is checked on demand and nightly, because we depend on its undocumented files.
- **Every parser of outside input is fuzzed.**

### 15.1 Layers

| Layer | What it covers | How it runs | When |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Unit** | One package, no processes, no sockets. Manifest mapping, arbitration tables, `ApplyTodo`, `TASKS.md` round trips, report validation, path rules | `go test -race ./...` | every PR |
| **Fuzz** | Every parser of input we don't control (§15.5) | seed corpora run as unit tests on every PR; `make fuzz` (1 min per target, capped) weekly | every PR (seeds), weekly (search) |
| **Integration** | A real `tm server` in an isolated `TERMINATR_HOME` (a `t.TempDir()`, with a short run dir under `/tmp`), driven through the CLI and the socket. Lifecycle, stale sockets, handshake, sessions, hooks, tasks, threads with the fake agent | `go test -race ./...` (packages under `internal/…` with `_integration_test.go` files) | every PR |
| **End-to-end** | The whole product as the user sees it: `tm` and `tm attach` running inside a **virtual terminal** (libghostty), keys typed, screens compared with golden files, windows closed, clients and servers killed | `internal/e2e`, `make e2e` (all) / `make e2e-smoke` (a core set under about 2 minutes) | smoke on every PR and on main; race-built smoke and the full suite weekly |
| **Real agent** | The same scenarios against the installed `claude`, to catch Claude releases that change hooks, screens, the session file, or the task tools | build tag `realclaude`, `make test-claude` | on demand, and nightly on a machine with a Claude login (not GitHub-hosted CI) |
| **Real Codex** | The Codex scenarios against the installed `codex`, to catch Codex releases that change hooks, screens, the rollout or `codex queue` (§15.4) | build tag `realcodex`, `make test-codex` | on demand only, on a machine with a ChatGPT login |

**On every PR** (macOS and Linux, `ci.yml`): gofmt, vet, build, `go test -race ./...` (unit, integration and fuzz seeds), `make e2e-smoke` (without `-race`, in its own job, two shards per OS: `make e2e-smoke E2E_SHARD=1/2`), `tm selftest`. The release snapshot (`make release-snapshot` on macOS) runs on every push to main and on PRs that touch the release build (`.goreleaser.yaml`, the Makefile, `go.mod`/`go.sum`, `scripts/release/`, `Formula/`, the workflows, libghostty bindings).

**Weekly** (`weekly.yml`, also by hand through `workflow_dispatch`; skipped when main hasn't changed since its last successful run): `make fuzz` (1 minute per target), `make test-race`, the full `make e2e` and `make e2e-smoke-race` (the smoke set with a race-built `tm`) on Linux and macOS.

**On demand or nightly, on a logged-in machine:** `make test-claude`. **On demand only:** `make test-codex`.

### 15.2 The end-to-end harness: `internal/e2e`

The harness is ported from the libghostty spike's `cmd/harness` (§14), and lives in the product so every feature can add scenarios to it. A test reads like the user's session:

```go
func TestDetachReattach(t *testing.T) {
	env := e2e.New(t)                         // isolated TERMINATR_HOME, short run dir, tm built once per run
	s := env.Start("shell")                   // tm session start, via the CLI
	w := env.Window(120, 40, "attach", s.ID)  // `tm attach` in a PTY, parsed by a libghostty "outer terminal"
	w.Type("seq 1 300\r")
	w.Key(e2e.CtrlBackslash)                  // detach, encoded the way Ghostty would send it
	w2 := env.Window(100, 32, "attach", s.ID) // another window, another size
	w2.WaitFor("300", 5*time.Second)
	env.AssertMirrorsServer(w2)               // client screen == server screen (digest)
	w2.CloseWindow()                          // PTY master gone: SIGHUP to the client
	env.AssertAlive(s)                        // server and session unaffected
	e2e.Golden(t, w2.Screen(), "detach-reattach.txt")
}
```

What the harness provides (`Env`, `Window` with its input helpers, golden screens, the orphan check, artifacts, the printer app and `AssertMirrorsServer`):
- **Running it.** Scenarios skip unless `E2E=1`, which `make e2e` and `make e2e-smoke` set, so `go test ./...` stays fast. The smoke set is every scenario named `TestSmoke*`. With `E2E_RACE=1` (`make e2e-smoke-race`, weekly) the harness builds `tm` with `-race` and fails any scenario whose `tm` printed `WARNING: DATA RACE`. PRs and main run the smoke set without it: a race-built `tm` takes about a second to start, and every agent hook starts one, which made the smoke step take 6–7 minutes.
- **`Env`**:
  - builds `tm` once per test run;
  - an isolated `TERMINATR_HOME` and `HOME` (so the fake agent's `~/.claude/` is private);
  - a short run dir for the socket;
  - `Start(app, args…)` (`"shell"`, a deterministic app by name, or any command), `CLI` (runs `tm …` and returns stdout, stderr and the exit code), `Screen`, `WaitFor`, `Keys`, `AssertAlive`;
  - `KillServer`, `RestartServer`;
  - cleanup that **fails the test if any process outlives it**, so orphaned agents can't go unnoticed.
- **No test server outlives its test.** Every server a test starts is stopped in its cleanup: `Env`'s cleanup runs `tm server stop --yes` (which works whatever the server speaks, §3.3) and SIGKILLs the server if it is still there; `internal/cli`'s binary tests stop any server they started the same way (`t.Cleanup`). For what a cleanup never gets to run (a `go test` timeout, ^C, SIGKILL), every test `tm` (built with `-tags testhooks`) gets `TERMINATR_TEST_OWNER=<pid of the test process>`: a server with it stops itself within a second of that process exiting. New harnesses that start servers must do both, and set `TERMINATR_LAUNCHD=off` so that on macOS no test server goes through the user's launchd (`internal/cli`'s `TestBinaryLaunchdStart` covers that path with a fake `launchctl`).
- **Test hooks in the server.** They exist only in a server built with `-tags testhooks` (the e2e and `internal/cli` harnesses build `tm` that way); any other build ignores the variables. `TERMINATR_TEST_HELLO=protocol=N` makes a server claim protocol N at the handshake, `=deaf` makes it hang up on every hello; `TestSmokeReplaceOldServer` uses them to play a server of an older version that `tm server restart`, `stop` and `tm doctor --fix` must replace, with the agents resumed.
- **`Window`**: a PTY running `tm` or `tm attach` (`env.Window`), or a shell the test types `"$TM" …` into (`env.Shell`), whose output feeds a libghostty terminal (the "outer screen"). It offers:
  - `Type`, `Key` (libghostty's key encoder, honouring the kitty flags the client pushed), `Paste`, `Wheel`, `Resize`;
  - `CloseWindow` (close the PTY master), `KillClient` (`SIGKILL`);
  - `WaitFor`, `Quiet`, `Screen`.
- **Several consoles.** A scenario opens several `tm` windows at once, of different sizes, to check that a view's consoles show the same thing (`TestSmokeViewsShared`: screens, the sidebar, latest-typist sizing with padding in the larger window, `--own` kept apart) and that the view survives `tm server restart` (`TestSmokeViewSurvivesRestart`).
- **Golden screens.** `testdata/golden/*.txt` holds the plain text of the viewport, plus an optional attribute layer (later). `make e2e E2E_FLAGS=-update` rewrites them. Volatile parts (session ids, pids, durations) are masked by named regexes (`e2e.Mask`, `e2e.DefaultMasks`) and read `<name>` in the file.
- **Consistency checks.** `AssertMirrorsServer` calls `view.digest` with the client's pid; the server sends that console a `view.digest` event on its view stream, and the client asks for an in-stream `DIGEST` and logs whether its mirror matches (`TERMINATR_ATTACH_LOG`): modes, the active screen, recent scrollback.
- **Artifacts on failure:** every window's last screen and raw bytes, the server log and `sessions.json` (`tm agent explain` for each session), saved under `$E2E_ARTIFACTS/<test>` and uploaded by CI.
- **Deterministic apps.** Scenarios use small purpose-built TUIs under `internal/e2e/apps/`: a stream printer, a full-screen mouse app, an inline redraw app. Real programs such as `vim` and `htop` vary between machines.
- **On Windows** the same scenarios run over ConPTY: a `Window` is a `plat/pty` console in a Job Object, the apps and the fake agent use `plat/term` for raw mode and sizes, and process groups become process trees (`proc.Group`, `proc.KillGroup`). Scenarios type sh syntax, so shells are Git for Windows' `sh.exe` (`$E2E_SH`, else `sh` on `PATH`, else Git's usual install), and git must be on `PATH`. A box without Go or cgo gets everything cross-built: `scripts/e2e-windows.sh arm64|amd64 OUT` writes `e2e.test.exe`, `testdata/` and `bin/` (tm.exe, the apps, fakeagent.exe, conpty.dll and OpenConsole.exe; the ConPTY files go beside `e2e.test.exe` too, for the harness's windows: the inbox conhost turns a pasted `\n` into ` \n`), and `E2E_BIN=OUT\bin` makes the harness use those binaries instead of building. Golden screens that differ on Windows (paths, the shell) live in `testdata/golden/windows/`, written by `-update` there only where the screen differs from the shared file. What only Unix has stays in `*_unix_test.go` (SIGSTOP, setsid and the controlling tty, `scripts/run.sh`); what differs on purpose is a per-OS constant in `checks_*_test.go`: ConPTY passes a terminal report the window sends only now and then (`passesReports`), and Windows can only kill a deaf server, not ask it to stop (`politeTerminate`). The fake Codex runs its hooks in PowerShell on Windows, as Codex does.

### 15.3 The scripted fake agent

Lives in `internal/e2e/fakeagent`, a small Go TUI. It behaves like Claude Code 2.1.289 as the spike recorded it, so state detection, threads and coordinator tests run in seconds, cost nothing, and give the same result every time.

- **Same interface as `claude`.**
  - It accepts the flags the manifest passes: `--session-id`, `--resume`, `--plugin-dir`, `--settings`, `--append-system-prompt-file`, `--dangerously-skip-permissions`, `-- <kickoff>`.
  - Tests use the **real `claude.toml`**, with only `launch.command` pointed at the fake. That way the manifest itself is under test.
- **Same screens.** Full-screen with mouse, focus and kitty flags; the title spinner and the `✳` title; the prompt box with dim ghost text; the permission and question dialogs; the trust and bypass screens; the restored prompt after Esc.
- **Same side effects.**
  - It runs the command hooks from the plugin's `hooks.json`, synchronously, with Claude's payload shapes.
  - It writes `~/.claude/sessions/<pid>.json` (under the test's `HOME`), appends a transcript with interrupt and `turn_duration` entries, and keeps `~/.claude/tasks/<sid>/`.
  - It emits `TaskCreate`/`TaskUpdate` and rotates its session id on `/clear`.
  - It reads `--settings` and refuses writes that the deny rules cover. That checks the policy we generate; the real enforcement is checked in the real-agent layer.
- **Driven by a script.** A TOML list of steps per scenario: `screen`, `hook`, `status`, `stream`, `dialog`, `await_key`, `todo`, `cancel_silently` (no closing hook, as with a real Esc), `clear`, `compact`, `subagent`, `run` (execute a `tm` command, e.g. `tm report`), `exit`. The spike's stale cases (s03, s04, s15), todo runs (s16–s18) and clear/compact run (s07) are ported as the first scripts.
- **Drift detection.** The nightly real-agent run records hook sequences, session-file states and screens for the same scenarios, and diffs them against the fake's scripts. A difference means Claude changed, and the fake (and probably the manifest) needs an update.

### 15.4 Real-Claude tests

- Build tag `realclaude`; `make test-claude` runs them against the `claude` on `PATH`, using Haiku. They cost a few cents per run.
- They run on demand (before a release, or after a Claude update) and nightly on a maintainer's machine or a self-hosted runner with a Claude login. GitHub-hosted CI has no login.
- They check:
  - `claude --version` against `last_tested` (logged; bump it once this suite passes on a newer version);
  - the session file still has `status`, `waitingFor`, `sessionId` and `messagingSocketPath`;
  - the hook sequences of the core scenarios (permission approve and deny, Esc cases, AskUserQuestion, subagent, `/clear`, `/compact`) and the task-tool payloads;
  - every screen rule still matches its screen;
  - `SessionStart` context re-injection;
  - the access policy: a thread can't write the project folder, interactively and under yolo.
- A failure files an inbox item in the `terminatr` project, or prints a summary when run by hand, naming the manifest lines involved.

**Real Codex**. Build tag `realcodex`; `make test-codex` runs it against the `codex` on `PATH` with gpt-6-luna and a ChatGPT login: `~/.codex`, or a dedicated `CODEX_HOME` logged in once (a fresh one has no login, and `auth.json` isn't copied). It skips when codex isn't logged in. A run takes about 3 minutes and 310k input tokens, mostly cached. On demand only, not in the weekly job. The regular e2e runs the same cases against the fake agent as Codex (`FakeCodex`: the fake called `codex`, under the real `codex.toml` and its Go agent). It checks:

- `codex --version` against `last_tested` (logged; bump it once this suite passes on a newer version);
- a first prompt (pasted: Codex reports its thread id only with its first hook), then prompts through `codex queue`;
- an approval approved and one cancelled with Esc (`Interrupt`), under the manifest's `-a on-request` (§8.6);
- `/compact` (`PreCompact`; `SessionStart` with source `compact` at the next prompt) and `/clear` (a new thread, which reports itself at its first prompt; the thread left ends a minute later with a `SessionEnd` that must change nothing);
- a question in plan mode (no hook; the screen rule), and the "Implement this plan?" menu after it;
- a resume after a server restart (`codex resume <id>`);
- the hook sequences against the fake's, and every dialog's screen rule. Screens captured live go to `internal/agent/testdata/codex/`, where every screen rule must decide at least one.

### 15.5 Race detector and fuzzing

- **Race detector:** `go test` runs with `-race` in CI on main and weekly (`make test-race`; pull requests run it without, for cost), and the e2e harness builds `tm` with `-race` for the weekly smoke set (`make e2e-smoke-race`). Concurrency is the server's core job: PTY readers, client queues, hook connections, the ticker.
- **Fuzz targets.** Each one checks that the code never panics, and round trips where a format has both a reader and a writer:

  | Target | Status |
  |---|---|
  | `proto.FuzzReadFrame` (frame round trip), `proto.FuzzHello` | exists |
  | `agent.FuzzParseManifest`, `agent.FuzzHookPayload` (every mapped event, `ApplyTodo`, trimming), `agent.FuzzSourceLines` (status file, JSONL tail, todo snapshot) | exists |
  | the control NDJSON request decoder | exists |
  | `mdfile` front matter; the `TASKS.md` parser with the property `parse(render(x)) == x` | exists |
  | the `REPORT.md` validator; `STATUS.md` | exists |
  | inbox items (`project.FuzzParseItem`), `gh` PR JSON (`ticker.FuzzParsePR`), nudge text (`ticker.FuzzNudgeText`) | exists |
  | the relay's WebSocket reader (`relay.FuzzReadMessage`) and hello check (`relay.FuzzCheckHello`, never accepts an unsigned hello) | exists |

- **Crashers.** Every crasher found by the weekly run is committed under `testdata/fuzz/<Target>/`, which makes it a regression test on every PR.

### 15.6 Invariants and properties

- **"Clearing the coordinator loses nothing."** It is tested from the start, not at the end.
  1. A fake-agent coordinator runs a script of actions: tasks, delegations, inbox handling, journal lines.
  2. The test captures `tm context`, then sends `/clear`.
  3. It asserts that the `SessionStart` re-injection carries exactly the role rules plus `tm context`, **byte for byte the same** as before.
  4. It asserts that no inbox item was lost or handled twice.

  It also holds across a server crash with resume, and a compaction.
- **No orphans:** after every integration and e2e test, no process from the test's session tree survives.
- **Arbitration table:** every row of §8.4 and of the spike's stale-case table (§8.6) is a unit test.
- **Data never becomes instructions:** fuzzed report and PR text never shows up in an injected prompt (§11.2).
- **Task rules:** agents can never set `done`, and threads can only touch their own task's steps. These run against a real server, with agent and human callers (§6.4, §11.1).

---

## 16. The relay

Two tm servers are joined through a relay (the T233 design, phase 1):
both dial out to it, so neither listens on the network, and TLS 1.3
with keys pinned at pairing runs inside the stream it copies. The relay
is `tm relay serve`, the same binary, which the user runs on their own
host: a VPS or a NAS, usually in Docker (`deploy/relay/`,
docs/OPERATIONS.md, **Running a relay**). It stores nothing and sees
only key fingerprints, IP addresses, sizes and timing; never aliases,
project slugs, messages, pane content or a pairing code. It can drop or
delay traffic; it can't read or forge it, nor join a room without a key.

**What a relay sees.** Whoever runs it, or breaks into it, sees: the
client IP address of every connection (or the proxy's); both server ids
(key fingerprints) of each link, since the hello names the sender and
its peer, so which servers are paired and when each is online; for a pairing, a
hash of the code (the pair-room id), never the code; the timing, length
and size of each connection and message; and the relay's own tm release,
which it chose. It never sees aliases, project slugs, messages, pane
content or a pairing code: the TLS 1.3 session between the two servers,
with keys pinned at pairing, runs inside the stream it copies.

**What a compromised relay can do.** Drop, delay or kick links, refuse
new ones, or lie about being up; ask a linked server to update only
through the checked path of §16.2 (the request is signed and holds no
version or URL). It can't read the traffic, change it undetected, forge
a server (it has no identity key a peer trusts) or pair one in without
the code. A relay that is wrong or hostile therefore costs availability
and the metadata above, not content. To limit the metadata, run the
relay yourself, on a host you trust, and keep `--log-ips` off (§16.5).

This section is the relay's side, and the link that dials it (§16.7).
Pairing, which makes the identity key and fills the peers, is §16.8;
what a paired server may read of the projects the user shares, §16.9;
what it keeps of theirs, §16.10; coordinator links to a paired server's
projects, §16.11; a paired server's panes in this server's consoles,
§16.12.

### 16.1 Transport

- **WebSocket** on `/v1`, over TLS (`wss://`, port 443): a request with
  `Upgrade: websocket`, `Sec-WebSocket-Version: 13` and
  `Sec-WebSocket-Protocol: tm-relay.1`, else HTTP 400 or 426. No
  extensions. `GET /healthz` answers `ok` to anyone, with `Tm-Auto-Update: on|off` when its config says and **no tm version**: the exact release tells a stranger which known bugs the relay has. The release goes only to a client that connects to `/v1`, in the challenge (§16.2). Any other path is 404.
- **Our own framing.** tm implements the RFC 6455 subset it uses
  (`internal/relay/ws.go`, both ends); there is no WebSocket module. Clients
  mask their frames, the relay doesn't; reserved bits are refused;
  fragments are reassembled; control frames are at most 125 bytes. A
  message is at most **64 KiB** (close 1009 `too-big`); the client splits
  a longer write.
- **Standard library crypto only:** `crypto/ed25519` for the hello,
  `crypto/sha256` for fingerprints and rooms, `crypto/tls` for the outer
  TLS, `crypto/rand` for nonces and masks, `crypto/sha1` for the
  handshake's accept value. The one module is `golang.org/x/crypto`'s
  `acme/autocert`, for the relay's own certificate (`--domain`) and
  nothing else: pairing and the link stay on the standard library.
- **TLS** is the outer layer only (TLS 1.2 or later, a CA certificate for
  the relay's name); what the servers trust is the pinned TLS inside.
  Certificate files are read again when either changes in size or time,
  so an ACME client renewing them needs no restart; a pair that fails to
  load keeps the last good one. With `--domain NAME` the relay gets the
  certificate itself from Let's Encrypt (autocert) by TLS-ALPN-01 on its
  own port (the CA connects to 443 with ALPN `acme-tls/1`; no port 80, no
  certbot), only for that name, on the first connection that asks for
  it; it keeps key and certificate in its state directory (`--state`,
  else systemd's `$STATE_DIRECTORY`, else `~/.terminatr/relay`) and
  renews them 30 days before they expire, without a restart. The relay
  offers ALPN `http/1.1` (and `acme-tls/1` with `--domain`), never `h2`.
- **A pinned relay** (`--self-signed`, for a relay on an IP address with
  no DNS name: Let's Encrypt's IP certificates need a profile autocert
  doesn't speak): the relay makes an ECDSA P-256 key
  (`self-signed.key`, PKCS #8, 0600) and a self-signed certificate
  (`self-signed.crt`, ten years, the `--ip` address as its IP SAN) in its
  state directory, keeps the key for good and issues the certificate
  again with the same key when the address isn't in it or it has less
  than a year to go. Its address is `wss://IP[:port]#sha256=<pin>`, the
  pin the hex SHA-256 of the key's SubjectPublicKeyInfo (64 digits, read
  in any case, kept in lower case); `serve` logs it, `install --ip`
  prints it. A client given a URL with a pin (`relay.Dialer`,
  `relay.PinnedTLS`) checks the relay's certificate by that key alone: no
  CA, no names, no dates; another key fails with `relay.PinError`, *the
  relay at HOST has a different key than when you paired*, never taken
  silently. A URL without a pin is checked against the system's CAs, as
  before. `--ip` defaults to the `--listen` address, else this host's
  own (the source address of a route out). A pin goes with `wss://`
  only.
- **Behind a TLS proxy** (`--plain`), the client address is the direct
  peer's, unless that peer is in a `--trusted-proxy` prefix: then it is the
  last `X-Forwarded-For` entry not itself trusted. A header from anyone
  else is ignored.

### 16.2 Hello

Text messages, JSON, before a room is joined; bytes are base64, ids hex.

1. relay → client: `{"t":"challenge","v":1,"nonce":"<32 bytes>","max":65536,"tm":"v0.28.0"}`.
   `tm` is the relay's release (older relays leave it out); the link
   keeps it as `relay_version` (§16.7) and the Peers tab shows it (§4).
2. client → relay: `{"t":"hello","kind":"link"|"pair"|"update","key":"<Ed25519 public key>","room":"<64 hex>","peer":"<64 hex>","sig":"<signature>"}`
   within 10 s of the upgrade (close 4408 `timeout`). `peer` is for
   `link` and `update` only.
   - `sig` is Ed25519 over `"tm-relay hello v1\n" ‖ nonce ‖ kind ‖ 0x00 ‖
     room ‖ peer`: a fresh nonce per connection, so a hello can't be
     replayed, and every field signed (close 4403 `bad-signature`).
   - A key's **fingerprint** is SHA-256 of the public key.
   - **link** rooms: `room` MUST be `SHA-256("tm-relay room v1\n" ‖
     min(fp, peer) ‖ max(fp, peer))` and `peer ≠ fp` (close 4403
     `bad-room`). Both servers compute the same room; the relay checks it
     from the key it just verified, so only the two key holders can sit in
     a link room. That proves who sits in a room, not that its two keys
     ever paired: the relay doesn't know which keys are tm servers, so
     anyone who makes two keys can use a link room of their own. What
     keeps the relay for the servers using it is its limits (§16.4):
     waiting rooms are capped so they can't take every connection, and
     bytes are capped per address, per room and in all, so a stranger's
     room gets a share of the bandwidth, never all of it.
   - **pair** rooms: `room` comes from the one-time pairing code, which
     the relay never sees, so it can't check it. Pair rooms are capped
     instead (§16.4).
   - **update** (an update request): `room` and `peer` as for the
     sender's link room, checked the same way, and signed the same way
     with the kind `update`, so a link hello can't be replayed as one.
     See **Update requests** below.
   - Anything else malformed: close 4400 `bad-hello` (an older relay
     answers an update hello so: "the relay's tm takes no update
     requests").
3. relay → client: `{"t":"wait"}` while the peer isn't there (the relay
   is up, the peer is not: the link shows "offline · peer"), then
   `{"t":"mated"}` once it is. "mated" always comes before the first
   byte from the peer.
4. Then **binary messages only**, copied unchanged to the other side, in
   order. Binary before "mated", or text after it, closes the connection
   (1002 `protocol`).

`v` is the relay wire version; an incompatible change gets a new
subprotocol and version, and a client refuses a version it doesn't
speak.

**Update requests** (`internal/relay/update.go`, `relay.Dialer.RequestUpdate`).
A server asks its relay to update itself to the latest official
release: the Peers tab's `u`, or `tm peer relay-update ALIAS` (§16.8),
in a connection of its own, signed with the server's identity key; the
link carries on. The request carries nothing but the wish: no version,
no URL. The relay answers `{"t":"update","result":R}` and closes (1000):

| `result` | when |
|---|---|
| `queued` | the key is one side of a link room that is mated now (the room it signed) and has carried bytes both ways: the relay wrote its request file |
| `not-linked` | no live link of that key through the relay (or one that has carried nothing yet) |
| `off` | the relay runs without `--update-request` (installed before update requests) |
| `too-soon` | the link has been up less than 10 minutes (`UpdateAfter`), this key's last request taken was less than an hour ago (`UpdateKeyEvery`), or anyone's less than 10 minutes ago (`UpdateEvery`); `"next"` is when it takes another |
| `failed` | the request file couldn't be written (the relay's log says why) |

**Any server with a live link through the relay may ask**: whoever can
use the relay can make it update, each key at most once an hour, all
of them together at most once every 10 minutes. That is all
it can do: the relay stays unprivileged (`DynamicUser`) and only writes
`update-request` in its state directory (`--update-request FILE`;
`FILE.last`'s time is the last request taken, so the 10 minutes hold
across the restart; the per-key times are kept in memory only, and a
restart makes every link new again). Root's `tm-relay-update.path` sees
the file and starts
`tm-relay-update.service`, `tm relay update` (§16.6), which removes the
request first (a failing update isn't started again), checks the
release's checksums and restarts the relay only for a newer release.

### 16.3 Rooms

- Kept in memory, keyed by kind and room id; one waiting connection per room.
- A connection with a **different key** mates with the waiting one.
- The **same key** again replaces the waiting connection (a server
  reconnecting after a dropped link); the old one is closed 4409
  `replaced`. In a mated room it ends the old pair: the old connection
  gets `replaced`, its partner 4410 `peer-gone`, and the newcomer waits.
- Another key in a mated pair room: 4409 `room-full`. (A link room only
  ever admits its two keys.)
- When one side leaves, the relay closes the other with 4410 `peer-gone`.
- On SIGINT or SIGTERM every connection gets 1001 `shutdown`.

### 16.4 Limits

Per address (an IPv6 address counts by its /64, and by its /48 for
waiting):

| Limit | Default | Over it |
|---|---|---|
| connections from one address | 16 (`--max-per-ip`) | HTTP 429 |
| new connections from one address | 30 a minute, burst 10 | HTTP 429 |
| connections in all | 1024 (`--max-conns`); without the flag, fewer when the memory limit (`GOMEMLIMIT`, or three quarters of the cgroup's) holds less at 160 KiB each, at least 16 | HTTP 429 |
| TCP connections, before TLS | from one address: `--max-per-ip` + 4; in all: `--max-conns` + a quarter (a `--trusted-proxy` counts only in all) | closed at once |
| connections waiting in rooms, in all | half of the connections in all | see below |
| connections waiting in rooms from one address | 4 | 4429 `rate-limited` |
| bytes one address sends | 4 MiB/s, burst 8 MiB (`--bandwidth`) | slowed, never dropped |
| bytes all connections send together | 16 MiB/s, burst 32 MiB (`--total-bandwidth`) | slowed, never dropped |
| bytes one mated room carries, both ways | 2 MiB/s, burst 64 MiB | slowed, never dropped |
| pings and pongs a client sends | 12 a minute, burst 10; their bytes count as the address's | 4429 `rate-limited` |
| a link room waiting | 1 h when it has mated on this relay in the last day, else 5 min | 4408 `timeout`; the client dials again |
| a pair room waiting | 10 min | 4408 `timeout` |
| a mated pair room | 2 min and 256 KiB in all | 4429 `pair-limit` |
| a frame | the relay pings every 30 s; 90 s without a frame closes | closed |
| the TLS handshake | 5 s | closed |
| the HTTP request | headers and request in 10 s, at most 8 KiB; the answer written in 5 s; 30 s idle between requests (none on a WebSocket, which has its own) | closed |

**Waiting rooms** take at most half the connections, so at least half
are left for rooms that mate. When the waiting are at their cap, a
newcomer that would wait makes room by closing the oldest waiter of
the address with the most waiting (4408 `timeout`, so it dials
again), when that address has at least two more waiting than the
newcomer's; otherwise the newcomer gets 4429 `rate-limited`. A
connection that mates a waiting one never needs a place: it doesn't
wait.

**What the limits guarantee**: a server's link through the relay
always finds connections to mate in, and at most a few waiters from
one address can be crowded out at a time; the relay never sends more
than `--total-bandwidth`, whoever uses it, and no single room or
address takes more than its own share of it. They don't keep a
stranger from using a room of their own within those shares (§16.2).

### 16.5 What it logs and keeps

Nothing on disk, but with `--domain` its certificate, key and ACME
account key in the state directory (with `--self-signed` its key and
certificate), and with `--update-request` the request file and its
`.last` stamp (§16.2). Its log (standard error) has the start and stop, refused
hellos, rooms mated and connections left, by room kind and the first four
bytes of the room id, with duration and bytes in, and update requests
with the first four bytes of the key's fingerprint and their result, and net/http's
per-connection errors (a failed TLS handshake) with every IP address in them replaced by
`(address)`. Client addresses only with `--log-ips`. Address counters are forgotten ten minutes after an
address's last connection.

### 16.6 Packaging

- `deploy/relay/Dockerfile`: the released Linux binary for the image's
  architecture (amd64, arm64), checked against the release's
  `checksums.txt` as `scripts/install.sh` does, on `distroless/cc`
  (glibc and nothing else), as uid 65532, listening on 8443 (published
  as 443, so it needs no capability). `TM_VERSION` pins the release.
  Each release's workflow builds it for linux/amd64 and linux/arm64 and
  pushes `ghcr.io/theclifmeister/tm-relay:<tag>`, and `:latest` unless
  a prerelease.
- `deploy/relay/compose.yaml`: certificate files mounted read-only.
  `compose.acme.yaml` with `Caddyfile`: Caddy gets the certificate by
  ACME and passes the WebSocket to a `--plain` relay on an internal
  network, trusting only that network's `X-Forwarded-For`.
  `compose.selfsigned.yaml`: a pinned relay (§16.1), `--self-signed
  --ip ${RELAY_IP} --listen :8443 --state /state`, its key in a mounted
  `./state`, published as 8443, running as `${RELAY_USER:-65532:65532}`.
  All three run `ghcr.io/theclifmeister/tm-relay:${TM_VERSION:-latest}`
  (a commented `build:` builds it locally instead) with a read-only root,
  no capabilities, `no-new-privileges`, `mem_limit: 256m` and
  `pids_limit: 256`.
- `tm-relay-docker.tar.gz`, a release asset listed in `checksums.txt`:
  the Dockerfile, the three compose files, the Caddyfile and
  `deploy/relay/README.md` in `tm-relay-docker/`, the compose files'
  default `TM_VERSION` replaced by the release's tag
  (`scripts/release/relay-docker.sh`, a goreleaser before hook; checked
  by `scripts/release/check.sh`).
- `deploy/relay/tm-relay.service`: a systemd unit with a throwaway user
  (`DynamicUser`), only `CAP_NET_BIND_SERVICE`, `--domain` with its
  `StateDirectory=tm-relay` for the ACME cache, and a strict sandbox;
  `MemoryMax=256M` with `GOMEMLIMIT=200MiB` (the Docker image sets the
  same under compose's `mem_limit: 256m`), `LimitNOFILE=8192`, and
  `StartLimitIntervalSec=0`, so restarts never leave it stopped.
  It is the unit `tm relay install` writes, for another name
  (`TestRelayUnitIsTheDeployFile`), with `--update-request
  ${STATE_DIRECTORY}/update-request --auto-update on|off` (the timer's
  state as install set it, which the relay tells in `Tm-Auto-Update`).
- `deploy/relay/tm-relay-update.service`, `.timer` and `.path`: the
  update units `tm relay install` writes
  (`TestRelayUpdateUnitsAreTheDeployFiles`). The service is a root
  oneshot running `/usr/local/bin/tm relay update` (15 min at most); the
  timer starts it daily (`OnCalendar=daily`, `RandomizedDelaySec=1h`,
  `Persistent=true`); the path unit when
  `/var/lib/private/tm-relay/update-request` exists (§16.2).
- `TestRelayDeployFilesUseRealFlags` runs each file's command line
  through `tm relay serve`'s parser.
- **`sudo tm relay install --domain NAME [--email ADDR] [--auto-update=on|off]`** (Linux with
  systemd, as root; else it refuses and names Docker): copies the running
  tm to `/usr/local/bin/tm` (unless it is that file), writes
  `/etc/systemd/system/tm-relay.service` and the three update units, runs `systemctl daemon-reload`,
  `enable` and `restart tm-relay`, `enable --now tm-relay-update.path`,
  and `enable --now tm-relay-update.timer` (auto-update, the default) or
  `disable --now` it (`--auto-update=off`), then asks `https://NAME/healthz` for up
  to two minutes. Then it prints `tm peer pair --relay wss://NAME`. It
  never changes a firewall: when `/healthz` doesn't answer it says to open
  TCP 443 and what NAME resolves to (or, when the service died, to read
  `journalctl -u tm-relay`), exit 3. Running it again rewrites the unit
  and restarts. An install from before the update units has none until
  install runs again (its relay answers update requests `off`).
- **`sudo tm relay install --ip [ADDR]`**: the same for a pinned relay
  (§16.1) on ADDR, else this host's own address (said so, as is a
  private one): it makes the key and certificate in
  `/var/lib/private/tm-relay` itself, before the service starts (systemd
  gives the directory and its files to the service's user), writes a unit
  running `tm relay serve --listen :443 --self-signed --ip ADDR --state
  ${STATE_DIRECTORY}`, checks `https://ADDR/healthz` by the pin (another
  key there is said as such) and prints `tm peer pair --relay
  wss://ADDR#sha256=<pin>`, and to back the state directory up.
  `--domain` and `--ip` don't go together.
- **`sudo tm relay update`** (by hand, or as `tm-relay-update.service`):
  first removes a linked server's request file, saying so; then the latest release (§10.1's client), its
  `tm_linux_<arch>.tar.gz` checked against `checksums.txt`, swapped in
  for `/usr/local/bin/tm` by a rename, then `systemctl restart tm-relay`
  and the `/healthz` check for the unit's `--domain`, or for its `--ip`
  by the pin of the key in the state directory, which it keeps. Nothing changes on
  a mismatch; a tm that is already the latest does nothing.
- **`sudo tm relay uninstall`**: `systemctl disable --now tm-relay`,
  removes the unit, `disable --now` the timer and path unit and removes
  the update units (those there are), `daemon-reload`; keeps `/usr/local/bin/tm` and the
  certificates in `/var/lib/private/tm-relay`.

### 16.7 The link

`internal/peer`: the server's side, one link per paired server, each its
own goroutine; no local call waits on one.

- **Identity.** `state/peer/identity.key`: an Ed25519 key (PKCS #8 PEM,
  0600, in a 0700 directory). The **server id** is SHA-256 of the public
  key (the relay's fingerprint), 64 hex digits, shown to people as its
  first eight upper case in two groups (`4F2A-91C3`). Pairing makes the
  key; a server without one has no links. A key that can't be read is an
  error and is never replaced: the peers have it pinned.
- **Peers.** `state/peer/peers.json`: `{"peers": [{"alias", "key", "relay"}]}`,
  the key the peer's Ed25519 public key (base64), the relay a `wss://`
