  URL, with `#sha256=<pin>` for a pinned relay (§16.1); aliases and keys
  unique. A tm before pinned relays reads such a URL but can't check the
  relay (it asks the system's CAs). Read at start and on `Manager.Reload`
  (pairing calls it): a new peer gets a link, a removed or changed one
  loses or redials it, a new identity key redials them all. A file that
  doesn't parse keeps the links as they are and shows its error.
- **Transport.** An interface: `Dial(ctx, wait) (net.Conn, error)`. The
  relay transport joins the link room `LinkRoom(own id, peer id)` with
  `relay.Dialer` (§16.2) and calls `wait` on the relay's "wait". A direct
  or tunnelled address (phase 5) is another transport under the same TLS.
- **TLS 1.3** inside the stream, nothing older, no resumption. Each end
  presents a self-signed certificate for its identity key and accepts
  exactly one certificate whose key is the peer's pinned key, never a
  CA's, and no names or dates are checked (no clock or expiry can break
  a link). The end with the lower server id is the TLS client.
- **Hello**: each end sends one NDJSON line, `{"link_protocol": 1,
  "version": "<tm release>", "caps": []}`, within 10 s. `LinkProtocol`
  is separate from the control protocol: two servers may run different
  releases. Methods and fields are only added; a capability names each
  feature (`watch`, §16.9, from a server that exports; `pane-vt`, `msg` as
  they are built), and a
  server uses a feature only when the peer's hello lists it. A peer below
  `MinLinkProtocol` (1) is refused (`version`).
- **Then NDJSON** both ways: a request `{"id", "method", "params"}`, a
  response `{"id", "result" | "error"}`, either end calling. `link.ping`
  every 30 s keeps the link's time and measures the round trip; 90 s
  without a line ends the link, whatever the transport says. An unknown
  method answers `unknown-method`, as does a `peer.*` method this server
  doesn't export. The peer's requests other than `link.ping` go to the
  peer dispatcher (§16.9), each in its own goroutine, at most 32 at
  once (more: `refused`), cancelled when the link ends. Binary frames
  carry remote panes between two lines (§16.12).
- **Backoff**: a link that ends or fails dials again after 1 s doubling
  to 60 s, each wait between half and all of that; a link that came up
  starts again at 1 s. A link room the relay timed out (its hour) is no
  failure.
- **State**, in `server.status` as `link: {id, short, error, peers: [{alias,
  id, transport, state, reason, since, error, retry, rtt_ms, version,
  link_protocol, caps, relay_version}]}` (none without an identity key;
  `relay_version` is the relay's `tm` from its challenge, §16.2) and in `tm
  server status`:

| state | reason | when |
|---|---|---|
| `connecting` | | dialling, or proving keys and saying hello |
| `up` | | the hellos are through; `rtt_ms` the last ping |
| `offline` | `peer` | the relay is up and the peer isn't in the room (waiting, or it left: 4410) |
| `offline` | `relay` | the relay can't be reached, refused us or closed (shutdown) |
| `offline` | `refused` | the other end's key isn't the pinned one, or it refused ours |
| `offline` | `version` | the peer's link protocol is too old |

  `since` is when the state began, `retry` the next dial, `error` the last
  failure in words. The server's log has each change.

### 16.8 Pairing

`tm peer`: the human pairs this server with another through a relay,
with a one-time code passed between the two people out of band (a chat,
a call), never through the relay. The name is `peer`, not `link`: `tm
link` is project links (§7.9). Pairing fills `state/peer/` (§16.7) and
asks a running server to read it again (`peer.reload`).

| command | does |
|---|---|
| `tm peer pair [--relay URL] [--as ALIAS]` | makes a code, prints it and waits up to 10 minutes for the other side to join |
| `tm peer join CODE [--as ALIAS]` | joins the pairing a code names |
| `tm peer list [--json]` | this server's id, then each peer: alias, id, relay, link state |
| `tm peer remove ALIAS` | drops the peer and its link at once |
| `tm peer rotate [--yes]` | a new identity key, after a `y`; every peer must pair again |
| `tm peer panes ALIAS off\|view\|write` | what the peer may do with this server's panes (§16.12); off by default |
| `tm peer relay ALIAS ADDRESS` | the relay the peer's link goes through: it moved, or has a new key (§16.1) |
| `tm peer relay-update ALIAS` | asks the relay of the peer's link to update itself to the latest official release (§16.2): it says what came of it, exit 1 with `relay-update-<result>` when not `queued` |

- **Human only.** Every `tm peer` command refuses an agent (`human-only`,
  from the server's view of the caller as for `tm relay serve`), and
  `peer.reload` is a human-only method (the human socket, §3.2). The
  CLI does the pairing itself, in the human's terminal, as the console
  does from the settings' Peers tab (`n`, `j`; §4), and writes
  `state/peer/` under a lock (`state/peer/peers.lock`); a server that
  isn't running reads it when it starts.
- **The code** is the relay and an **80-bit secret** `T`, as 16 Crockford
  base32 digits in four groups: `relay.example.net/7Q4M-K2XD-9HVA-PT3C`.
  The relay part is its host (`wss://` and `/v1` implied), or the whole
  URL when it is anything else (`ws://127.0.0.1:8080/7Q4M-…`). Reading
  it back ignores case, hyphens and spaces, and takes `O` for 0 and `I`
  or `L` for 1. `--relay` defaults to the relay every pinned peer uses;
  with none, or several, it is needed. A code works once: the pairing
  ends after one join, whatever the outcome.
- **A pinned relay's code** carries its pin after the host:
  `203.0.113.5#sha256=<pin>/7Q4M-K2XD-9HVA-PT3C` (the secret is still the
  part after the last slash; a pin has none), so the joiner checks the
  relay by it and pins the same URL. A tm before pinned relays refuses
  such a code (*a host name, or a wss:// URL*) rather than dial without
  the pin; the pairer says the other server needs this release or later.
  Pairing or joining through a relay on a private address (RFC 1918,
  `100.64.0.0/10` as Tailscale's, ULA, link-local) warns that
  it only works where that network reaches.
- **Relay.** `tm peer relay ALIAS ADDRESS` (the Peers tab's `e`) sets the
  peer's relay URL, read as `--relay` is; the link redials through it.
  Each side sets its own: it is how a relay that was set up again with a
  new key, or moved, is taken, never by itself.
- **Room.** Both sides join the pair room `SHA-256("tm-pair room v1\n" ‖
  T)` with their identity keys (§16.2; made now if the server has none).
  The relay can't get `T` from it. The pairer prints the code once the
  relay has it waiting (so the code works as soon as it shows) and waits
  the relay's 10 minutes (§16.4); a joiner the relay tells to wait gives
  up at once: nobody holds that code (mistyped, expired or used).
- **TLS 1.3** inside the stream as on a link (§16.7), the joiner the
  client, each end presenting its identity key and taking the other's
  as it comes.
- **Proof.** Each side sends `{"proof"}`: HMAC-SHA-256 keyed with `T`
  over `"tm-pair proof v1\n" ‖ role ‖ 0x00 ‖ fp(pairer) ‖ fp(joiner) ‖
  E`, role `pair` or `join`, E the TLS session's exported keying
  material (label `tm-pair v1`, 32 bytes), and checks the other's. A
  relay or anyone else in the middle doesn't know `T`, so its proof
  fails (`the code doesn't match`) and the side that sees it closes
  before anything else crosses.
- **Names.** Then each sends `{"name", "version"}`: its host name (first
  label, lower case) and tm release. The alias defaults to the other's
  name (`a-z`, `0-9`, `-`, at most 32; else `peer`), with `-2`, `-3` …
  when another key has it. `--as` sets it, and replaces a peer already
  called that (pairing again after that peer rotated its key). A key
  already pinned is paired again: its entry is replaced, and keeps its
  alias without `--as`.
- **Check.** Both terminals show the other's name, server id and six
  digits, `SHA-256("tm-pair check v1\n" ‖ fp(pairer) ‖ fp(joiner))`'s
  first 8 bytes, big-endian, mod 10⁶, as `418 207`; the same on both
  sides only when each holds the other's real key. Each person answers
  `y` once the digits match the other's (read aloud, or in the chat);
  each side then sends `{"ok": true|false}`. A side pins the other's key
  in `peers.json` only after its own `y` and the other's `ok`; otherwise
  nothing is written. The answers fit the relay's 2 minutes for a mated
  pair room (§16.4). Standard input needn't be a terminal: a script that
  answers is the human's.
- **Outcomes.** Exit 0 once paired; exit 1 with `declined` (either
  side's no), `bad-code` (the proof failed, or nobody holds the code),
  `own-server` (the two ends are one key), `unknown-peer` (remove);
  exit 3 for the relay or the network.
- **Remove** drops the entry; the other side's link then shows `offline
  · peer` until its user removes it too. **Rotate** replaces
  `identity.key` and keeps `peers.json`: each peer refuses the new key
  (`offline · refused`) until the two pair again, which replaces the
  entries on both sides.
- **Lines.** `tm peer list`, without a server: `link not running`;
  `--json`: `{"id", "short", "peers": [{"alias", "id", "short", "relay",
  "link", "panes"}]}`, `link` as in `server.status` (§16.7), `panes` the
  pane switch (§16.12); a switch that isn't off ends the peer's line
  (`; panes: write`; `panes` in `--json` too). `tm doctor`'s `peers`
  group: nothing for a server never paired; else a `paired servers`
  line (`ok`: this server's id and how many are paired). With the
  server stopped that is all, plus the relay lines; with it running,
  **a line per paired server** (`peer ALIAS`): link state and why,
  latency (the last ping), when it was last seen (now while up; else
  when the link went down, or "not seen since the server started"), its
  tm version (warn when it is `SkewMinor` = 2 minor releases or more
  from this tm, or another major), the pane level each side allows
  (`off`, `view`, `write`: this side's from `peers.json`, the peer's from
  the mirror), the projects shared each way (this server's shared
  count; the peer's from the mirror), and the messages waiting: queued
  in the outbox, undelivered (refused or expired; `link.Backlogs`).
  **A line per relay in use** (`relay URL`, one for the peers that
  meet through it): `GET /healthz` with a short timeout; for a CA
  certificate the days left, for a pinned IP relay whether it shows
  the pinned key (dates aren't checked there); the relay's tm version
  (what the links' challenges said; `/healthz` doesn't tell it) and `update available` when a newer release is out; and
  `auto-update on|off` when the relay says it (the `Tm-Auto-Update`
  header, set from `Config.AutoUpdate`; the installed units don't set
  it yet, so doctor shows nothing). Levels: `ok` when all is fine;
  `warn` for an offline peer, a skewed tm, undelivered messages, a
  CA certificate with under 14 days left (or expired), an
  unreachable relay or one that is behind; `fail` for a pinned relay
  that shows another key, or an identity key or `peers.json` that
  can't be read. `--json` carries the same fields on each line: `peer`
  (`alias`, `id`, `relay`, `state`, `reason`, `latency_ms`,
  `last_seen`, `version`, `skew`, `panes_mine`, `panes_theirs`,
  `shared_out`, `shared_in`, `queued`, `undelivered`) and `relay`
  (`url`, `pinned`, `peers`, `reachable`, `error`, `cert_days`,
  `pin_match`, `version`, `update_available`, `auto_update`). The
  human's: agents see only `server.status`.

### 16.9 Sharing projects

What a paired server may read of this one. Nothing crosses until the
user shares a project, and nothing that crosses can act here.

- **Share** is a project's own setting, the human's: `share = true` in
  its `[projects.<slug>]` table (§11.2), set in the project's Settings
  tab, which asks first. `[defaults]` can't set it. A shared project is
  offered to every paired server while it is active and not archived;
  turning share off, deactivating or archiving it hides it from the
  next request on.
- **The peer dispatcher.** A link's requests (§16.7) reach their own
  short list of methods, under the caller `peer:<alias>` (§11.1), never
  the control API: no `session.*`, `project.*`, `server.*` or
  `cli.run`, nothing that writes. The server lends it `peer.Exporter`,
  a narrow view that answers only for shared projects; `peer.panes`
  (§16.12) only sets what this server knows of the peer's own pane
  switch. A server that exports lists `watch` in its hello; an error that isn't a protocol
  error crosses as `internal`, without its words.
- **`peer.projects`** → `{"projects": [{"project", "goal", "paused",
  "needs_you", "threads", "ready"}], "now"}`: the shared projects, by
  slug, with the goal line and counts from their `ProjectWatch`. `now`
  is the server's clock as it answers, here and in `peer.watch`, for the
  reader's clock offset (§16.10).
- **`peer.watch`** `{"project", "rev", "wait"}` → `{"rev", "now", "watch",
  "sessions"}`: a long poll. It answers at once when the project's state
  differs from `rev` (none: at once), else when it changes (the
  server's own wakes, or a re-read every 2 s), else after `wait`
  seconds (at most and by default 50) with the same state. `rev` names
  a state (a hash of its JSON, without `rev` and `now`). `watch` is the project's `ProjectWatch`
  as `project.watch` gives it (§3.3, **Watch**), its `links` only to
  other shared projects, copied field by field as `sessions` are: no
  mods' cells, no question's text (a thread's `needs_you` and a need's
  `question` say `a question in its session` instead), and of each inbox
  item only its kind, count, task, title and whether it needs the user,
  never its summary. `sessions` are the project's sessions, each
  `{"id", "role", "agent", "project", "thread", "task", "created",
  "state", "reason", "todos_done", "todos_total", "current",
  "queued_prompts", "remote_control", "model", "asking", "context",
  "context_window"}`: copied field by field, so a field added to
  `SessionInfo` stays home until added here. Never `argv`, `cwd`,
  `pid`, `agent_session_id`, the terminal title, mods' cells or a
  question's text (`asking` says one is open).
- **Refusals.** A slug that isn't shared answers `not-shared` (`no
  shared project "x"`), the same whether the project exists, is
  unshared, inactive or archived; no slug is `bad-params`.
- **Never crosses**: `config.toml`, `ui.json`, keys, tokens, `state/`,
  `run/`, paths, repos, worktrees, briefs, reports' bodies, library
  files, uploads, memory, the journal. No method reads them.

### 16.10 The peer mirror

What a server keeps of its paired servers' shared projects, for its own
consoles: a remote project has no folder here, so a console can't read it
from disk as it reads a local one (`tui.ServerSource.Load`, from the
server's `projects.watch` or the files) and asks its own server instead. The console never talks to a link.

- **Reading.** `peer.Mirror` follows the links (§16.7) every 0.5 s.
  While a link is up and the peer's hello lists `watch`, it calls
  `peer.projects` (at once, then every 30 s: a project shared or unshared
  there shows within it) and long-polls `peer.watch` on each shared
  project, with the `rev` it has. A project whose watch answers
  `not-shared`, or that the list leaves out, goes. A failed read waits
  2 s; the server's log has the first failure of a run, not each.
- **Memory only.** The mirror is the latest answer per server id and
  slug, rebuilt after every restart and every link up; nothing of a peer
  is written to disk. The same slug on two servers is two entries.
- **Stale.** A link that leaves `up` keeps its projects' last snapshots
  and marks them stale, as does a failed read; the first watch after it
  comes back asks with no `rev`, so it answers at once and clears stale
  without waiting for a change. `synced` is when this server last read
  the project, on its own clock.
- **Clock offset.** Each answer carries the peer's `now`. The offset is
  `now − received + rtt/2` (the link's last ping), the peer's clock
  minus this one; the peer's own times (a session's `created`, the
  ticker's) less the offset are this server's. Nothing compares the two
  clocks for any decision.
- **`peer.list`** (the human's: the human socket, §3.2; a link never
  reaches it) → `{"gen", "peers": [{"alias", "id", "state", "reason",
  "since", "watch", "offset_ms", "rtt_ms", "projects": [{"project", "goal",
  "paused", "needs_you", "threads", "ready", "stale", "synced", "rev",
  "watch", "sessions"}]}]}`: every paired server by alias, its link as it
  is now (`watch`: its hello lists the capability; without, no
  projects; `rtt_ms`: its last ping while up, which alone doesn't bump
  `gen`), and its projects by slug, the counts from the last
  snapshot, `watch` and `sessions` as `peer.watch` gave them (none before
  the first). A project is stale while its link isn't up. `gen` counts
  the mirror's changes, so a console that sees the same `gen` has
  nothing new; each change also wakes the server's watches. A server
  without an identity key answers `{"gen": 0, "peers": []}`.

### 16.11 Links across servers

Coordinator links (§7.9) to a project on a paired server. Only messages
cross, each server checks its own side, and the text stays data.

- **The key.** `[projects.acme.links] "mini/api" = "both"`: the paired
  server's alias (§16.8) and the project's slug there, quoted. `ask`
  is on unless the entry says `ask = false`, so a coordinator's message
  waits for the user's OK by default. `tm project link acme mini/api
  [both|send|receive|off] [--ask|--no-ask]` writes only this side (the
  other is that server's user's) and refuses an alias that isn't paired
  (`unknown-server`); so does the Settings tab's Linked projects list
  (§4), which lists each paired server's shared projects from the
  mirror (§16.10) and says what the messages tell of the other side
  (`linked back both ways`, `refuses ours`, `their side unknown`); `tm doctor` warns about an entry whose server
  isn't paired. Messages live in `messages/@mini/api/` (no slug has an
  `@`).
- **Link requests.** `tm project link acme mini/api [both|send|receive]
  --request`, or `r` in the Linked projects list (§4), sets this side
  (as above) and asks mini's user to set theirs: a request `R-<fp>-<n>`
  (the message counter) queued in the outbox as `peer.message` kind
  `link-request` with `allow`, this side's direction, and `by =
  "human"`. Requests are the human's (`human-only` for an agent); one
  waits per pair (`request-pending`), and at most 5 an hour go to one
  server (`rate-limited`). The receiving server takes one only for a
  project that is there, not archived and shared (§16.9), else
  `not-shared`, alike for each; the `fp` must be the sending peer's
  (`bad-message`); at most 5 an hour from one server (`rate-limited`).
  It keeps the request (`state/links.json`, the newest per project,
  peer and direction; a newer one from the same project replaces one
  still waiting) and raises a `link-request` item, `needs_user`,
  subject `studio/acme`, in fixed words (`studio/acme asks to link with
  api: send only on its side, so receive only here; accept or decline
  it in the Messages tab (or tm link accept|decline R-4F2A-18)`); NEEDS
  YOU counts it (`1 link request waits`, enter opens Messages). Its
  user accepts (`A`, §4; `tm link accept R-… [both|send|receive]
  [--ask|--no-ask]`, the mirror and ask first by default, or the
  entry's ask when there is one) or declines (`x`; `tm link decline
  R-…`); the coordinator can't (`human-only`) and tells the user.
  Accepting writes only that server's own entry, `config.SetLink`;
  declining writes nothing; no server ever writes the other's
  config. Either way the item is done and the answer goes back the
  same way, kind `link-answer`, the request's id and `allow` that
  side's entry (`off` declined). The asking side marks its request
  `accepted` or `declined` with the answer, raises a `link-answer`
  item (no `needs_user`) and the Linked projects list's other-side
  column says it. A request not delivered in 72 hours, or refused
  there, is `undelivered` with a `link-undelivered` item in fixed
  words per code (`not-shared`, `rate-limited`, and for `bad-message`
  `a tm before 0.28.0 doesn't take link requests; its user sets that
  side by hand`); one unanswered for 7 days `expired` on both sides,
  the item done. Journaled: `link.request mini/api R-4F2A-18 (both)`,
  `link.accepted` / `link.declined` and `link.request-expired` here;
  `link.requested studio/acme R-4F2A-18 (both)`, `link.accept
  studio/acme R-4F2A-18 (receive ask)` / `link.decline` there; never
  more than the id and the direction. **Compatibility:** the link
  protocol and the hello's capabilities don't change: a server before
  0.28.0 answers the unknown kind (and the `R-` id) `refused`,
  `bad-message`, and takes its `seq`, so the outbox goes on; its
  answer never stalls the messages behind it.
- **Both sides.** The sender checks its entry sends; the receiving
  server checks its own entry for `<its alias for the sender>/<sending
  slug>` receives, the alias taken from the link, never the message. A
  project there that doesn't exist, is archived, or doesn't receive
  answers alike, `not-linked`.
- **Ids.** `M-<fp>-<n>`: `fp` the first four hex digits of the sending
  server's id (§16.7), `n` the server-wide message counter. The
  receiver refuses an id whose `fp` isn't the sending peer's.
- **Outbox.** Sending (or approving a held message) keeps the sender's
  copy as `queued` and writes the message to
  `state/link/outbox/<alias>/<seq>.json`; the rate token and the breaker
  count are spent then. `send` says `queued M-4F2A-17 for mini/api …`.
  Attachments don't cross: `--attach` on such a link is
  `bad-attachment`. The server's flusher wakes on a send and every 3 s,
  and delivers each peer's entries in sequence order with
  `peer.message` while its link is up and lists `msg`; a failed call or
  a `retry` answer stops that peer until the next wake.
- **`peer.message`** `{"epoch", "seq", "kind", "from", "to", "id",
  "created", "re", "by", "text", "allow"}` → `{"status", "code"}`: `kind` is
  `message` or `read`; `status` `ok` (taken: the delivered receipt),
  `duplicate`, `retry` or `refused`, with `code` one of `not-linked`,
  `link-paused`, `bad-message`, `rate-limited` (and for a
  `link-request` `not-shared`). `kind` may also be `link-request` or
  `link-answer` (Link requests, above), with `allow`. It is in the peer
  dispatcher's list (§16.9) only when the server takes messages, under
  the caller `peer:<alias>`; the hello lists `msg`. Each `seq` of an
  `epoch` (a random value per sending store) is taken once: one at or
  below the highest taken answers `duplicate` and writes nothing.
- **Arrival.** The text is cleaned and checked (8 KB) again and written
  to the receiver's copy only; the item is a `link-message` with
  subject `studio/acme` and the fixed summary (`studio/acme wrote
  M-4F2A-17 (1.1 KB); read it with tm link read M-4F2A-17`); the read
  frame says `(a project on the paired server studio)`. The receiver
  keeps its own bucket for the direction (over it: `retry`) and its own
  breaker (paused: `refused`, `link-paused`); `tm link resume
  studio/acme` lifts this server's pause only.
- **Receipts.** `ok` moves the sender's copy to `sent`. Handling the
  item there (`tm inbox done`) marks the receiver's copy `read` and
  queues a `read` entry back, which marks the sender's copy `read`. No
  item on either side.
- **Undelivered.** An entry refused there, or still in the outbox after
  72 hours, goes: the sender's copy becomes `undelivered` and its inbox
  gets `link-undelivered` (`your message M-4F2A-17 to mini/api wasn't
  delivered: mini/api doesn't take messages from acme: …`), fixed words
  per code, never the other server's; the copy keeps the code
  (`refused = "not-linked"`, or `expired`). A receipt that isn't taken goes
  quietly.

### 16.12 Remote panes

A paired server's coordinator and thread panes, shown in this server's
consoles and typed into like this server's own. Both servers are the
user's own (§16.8); the far server's human decides what crosses.

- **The switch.** Per paired server, the human's: `off` (the default),
  `view` (watch) or `write` (watch and type), set with `tm peer panes
  ALIAS off|view|write` or the panes row of the settings' Peers tab (§4), which ask
  the server to read the peers again. Pairing again keeps it. `write`
  was called `type` before (tm 0.27.0): `tm peer panes` and `peers.json`
  still take `type`, and on the wire and in `peers.json` (`"panes"` on
  the peer's entry, §16.7, no key for off) the level stays `type`, so a
  0.27.0 server sends and reads what it always did, and a server that
  goes back to it keeps its switch. It applies on top of sharing
  (§16.9): only the coordinator and the threads of a shared project
  cross, never a shell or another session. A change acts at once: `off`
  closes the peer's streams (`panes-off`), `view` drops their next keys
  and `write` lets their next ones through, on streams already open
  too. The server also tells the peer at once, `peer.panes`
  `{"panes"}` (sent only when the peer's hello lists `pane-level`, which
  every tm since 0.28 does), so the peer's open panes follow: its mirror
  takes the level, a pane opened at view takes keys once raised (and
  stops when lowered), and its status bar drops or gains `view-only`,
  without reopening anything. `peer.projects` carries the switch too
  (`panes`), so a peer that doesn't take `peer.panes` knows it within
  30 s, and a stream it opens says it (`input`); `peer.list` has it
  (`pane`, the peer's hello lists `pane-vt`, and `panes`, its switch for
  this server, with its `version`).
- **`peer.attach`** `{"project", "session", "stream", "cols", "rows"}`
  → `{"input", "session"}`: in the dispatcher's list (§16.9) when the
  server has panes (its hello lists `pane-vt`). The caller picks the
  stream id; `input` says the stream may type, `session` is the session
  as `peer.watch` shows it. Refusals: `panes-off`, `not-shared` (as
  §16.9), `unknown-session` (no such session, or not the project's
  coordinator or one of its threads), `refused` (8 streams at once).
- **Frames.** Once both hellos list `pane-vt`, either end may write a
  binary frame between two lines: `0x00` (a line starts with `{`), the
  stream (u32), the type (u8), the length (u32), big-endian, then at
  most 64 KiB (16 KiB as this tm writes them). Far to near: `20`
  repaint (u16 cols, u16 rows, then `emu.Repaint`'s bytes for a fresh
  emulator of that size; output frames that follow continue it), `2`
  output, `3` resize, `6` closed (a fixed word: `exited`, `not-shared`,
  `panes-off`). Near to far: `10` keys, `22` size, `21` ack (u32: output
  bytes taken), `23` repaint request (a console's `prefix+R`, which
  reaches this server as the attach frame `17`), `13` detach. The type says the
  direction, so each end's stream ids are its own; an unknown type or
  stream is ignored. Pending lines go before frames, and frames take
  turns across streams, so a busy pane never holds up a ping.
- **The repaint** is plain VT (`emu.Repaint`): the active screen with its
  styles, modes, cursor, scrolling region, origin mode, keyboard flags,
  charsets and tab stops, and the scrollback, so the two servers needn't
  run the same build (an attach's snapshot needs it, §3.3). The stream's
  first repaint, and one asked for (`23`), carry the whole scrollback (up
  to its 10,000-line limit), sent through the flow control below like
  output; one in place of a backlog (**Slow links**) only its last 200
  rows. It loses the inactive screen, hyperlinks and soft wraps.
- **Slow links.** The far end keeps at most 256 KiB of a stream's output
  unacked; the near end acks every 32 KiB it has fed. Past 1 MiB queued
  (`session.AttachRemote`; a repaint waiting at the head doesn't count)
  the backlog is dropped for a fresh repaint, at most one per 250 ms: the
  pane skips ahead rather than lag. Each end holds the other to that: the
  near end drops a repaint that comes sooner, with the output after it,
  and asks for a fresh one once the 250 ms have passed; and the sizes in
  either end's repaint, resize and size frames are taken up to 1000 × 500
  (a zero not at all).
- **Keys and who types.** On the far server the caller stays
  `peer:<alias>`, an agent to every check, and its keys go to the PTY
  only while the switch is `write`, whatever it was when the stream
  opened. The first key
  (or paste, or mouse press; never the console's focus reports or a mouse
  release, motion or wheel, which it sends with no one typing)
  into a thread's pane on a stream raises a `takeover` item there for
  its coordinator, `the user typed into T7's pane from the paired server
  studio`, and the journal line `peer:studio thread.takeover t-0004`
  (§4); a coordinator's pane raises nothing. A size frame is that
  server's `view.input` from a console outside its views: the session
  takes the near pane's size, and its own consoles crop or pad until
  someone types there. Colours and the colour scheme don't cross.
- **The near server** keeps a **proxy pane** (`session.Proxy`) per remote
  session it shows: an emulator fed the stream, which consoles attach to
  as to a session, with the identical-build snapshot (§3.3), so a
  console's mirror, scrollback (the far pane's, from the repaint), selection,
  digest check and OSC 52 work as for this server's panes. One stream
  serves every console showing it and closes 10 s after the last leaves.
  The pane is the session `<alias>/<id>` (no local id has a `/`): a
  view's focus and `view.attach` may name it (since protocol 16), and
  the console attaches it as any session; showing it never resizes it,
  only a console's keys do (`view.input`). Its info panel (§4, **Info
  panel**) is drawn from the mirror, read-only, with no mod sections. Every
  `session.*` method refuses it (`refused`: `not on mini's panes: open
  it on mini`). Only the human's attach opens it; no command, method or
  mod types into a remote pane, and `session.keys`, `session.read` and
  `session.prompt` stay this server's.
- **The console.** The status bar leads with the server
  (`mini › acme › T7 Billing export`) and says `remote · 38 ms`, `view
  only`, or `⚠ mini offline · keys aren't sent`; its keys go to the
  program as in a local pane except on a view-only pane or while the
  link is down, where they are dropped with that note (tm's own, the
  prefix commands, still work). There the mouse is tm's whatever the
  program tracks: the wheel scrolls the local scrollback (3 rows a turn)
  and a left drag selects, copied on release with OSC 52, as over a
  local pane whose program doesn't take the mouse (§4). A view-only pane
  keeps the far session's size, drawn from the top left, cropped or
  padded as a local pane at another size is (§3.3); when that isn't its
  rectangle's, the note says it: `view-only · 120×40 on mini · 38 ms`.
  The near server raises no takeover. `SessionInfo` has `server` (the alias) and
  `view_only` for such a pane.
- **The link drops.** The pane freezes on its last screen and keys are
  dropped, never kept for later. While a console shows it the near
  server tries again every second; once the link is back it finds the
  session again by its project and thread (or the coordinator) in the
  mirror, since a restarted server may run it as another session, and
  the fresh repaint replaces the frozen screen. A stream that ends
  otherwise closes the pane, in this server's words: `session exited on
  mini`, `mini stopped sharing acme`, `mini turned its panes off for
  this server`, `the session on mini is gone`. The view's change and the
  stream's end come on separate connections: a console whose view loses
  its last session first waits up to 2 s for the stream's words, so it
  prints them rather than a bare detach.
